Modular Development Platforms and Creative Ecosystems: 
Design & Deployment for Wide Impact Across Fields 
 
by 
 
Ali Shtarbanov

May 16, 2025, in partial fulfillment of the requirements for the degree of  
 
Doctor of Philosophy  
ABSTRACT 
Physical, digital, and conceptual tools and building blocks are fundamental enablers and accelerators of 
humanity’s progress in technology, science, medicine, art, and even in abstract fields like mathematics, 
philosophy, and social sciences . Hardware development platforms  present a special class of tools and 
building blocks, facilitating and accelerating innovation, prototyping, and research. They drastically reduce 
prototyping time and complexity, improve efficiency for experts, democratize access to innovation, and 
even inspire entirely new ideas.  This research investigates how to design, develop, and deploy 
development platforms in ways that maximize their real-world impact potential. It focuses not only on the 
technical and engineering aspects, but also on the complete ecosystem a platform needs in order to have 
impact, including community building, engagement with users and volunteers, content strategy, online 
presence, publicity, deployment, feedback loops modularity, financial viability,  and symbiotic 
relationships.  A comprehensive Design & Deployment Framework is introduced as a conceptual tool for 
creating high-impact platforms and creative ecosystems, recognizing and fostering the positive feedback 
loops that sustain them and that shape their evolution and growth. This framework is applied in the 
development and deployment of multiple novel platform and ecosystem projects, including FlowIO, 
SleeveIO, and ModiStrap, as well as the ecosystem SoftRobotics.IO. Those works have benefited thousands 
of people around the world, providing researchers,  designers, and engineers with powerful, 
reconfigurable, modular enabling artifacts  that streamline prototyping, accelerate research, and lower 
barriers in fields like soft robotics, haptics, assistive technology, shape-changing interfaces, interactive arts, 
and more. A multitude of research, art, and engineering projects made possible b y FlowIO and 
SoftRobotics.IO are presented, as well as over a dozen case studies showcasing how other users across 
disciplines have adopted, utilized, and extended these systems to advance their own creative, educational, 
and technical endeavors. Additionally, this thesis also investigates various deployment models for 
hardware and introduces a new hardware deployment model for equitable access to expensive hardware 
that may otherwise be financially out of reach for many users, as well as an “earned open-source” model, 
which preserves the essence of the traditional open-source model, while eliminating many of its pitfalls.

Joseph Paradiso

 “Cheap, functional, reliable things that have the potential to 
serve as building blocks unleash the creativity of people 
who then build stuff that you could not imagine. There’s no 
way of predicting the internet based on the first transistor.” 
1:  
 
 - George Whitesides  
 “Our progress has been largely a matter  
of inventing new tools and  
improving the old ones.”

P h. D. D I S S E R T A T I O N  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
1: Introduction 
Chapter 1 establishes the foundation for this dissertation and frames the scope of this work . It 
opens with a prologue exploring the motivations and rationale that led to the pursuit of this research. The 
prologue is organized around four motivational pillars: (1) institutional influences, (2) impact 
considerations based on project type, (3) impact considerations based on field, and (4) democratizing 
creativity to accelerate progress. Specifically, I begin with an overview of notable projects, initiatives, and 
spinoffs originating at the MIT Media Lab, that have had transformative impact on the world. Then, I 
deduce a quantitative definition for the impact of a project, which I use to compare the maximum impact 
potential of different types of technology projects, arguing that general-purpose tools and building blocks 
offer some of the highest impact potential among technological projects . I also show  how impact 
optimization considerations directly led to the choice of field this work initially focused on. And fourth, I 
discuss motivations around making creativity, innovation, and project realization faster for experts and 
more accessible to people from diverse socioeconomic, educational, and geographic backgrounds – which 
ultimately helps accelerate technological, scientific, and medical progress, thus benefiting society at large. 
Following the prologue, I continue with a section about tools and building blocks, which I categorize 
into one of three types – physical, digital, conceptual – and I explore their role as enablers and accelerators 
of human progress. I also introduce development platforms as a special subset of tools & building blocks 
— discussing their characteristics and the roles they play in facilitating and expediting progress, laying the 
foundation for this dissertation. Then, I list the central research questions of the  thesis along with a 
broader subset of practical questions that this thesis also addresses. I outline my methodologies used to 
address those questions and my metrics for evaluation. 
Finally, I conclude with a summary of each chapter of this dissertation, accompanied by an 
infographic that visually depicts the content of each chapter and the relationships among them—offering 
a clear, pictorial overview of the dissertation’s overall structure. 
  
Chapter  1 
13 
 
1.1 Motivations and Deductions 
There are four main factors that have influenced the research I pursued during my PhD. Those can 
be broadly categorized as (1) Institutional influences ethos of the MIT Media Lab, (2) impact optimization 
based on project types, (3) impact optimization based on research fields , and (4)  reducing barriers to 
creativity, innovation, and project realization  for both experts and for people from all backgrounds, 
thereby directly and indirectly accelerating the scientific, technological, and medical progress of humanity. 
I explore each of those motivations in this prologue and show how each of them individually and all of 
them collectively have shaped this thesis.  
 
1.1.1 Institutional History and Influences 
Being based at the highly interdisciplinary MIT Media Lab, which the press often described as MIT’s 
“Future Factory” [1,2], made the question of what research to do for a PhD uniquely challenging for three 
reasons – unprecedented creative freedom, interdisciplinary hands-on approach to research, and personal 
desire to produce a work that is on par with other world -renowned and disruptive works this institution 
has produced. The Media Lab gives students relatively unrestricted creative freedom, compared to a 
traditional academic department, and students are encouraged to pursue highly interdisciplinary work – 
often at the intersection of not just two but a multitude of different fields – merging engineering, design, 
science, and art. Over the past four decades, the Media Lab has cultivated a rich history of groundbreaking 
projects, initiative, and innovations with widespread real-world impact. 
Early impactful examples from the Lab include the development of touchscreen interfaces, e-
readers, and videoconferencing - with Nicholas Negroponte predicting in 1984, a future where such 
technologies would become commonplace [3,4] just as they did decades later. In 1989, the Lab introduced 
"Back Seat Driver," a turn-by-turn navigation system that predated the GPS systems found in smartphones 
today [2,5]. The Media Lab also played a pioneering role in  wearable computing, with projects like the 
1993 Pathfinder system, which integrated a wearable computer with GPS and radiation detection 
capabilities [ 6]. The tradition of groundbreaking  innovations at the Lab continued in later years with 
projects like LEGO Mindstorms [7], which redefined educational robotics and became a foundational tool 
for teaching  engineering to children around the world; Scratch [8], a visual programming language and an 
online platform that democratized coding for children and empowered millions of young learners to create 
and share interactive stories and games;  and SixthSense [9], a wearable gestural interface that predicted 
the rise of AR and XR technologies.  
Multiple initiatives also  originated there. One Laptop Per Child  [10] aimed to provide access to 
digital education in underserved regions. The Fab Lab initiative [11], which emerged from the Center for 
Bits and Atoms at the Media Lab, sought to democratize access to digital fabrication tools and has since 
expanded into a global network of over 2,000 labs, empowering local innovation in communities around 
the world  in more than 100 countries . Radical Atoms  [12] a vision for material interfaces that can 
dynamically change form & function gave rise to virally popular projects like InFORM [13] and TransFORM 
[14], reimagined the physical-digital convergence. Affective Computing—pioneered by Rosalind Picard [15] 
— emerged as an entire new field creating new paradigms in Human-Computer Interaction and gave rise 
to wearable platforms for physiological monitoring research like Q Sensor  [16] and Empatica E4[17]. In 
health and medicine, Media Lab researchers  from Biomechatronics  also developed brain -controlled 
prosthetics that interface directly with the nervous system to restore natural walking [ 18]. In synthetic 
biology and material science, the Mediated Matter Group developed programmable materials [ 19], 
biologically-grown architectural structures [ 20,21], and optically transparent 3D -printed glass [ 22], 
pushing the boundaries of how materials and computation merge. 
 Over the years, the MIT Media Lab has served as the launchpad for hundreds of startups, many of 
which began as research projects. One of the earliest and most commercially successful spinouts is E Ink 
14 
 
Corporation, founded in 1997 to commercialize electronic paper technology originally developed at the 
Lab in the mid -1990s. In the early 2000s, Rethink Robotics and Jibo explored new paradigms in human -
robot interaction. The mid -2000s saw the rise of Ginge r.io and BioBeats in digital health, and Ginkgo 
Bioworks in synthetic biology. Affectiva and Cogito, launched around the same period, based around 
advances in affective computing and behavioral analytics. Formlabs and Desktop Metal, founded in the 
early 2010s, emerged as leaders in personal and industrial 3D printing. Empatica and Halo Neuroscience 
pioneered consumer and clinical wearables for biosignal monitoring and brain stimulation. More recent 
ventures include Superpedestrian (urban mobility), Biobot Analytics (wastewater epidemiology), and Tulip 
Interfaces (human-centric manufacturing software).  
Together, these projects, initiatives, innovations, and startup ventures reveal an ethos of bold 
experimentation that is not constrained by field boundaries, and an emphasis on building and deploying 
systems, platforms, and ecosystems with real-world relevance that profoundly shape the world and that 
create broad societal impact. Being immersed in this culture and environment established a high personal 
bar not only for academic innovation but of impact and real-world relevance. This context created both a 
privilege and a pressure: the privilege of being supported to pursue highly interdisciplinary research with 
minimal creative constraints, and the pressure to contribute something that is not merely academically 
novel and valuable for a small group of researchers, but also societally beneficial, valuable to a wide range 
of people across fields, and resulting in real-world impact.  
 
1.1.2 Maximizing impact potential based on project type 
While the Media Lab works mentioned above have been highly successful in terms of direct and 
indirect impact, there is a much larger number of works which did not have such wide recognition or 
impact success. Highly successful and highly impactful research and initiative  are in fact the exception 
rather than the norm, even at an institution like the Media Lab . For every high-impact project, there are 
dozens or even hundreds that don’t share such success. Furthermore, i n academia, countless promising 
and exciting projects go dormant or discontinued after publication, after a student graduates, or after 
funding dries up. Over the years, I witnessed this phenomenon in hundreds of projects form across MIT 
and other institutions including many of my own early works. This made me wonder, what could one do 
to optimize for impact? What distinguishes research projects that are highly impactful from those that 
aren’t? More importantly, are there paths one can take as a researcher that are intrinsically more impactful 
than other paths? And what would those paths be like?  
Answering these questions was especially important to me early on, because I wanted the time 
spent on my PhD to be optimized for delivering wide impact towards advancing humanity’s technological 
progress. Specifically, I sought to have an impact that is both immediate and long -lasting with unlimited 
potential. I also wanted my PhD research to have real -world benefit for people and society, and not just 
an academic benefit. And finally, I sought to have a reach across fields. I also aspired to witness the fruits 
of my work in real -time, rather than decades later or not being able to witness it at all in my lifetime. I 
wanted to avoid the fate of many scientists and inventors throughout history whose discoveries or 
inventions revealed their true impact only posthumously, who never tasted the impact of their work while 
still alive. 
From a deductive reasoning perspective, it is possible to deduce some initial hypotheses to try to 
answer those questions . The dictionary definition of impact is: a powerful effect or influence that 
something, especially something new, has on a situation, person, or outcome.1 While many paths can lead 
to such outcomes, as evidenced by the breadth of highly-impactful work that has come out of the Media 
Lab, which path or which type of project, among technology-oriented projects specifically, could would 
 
1 https://dictionary.cambridge.org/dictionary/english/impact 
15 
 
have the highest and widest impact potential? Answering this optimization question can begin by coming 
up with a working definition that can be formulated quantitatively. 
First, the impact of any technology project 
 (project) is reflected by the beneficiaries (B) of that 
project, which may include those who utilize the technology for its intended purpose and other creatives 
who learn new principles from that technology, which they can apply in their own creative endeavors. 
Additionally, there are academic benefits which include finding answers to specific questions that the 
technology was designed to address through user studies. Thus, a technology project’s Impact is directly 
proportional to the number of beneficiaries it serves.  From this, it follows that deployment is highly 
important as a means of increasing that number.  Secondly, the potential impact of a project is also directly 
proportional to the number of utilities (Utils) it offers to those beneficiaries. For example, a technology 
that serves five different needs has a higher impact potential than a technology serving only one of those 
needs, all else being equal.  Thus, impact is also proportional to the number of utilities. And third, if a 
project engenders new discoveries and subsequent projects, it also carries the cumulative impact of all 
technological progenies it enables 
 (progenies), recursively. For example, the transistor carries the impact 
of all modern technology that is enabled by transistors , and even all future technologies yet to be 
developed that are directly or indirectly related to transistors. This working definition can now be written 
as in the form of an expression: 
 (project) = B * Utils + 
(progenies). The net impact of a technology project 
is equal to the number of beneficiaries it serves times the utilities it offers, plus the cumulative impact of 
all its technological progenies. 
Having constructed a working definition, even though it makes several simplifying assumptions, it 
now becomes possible to continue exploring the question of what types of technology projects lead to 
maximum impact according to this definition. It follows then that most impactful technologies, are those 
that contain and maximize all three of those components – Beneficiaries, Utilities, and Progenies.  Thus, 
we can consider at least four different categories of technology projects. 
 
(proj) = B * 1 + 0 Single-Application / Application-Specific projects without children 
(proj) = B * 1 + 
 (progenies) Application-Specific projects that also give rise to children projects 
(proj) = B * N + 0 Multi-Application / General-Purpose projects without children 
(proj) = B * N + 
 (progenies) General-Purpose projects that also produce children projects 
 
Application-Specific projects that don’t engender new projects or discoveries are fundamentally 
limited in their impact – to the total number of beneficiaries or potential beneficiaries in the world – which 
is ultimately finite. General-Purpose projects that don’t enable new projects or discoveries have a much 
higher maximum impact potential limit, which would be a multiple of the total number of potential 
beneficiaries; nevertheless, it is still finite. Application-Specific or General-Purpose projects that directly 
or indirectly produce progenie s have the potential for an unlimited  impact, according to this working 
definition. In the case of application -specific projects, progenies typically happen indirectly, where the 
original project may serve to inspire or pave the way for one or more new projects.  For general-purpose 
projects children may result not only indirectly but also directly if the project serves as a direct enabler – 
such as in the case of a tool or a building block. Thus, the highest likelihood of wide impact would be for 
research focused on developing and deploying general-purpose projects that offer a multitude of utilities, 
to a very broad range of beneficiaries, and that can produce other projects  both directly and indirectly. 
General-purpose tools and building blocks are some of the only types of projects meeting all those criteria. 
Technological tools and building blocks have multiple levels of impact. First is impact on their  
including those utilizing them as intended and those who adapt them in novel ways. Second is impact from 
the products, innovations, or other creations enabled by the tools / building blocks, which then serve 
beneficiaries who often have no awareness even of what enabled the existence of the product they’re 
16 
 
using. And third, impact on other innovators who are not users of the tools, building blocks, or products 
created by them – but they are merely inspired by them to then create novel or derivative works or even 
other tools based on or motivated by those ide as, principles, or problems those original inventions 
revealed. And Fourth, when the tools / building blocks are directly utilized to create other types of tools / 
building blocks either in the same or entirely different domains, thus initiating recursive cycles of 
innovation that exponentially amplify their overall influence. Two examples of such tools and building 
blocks with unlimited impact are the first cutting tool and the first transistor (that served functional roles), 
respectively. (Other examples of such impact recursions will also be demonstrated later in this thesis, with 
some of the core projects comprising this dissertation, as evidenced by case studies.)  
Arguably, the most impactful technologies historically have been general-purpose tools (e.g. knife, 
microscope, printing press, computer) and building blocks (e.g. optical lenses, transistor, integrated 
circuit). Examples like those mentioned impact the lives of every person today and possibly even every 
moment of people’s lives – through the products, services, systems, and institutions that could not have 
come to existence without those technologies historically. Additionally, this also applies to concept ual 
tools like equations, languages, frameworks, and models , some of which have had profound societal 
impact and have shaped the course of history. 
This deductive reasoning process made it clear to me that optimizing for impact across fields meant 
focusing my research on the design and deployment of novel tools, building blocks, and frameworks that 
serve such functional roles. If I could develop technologies and frameworks that were sufficiently general 
to be suitable, valuable, used across multiple different fields, and useful for building other tools with them 
as well, that would have a multiplicative and recursive impact through first, second, third, and higher order 
effects. Furthermore, this also offered unparalleled flexibility to potentially create or co-create dozens of 
different projects across many different fields by being a user of my own tools or by collaborating with 
users of my tools from different fields, rather than isolating myself within just one specific field. 
 
1.1.3 Impact considerations and optimizations based on research field 
Within every scientific, engineering, medical, and even social -science field there are ample 
opportunities for creating and deploying novel physical, digital, or conceptual tools and building blocks. 
But the level of cumulative impact would vary significantly from one field to another. Some of the factors 
of consideration when choosing a field include: 
• Popularity: How many participants are there in a particular field, very roughly? 
• Growth: Is the domain growing, stagnating, or shrinking, and at what rate? 
• Significance: How important is that field for advancing society’s technological progress? 
• Contribution: How likely is that an individual can have major impact during the span of a PhD? 
• Barriers: What is the cost to develop and deploy new important tools within that field? 
• Unmet Needs: Is there a clear lack of tools for drastically improved productivity and innovation? 
• Competition: How much competition is there when it comes to deployment? 
• Time: How long does it take for contribution to have real-world impact – years or decades? 
• Duration: How long does a contrition’s impact typically last before it becomes obsolete? 
• Dependencies: Do many other fields depend on that field or borrow novel advances from it? 
Through the lens of those questions in relation to the impact-objectives stated previously, it is possible 
to eliminate many fields and subfields as being unsuitable for the criteria.  Obvious candidates for 
elimination include fields where: major contributions take too long, translation from research to practice 
takes decades, deployment is highly capital intensive, the field is stagnating or shrinking, very few or no 
other fields depend on it, and so on.  
17 
 
For some of the bullet points above, it is not obvious what the most desirable answer would be. For 
example, in a highly popular field that many other ones depend on (AI, Computer Science, etc.) there is a 
very large number of people who could potentially immediately start benefiting from a new tool. However, 
a large number of participants is also a drawback , because advances are happening rapidly and even a 
major contribution will quickly become obsolete, thus the contributor’s impact will  be short -lived or 
unnoticed. Furthermore, in a field with many participants including tech giants with practically unlimited 
resources operating in certain areas, opportunities for major contributions are more difficult to find, and 
there is a higher risk that the promising research opportunities are currently being pursued by others even 
if not published yet. Conversely, a field that is not popular is also problematic for impact. It would not carry 
many of the same risks that a highly popular field might, and there are li kely to be many promising 
opportunities for a major contribution in that field; however, the cumulative impact of a contribution is 
likely to also be low . The impact -optimizing choice, when it comes to popularity, is somewhere in the 
middle of these two extremes. 
Choosing a field that would yield the highest impact-return on investment  is similar to choosing a 
stock that would yield the highest financial-return on investment. When choosing a new stock to add to 
a portfolio, one generally seeks to find high -growth companies, with excellent fundamentals, strong 
balance sheets, early in their growth phase, with strong moats, with significant growth opportunities, 
while they are still undervalued, and before other investors have arrived. In the case of choosing a field 
that would yield high impact-return on investment, the factors of consideration are similar.  The ideal field 
for impact would be one with a high growth rate, wide reach of applications, with multiple other areas 
depending on it. But at the same time, it should be still early in its growth trajectory, before everyone has 
started flocking to it, before there is heavy competition from tech giants and industry, with many unmet 
needs, and with clear opportunities for novel tools or building blocks that could ha ve a wide -reaching 
impact across many different domains. 
The emerging field of soft robotics is one of very few that meets all those characteristics. Soft robotics 
as a field is less than two decades old as of this writing. Yet, the methods, techniques, and advances from 
that field are already now permeating and being used across numerous other domains – including 
interactive interfaces, haptics, robotics, medicine, assistive technology, wellness, art, education, 
automotive, ergonomics, automation, and more.  
Furthermore, through my own prior works, I had already witnessed first -hand the clear lack of 
hardware and software tools in soft robotics, and the salient opportunities that existed to make a 
significant and wide -reaching impact through the development an d deployment of much needed tools 
and platforms. This was also a field with many opportunities to lower its barriers to entry and to drastically 
reduce its prototyping complexity and iteration times, thereby rapidly accelerating the rate of progress 
and technological innovation not just in soft robotics but also in all other domains it touches.  
 
1.1.4 Democratizing Creativity to Accelerate Societal Progress 
In addition to the impact-optimization considerations and institutional influences discussed 
above, a set of more personal motivations and values has also shaped the research direction of this work. 
There is a common mission underlying nearly all my creative , technical, and social pursuits: to make 
prototyping and innovation  – especially in emerging fields – faster for experts and more accessible to 
people from all backgrounds, reducing the barriers posed by financial, educational, technological , 
geographic, and temporal constraints – ultimately accelerating humanity progress across all fields. 
This mission stems from the recognition that people have an immense creative talent, yet only a 
miniscule fraction of humanity has the opportunity to realize any of that creative potential. There is a 
tremendous amount of talent lost simply because of educational, technological, financial, temporal, and 
other addressable  constraints. This loss of talent is a loss for every person individually and for all of 
18 
 
humanity at large – because solutions could have already been identified and implemented for many of 
the problems that exists today on both personal level (disease, accident, disability) and societal level 
(hunger, poverty, conflict). As Max Roser writes, “The world needs ideas and innovation to make progress. 
Creative and talented people are everywhere, but the opportunity to develop is limited to only a small 
number of well-off children. The world loses out as a result.” [23].  
Our technological, scientific, and medical advances we enjoy today are the result of a tiny fraction 
of people who lived on this earth – not necessarily because those contributors were the most talented or 
most creative, but because they were among the few who had access to the resources and opportunities 
needed to pursue their curiosity. Talent, intelligence, and drive do play a role, but no matter how gifted or 
motivated an individual may be, without the opportunity to put those faculties into action, that potential 
remains unrealized.  
A common but mistaken undertone in social discourse suggests that societal progress is 
constrained by a lack of creativity — that humanity is not further advanced in medicine, technology, art, 
or science because creative capacity itself is insufficient. In reality, this is almost entirely false. Humanity 
is not lacking in creativity  but is remarkably poor at capturing and cultivating the creativity that already 
exists all around us. There are dozens of ways in which creative talent and potential are squandered, 
including wars, poverty, traffic congestion, process inefficiencies, unnecessary complexity, bureaucratic 
obstacles, production inefficiencies, systemic inequities, lack of access to resources, financial pressures, 
distractions that off er no individual or societal value, preventable stress and anxiety, preventable 
accidents, poor education, crime, fraud, corruption , cultural suppression, and political oppression . 
Creativity and inventiveness are abundant, but it is these and similar factors that ultimately diminish how 
much of humanity's creative potential is realized and brought to bear on advancing society. 
The internet and initiatives like Fab Labs, Open-Source, Open-Innovation, Wikipedia, Arduino, and 
others like them have made access to information, hardware tools, software tools, and creative 
opportunities more accessible than ever before. And Fab Labs have also demonstrated time and again that 
– that even in places where no technological innovation existed previously – creativity and innovation 
starts flourishing once people are empowered and given access to tools and resources; and then as a result 
entire neighboring community and region prosper. This shows both the positive side that creativity is 
everywhere, but also the negative side that so much of it is being lost and squandered when creative 
opportunities are absent. 
This raises an important question: how much more advanced could humanity be today if we were 
better at capturing and extracting humanity’s creative resources ? It is conceivable that by now, society 
could have already developed cures for degenerative neurological conditions, preventive vaccines for 
various forms of cancer,  fully-functional synthetic organs ready for transplant, and lifespan itself could 
have been extend to well over a century. Transportation challenges and road-safety accidents – which are 
currently responsible for over 1.3 million deaths annually  worldwide according to the World Health 
Organization – could have been largely eliminated.  We could have built permanent human habitats on 
other planets, widespread physical telepresence enabling remote physical interaction, and robotics 
performing the most hazardous and undesirable jobs. Diagnostics could  have been near-instantaneous, 
eliminating long waiting periods for critical results, while misdiagnoses became virtually nonexistent. Air 
pollution could have been reduced to pre-industrial levels; rivers and oceans could be clean and pristine 
once again. Mathematics and physics could be vastly more advanced, perhaps already unifying the 
fundamental forces of nature under a single theory of everything. Artistic expression could have reached 
unprecedented heights. Problems we believe today to be unsolvable might have already been solved had 
we been able to better capture and extract even a fraction more of humanity’s creative resources that are 
otherwise squandered and lost. This is not just a problem of the past, but one of the present, as colossal 
loss of talent and human creative resources is happening every day.  
19 
 
Some might  view the advancement of science, technology, or medicine as abstract goals, 
disconnected from everyday concerns. But those goals become deeply personal when individuals face life-
altering challenges—whether it is a medical condition without a cure, an accident without a preventable 
solution, or a societal problem without an effective remedy. The pace of societal progress ultimately  
shapes the treatments and options available to every person when they need them. Thus, even those who 
may not feel particularly interested or invested in advancing technology or innovation are completely 
dependent on that progress in – especially when it comes to medicine. Yet, much of society seems to fail 
to grasp the connection between technological advancement and the diagnostic and treatment options 
available to them when they visit a doctor. This disconnect is reflected in how governments allocate 
resources, often dedicating only a small fraction of public budgets toward advancing science, technology, 
and medicine compared to areas like defense, roads, welfare programs, property development, and 
economic stabilization. This pattern is not solely a fai lure of political leadership but also a reflection of 
public values and societal priorities. Because most people do not perceive scientific and technological 
advancement as directly impacting their immediate lives, they exert little political or social pre ssure to 
prioritize it—focusing instead on concerns such as infrastructure, stock market, and military.  
Tapping more of the creative talent available to find solutions to today's problems and to get closer 
to the better future envisioned can be approached from many different directions and angles. Addressing 
any one of the problems and barriers mentioned two paragraphs above —such as inequity, bureaucracy, 
financial constraints, or preventable inefficiencies—would already have a meaningful effect in unleashing 
more human potential. Each of these barriers represents a leakage of creativity and progress that, if  
mitigated even partially, could accelerate societal advancement. However, from a technological 
perspective, one of the most strategic starting points is to focus on both the development and deployment 
of new tools and building blocks that lower the cost, complexity, and time required for innovation. First, 
by developing novel tools that dramatically improve the speed and efficiency of prototyping and creation, 
we enable those who are already innovating to accomplish more in the same amount of time, amplifying 
their creative output. Second, by deploying these tools in ways that are accessible and suitable to people 
from diverse educational, technological, socioeconomic, and geographic backgrounds, we broaden 
societal participation in the innovation process itself. Both effects—not only enabling existing innovators 
but also empowering new ones —compound over time, resulting in higher -order advancements across 
science, technology, medicine, transportation, safety, exploration, art, and beyond.  
The platforms developed as part of this dissertation – FlowIO, SleeveIO, HydroIO, ModiStrap, etc. 
– are a direct response to the first of those two areas of focus. The Earned Open-Source deployment model 
(last section of chapter 4) is a direct response to the second focus area. The Framework and ecosystem 
parts of this thesis provide a generalization for how this approach and strategy can be applied more 
broadly to other areas, technologies, and fields as well. 
 
1.2 Contributions 
All the motivational factors and their corresponding deductions  – presented throughout the 
different parts of Section 1.1  – ultimately converged to similar conclusions  centered around the 
development of tools and building blocks, their deployment, versatility, and accessibility, particularly in 
emerging fields with high impact potential . Consequently, those areas became central of this research – 
focusing on the design and deployment of novel development platforms and creative ecosystems  to 
reduce complexity, streamline prototyping & innovation, and democratize access to emerging fields soft 
robotics, haptics, and tangible Interfaces. The focus extended beyond tool development to encompass 
every aspect necessary for maximizing a platform’s real-world impact. These areas of focus — which also 
constitute the key contributions of this dissertation — are summarized below: 
20 
 
(1) Development Platforms: Creating general -purpose, user-friendly, and highly versatile tools for 
soft robotics and adjacent domains like haptics, assistive technology, and interactive interfaces. 
(2) Deployment: Enabling researchers, designers, engineers, and makers with creative ideas to access 
the tools, even when financial barriers may otherwise prevent them. 
(3) Deployment models: Establishing new hardware deployment strategies focusing on maximizing 
societal impact rather than profit, which are still financially viable and maintain the essence of 
open-source, while eliminating many of the pitfalls that come with the traditional open-source 
deployment strategies.  
(4) Community: Building vibrant communities – in-person and online  – around the platforms, and 
creating digital infrastructure for users to post projects, learn, get inspired, and find collaborators.  
(5) Applications & Collaborations : Collaborating with users on co -creating novel applications and 
projects enabled by the development platforms across multiple fields. 
(6) Tools making tools : Developing new kinds of general-purpose tools, building blocks, or entire 
platforms directly enabled by the original platforms, which then give rise to entirely new classes 
of applications across even more diverse fields and domains. 
(7) Framework development: Codifying a generalized methodology for designing and deploying 
impactful hardware development platforms across domains, which serves as a conceptual tool for 
both developing impactful development platforms & ecosystems, as well as for analyzing ones. 
(8) Framework utilization : Using the framework to guide the ideation, implementation, and 
deployment of new platforms and the evolution of existing ones. 
(9) Case Studies: Analysing how people have replicated, modified, augmented, extended, and utilized 
the platforms presented in this work to serve their specific project needs and goals. 
This research resulted in the creation of platforms like FlowIO, SleeveIO, HydroIO, ModiStrap, and the 
SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem. The FlowIO platform, for instance, has become one of the most well -known 
tools in the entire soft robotics community today; has been utilized by hundreds of researchers, makers, 
engineers, designers, labs, and institutions on four continents across more than a dozen different domains 
and has positively impacted many people’s lives in ways both small and large. Tens of research papers and 
art projects, as well as multiple master’s theses have been made possible thanks to FlowIO across many 
fields in over 18 countries to date. It has been integrated into university teaching curricula at institutions 
like MIT , ETH Zurich, Harvard, a nd others, empowering hundreds of students through courses in soft 
robotics, digital fabrication, and tangible interfaces. The platform has also been utilized during workshops 
and hackathons, providing hands-on access to innovation tools for hundreds of participants around the 
world.  
The SoftRobotics.IO community website has been visited by tens of thousands of people, many of 
whom have posted projects, applications, and tutorials on the website, made new interpersonal 
connections, established new collaborations, and found new career opportunities because of it. In some 
cases, entire lives have been transformed, lifegoals altered, and career trajectories changed because of 
the opportunities this work has unlocked for some beneficiaries and the ripple effects from it.  
The Design & Deployment framework for development platform has provided a streamlined approach 
to the process of creating new platforms and has been employed to give rise to the platform discussed in 
this thesis. It is also now serving as a guide for others working on platform technologies.  
Hundreds of applications, projects, and ideas – many of which published or exhibited – have also been 
enabled by this research both directly and indirectly. The tools presented in this work have also saved 
users tens of thousands of hours of development time across all user groups by reducing complexity and 
prototyping time. Some users interviewed indicated that they were able to do in under a week that would 
otherwise have taken them 3-6 months to accomplish, thereby accelerating innovation and technological 
21 
 
progress. Additionally, these time savings have resulted in redistribution of that time towards pushing 
projects to new frontiers or creating additional projects altogether. 
This journey affirmed that prioritizing the creation and deployment of novel development platforms 
(a special class of tools / building blocks ) can create profound, far -reaching impact. By focusing on 
improving the innovation process itself and widening access to innovation opportunities to more people, 
this work has contributed not only to individual projects but to the broader acceleration of technological, 
scientific, and medical progress — advancing the mission that inspired it from the very beginning.  
Additionally, the impact achieved to date even before the completion of this dissertation fulfills the original 
intent of having a real-world impact that is immediate yet long-lasting and across multiple fields.  
 
1.3 Tools and Building Blocks as Enabling Artifacts 
Tools and building blocks serve as foundational enablers of creativity, invention, and technological 
progress. They are not merely instruments used in the process of innovation; they are what make 
innovation possible in the first place, provided that the social, societal, and economic conditions necessary 
for innovation and discovery also exist. As W. L. Goodman observed — and as cited by Henry Petroski in 
The Evolution of Useful Things [24]— “Our progress has been largely a matter of inventing new tools and 
improving the old ones .” Tools extend human capabilities, reduce the time, skill, and cost required to 
attempt new ideas, and expand the boundaries of what people can explore, create, and achieve. 
Throughout history, advances in civilization have been driven not only by specific inventions or discoveries, 
but by the emergence of new tools that empowered others to invent, discover, and build. From the first 
cutting tool to the printing press, from the scientific method to the microprocessor, every major leap in 
civilization has been shaped not merely by what tools do directly, but by what they allow others to do. A 
single well-designed tool can enable hundreds of derivative inventions, seeding entire new industries and 
disciplines. 
Closely related to tools are building blocks — simple, fundamental elements that can be composed, 
recombined, and extended into complex systems. Building blocks do not merely solve problems; they 
serve as substrates for emergent creativity. As George Whitesides articulated in his 2010 TED talk, Toward 
a Science of Simplicity, “Cheap, functional, reliable things that have the potential to serve as building blocks 
unleash the creativity of people who then build stuff that you could not imagine. There’s no way of 
predicting the internet based on the first transistor.” Even basic building blocks such as stones have enabled 
the creation of highly complex structures — shelters, monuments, cathedrals — as well as tools made of 
them, such as hammers, chisels, grinding stones, and axes, which in turn gave rise to entirely new forms 
of making, shaping, and building.  The true power of building blocks lies not in their individual 
sophistication, but in the generative freedom they grant others to recombine and extend them toward 
increasingly complex and transformative outcomes. Like tools, building blocks serve as catalysts of 
innovation — but their impact often grows exponentially over time, as they are recombined, layered, and 
repurposed in ways that give rise to entirely new domains, applications, and ways of thinking that were 
never imagined at the outset. 
The Meriam-Webster dictionary defines tool as: (a) a handheld device that aids in accomplishing a 
task; (b) something used in performing an operation or necessary in the practice of a vocation or 
profession; (c) an element of a computer program that activates and controls a particular function; and 
(d) a means to an end2. Building block is defined as (a) a unit of construction or composition; (b) 
 
2 https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/tools 
22 
 
something essential on which a larger entity is based3; (c) a component that fits with others to form 
something larger; and (d) something that is necessary for making or developing another thing.4 
I offer an even more broad definition that unifies those two concepts , where I define tools and 
building blocks collectively as enabling artifacts – physical, digital, or conceptual constructs that extend 
human capacity to create, solve, or build — either by directly enabling purposeful action (as a tool) or 
by acting as a unit within a larger system  (as a building block).  In other word, both tools and building 
blocks are enablers of a particular end-result, with the main difference that tools typically don’t become 
part of that end result while building blocks do. Worth noting is that some tools are consumable — they 
are used up, worn out, or transformed in the course of performing their function. Examples include solder, 
dynamite, cutting blades, sandpaper, or certain reagents in a chemical process. These tools play a 
functional role in enabling the result, but do not persist as components within it. They differ from building 
blocks in that their contribution is process -based rather than structural — they enable creation but are 
not incorporated into what is created.  
The line between tools and building blocks can also be blurred in some cases . Many artifacts can 
function as both tools and building blocks, depending on how they are used. A  microcontroller or a 
development platform like Arduino , for instance, may act as a tool during prototyping, enabling rapid 
testing and iteration, and later serve as a building block when embedded in a final product. Similarly, a 3D-
printed part might first be used as a jig or fixture — temporarily enabling construction — and later become 
a structural component within a larger assembly. This functional overlap is precisely why this thesis adopts 
a singular definition — enabling artifacts — to encompass both tools and building blocks. It reflects the 
practical reality that many components transition between roles across different phases of exploration, 
development, and deployment, and that their true significance lies in how they extend cr eative and 
constructive capacity in those contexts.  
I posit that all tools and building blocks — which I collectively define as enabling artifacts — fall into 
one of three fundamental categories: physical, digital, or conceptual, including combinations of these. 
• Physical enabling artifacts – tangible items that serve a useful function in the physical world like the 
plow, hammer, pen, printer, computer, camera, transistor, stone, timber, etc. 
• Digital enabling artifacts – exist as bits and can process other bits; those include programs, apps, 
operating systems, firmware, IDEs, some aspects of the internet, etc.  
• Conceptual enabling artifacts – ideas that aid in completing tasks without necessarily requiring any 
physical or digital manifestation to be useful. Examples include language, alphabet, equations, 
models, policies, data structures, algorithms, numbers, letters, etc. 
Each field has a characteristic set of enabling artifacts — physical, digital, and conceptual tools and 
building blocks — that serve as the enablers of progress within that domain.  This includes not only physical 
sciences and engineering fields but also social sciences and humanities. From agriculture and construction 
to biology and physics, from electronics and computing to mathematics and philosophy, each domain has 
its own recognizable collection of enabling artifacts. In molecular biology, for instance , tools include 
pipettes, centrifuges, and gene-editing enzymes, while building blocks comprise DNA sequences, primers, 
and plasmids. In architecture, tools include drafting software, fabrication machines, and physical models, 
while building blocks may inc lude materials, prefabricated components, and structural modules. In 
software development, tools include compilers, integrated development environments (IDEs), and 
debuggers, while building blocks take the form of reusable functions, libraries, and applica tion 
programming interfaces (APIs). This correspondence between domain and artifacts is consistent across 
disciplines. 
 
3 https://www.merriam-webster.com/dictionary/building-block 
4 https://dictionary.cambridge.org/dictionary/english/building-block 
23 
 
Within any well-established field or domain today, there is also a well -established set of commonly 
used tools and building blocks – physical, digital, and conceptual – that serve as the backbone s and 
enablers of that field. If one thinks of any field – Agriculture, Biology, Physics, Electrical Engineering, 
Mathematics, and even Philosophy – they can easily list some of the  common tools and building blocks 
serving as enablers of progress within that field. Even abstract fields like philosophy and political science, 
rely on physical, digital, and conceptual tools for producing and communicating ideas. For example: 
• In molecular biology, tools include pipettes, centrifuges, thermal cyclers, and gene -editing enzymes, 
while building blocks comprise DNA sequences, primers, plasmids, and regulatory elements.  
• In architecture, tools range from drafting software and fabrication machines to physical modelling 
instruments, while building blocks include structural modules, materials, and prefabricated 
components.  
• In software engineering, tools include compilers, integrated development environments (IDEs), and 
debuggers, while building blocks take the form of reusable functions, libraries, and application 
programming interfaces (APIs).  
• In mathematics, tools such as notation systems, proof assistants, and symbolic computation software 
work alongside conceptual building blocks like axioms, theorems, and algebraic structures.  
• In philosophy, tools may include logic systems, conceptual analysis methods, writing utensils, and 
word processors — all used to articulate and refine arguments — while building blocks consist of 
fundamental concepts, distinctions, and frameworks of thought that can be combined or extended to 
generate new insights. 
The tools that are available in a given field have a direct influence on  the pace and trajectory of 
progress within that field. The rate of progress in any given field is not just a function of talent and people 
but also a function of the tools and building blocks available within that field and the characteristics of 
those enabling artifacts! When enabling artifacts are difficult to access, expensive, time-inefficient, scare, 
or complex to operate, they can create bottlenecks that (1) slow down innovation, iteration, and testing 
cycles or (2)  restrict participation to a narrow group of experts. In contrast , when they are affordable, 
robust, easy to learn , user friendly , and widely available, they can accelerate discovery, democratize 
participation, and foster more rapid iteration and dissemination of ideas. Factors such as cost, complexity, 
learning curve, size, durability, maintenance needs, availability, and interoperability  of the enabling 
artifacts in a field all contribute to how rapidly a field can move forward. In this way, the characteristics of 
a field’s enabling artifacts serve as a throttle or accelerator for innovation — shaping not only what gets 
built, but how quickly and broadly progress unfolds. 
The relationship between improvements in a field’s enabling artifacts and the pace of progress within 
that field is evident across many domains . It operates through two primary mechanisms : (1) greater 
efficiency in prototyping, testing, and iteration, and (2) broader participation  by individuals and 
institutions previously excluded due to technical, financial, or logistical barriers . In electronics and 
embedded systems, the proliferation of low-cost microcontrollers  and development platforms has  
accelerated innovation by making prototyping faster and more accessible to hobbyists, students, and 
researchers alike [25]. In molecular biology, CRISPR -Cas9 has dramatically reduced the time and cost 
associated with gene -editing, making advanced genetic manipulation feasible for labs lacking access to 
earlier, more expensive technologies [ 26]. While tools like open -source DNA assembly kits have made 
genetic experimentation far more accessible  even to community labs and citizen scientists  [27]. In 
professional and industrial domains, a single accountant using spreadsheet software can now perform the 
work that once required a full team [ 28], while modern agriculture technologies—such as GPS -guided 
tractors and sensor-driven irrigation—enable individual farmers to achieve yields previously only possible 
with vast labor forces [29]. In creative industries, tools for video editing, animation, and music production, 
once exclusive to professional studios, are now widely accessible through user-friendly platforms such as 
24 
 
Scratch, GarageBand, and Blender  [30,31,32]. Some roles have even been  completely transformed or 
entirely displaced through automation in manufacturing, retail, service, and other industries – as new and 
improved enabling artifacts like Artificial Intelligence (AI) can produce the same outcomes with far fewer 
or no people required at all [33,34,35]. Collectively, these examples underscore how advances in enabling 
artifacts not only accelerate innovation but also democratize who can meaningfully contribute to it. 
While many enabling artifacts are developed for use within a specific field, others are intentionally 
designed to be general-purpose — capable of supporting creative, technical, or investigative work across 
a broad range of domains. These general -purpose tools and building blocks are often among the most 
strategically valuable, because they can accelerate innovation in multiple discipl ines simultaneously. A 
microcontroller, for example, can be used by a roboticist, an artist, an educator, or an agricultural  
researcher — each applying it in completely different ways to prototype, sense, automate, or explore. 
Programming languages, spreadsheets, and even general design principles fall into this category as well. 
These artifacts transcend the boundaries of indi vidual fields and instead act as amplifiers of capability 
across the entire landscape of human creativity. Their usefulness is not limited to a particular set of 
problems but instead lies in what they enable others to do — often empowering unexpected innovations 
far beyond their original context. 
Advances in physical, digital, and conceptual tools and building blocks are among the most powerful 
accelerators of a civilization’s progress in science, technology, medicine, humanities, and art. Such 
advances make creative and technical endeavors faster, easier, and more efficient, while also expanding 
access to individuals and institutions that were previously excluded due to prohibitive cost, complexity, or 
learning curve. This enables greater productivity and more creative output per person during a ty pical 
lifetime – including field experts and complete novices – leading to better capturing of talent and less 
squandering of humanity’s creative resources at large.  Crucially, the impact of enabling artifacts is not 
limited to their immediate use; they also have ripple effects. And through such ripple effects and a chain 
of events resulting from them , the introduction of new or improved tools and building blocks  in one 
domain often has unimaginable ramifications in another. Historically, some of the most profound 
accelerations in human progress have stemmed from the development of powerful, general-purpose, and 
foundational enabling artifacts : the printing press, the microscope, the transistor, the internet.  Each 
catalyzed cascades of  new discovery, invention, creativity, and cultural evolution across numerous 
domains. Designing and deploying enabling artifacts —especially ones are general purpose — can act as 
force multipliers for societal progress. They empower individuals to create solutions we cannot foresee, 
and they seed innovations that ripple across industries, fields, and generations. 
  
1.4 Toolkits, Development Platforms, and Creative Ecosystems 
Among the most influential categories of enabling artifacts are toolkits, development platforms, 
and the broader creative ecosystems they enable. The y not only provide functional capabilities but also 
structure the workflows, constraints, and affordances through which users engage in creation and 
innovation. Toolkits typically offer curated sets of components tailored to a specific domain or user base, 
streamlining the process of achieving targeted outcomes. Development platforms, in contrast, offer more 
extensible and modular infrastructures that support a wide variety of use cases and often evolve through 
community input. When successful, both give rise to creative ecosystems—communities of practice that 
generate derivative work, share knowledge, and contribute back to the evolution of the underlying artifact 
itself.  
Toolkits are curated sets of components —physical, digital, and conceptual—designed to make a 
specific class of problems more tractable. Their strength lies in abstraction and constraint: they provide a 
simplified, bounded environment where users can explore possibilities without needing to understand or 
build every l ayer from scratch. Toolkits such as LEGO Mindstorms, Scratch, BioBuilder, or the LilyPad 
25 
 
Arduino have made robotics, programming, synthetic biology, and wearable electronics accessible to 
broad audiences, including children, educators, and non -specialists. By lowering the threshold for 
experimentation and success, well-designed toolkits activate latent creativity and accelerate domain entry.  
Beyond their functional utility, toolkits also serve as vessels of institutional knowledge and catalysts 
of creative recombination in innovation -driven environments. As Paradiso reflects on the dynamics of 
interdisciplinary collaboration at the MIT Media Lab, “a group can encapsulate a sliver of its expertise into 
such a toolkit, which can then be used by other groups for entirely different (and quite unanticipated) 
applications... Thinking of this as a pot on the stove, the toolkits are the transportation mecha nism for a 
convective process of learning and communication ” [36]. Toolkits, in this sense, do more than provide 
components—they propagate ideas, abstract expertise, and carry them across disciplinary boundaries. 
This scaffolding effect allows people to engage meaningfully with technologies and concepts they might 
not otherwise encounter, enabling them to innovate from unfamiliar starting points. When embedded in 
a diverse creative environment, toolkits amplify innovation not by prescribing solutions, but by seeding 
unpredictable experimentation. Their most profound imp acts often arise not from their original intent, 
but from how others interpret and adapt them —producing ripple effects that stimulate invention, 
hybridization, and the emergence of entirely new practices. 
Development platforms, while often overlapping with toolkits in form and function, are typically 
broader in scope and more modular and extensible in structure. They are designed not only for bounded 
exploration but for  open-ended extensible innovation  across diverse applications . Platforms such as 
Arduino, Unity, Raspberry Pi, and the web browser are open-ended scaffolds that support a vast range of 
applications often across many domains. Platforms function both as prototyping tools and as integration 
substrates—allowing users to build, deploy, and sometimes distribute new technological artifacts atop 
them. They often have features that allow them t o be repurposed, expanded, and integrated into both 
prototyping and final products. Unlike toolkits, which often scaffold learning through constraint, platforms 
foster creativity through openness and flexibility. They support wide adoption not only through  their 
technical affordances but also by cultivating shared libraries, extensions, tutorials, and user communities. 
Those user communities often drive the evolution of platforms through contributions and derivative work.  
A software development platform  is an integrated environment that provides the tools, 
frameworks, and infrastructure necessary to create, test, and deploy software applications. It typically 
includes programming languages, compilers, libraries, application frameworks, development 
environments (IDEs), version control systems, and services for debugging, collaboration, and deployment. 
These platforms often support both graphical and code -based workflows and may include cloud -based 
services, simulation tools, and user interface builders. Examples of software development platforms 
include Scratch, AWS, Microsoft .NET, Wix, and the Unity game engine. They are designed to streamline 
the development process, reduce friction, and support scalable and rapid innovation. 
A hardware development platform consists of a combination of physical hardware components 
and software tools used to design, prototype, test, and deploy interactive or embedded systems. Core 
components typically include development boards (e. g. microcontrollers or single -board computers), 
firmware, bootloaders, hardware abstraction layers, drivers, and integrated development environments 
tailored to the platform. These platforms are often bundled with documentation, example projects, and 
libraries to facilitate learning and experimentation. Examples include Arduino, Raspberry Pi, BeagleBone, 
Particle.io, Adafruit.io, and Lego Mindstorms. Hardware platforms are used in domains such as robotics, 
wearables, IoT, and physical computing, and are typically support ed by active communities that share 
resources, tools, and tutorials. 
It is important to note that hardware development platforms often include just as much —if not 
more—software than software development platforms. In addition to the physical components, they 
require firmware, drivers, libraries, software stacks, GUI’s, debugging tools, and often even IDEs. Moreover, 
each of those must  be designed, tested, and maintained in tight coordination with the hardware. 
26 
 
Hardware platforms can be just as software intensive as software platforms, which is one reason why 
making a hardware development platform is often far more difficult than making a software development 
platform. But an even greater challenge stems from the nature of hardware itself: it is materially 
constrained, expensive to produce, slow to iterate, and subject to logistical and supply chain complexities 
that have no analogue in pure software development. Every physical prototype requires time, resources, 
and planning—not only to build, but also to test, revise, and reproduce at scale. These realities make 
hardware development platforms significantly more demanding to create and maintain. 
Development Platforms, especially Hardware-based, enable and accelerate technological progress 
through three distinct mechanisms: (1) by enabling faster task completion during prototyping, testing, 
and iteration; (2) by enabling more individuals and institutions  to realize creative and innovative ideas 
that were formerly out of reach; and (3) by inspiring entirely new ideas and applications that arise only 
because of the platform’s existence. For example, the ideas for many of the phone apps today were born 
only because of the existence and proliferation of smartphones. The same applies to many computer 
programs. The ideas for them came because platform technologies like computers and phones became 
available and people saw opportunities to do something new with those platforms. Bill Gates is credited 
for saying, “If you give people tools, and they use their natural abilities and their curiosity, they will develop 
things in ways that will surprise you very much beyond what you might have expected . Empowering 
individuals and institutions through platform technologies unleashes unexpected innovation by harnessing 
widespread creativity and curiosity. 
Hardware Platforms like Raspberry Pi and Arduino exemplify all three mechanisms. They not only 
made portable computing and electronics prototyping easily accessible for novice users, but also, they 
drastically improved the prototyping efficiency of researchers, engineers, and other experts - allowing 
them to iterate rapidly and bring early research or engineering projects to later prototyping phases faster. 
Furthermore, they also have inspired and continue to inspire countless new ideas. An arbitrary electronics 
project that a few decades ago may have required engineering expertise and a week of work in a lab with 
expensive equipment, today a similar arbitrary project - could be done in a weekend, by a high -school 
student, in their living room.  
While hardware -development platforms played a major role in this transformation of how 
electronics prototyping became accessible and simple in recent decades, there were many other factors 
too that go beyond the platforms themselves, including: 
• The advent and proliferation of the internet enabling rapid sharing and access to information. 
• New online platforms for content creation, sharing, and social interaction. 
• New business models that incentivize independent creators to make high -quality educational 
content that they can monetize while sharing it freely. 
• The emergence of modules designed specifically for popular platforms like Arduino and Raspberry 
Pi - enabling countless augmentation possibilities without requiring any electronic design skills. 
• The emergence of entire companies like Adafruit and Sparkfun dedicated to the creation of 
modules and parts compatible with popular platforms. 
• Various social and educational movements toward empowerment, making, and personal 
fabrication with names and acronyms like Maker Movement, Fab Labs, STEM, STEAM, etc. 
• Growing communities of people that are continuously contributing projects, ideas, content, and 
many other forms of resources - helping to lower barriers even further and to empower and 
inspire even more people to be makers and contributors. 
• And most recently – AI, chatbots, and code generation tools that make the process of writing, 
learning, and debugging code effortless simple. 
This partial list shows that it was not any single technology, development platform, idea, or business 
model that led to the widespread democratization of electronics prototyping in recent decades. Rather, it 
27 
 
was the emergence and evolution of creative ecosystems —complex, interdependent networks of 
platforms, tools, contributors, and institutions, often centered around specific platforms—that collectively 
transformed electronics from an esoteric, expert -only activity into something accessible, learnable, and 
widely practiced. These ecosystems are self -reinforcing environments in which users, creato rs, 
documentation, modules, tutorials, and community infrastructure co -evolve around a core platform. 
Ecosystems play a critical role in scaling the impact of development platforms by distributing knowledge, 
supporting newcomers, and enabling reuse, remixing, and iterative improvement. Ecosystem elements 
such as forums, open-source libraries, community showcases, tutorials, and marketplaces help transform 
a standalone platform into a living infrastructure that supports long -term innovation. Platforms like 
Arduino, Scratch, and Unity owe much of their success not only to their technical design, but to the 
vibrancy of the ecosystems that grew around them —enabling reuse, remixing, collaboration, and 
sustained innovation. Just like in a biological ecosystem, the various members in a technological 
ecosystem—whether they are individuals, companies, tools, ideas, or institutions—play essential roles in 
maintaining the health and evolution of the whole, bound together through feedback loops, mutual 
dependencies, and shared resources. Understanding these ecosystems and their dynamics is essential to 
understanding how platforms scale their impact and will be explored both abstractly and pragmatically in 
chapters 2 and 4. 
 
1.5 The Critical Role of Communities in Advancing Progress 
While tools and building blocks are critical  enablers of innovation, their full potential cannot be 
realized outside the context of the communities they serve. The transformative power of enabling artifacts 
depends significantly on whether they are adopted, shared, remixed, and improved upon by a broader 
community. It is in this engagement process that new affordances are often recognized, new applications 
emerge, and new variants of tools are developed.  Community is not just an external factor —it is an 
integral part of the innovation process. This process is recursive. As members of a community engage 
with an artifact, they generate feedback, create derivative works, share documentation, and develop best 
practices. These contributions make the artifact more usable and impactful for others, leading to broader 
adoption and further innovation. Over time, these self-reinforcing dynamics drive progress in a way that 
is often faster, more diverse, and more sustainable than what a single creator could achieve alone. 
As Henry Petroski observes in his 1992 book, The Evolution of Useful Things [37], the development 
of new tools and artifacts is often motivated less by the search for perfection and more by the desire to 
correct specific failures or limitations of existing solutions. It is precisely through use —especially across 
varied contexts and by diverse communities—that these imperfections become visible. By engaging deeply 
with artifacts, communities act as a distributed mechanism for surfacing such design shortcomings and 
latent possibilities. In this way, they drive the very kind of iterative, problem -oriented evolution that 
Petroski identifies as central to technological progress. 
Communities also play a central role in surfacing new affordances that original creators may not 
have envisioned, and these affordances often lead not only to novel applications but also to 
transformations of the original artifact—sometimes giving rise to entirely new categories of tools or even 
platforms and ecosystems. One example is Makey Makey [38], a simple circuit board designed to let users 
treat everyday objects as keyboard inputs. Originally aimed at playful exploration and STEM education, 
communities of artists, therapists, and accessibility advocates quickly discovered its affordances for 
adaptive interfaces, interactive installations, and low-cost assistive technologies [39,40,41]—applications 
the creators had not specifically anticipated. As the community around Makey Makey adapted the device 
to new contexts, they uncovered limitations, proposed enhancements, and developed assistive toolkits 
that repackaged the original board into more specialized formats.  A second example is the Microsoft 
Kinect depth sensor [42], originally developed for gaming. Shortly after its release, a global community of 
28 
 
hackers and researchers repurposed the device for motion capture, 3D scanning, robotics, and gesture -
based interfaces [43,44,45]. These adaptations led to the development of open -source skeletal-tracking 
libraries and purpose -built 3D scanners tailored to domains such as healthcare and human -computer 
interaction. This wave of grassroots innovation prompted Microsoft to release an offi cial SDK for non -
gaming applications, acknowledging the new affordances uncovered through community engagement. A 
third example is the use of capacitive touchscreens , o riginally engineered for direct manipulation 
interfaces on smartphones. HCI researchers and maker s discovered they could be repurposed for non -
touch sensing, such as proximity, fluid level detection, and even biosensing. Projects like Touché [46] by 
Disney Research illustrated how a single sensing input could be reinterpreted for entirely new classes of 
interaction. In all of these cases, the communities surrounding the artifact not only surfaced previously 
unrecognized affordances but also drove transformations that reshaped the artifact’s identity and 
extended its impact across domains. 
Additionally, when people come together, they build off each other’s ideas in ways that can lead to 
outcomes far beyond what any individual could have produced alone. This collective creativity—is not 
simply the result of task division or parallel contribution; it arises when participants engage deeply with 
one another’s thinking, iteratively building on partial ideas , offering alternative framings, and refining 
concepts in response to shared challenges. Through sustained, idea -driven collaboration —whether 
occurring synchronously or asynchronously—individuals actively shape and extend each other’s work.  
For example, in the development of a new product, one person might propose an initial design 
approach, another might suggest a structural change that improves function, and a third might refine the 
interface to better support usability. This improvement th en sparks a new idea from the original 
contributor, prompting yet another revision. Through this loop of exchange, reinterpretation, and 
reinvention, the group collectively generates outcomes that would not have emerged through isolated 
effort. The process is cumulative and evolutionary, with each iteration informed by the last and shaped by 
the perspectives of others. In such cases, the artifact is not simply the sum of its parts, but the result of a 
co-creative trajectory—what Sawyer describes as the emergent, generative nature of group genius [47]. 
Over time, these sustained iterative interactions between communities and artifacts give rise to 
entire ecosystems that co-evolve. In such ecosystems, the original artifact often becomes just one part of 
a much larger assemblage of components and practices. These systems exhibit resilience, adaptability, and 
longevity not because of any single technical feature, but because of the com munity structures that 
support them. The emergence of shared norms, modular extensions, third-party tools, learning resources, 
and social infrastructure allows these ecosystems to persist and evolve even as the original creators step 
back, and even as the initial enabling artifact becomes obsolete. What endures is not the initial tool itself, 
but the capacity of the community to generate new tools, applications, and practices, and carry forward 
the underlying principles that made the original artifact impactful in the first place. 
 
1.6 Soft Robotics Opportunities Across Fields 
Soft robotics has rapidly emerged as a fertile domain of innovation, offering transformative 
potential across an unusually broad spectrum of fields—from HCI and assistive technology to architecture, 
space exploration, and the arts. Its unique capabilities —enabled by compliant materials, bio -inspired 
mechanisms, and programmable physical behavior—make it especially well-suited for interaction-rich and 
application-driven contexts. However, despite this growing interest and clear relevance across disciplines, 
the field remains constrained by technical barriers that limit access and slow progress. This section 
examines the breadth of current engagement with soft robotics and argues that the time is ripe for 
developing enabling tools and ecosystems that can unlock its full cross-disciplinary impact. 
Fields as diverse as Human -Computer Interaction (HCI), Human-Robot Interaction (HRI), Haptics, 
Surgery, Assistive Technology, Space Exploration, Art, Architecture, and many other domains are 
29 
 
increasingly leveraging soft robotic actuation strategies, advanced materials, and design approaches from 
the emerging field of soft robotics. This is evidenced through recent emergence of soft robotics workshops 
in premiere academic conferences on Human -Computer Interaction, Design, and Inte rfaces [ 48,49]; 
various application-oriented research projects like Peristaltic Suit [ 50] for space exploration and Bubble 
[51] for assistive grasping; a surge of soft -robotics-based art and architecture projects like NugiTex [ 52] 
and PneuBots [53]. In the domains of haptics and interaction design, researchers are increasingly turning 
to soft actuators to create more lifelike, deformable, and conformable interfaces  [54,55,56,57]. These 
developments underscore the field’s growing relevance and its unique potential to impact multiple 
disciplines simultaneously. 
Soft robotics opens opportunities for highly dynamic tangible interfaces [ 58], haptic feedback 
technologies that feel more realistic [ 59], and user experiences that are more natural [ 60]. Soft and 
compliant actuated materials can better conform to different surfaces, including the human body; enable 
the adoption of bio-inspired principles, technologies, and actuators; exhibit infinite degrees of freedom; 
and offer the ability to programmatically control physical structures at the material level [ 61]. Example 
characteristics include electrical conductivity under large strains [ 62], thermoregulation at thin form 
factors [63], high energy density [64], self-powered actuators [65], computation on compliant deformable 
substrates [66], on-demand stiffness control [67], and self-healing sensors [68] among others [69]. These 
abilities vastly expand the hardware design palette of research engineers and interaction designers and 
offer potential for disruptive changes in interfaces, haptics, art, education, and personal fabrication. 
However, the world of soft robotics today is analogous to the world of electronics several decades 
ago in terms of its high prototyping complexity, low availability of resources, and difficulty of access. Soft 
robotics today has high barriers to entry for outsiders. And even those who are working in the field face 
major challenges in terms of information resources, cost, complexity, development time, lack of standards, 
and lack of viable development platforms. For instance, when developing a new project wit h soft
