actuators, one may spend most of their time creating a control system required for their project, rather 
than focusing on the actual soft device or the application they are ultimately trying to create. This not only 
creates significant time inefficiencies, but it also discourages the pursuit of certain ideas requiring more 
sophisticated control hardware & software. Moreover, because this endeavor necessitates expertise in 
hardware, electronics, software, and fabrication, those who lack the expertise in all of those areas, or are 
not in an interdisciplinary environment, may feel immediately swayed and discouraged from pursuing 
works based on soft robotic techniques and principles, thereby limiting the advent of this emerging field 
and also limiting the advancement of other fields that may benefit from soft robotics. 
Given the similarity between the state of the soft robotics domain today and the state of the 
electronics domain in decades past in terms of their barriers to entry, lessons from the democratization of 
the electronics domain can be applied toward efforts of democratizing soft robotics as well. While it took 
decades for the barriers to electronics prototyping to fall and for the field to become more democratized, 
it does not have to take decades for that to also happen with soft robotics prototyping. By taking lessons 
from fields that have already become widely accessible (e.g., electronics, portable computing, hobbyist 
robotics) and then applying them to soft robotics, the rate of progress and the path towards democratizing 
the fields can happen on a much-accelerated timeline compared to what it took for other fields to reach 
where they are today. Just because electronics and portable computing needed decades to go from being 
esoteric to widely accessible, it doesn’t have to take decades for soft robotics to reach that status as well 
if the lessons and the approaches from those other fields that have been on this path are applied to the 
soft robotics domain rather than having to be rediscovered. This is one of the aims of this thesis. 
The advent of hardware development platforms and ecosystems around them played a vital role in 
the democratization of electronics and embedded computing. Thus, the journey of democratizing soft 
robotics can also begin with development platforms that lower the barriers to entry into the field, and that 
reduce time and complexity for prototyping. From there the next step is deploying the platforms and 
30 
 
focusing on ensuring that users have everything else they need besides a platform. This would mean 
creating an entire ecosystem for prototyping, innovation, and research comprised of not only platforms  
but also informational resources, standards, examples, tutorials, videos, community resources, 
participatory development, open deployment model, a virtual town square, a place to share ideas and find 
inspiration from others, a place to find answers to questions and ask new ones, a marketplace for exchange 
of tools and resources, and everything else that a technologic ecosystem for creativity needs. 
 
1.7 Thesis Objectives, Research Questions, and Evaluation Metric 
1.7.1 Thesis Objectives 
The endeavor of creating an entire ecosystem is complex , daunting, and requires an in -depth 
understanding of how other platform -oriented technological ecosystems operate, the complex 
interrelationships within such ecosystems, a generalized transferable model for ecosystems, an elaborate 
roadmap for how to create and deploy impactful platforms and stable ecosystems, and a comprehensive 
strategy optimized for wide impact across fields. Providing such a theoretical framework, demonstrating 
how it is utilized in practice, creating & deploying development platforms and entire ecosystems according 
to it, and showing the resulting impact across fields ensuing from those multifaceted efforts – are some of 
the core contributions of this dissertation. Section 1.1 also showed these contributions broken down into 
nine contribution segments : Development Platforms, Deployment, Deployment Models, Community 
Building, Applications & Collaborations, Tools Making Tools, Framework Development, Framework 
Utilization, Case Studies. 
 
1.7.2 Research Questions and Practical Questions 
The main research questions this dissertation explores are the following dissertation are as follows:  
How to identify opportunities, create, and deploy general-purpose development platforms that have wide 
impact across a broad range of fields and applications? How to design and develop a platform in a way 
that it serves both experts and novices, even though they have very different needs, feature requirements, 
and levels of expertise? How to make expensive hardware technology accessible to people regardless of 
their ability to afford it financially? How to create self -sustaining ecosystems around development 
platforms? What are best practices for platform design and deployment? Besides technology, what are the 
critical constituents needed in order for a platform to have some non-trivial level of adoption?  
These main questions can be broken down into many different simpler questions. Furthermore, 
this thesis also addresses a multitude of other practical questions related to the aforementioned research 
questions and to the motivational objectives. 
Progress and Impact: What types of technology projects offer high-leverage avenue for maximizing 
impact and technological progress? What is a way to define a project’s impact? How do general-purpose 
tools and building blocks serve as both enablers and accelerators of progress? What are tools and building 
blocks, and why is it  conducive to view both of them more generally as enabling artifacts?  Are 
development platforms tools or building blocks? What is special about development platforms and 
through what mechanisms do they foster creativity and advance technological, scientific, and artistic 
progress – both directly and indirectly? Some of these questions have already been answered earlier in 
Chapter 1, while the answers to others lie ahead. 
Platform Ideation: How to identify platform opportunities? Why can looking at prior work too early 
and in the wrong way limit creativity? When is the right time to consider prior work? Through what lens 
should prior works be viewed? How to scope the specifications of a new platform? What approaches can 
be used to conceive of a platform that would be beneficial, valuable, and useful to many? What are the 
31 
 
most important characteristics that a name must satisfy for broadest adoption ? Is there a process for 
coming up with great project names quickly and easily, and what would such a process be like? 
Platform Realization: How to create a platform that is general -purpose and suitable for a broad 
spectrum of applications, yet highly optimized for each region of the applications spectrum, rather than 
being optimized for the average application? How to engage with stakeholders early in the design process 
and what pitfalls to avoid? Why should a platform be general -purpose, modular, and expandable? What 
are the drawbacks and advantages of a modular design? In which circumstances should a product be 
modular and in which it should be interconnected instead? How to avoid feature creep , especially in a 
general-purpose product that is supposed to have many features?  
User Engagement: How to engage with users early in the design process? Why is asking users what 
they want rarely a good idea, and what is a better approach instead? How to choose which users to engage 
with? Why is having fewer active users better during the development p hase and what is the optimal 
number of active users to engage with? Why does meeting the needs of just 2 users at a time lead to 
greater long-term success than aiming to meet the needs of tens of users at once? 
Creative Ecosystems: Why must a development platform always be part of an ecosystem to be 
impactful? What else besides the technology package does a platform need to be impactful? What are the 
primary constituents for a thriving platform -oriented technological ecosystem? Wha t interrelationships 
and feedback loops are there between the different parts of the ecosystem? How can the constituents of 
an ecosystem be leveraged to accelerate its evolution? What are the benefits and drawbacks of creating a 
new ecosystem compared to those of joining one that already exists? If a platform is integrated into an 
established ecosystem, can it still break away from its grip and form a separate ecosystem; or is there a 
point-of-no-return, and where might such a point be?  
Community: Where would the community -building process begin? What strategies are there to 
help build a community around a platform? How to leverage existing communities around other platforms, 
and to what extent? How to engage with users, collaborators, and volunteers, and what pitfalls to avoid? 
What infrastructure is needed for growing and managing a community? What vetting processes should be 
implemented to guard against bad actors? How to leverage the community to drive the evolution of the 
platform? 
Content: Why is content necessary for a platform to achieve wide adoption? What types of content 
are needed? What is the optimal order in which content should be created? Why do most academic 
projects get the content strategy completely wrong? When should a research paper be published and how 
a wrongly timed publication can kill a platform’s impact potential? What content is needed both online 
and offline, besides publications, for a project to be impactful? How to incentivize user-generated content 
creation? 
Deployment: Why do even well-engineered projects often fail? What are the pitfalls of some of the 
existing deployment models? Why does a deployment model that works for one platform not work for 
another platform? What is the fundamental problem with the traditional open-source model, and why 
does it cause most projects to fail? How to remedy the problems with the traditional open-source model? 
Can there be a new open -source model that preserves the core philosophy of openness but is free from 
the pitfall of how open-source deployment is done today? What would such a model look like, and how 
would it work in practice? Why does a deployment model need to adapt and evolve, rather than being 
rigid and unyielding? How to optimize for impact and financial viability rather than for short-term profit? 
How to make an expensive hardware resource available even to those for whom it may be out of reach 
financially? 
This thesis addresses those, and other questions related to creating and deploying impactful 
development platforms. It offers guidelines, practical case studies, and a theoretical framework serving as 
a conceptual tool for creating & deploying new platforms and for analyzing existing  platforms regardless 
of where they may be in their deployment journey. Those research and practical questions are answered 
32 
 
both theoretically and practically through the study of existing development platforms and through the 
development & deployment of novel platforms, an ecosystem, a website, a novel deployment model, and 
dozens of projects across many different fields enabled by the platforms. Furthermore, the discussions are 
also informed by collaborations, workshops, community engagement activities, users, surveys, analysis, 
feedback received, and a variety of other types of work conducted as part of this dissertation.  
 
1.7.3 Metric and Method of Evaluation 
One can evaluate technical or scientific work based on how technically sound it is, how it performs 
under various stresses and conditions, how well it predicts a phenomenon, based on surveys and user 
studies where people are brought to a lab to interact with it for a few hours, and based on various other 
lab-based methods of evaluation. However, even if all those metrics show success, that still doesn’t mean 
that people would use it or want it, or that the work is valuable in the real-world. Conversely, if people are 
already benefiting from a given technology, if it serves them well, if they are using it and want to keep 
using it, if it is clearly adding value to the world, and if it is  having an effect in the real world, then that 
validates that the work meets all the metrics that are a prerequisite for th at outcome. Therefore, real-
world impact is one of the h ighest metrics of success, because it requires many other metrics to be met 
successfully first. Therefore, the evaluation metrics for this this work are impact across fields and value to 
diverse groups of people. 
My method of evaluation is through case studies and data showing (1) how people from different 
backgrounds have benefited and been impacted by this work, (2) how novel research, art, and engineering 
projects from across a broad range of fields have been enabled by this work, and (3) how wide the extent 
of this work is geographically, across fields, and across backgrounds.  
Moreover, there is also a unique characteristic to this thesis, where the case studies of one project 
are themselves major research projects, which themselves have case studies, which are also major 
research projects, and then those too require case studies. This is a consequence of the earlier discussion 
on how enabling artifacts give rise not only to applications but also to entirely different kinds of enabling 
artifacts (tools & building blocks ), and then those themselves enable even more types of tools,  building 
blocks, and applications – in a recursive cycle. Some of the platforms of this thesis – namely SleeveIO and 
ModiStrap, both enabled by FlowIO – are examples of this process of tools in one domain giving rise to 
tools in different domains which then enable new applications in the secondary domains. 
 
1.8 Thesis Structure and Overview of Chapters 
Chapter 1 provided the philosophical underpinnings of this body of work, rooted in the mission of 
accelerating the world’s scientific and technological progress. It showed why tools and building blocks are 
the fundamental enablers of progress in any field, and why progress without those is impossible. A three-
fold categorization of tools & building blocks was provided – physical, digital, conceptual – and a new 
definition was offered for tools and building blocks that shows the similarities and differences between 
the two. Development platforms were then presented as a special category of general -purpose projects 
that can serve as both tools and as building blocks, that not only enable but also accelerate scientific and 
technological progress through not one  but two distinct mechanisms. The concept of a technological 
ecosystem was also introduced as a critical requirement for a platform’s potential for creating impact. 
Examples were also given of how development platforms have played a key role in democratizing certain 
fields in recent history, and also why it was not platforms alone but entire ecosystems that enabled those 
outcomes. The case was also made for why general -purpose tools and building blocks, such as 
development platforms, offer unlimited impact potential across fields with recursive effects through the 
spawning of new tools and projects they enable and through ripple effects stemming from accelerated 
productivity and reduced entry barriers.  
33 
 
A strategy was also presented for how to select a field based on the objective of optimizing for 
wide impact; and an argument was made about why soft robotics is one of the fields uniquely positioned 
for that objective. A brief overview was also shown about the wide reach soft robotics has in many other 
fields, how various domains have started depending on soft robotics, and what opportunities exist to 
create platform for soft robotics that influence many other areas as well.  
Finally, this chapter showed the various research questions this thesis answers, the many practical 
questions that also were answered throughout the research process, the objectives, and the metric & 
method of evaluation. All of those were ultimately rooted in the core mission of accelerating humanity’s 
progress, which requires real-world impact across fields and maximizing the potential for impact that is 
continuous and unlimited.  
Chapter 2  will go in depth into the idea of technological ecosystems around development 
platforms. It will establish why an ecosystem is needed for a platform’s impact success. It will answer what 
are the benefits and drawbacks of integrating a new platform into an  already existing ecosystem versus 
creating an entirely new one from the ground up, and how to potentially leverage both of those 
approaches. It will then focus on how to create impactful development platforms and what it takes to 
create a new ecosystem around a platform. A complete ecosystem framework model will be established 
for analyzing, developing, and deploying impactful platforms with five key elements: Technology Package, 
Community & Interactions, Content & Resources, Online Community Platf orm, and Viable Deployment 
Model. The role of each of those components will be discussed as well as the various subcomponents 
within them. Additionally, various positive feedback loops and symbiotic relationships between different 
constituents of the ecosystem will be identified and discussed at length. It will be shown how fostering 
such feedback loops can lead to a rapidly growing and self -sustaining ecosystem, and, conversely, how a 
platform ecosystem can quickly collapse if there is something preventing the natural feedback loops from 
happening. The chapter will then dive deeply into the first component – the Technology Package and all 
its subcomponents – answering all the questions related to the technology aspect of the platform 
development process. How to identify platform opportunities within an emerging field? How to scope the 
specifications of a development platform? What traps to avoid? Why considering prior related works could 
be dangerous if done too early? How to choose a name and what is one s ystematic approach for coming 
up with a name quickly? What are the important characteristics that a name should have, and why 
relatability is not within the top two? A deep dive into modularity will follow, listing over 90 advantages 
and disadvantages of m odular vs. interconnected systems on metrics like Customization & Flexibility, 
System Complexity, System Design, Testing & Troubleshooting, Evolution & Branching, Project 
Management, Strategy & Business, Viability, and Community. It will be also shown how the model can be 
utilized in practice both to analyze existing platforms and to develop new ones. The remaining four 
elements of the theoretical framework model will be covered in depth in Chapter 4.  
Chapter 3  will offer an example of how the theory of the first part of the framework – the 
technology package – can be put into practice.  It will introduce the first development platform of this 
thesis – the FlowIO Platform – and focus specifically on the technical aspects of it. The first half of the 
chapter will cover the FlowIO hardware package, discussing each hardware module at length. The second 
half of the chapter will cover the software package, discussing each layer of the FlowIO software stack in 
depth. There will also be discussions about system integration, various design challenges faced, how and 
why certain design and engineering decisions were made, lessons learned, and various tradeoffs faced. 
Many general questions will also be addressed in this process and practical guidelines will be provided 
about the design and deployment of platforms and ecosystems so that designers, engineers, scientists, 
and entrepreneurs can apply them in their own creative endeavors.  
Chapter 4 will step away from technical and engineering matters and will return to the ecosystem 
idea from Chapter 2, continuing the deep dive discussion into the remaining four components of the 
ecosystem framework model. All of this will be discussed both theoretically and also practically in the 
34 
 
context of creating the SoftRobotics.IO Ecosystem. The discussion will introduce five additional 
development platforms for a complete set, cover aspects of community building, providing inspiring use 
case examples and tutorials, creating content for education and inspiration, devising a content strategy for 
maximum impact, creating physical and digital spaces that foster interactions between and contributions 
from the community being served by a platform. Finally, a major part of the chapter will focus on 
deployment considerations including discussion and analysis of various existing deployment models and 
philosophies. Some of the advantages and disadvantages of various deployment models will be 
considered, especially in the context of hardware. Lessons lear ned from the FlowIO deployment will be 
shared and how the FlowIO deployment model has evolved significantly over time, by borrowing from 
other philosophies, models, and initiatives including from open source, form Fab Labs, and from the 
sharing economy philosophy. A new open-source deployment model called “earned open source” will be 
also introduced that offers the same advantages of the traditional open-source model but removes many 
of its pitfalls. This would be one of the longest chapters of the thesis, and it will provide answers to most 
of the research and practical questions from Chapter 1.  
Chapters 5 and 6  will cover two additional hardware development platforms, SleeveIO and 
ModiStrap, respectively. SleeveIO is a modular and reconfigurable hardware development platform for 
wearable haptics with a family of different types of soft robotic actuators, all sha ring the same type of 
interconnects and all of which are magnetically connected, enabling rapid & seamless reconfigurability 
and application possibilities. ModiStrap is a modular and reconfigurable robotics development platform 
based on a robotic strap that is at the intersection of traditional robotics and soft robotics. ModiStrap is 
suitable for both desktop and wearable applications. Both SleeveIO and ModiStrap were developed in 
accordance with the Framework Model introduced in Chapter 2.  Additionally, while SleeveIO and 
ModiStrap are independent platforms of their own, they can also be seen as children of FlowIO, because 
they not only rely on FlowIO but were largely inspired by FlowIO. Thus, in addition to serving as core 
projects of this thesis, they simultaneously also serve as case studies of FlowIO.  
Chapter 7 and 8 are dedicated to applications, use cases, and case studies of the FlowIO Platform.  
More than 20 different research, art, engineering, and design projects are covered within those chapters, 
as well as educational and other utilities of the platform. Chapt er 7 focuses on major research projects 
enabled by FlowIO across fields including programmable materials, assistive technology, interactive art, 
education, design, performance art, and wellness in zero-gravity space. Chapter 8 focuses on case studies 
of FlowIO beyond its use in research projects. There are three case studies on how FlowIO is used in the 
classroom, three on how it has enabled Master theses, multiple cases of user s assembling FlowIO 
themselves, as well as three case studies of people extending the platform with new features and 
capabilities to serve very specific needs.  
Chapter 9 is the final chapter of the dissertation, which offers reflections about various aspects of 
this dissertation including the role and impact of modularity in each of the core projects and the core 
framework, some takeaways from this research,  limitations, opportunities for future work, broader 
implications, and a brief conclusion. 
 
1.8.1 Thesis Structure vs. Chronological Order of this work 
This section provides an infographic that offers a visual overview of the structure of this thesis and 
the logical progression and connection between the different chapters. After the infographic there is also 
a brief summary of what each chapter is about.  
It should be noted, however, that the thesis structure is not based on chronological ordering of the 
projects, platforms, ecosystems, or frameworks presented here. Rather it is based on coherent ordering 
of this work – going from highly abstract concepts and ideas  to general frameworks, to then practical 
35 
 
realizations of those frameworks into platforms, and finally concrete case studies as outcomes of the 
implementation and deployment of those platforms.  
The chronological order of the work contained within this thesis is completely different form the 
more coherent general-to-specific structure in which this thesis presents all the work.  The chronological 
order and the flow of ideas was often completely the opposite – form the more specific to the more 
general. But it also involved many instances of general to specific. Thus, ultimately, the chronological idea 
flow was a combination of a top-down and a bottom-up approach, highly iterative, and highly oscillatory 
– sometimes taking something specific and generalizing it and then taking that general thing and making 
something different very specific out of it. And example of this is how project Bubble was one of the 
earliest projects, which led to the idea of FlowI O. Then FlowIO led to ideas of other sister platforms like 
HydroIO and TendonIO. Afterwards, the idea of an ecosystem around the platforms emerged, which would 
later become SoftRobotics.IO. Meanwhile, many different application -specific projects were being 
developed with FlowIO. Moreover, also at the same time, comparative research was also being done about 
development platforms, modularity, community building, and deployment  – which then led to a first 
version of a Design and Deployment framework. This early version of the framework was then employed 
to guide the development of SoftRobotics.IO, which then in turn helped refine the framework and iterate 
on it. And similarly, the projects that were being done with FlowIO were also informing the evolution  of 
FlowIO. Later, SleeveIO was developed and subsequently ModiStrap, which were both guided by some 
subsets of the framework and they also helped refine it. Throughout all of these processes, case studies 
were also conducted with FlowIO. Because of how co mplex, nonlinear, and multithreaded the 
chronological order of events and idea-flows has been, a chronological thesis structure would have been 
incoherent and difficult to follow for the reader, which is why a nonchronological, but highly-coherent 
general-to-specific thesis structure was employed instead.  
This structure of the thesis chapters is shown graphically on Figure 1-1 on the next page. The most 
valuable utility of this graphic is to continuously refer to it after finishing reading each chapter. As some of 
the chapters go deeply into technical and philosophical discussions at times, the reader may easily  lose 
track of the broader context in which those discussions are happening. This is when coming back here to 
the chapter summary graphic would be highly beneficial to put things back into perspective. The same can 
be said about this entire first chapter; a second reading of Chapter 1 – after finishing the full thesis once – 
would give readers quite a different perspective than a first reading would. 
  
36 
 
 
Figure 1-1. Graphical representation of chapters, projects, and case studies contained within this dissertation.

P h. D. D I S S E R T A T I O N  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
2: Platform Design and Deployment Framework 
I begin this chapter by exploring the context in which development platforms exist, arguing that an 
ecosystem is a required condition for a hardware development platform to have nontrivial impact in the 
world. I explore, from the perspective of a platform designer, the pros and cons of integrating a new 
platform into an established ecosystem versus building an entirely new ecosystem around a novel 
platform. Then, I specifically focus on the latter of the two scenarios, and I provide a comprehensive 
framework for design and deployment (D&D) of impactful hardware development platform technologies 
– capturing many of the lessons I’ve learned in nearly a decade of u sing, studying, developing, and 
deploying hardware platforms. This D&D framework has coevolved and been informed by all the research 
projects and case studies I present in subsequent chapters of this thesis , as well as by observations and 
studies of other existing development platforms with different levels of adoption. Thus, even though the 
framework of this chapter is in many ways an outcome of my research, it has also served as  a guide for 
driving the research itself – a guide that has been shaped and modified through many trials, errors, and 
iterations informed by each step along the way, in the process as illustrated in Figure 2-1 below. 
 
 
Figure 2-1. Coevolution of the Design and Deployment framework. Existing platforms inform the framework; the 
framework guides the development of new platforms; insights from those platforms refine the framework, which 
is then reapplied to further guide platform evolution—forming a continuous, iterative loop. 
Chapter  2 
43 
 
I cover each of the different constituents and sub constituents captured by this framework model 
– including exploring other more general topics in the process such as pros and cons of modularity, 
approaches to identifying platform opportunities, characteristics to consider when choosing a name, and 
many other lessons. Additionally, I also the specific interrelationships and feedback loops existing between 
the core constituents. I argue that a platform designer building such an ecosystem around a new platform 
should focus not only on establishing all the constituencies, but also on establishing pathways for the 
feedback loops to be able to take place in order to maximize the impact and the rate of evolution of their 
technology.  Finally, I show how this model can be applied in practice for both analyzing existing platform 
in any state of their development and deployment journey and for guiding the process of creating entirely 
development platform with high real-world impact potential. 
One goal of chapter is to provide a conceptual tool that platform designers can immediately apply 
in their creative endeavors to make their existing or intended platforms more impactful in the real world. 
It balances theoretical insights with actionable advice . Another goal  of this chapter  is to provide  the 
framing through which all the core projects covered in subsequent chapters can be considered, 
understood, and analyzed. The latter is also the reason I choose to put this chapter near the beginning of 
this dissertation rather than the end, even though chronologically it is part of the results from the research 
works presented in later chapters. In this way, I effectively start by showing some of the conclusions of my 
research, and then in later chapters I show the works that informed these conclusions. 
 
2.1 The Need for a Framework  
Across disciplines —from product design and entrepreneurship to modularity and open -source 
innovation—numerous frameworks offer guidance for building products, launching ventures, and 
cultivating user communities. However, none of these models adequately address the unique challenges 
faced by creators of hardware development platforms, especially those seeking to make their platforms 
general purpose, impactful across multiple fields, and suitable for many different kinds of users.  
Most existing frameworks in related domains focus on the product or venture as the primary unit 
of analysis. Product development models like Ulrich and Eppinger’s structured process for concept 
generation and prototyping [70] are valuable, yet they largely treat the product in isolation rather than as 
a seed within a larger ecosystem. In the field of design, Don Norman’s work on human -centered design 
and emotional usability [71] highlights the importance of intuitive and delightful user experiences—but it 
does not extend into how those products are disseminated, maintained, or evolved through community 
and ecosystem engagement. 
Entrepreneurship frameworks such as Aulet’s Disciplined Entrepreneurship [72] and Eric Ries’s Lean 
Startup [73] provide highly actionable guidance for navigating uncertainty in early -stage product 
development. They emphasize iterative testing, customer discovery, and achieving product-market fit by 
targeting narrowly defined user segments and use cases. These principles are effective for launching 
focused ventures, including hardware startups. However, their strategic orientation—favoring specificity, 
niche targeting, and minimal viable scope —runs counter to the goals of many hardware development 
platforms, which often seek to be general-purpose technologies applicable across domains and use cases. 
As a result, w hile these entrepreneurship frameworks support early validation and iteration, they offer 
limited guidance for cultivating the diverse, multi -constituent ecosystems required to support open -
ended, community-driven, extensible, and often open-source platforms. 
Hardware development platforms are typically modular and extensible. The modularity literature, 
such as Baldwin and Clark’s theory of design rules [74], contributes essential insights about the economic 
and technical benefits of decomposable architectures and option value. These ideas are foundational for 
building extensible hardware platforms  that can be impactful across fields , but they don’t provide a 
44 
 
wholistic approach – one that also focuses on community engagement and integrates with the 
philosophies of open innovation and open source.  
Similarly, open -source hardware platforms like Arduino and RepRap have demonstrated how 
community-driven ecosystems can enable wide adoption, yet the lessons from these platforms are often 
conveyed through anecdotes rather than formalized frameworks [75].  
Frameworks like Simon Sinek’s Start With Why [9] emphasize the value of anchoring a product or 
organization in a strong sense of purpose. While such models can be helpful for shaping vision and culture, 
they do not provide the operational scaffolding needed to build the supporting technical and soci al 
systems that sustain real-world hardware platforms. 
In her dissertation, Business Models for Open-Source Hardware, Laetitia Thomas [ 76] presents a 
multi-phase spiral model that maps how open-source hardware evolve s through stages of  growth, 
strategic alignment, and distributed value creation. While her model addresses long-term organizational 
dynamics and business viability, it does not focus on the earlier phases of platform development—where 
creators must establish the foundational ecosystem components that enable a platform to take root and 
begin growing. 
In the absence of a dedicated framework for hardware development platforms, platform designers 
are left to navigate a fragmented landscape. They typically resort to one of three strategies: figuring things 
out as they go; borrowing ideas and principles from various frameworks—like those mentioned here—and 
attempting to adapt the m to the unique challenges of platform creation; or emulating established 
platforms like Arduino or Raspberry Pi. Each of these paths presents limitations. Ad hoc experimentation 
is time-consuming and error -prone; adapted models may fail to account for the unique dynamics of 
platform ecosystems; and imitation can be misleading when the deeper factors behind a platform’s 
success—such as its ecosystem, community scaffolding, or funding model—are not fully understood or 
replicable. As a result, most platform projects—even those that are technically impressive—fail to achieve 
direct or meaningful impact beyond the research lab. 
This gap is particularly evident in the human -computer interaction (HCI) and robotics literature, 
where numerous research publications introduce novel development toolkits and hardware platforms. 
While these projects often reflect significant technical innovation, they rarely achieve sustained adoption 
or real-world integration. In "Evaluation Strategies for HCI Toolkit Research" Ledo et al. [77] analyzed 68 
toolkit papers and highlighted challenges in evaluating and sustaining toolkit adoption . In many cases, 
authors explicitly express the hope that their works will be reused, extended, or adopted by other 
researchers and practitioners. However, a closer examination reveals that nearly all of these platforms or 
toolkits do not persist beyond the publication. They frequently lack essential elements such as 
documentation, user support mechanisms, communities of user and contributors, appropriate incentive 
structures for sustained persistence and growth, and long-term deployment strategies. In the absence of 
an ecosystem with such characteristics to sustain and evolve them, those works typically remain as static 
research artifacts —technically sound but ultimately inert —rather than maturing into living, evolving 
technologies capable of broader impact.  Even among those who attempt to build an ecosystem around 
their platform, guidance is scarce on how to  do so, on  what are the critical component and 
subcomponents, the necessary interconnections and relationships between them, and the best practices 
for creating, maintaining, and growing those constituents.  
This chapter introduces a new framework to address that need. Unlike prior models, which tend to 
isolate technical or market -facing aspects, this framework explicitly considers the entire ecosystem 
required for a hardware development platform to achieve sustained real -world impact. It maps the 
constituents of that ecosystem and the feedback loops between them, drawing. The framework provides 
a structure for analyzing platforms at any stage of their lifecycle, while also serving as a guiding tool for 
creators seeking to design and deploy new platforms.  
45 
 
This framework did not emerge from theory—but from personal necessity and practice. Even long 
before my PhD, I was developing general-purpose projects and platforms with the ambition for them to 
be useful and utilized by people. The lack of frameworks, roadmaps, or structured guidelines made that 
process long, filled with trials and errors, and often resulting in  nothing more than a publication at best 
[78, 79] – thus no direct real-world impact. Through years of direct experience and repeated efforts to 
achieve real-world impact as a platform creator, I came to realize that this was not an isolated struggle, 
but a systemic challenge shared by many in the research and innovation community. In response, I set out 
to identify the principles underlying impactful development platforms—not optimizing for profit or narrow 
use, but for broad, cross-disciplinary relevance and longevity. I began by analyzing well-known successful 
platforms and ecosystems (Arduino, Raspberry Pi, Adafruit Feather, etc.) identifying common patterns and 
shared design characteristics. I then contrasted these with less successful cases which also included earlier 
platforms of my own, to understand what was missing. From this initial comparative study, I formulated a 
basic set of principles  and hypotheses. Over the following five years  I tested these hypotheses through 
projects and case studies and continuously refined, expanded, and reorganized those principles in to a 
more robust model—ultimately codifying them into the framework presented here. This process also led 
me to examine not just the individual components of platform ecosystems, but the critical symbiotic 
relationships and feedback loops that link them together and drive growth. 
 
2.2 The Need for an Ecosystem 
Development platforms, toolkits, and related technologies, no matter how well-engineered, cannot 
create direct impact in isolation. Even if one makes the most well-engineered and most amazing platform, 
it will have little to no direct impact in the world, unless people —whose needs the technology serves —
can obtain and use that technology. Such an impact requires many steps to be taken beyond design, 
engineering, and publication. It requires deployment and a community of people. It requires an ecosystem 
around the platform that addresses all the challenges associated with obtaining, using, sustaining, and 
growing the platform. This includes having high publicity, ease of discovery by prospective users, name 
stickiness and memorability, financially viable deployment model, value chain, transaction processing, 
affordability, shipping logistics, replication challenges, troubleshooting and repairs, licensing, intellectual 
property protection, developers and maintainers, a website with information, communication channels 
for users, collecting and processing feedback, cloud service provisions, integration with other products, 
usage documentation, examples, tutorials, inspiring use-case projects, community building, competition 
considerations, and so much more.  
Ecosystems represent the set of conditions that sustain life [80]. When applied to business (which 
includes the deployment of development platforms), ecosystems are complex and a vital source of 
dynamics and innovation for many technologies, products, and services [81,82,76]. A platform ecosystem 
includes all the all actors and all the resources that play a role in a sustaining the “life” of a development 
platform and evolving it. In a 91-page paper titled “ Innovative Entrepreneurial Business Ecosystems: Old 
wine in new bottles?”, Scaringella & Radziwon [83] provide a systematic literature review of ecosystems 
based on a selection of 383 articles and 10 books, identifying and discussing four types of ecosystems – 
business, innovation, knowledge, and entrepreneurial. 
Platform creators face a choice: integrate their platform into an existing ecosystem or build one 
from scratch. Each approach has unique challenges and opportunities. This chapter focuses on the latter, 
providing a framework for how to design and deploy development platforms with entire ecosystem around 
them for wide reaching read-world impact.  This includes identifying the essential elements that need to 
be present, understanding the interrelationships and feedback loops between them, and providing 
blueprint for creators aiming to maximize their technology’s real-world impact. This framework strikes a 
46 
 
balance between complexity and ease of use, serving as a powerful conceptual & visual tool that can be 
applied for both analyzing existing technologies and for creating & deploying new ones. 
 
2.3 Leveraging Existing Ecosystems: Pros and Cons 
A platform must be part of an ecosystem for it to be impactful.  While this chapter primarily focuses 
on creating new ecosystems, it is crucial to consider the easier alternative of integrating a platform within 
an already established ecosystem. This approach offers notable advantages but also comes with significant 
drawbacks, which must be weighed carefully. 
Tapping into a pre-established ecosystem can save a tremendous amount of time in the short term 
by allowing platform designers to focus on improving their platform’s functionality rather than on 
establishing physical, digital, conceptual, community, and logistical infrastructures needed to make their 
platform discoverable, obtainable, manufacturable, and usable by people. Moreover, such ecosystems 
typically have an existing user base, providing platforms with immediate visibility and an opportunity to 
reach audiences that are already engaged and familiar with the ecosystem’s standards. Another advantage 
lies in the availability of support networks  and resources that facilitate platform adoption, such as 
documentation, tutorials, and community forums.  
However, by taking this path, creators give up significant control including in terms of deployment 
model, community interactions, and even technological evolution of their platform, constraining creators 
from fully realizing their vision or addressing user needs that fall outside the ecosystem’s predefined 
norms. Reliance on an established ecosystem also creates risks of vendor lock-in, dependency on specific 
community infrastructures, tools, payment processors, and more. If the ecosystem provider makes 
unexpected changes, platform creators may find themselves unable to pivot without incurring significant 
costs or disruptions. There would also be financial costs associated with joining an established ecosystem, 
typically in the form of revenue sharing for the benefit of leveraging existing resources and tapping into 
an established community. Although leveraging an established ecosystem reduces initial investment, 
recurring costs may eventually outweigh the benefits, especially if the ecosystem imposes limitations that 
hinder the platform’s growth or evolution. 
Conversely, creating an entire ecosystem from the ground up is very challenging and time 
consuming, and one will almost always make mistakes, may miss many essential elements, and may not 
even know what they are lacking (without a framework to guide them).  The likelihood of failure is 
significantly higher, but the creator maintains full control over everything, can pivot and try new 
deployment models, and can make changes to any aspect of the ecosystem – not just the product itself. 
Moreover, once an ecosystem has been created, then additional platforms, technologies, and features can 
be added into that ecosystem. Thus, creating a new ecosystem is especially beneficial if the goal is not to 
provide just one but many different platforms, products, and service s over time – all under the same 
umbrella. Thus, the upfront investment to build an ecosystem will be distributed over many products.  
Some examples and analogies include – launching a project within the Adafruit ecosystem versus 
doing so independently; tapping into the Scratch community versus building a community from scratch; 
building and hosting a website on a service like Wix versus making one from code and hosting it on a 
personal server. A web-building platform like Wix includes a site builder, hosting, account creation tools, 
e-commerce tools, and more, while a manual approach may require custom website creation, setting up 
a server, creating a database to hold user details, developing a user-account service, software to monitor 
visits to the website, and manually processing transactions. The Wix approach is order of magnitude easier, 
but the website owner loses significant control,  and if the website becomes large and complex – then 
migration away from Wix becomes extremely difficult and costly because the service does not provide 
exporting capability, and everything would have to be recreated during the migration phase. 
 
47 
 
Recommendation: 
 Leveraging existing ecosystems is the path of least resistance and can be valuable, especially in 
the short-term, and only up to a point where the platform can still be easily detached from the ecosystem. 
However, there is a danger that a point of no return may be crossed where the platform becomes so deeply 
integrated into the ecosystem it joined that disentangling it and leaving the ecosystem becomes nearly 
impossible. For these reasons, while leveraging an existing ecosystem offers a straightforward pat h to 
adoption and growth, creators must balance these benefits against the long -term risks of dependency.  
Advanced planning is essential to navigate this balance effectively should one choose this path. Creators 
can achieve the best of both words by taking advantage of established ecosystems early in their 
deployment trajectory, while in the meantime laying the foundations for creating an independent 
ecosystem, and then launching their own ecosystems before having crossed the point of no return. 
 
2.4 Platform Design and Deployment Framework - Overview 
All widely adopted development platforms have certain characteristics in common, and there is 
always a supporting ecosystem around any successful platform. By identifying these shared traits, 
analyzing the components of supporting ecosystems, and understanding the interrelationships and 
feedback loops between them, we can establish a foundation for designing and deploying platforms with 
significant potential for impact and widespread adoption.  
The diagram that follows is the visual representation of the development platform ecosystem 
framework encapsulating the critical elements required to create  and deploy  general-purpose 
development platforms capable of achieving a broad-reaching influence. This framework categorizes many 
key properties into five overarching groups along with a structured approach. 
The framework is derived from evaluation of well -known development platforms (Arduino, Lego 
Mindstorms, Raspberry Pi, Scratch, etc.) comparing those that have achieved high levels of success with 
others that have struggled to gain traction. Successful platforms consistently embody the characteristics 
outlined in this model. In contrast, platforms that fail to meet these benchmarks often lack one or more 
of these essential elements, underscoring the value of a comprehensive framework. 
Developing this model was a multi-year iterative process, informed by the design and deployment 
of various platforms discussed in this dissertation. Each iteration of the framework involved theorizing a 
model, implementing aspects of it, and evaluating rea l-world outcomes. These evaluations informed 
refinements to address gaps or oversights, leading to a more robust and comprehensive understanding of 
ecosystem dynamics. This cycle of creation, testing, and refinement was repeated until the framework 
captured critical constituents and their interrelationships within a platform ecosystem.  This iterative 
approach also highlighted the dynamic interplay between ecosystem components. The framework serves 
as a practical guide, a conceptual tool, and a blueprint for those seeking to design, evaluate, or refine a 
development platform with the goal of achieving nontrivial and sustained direct impact. 
 
48 
 
 
Figure 2-2. Ecosystem Framework for the design and deployment (D&D) of development platforms. 
#1: Platform Technology Package 
For a hardware development platform to achieve wide adoption, it must be offered as a 
comprehensive suite that includes both hardware and highly user-friendly software. The platform should 
be general-purpose, extendable, and straightforward to set up and use. The platform must serve the needs 
of both experts and nonexperts, reducing prototyping complexity without compromising functionality or 
features. Finally, the platform should have a well-chosen name that is concise, sticky, and unique - so that 
people can remember the name from hearing it just once, find it easily in a search engine without 
ambiguity, and pronounce it effortlessly in casual conversations (e.g. Arduino, Raspberry PI, FlowIO). 
 
#2: Community and Interactions 
A thriving community of users, contributors, and institutions is integral to the success of any 
platform. This community not only drives the platform's development and applications but also amplifies 
its value through the network effect —the larger the community, the more beneficial it becomes for all 
users, and the easier it becomes to find help. Fostering interactions between community members is as 
important as growing the community itself. Those interactions may happen through workshops, 
hackathons, collaborations, and especially online. The connections formed within the community foster 
knowledge sharing, innovation, and engagement, further enhancing the platform's impact and reach. 
 
#3 Content and Digital Resources 
 Content is critical to showcase what is possible with the platform, to educate how to use it, and to 
inspire people to unleash their own creative ideas. It is important  to have not only technical and 
educational content showing what is, but also inspirational and aspirational content showing what could 
be. Technical content would include documentation, application examples, project videos, and 
instructional tutorials. Inspirational content would include demonstration videos, descriptions of ideas yet 
to be realized that are possible with the platform, as well as renderings and animations of those ideas. 
Content is also needed for conveying to potential users the experience they can expect with the platform. 
This may include videos of people using the platfo rm, reviews and testimonials from users, projects by 

49 
 
actual users, and various other forms of media allowing a person to feel the user experience through the 
lens of real users. Digital resources are equally essential and encompass a broad spectrum, from a bill of 
materials and downloadable CAD models to source codes, schematics, and example tutorials. 
 
#4: Online Community Platform 
One or more places on the internet are needed to serve the dual purpose of (a) hosting all digital 
resources related to the platform, and (b) fostering interactions between community members. Platform 
users need to have a way of asking questions, getting help, finding collaborators, finding inspiration, and 
finding various digital resources. This community platform could be one large website that provides a 
variety of services including content, forums, blogs, chats, community-pages, wiki-pages, and more. Or it 
can be a basic website that aggregates services from other providers including YouTube, GitHub, Discord, 
Facebook, Instagram, Instructible, etc. Ultimately, the role of the online community platform is to reduce 
the friction for using the platform to realize creative ideas and to connect with others who can help.  
 
#5: Viable Deployment model 
A hardware development platform must be available and accessible to people for it to be 
impactful. There must be a way for people to obtain or replicate the platform in order to use it. And this 
must happen in a financially viable way for the platform to survive long -term. A development platform 
that cannot be easily obtained or used by people is never going to reach wide adoption. Therefore, a viable 
deployment model for the platform is paramount. Furthermore, if there are components, equipment, or 
materials that are commonly used alongside the platform for the realization of projects, then the 
deployment model should focus not only on the platform alone, but also on those other items as well. 
That is because if people can obtain the hardware development platform but not the other supplies and 
materials needed for their projects, then the platform will be of little utility to them, and they will have no 
reason to obtain or replicate it. A thoughtful deployment model addresses user needs holistically. 
 
What follows in this chapter and beyond 
The following sections will explore in more depth some of the constituents of this framework, the 
interrelationships that exist between them, using this model as a tool to analyze existing platforms, and 
how to utilize this model to design and deploy impactful development platforms. Specific topics that will 
be covered in more depth in the process include - identify new platform opportunities, the power of 
modularity, advantages and disadvantages of modular design, content strategies for maximizing impact, 
and deployment considerations. Then, in Chapters 3 and 4, we will show how the FlowIO platform and the 
SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem, respectively, we built and deployed in accordance with this framework. 
 
2.5 Feedback Loops and Symbiotic Relationships in the Model 
In an ecological ecosystem, there are numerous interrelationships, symbiotic dynamics, and 
feedback-loops between different organisms – plants, animals, microbes – and everything else in the 
environment. Some organisms are producers, others are consumers, and others - decomposers. There are 
predator-prey relationships that control the population of different species. Resources in the environment 
such as bodies of water, vegetation, and their changes due to weather patterns, further influence 
population sizes, migration, and distribution. Ecosystems are always changing, evolving, and adapting to 
internal and external factors. There is a constant flow of energy and resources between different parts of 
the ecosystem, and this is the defining feature of an ecosy stem. If the energy and resource flow to any 
part of the ecosystem stops, that part ceases to exist, which then adversely affects all its dependencies, 
and the dependencies of those dependencies, which could ultimately bring down the entire ecosystem – 
50 
 
unless it can adapt itself. Every part of an ecological ecosystem has a role to play and is indirectly connected 
to everything else in the ecosystem in a complex web of dependencies and feedback loops.  
The characteristics found in an ecological ecosystem are also applicable to a technological 
ecosystem; Figure 2-3 maps the feedback loops and interrelationships found in our development platform 
ecosystem, and the dependencies between the different constituents. Each of them will be explained 
below, and the notation (a →b) will be used to specify the arrow being referenced. For instance (1 →2) 
denotes the arrow going from “Platform Technology Suite” to “Community & Interactions”. 
 
 
Figure 2-3. Feedback loops and interconnections within the ecosystem framework for development platforms. 
There are three major positive feedback loops between the platform technology package and 
community (1→2→1), between community and content (2→3→2), and between platform and content 
(1→3→1). The development platform (technology package) enables the community to realize their 
project ideas and applications, which are then published or posted online adding to the content branch of 
the ecosystem. The more users and more projects there are, th e larger the volume of content and 
resources created.  The more conten t there is, the more people become aware of the platform and 
motivated to use it, which then makes the community larger and stronger, producing even more projects 
and content, resulting in a virtuous cycle. Furthermore, this also benefits the technology package through 
feedback, feature requests, and technical contributions, making the technology better and more feature 
rich. And the more general purpose the platform is, the wider number of needs it serves, the larger the 
community around it becomes, resulti ng in another virtuous cycle. Furthermore, the content directly 
informs the platform developers how to improve and augment the technology.  
There is also a set of feedback loops between deployment model  and platform technology 
(1→5→1) and between deployment model and community (2→5→2).  For the platform to go into the 
hands of users it needs a deployment model. And the deployment model would be informed by and adapt 
to the response from users in an iterative process. Furthermore, the revenue received as a result of a 
financially viable deployment model is the fuel that sustains the platform and enables it to evolve. 
The online community platform is the place where all the content and resources are stored (4→3), 
and where the community can go (4→2) either to get something (help, inspiration, collaborators) or to 

51 
 
give something (post a project or tutorial, submit a feature request, make any type of contribution). Both 
community and content have a dependency on the online community platform. While the online 
community platform itself has a dependence on the deployment model (5→4).  
Every component of this ecosystem framework has either a direct or indirect dependency on every 
other component, and each is essential for maintaining equilibrium. If one component is weakened, it will 
cause the others to also weaken. These dependencies ar e the reason why when developing a platform, 
the designer needs to focus not just on the technology suite but also on everything else needed for the 
technology to have impact in the world.  
The positive feedback loops are what gives life to the ecosystem, and what enables it to evolve and 
grow because of the virtuous cycles they create, which not only enable but accelerate growth. The stronger 
those links are, the more rapid the growth and ev olution of the ecosystem is. The connections between 
the different components indicate how resources would naturally flow within the ecosystem, but this flow 
can happen only if there are bridges created to facilitate that resource and information flow. For example, 
if there isn’t a low-friction way for users to submit feedback or make feature requests, then the 2→1 bridge 
is going to be weak, as users have to find other ways to submit their requests, and only the most 
determined ones would be willing to overcome this extra friction. The same can be said about the other 
arrows in the diagram and the metaphorical bri dges those arrows represent. Therefore, it is still not 
enough for the ecosystem architect to focus on establishing the five components and their subcomponents 
depicted on Figure 2-2; they must also create all the bridges required to facilitate the flow of resources 
between them depicted in Figure 2-3.  
This is analogous to neighboring cities who naturally want to exchange goods, services, human 
capital, and other resources. But if there is no infrastructure to connect them, then this demand for trade 
will remain unrealized, which is to the detriment of both cities. Conversely, when a trade route is present 
to facilitate the exchange of goods, services, and capital, this pent-up demand can be released. Moreover, 
the faster, cheaper, and more frictionless the transportation network becomes, the more it facilitates the 
flow of people, goods, and capital, to the point where the infrastructure even induces new demand that 
wasn’t there to begin with. Induced demand is a well -known phenomenon in urban planning 
[84,85,86,87,88]. Similarly, by creating bridges within the platform ecosystem, they enable the feedback 
loops to start working and to accelerate the growth and evolution of the ecosystem. 
This mapping diagram serves as a tool for creating new ecosystems and for analyzing existing ones 
for inefficiencies and optimization opportunities. This ecosystem framework model was developed 
through an iterative process that involved both (1) analyzing other platforms and (2) developing and 
deploying an entirely new platform and corresponding ecosystem. Over several years, changes and 
refinements were made in identifying key components and subcomponents, defining naming conventions, 
and mapping relations hips between them. While future insights may prompt further refinements, this 
framework as it stands today has demonstrated itself to be a powerful and pragmatic tool for analyzing 
existing development platforms and for creating and deploying new ones with high impact.   
 
2.6 Platform Technology Package: Practical Considerations 
This section will dive more deeply into the important considerations from design-engineering and 
user-centered-design perspectives when creating a new development platform. The focus will be on 
technology itself along with some lessons and guidelines for platform architects. 
 
2.6.1 Identifying Platform Opportunities and Scoping Specifications 
Development platforms can be built for various technological fields and subfields , but how can 
one identify whether a field has a need for a platform solution and if so then what features a platform 
52 
 
should have. There are at least three distinct approaches that can be employed to identify platform 
opportunities and scope the specifications: 
Research Synthesis: examining a broad range of research papers, projects, and theses within that 
field and related fields; identifying technological similarities, prototyping challenges, and shared 
components between projects; and then packaging all those complexities into o ne or more general -
purpose hardware and software solutions that can serve the needs of all those projects.  
Stakeholder Engagement: interviewing people working in fields being considered, asking them 
about the challenges they encounter and the needs they have, then developing general-purpose solutions 
that can meet most of those needs.   
Field Immersion : direct involvement in the field of consideration by developing a variety of 
projects and encountering different challenges firsthand; then identifying opportunities for addressing 
those challenges in a general-purpose – rather than application-specific – way; and finally reasoning from 
first principles about what may be other related challenges and opportunities that can be solved in a 
general-purpose way.  
Each of the three approaches has some advantages and limitations. Research Synthesis of prior 
works and finding commonalities between them is based on the past rather than being forward -looking. 
A development platform created according to that approach may be able to serve the needs of prior works, 
but it may not be suitable for ongoing a nd future works. Stakeholder Engagement  of interviewing 
practitioners about their challenges and needs may offer a more forward-looking perspective. However, it 
has the drawback of assuming that people are fully aware of the challenges they face, know what they 
need or want, can articulate clearly what they are lacking and desiring, and can think in a general-purpose 
way. Field Immersion – working on various projects then extrapolating and reasoning from first principles 
about a general -purpose solution – has the drawback that it could potentially be narrow in scope. A 
development platform created in this way may not be general enough to cover a broad diversity of needs.  
Since each approach has its own advantages & drawbacks, a combined approach incorporating all 
three would be superior to any of them individually. This may involve (1) Starting with research synthesis 
to map the field and identify initial patterns ; (2) Following up with stakeholder engagement through 
interviews and surveys  to validate findings and refine priorities ; (3) Immersing oneself in the field  by 
working on a few different projects to identify challenges that may not be discussed in the literature and 
that that may not be brought up during interviews. A strategy that can be included with all three is 
crowdsourcing to gather ideas and input from the broader community. 
Other approaches area also possible. One that seems commonsense is to consider what other 
platforms or related technologies are already present in the field of interest and adjacent fields, study 
them in detail, understand their advantages and drawbacks, and then improve upon them. Based on 
observations, conversations, and direct personal experience  working in several different academic and 
industry research institutions , this appears to  even be the  standard practice for researchers in both 
academia and ind ustry whenever they start any new technical project of any kind . However, there are 
hidden and irreversible dangers to this approach! By focusing too early on how others have solved a given 
challenge, a designer may inadvertently reduce the space for innovative thinking, becoming overly 
influenced by existing designs ; and this action is irreversible. When not knowing how a certain 
implementation challenges have been addressed previously by others, the platform designer is forced to 
come up with their own ideas and envision possible solutions, without their creativity process being 
nudged in a given direction. Furthermore, in engineering fields, there are many practices, approaches, and 
solutions to problems that are accepted as standard with little to no reason to question them. Prior to 
knowing certain accepted standard solutions to problems, one would perceive variables for the solution 
space of those problems, but after they become aware of the standa rd solutions, they would start 
perceiving constants for the solution space of those problems, ultimately reducing the design space of 
possibilities they are exploring. Considering how others have done things is valuable, but that should only 
53 
 
happen after one has independently generated and prototyped some solutions. Looking at other works 
then serves not as a reference for inspiration but rather as a reference for comparison. 
One might bring the counterargument that by avoiding examining other platform attempts in the 
field of consideration, before embarking on creating a new platform, is akin to reinventing the wheel. This 
perspective holds true only if a platform already meets the needs of most users in the fi eld. However, in 
cases where existing platforms fail to address most users’ needs, they are likely to contain inherent flaws 
in their design, whether those flawed design choices are apparent or subtle. E xamining suboptimal 
platforms too early in the design process can result in  learning wrong lessons or reinforcing ineffective 
practices. In contrast, the wheel is a universally accepted  solution that has evolved and been perfected 
over millennia. Thus, if there is a solution that has stood the test of time and has been accepted over 
generations, learning from it is beneficial. But when a solution only partially addresses a problem, serves 
only a subset of user needs, and has not undergone extensive refinement over long periods, then studying 
and examining it in detail during the early exploration and ideation journey poses risks for the creative 
design process. It may inadvertently narrow the design space and prevent the exploration of  more 
innovative solutions that address broader needs. 
The platforms discussed throughout this dissertation, including FlowIO, SleeveIO, and ModiStrap, 
were all created using the three preferred approaches. A deliberate decision was made not to study other 
platforms in the field at the outset. This approach allowed for a fresh, first -principles perspective during 
the ideation and prototyping stages, ensuring that the resulting platforms were uniq ue and innovative. 
Had prior platforms been studied too early, it is likely that the resulting designs would have bee n more 
similar to existing solutions, rather than breaking new ground and creating more versatile architectures. 
Aspiring platform architects can begin identifying platform opportunities and scoping their design 
space based on a combination of a top -down approach involving research synthesis, a bottom -up 
approach based on field immersion, and a stakeholder engagement approach based on interviews. They 
can also leverage crowdsourcing to help them along the way. And although they may be tempted to 
examine how other general -purpose solutions in their chosen field have been designed, they would be 
better off to delay that step until they first ideate and prototype a few solutions independently, and only 
then compare their solution to the prior alternatives. If at that point they find that their solution is inferior 
to the alternative, they can always make changes to it.  From a purely solution -focused engineering 
perspective, one may argue that this as time-wasteful, especially if a superior solution already exists. But 
such a perspective ignores the many other – often intangible – benefits gained when engaging in  this 
creative thought process. Moreover, knowing that a particular solution is inferior or doesn’t work can in 
some contexts be even more beneficial and useful than knowing that a given solution works. 
 
2.6.2 Choosing a Name 
 A well -chosen name is highly important for wide adoption and recognition of a platform 
technology. The primary objectives that a name should satisfy are uniqueness and stickiness. These factors 
are much more important than even relatability. The goal of the name is to allow people to instantly 
remember it as soon as they hear it, and to not confuse it with anything else. This is especially important 
because, as revealed by the data on FlowIO adoption, people —particularly in academic circles —often 
discover new platform technologies primarily through word of mouth. Thus, it’s vital to ensure that the 
name stands out, is easy to remember, and easy to pronounce. 
When naming a new project, relatability to a project’s purpose or features is what people often 
try to optimize for. However, making the name relatable to the project is of far less importance than 
making the name sticky and unique. Some of the most well -recognized platform technologies in the 
electronics domain – Arduino, Adafruit Feather, and Raspberry PI – have no semantic relatability to the 
projects they represent, but they are highly sticky, distinctive, and easy to pronounce. Platform designers 
54 
 
should never compromise stickiness and uniqueness purely for sake of relatability. Nevertheless, it is ideal 
if all three characteristics are present in a name – stickiness, uniqueness, relatability. 
One recipe for coming up with a name that is unique, sticky, and relatable is as follows. Start by 
listing all the prefixes, suffixes, and words that have any relevance to the project. The words should include 
adjectives, nouns, and verbs related to properties, characteristics, behaviors, and utilities the project can 
achieve. This can be done by just creating a table with three columns – prefixes, words, and suffices – and 
adding at least 20 to 50 entries in each column. Afterwards the task is simply mixin g and matching the 
items in columns to generate about 5 to 10 unique names, making refinements to those names, and 
eventually narrowing down the list to just 2 or 3 names. The final step is to evaluate with real people which 
name is stickier and to choose the stickier name regardless of whether it is the most liked name am ong 
the alternatives. All platforms in this thesis were named based on this naming strategy. 
Another strategy that has become broadly available only in the last two years is to use an AI 
chatbot like ChatGPT to get some suggestions for a name. However, the problem with this approach is 
similar to the problem of looking at prior works too early discussed earlier in this chapter, as it may limit 
one’s creative thought process. Additionally, this is also the easiest of the different approaches, which is 
why it can be extre mely tempting to start with this one. However, it’s best to avoid this temptatio n, try 
coming up with a name manually, and only after having some really great name candidates should the use 
of AI tools be considered. This is how one can avoid the unintended consequence of AI limiting one’s 
creativity rather than expanding it. 
 
2.6.1 Modularity, Reconfigurability, and Expandability 
To serve a diverse range of application needs, a hardware development platform should be general-
purpose, which can be facilitated by making it modular, reconfigurable, and expandable. An arbitrary 
hardware development platform has characteristics such as size, mass, power consumption, power 
output, battery life, price, etc. Given that it is intended for a wide range of users and user needs, there 
would be conflicting and mutually exclusive specifications that different groups of users would ideally 
want. Some users may want to use it for wearable applications where miniaturization, power efficiency, 
and battery life are critical. Others may use it for desktop or even industrial applications where high-power 
output would be of vital importance while size, battery life, and power efficiency may be of little relevance. 
The same may apply for cost – where some may be highly sensitive to it, while others would rather pay 
higher price for better performance. In circumstances like these, and many others like them, the platform 
architect faces a dilemma of what design choices to make because certain specifications are mutually 
exclusive, and any design choice would be beneficial for some and detrimental for other users.  
One can imagine user needs falling on a spectrum, with the number of users being either normally 
distributed around the midpoint, binomially distributed around two points, or following some other 
distribution type. If the goal of the platform is to serve as many users as possible, the platform designer 
would often choose to optimize for the point on the spectrum around which the number of users is the 
highest, which would often be near the center. But no matter what choice the architect makes and what 
the distribution is, there would be those whose needs are not optimally met by the platform. Especially if 
the users are distributed around multiple camps such as wearable applications and desktop applications, 
then a platform that is designed for the midpoint would be suboptimal for both camps, but at least some 
people form the wearable camp, and some from the desktop camp can both use it.  
However, the architect can avoid this design -choice dilemma by making the platform modular, 
reconfigurable, and expandable. In this way, optimization becomes possible not just for a single point or 
region on the spectrum of needs, but for many different re gions simultaneously. Doing so requires 
establishing an architecture that suits a broad range of the needs-spectrum, and then creating a set of 
swappable modules where each module is optimized for a specific point or a very narrow range of the 
55 
 
spectrum. Furthermore, by making the platform not only modular and reconfigurable but also expandable, 
that leaves opportunities for new modules to be created and added by the platform architect or by the 
end users for needs that may only arise later in the future, including ones that cannot be predicted.  
There are many important advantages, but also some drawbacks to modular designs that a platform 
architect should be aware of, that will be covered in the remainder of this section after first providing some 
working definitions. An excellent starting point on the study of modular systems is the textbook Design 
Rules: The Power of Modularity by Baldwin and Clark [89]. The content below draws on and expands upon 
dozens of books, research papers, theses, and articles related to modularity and reconfigurability, many of 
which are referenced and discussed within the following theses – [90,91,92,93,94,95]. 
 
2.6.1.1 Modularity foundations, definitions, and related concepts 
Modularity is a design structure in which parameters and tasks are interdependent within module 
and independent across them. The opposite of modularity is interconnectedness. In a complex modular 
system, there may be a nested hierarchy of building blocks, with multiple modules at each level - ranging 
from the lowest level device and circuit constructs up to the high -level system software and application 
programs. Modules are units in a larger system that are structurally independent of one another but work 
together. The structural elements are strongly connected among themselves but weakly connected to 
elements in other units [89].  
A modular architecture is characterized by independence of structure and integration of function. It 
specifies which design parameters will interact inside a module (called hidden parameters) and which
