outside the modules (called visible parameters). Encapsulating design parameters into modules with 
simple interfaces reduces the overall complexity of t he system. A module’s interface is an abstraction of 
the complexity that lies within the module. In a modular system, the interfaces serve as the design rules 
or the common standards that all modules must follow. 
Changes to any modules can be made without affecting the rest of the system, as long as the interface 
and behavior of the module stays the same. For instance, in the case of software, functions are modules, 
and one can make improvements to a function’s imp lementation without affecting any other code that 
relies on that function, as long as the function’s interface and behavior remains unchanged. This is one of 
the most valuable advantages of modular  architectures, that different modules can be developed, 
updated, and maintained completely independently and asynchronously once the architecture has been 
established and interfaces defined.  
By contrast, in an interconnected architecture, all design parameters are visible and there are no 
interfaces in-between. An interconnected system can achieve greater efficiency (in terms of components, 
size, lines of code, etc.) than an equivalent modular system, because it would not have the overhead of 
interfaces. However, making any substantive change to an interconnected system will affect other parts of 
the system, and those would in turn affect other parts. Beyond a certain point of complexity such changes 
become nearly impossible because a change in one parameter would require a change in another 
parameter, which would then require a change in a third, and, and so on. This is referred to as design 
parameter cycling , and the point of paralyzing complexity in an interconnected system where any 
substantive change becomes impossible is called the complexity catastrophe [89]. Another challenge with 
interconnected systems stemming from those linkages between design parameters is that teams cannot 
work independently and asynchronously on different parts of an interconnected system; everyone must 
continuously coordinate with eve ryone else when making any substantive changes. Interconnected 
architectures are valuable when optimizing for a very specific application or need, because there is no 
overhead on creating interface s between modules. But when designing something that is intended for 
general use, a modular architecture is preferable. 
Modular architectures also offer a way to avoid the complexity catastrophe and to preserve flexibility 
within complex systems. The ideal scenario is to have a development platform based on a modular 
56 
 
architecture to serve many different needs and applications. But then, if a user wishes to use the platform 
for a very specific application at scale, to be able to offer to them an interconnected version of the platform 
specifically optimized for that application – which includes only the components they need and removes 
all the overhead associated with module interfaces, thus reducing component count, costs, and size of the 
device. Going grom a modular architecture to an interconnected one is very easy and  reduces costs; but 
going the opposite route of modularizing a system that is currently interconnected is difficult and costly.   
Systems can exhibit different degrees of modularity with the highest degree being full plug-and-play 
capability. The modularization process for a system typically evolves in stages from lower to higher degree 
of modularity rather than in a single step. This phased evolution applies for the modularization of both 
software and hardware systems. 
Configurability is the ability of a modular system to offer flexibility, adjustments, and customization 
during the system’s assembly or initial setup before it is deployed. Options and parameters can be selected 
or adjusted to achieve the desired functionality through software settings, hardware settings, and internal 
component addition or subtraction. An example of a configurable system is a desktop computer with its 
various modular blocks. A desktop computer is an example of a configurable modular system , whose 
modules like the chassis, motherboard, hard drive, memory, optical driver, microprocessor, etc. can be 
changed before the system is deployed. On the other hand, reconfigurability is the ability of a modular 
system to change its configuration dynamically, post-deployment, to accommodate changing needs and 
requirements. Reconfigurable modular systems offer the ability to add, subtract, swap, and reconfigure 
modules on-the-fly with minimal effort. Examples of reconfigurable modular systems are the SleeveIO and 
FlowIO platforms, presented later in this thesis, whose modules can be placed in many different 
combinations and be swapped within seconds on-the-fly even during operation.  Configurability is about 
initial setup and customization, focusing on crea ting the right system for a predefined purpose. 
Reconfigurability is about ongoing adaptability and flexibility, enabling the system to evolve and respond 
to new or changing demands. Further discussion and examples about those topics can be found in [96] – 
which is a review paper about modular soft robotics.  Expandability in the context of modular systems is 
the ability of the modular architecture to accommodate additional modules beyond the existing set, 
including entirely new types of modules and not merely new variations of the existing module types.  
 
2.6.1.2 Advantages and Disadvantages of Modularity 
This section provides a comprehensive summary of the benefits and drawbacks of adopting a 
modular design approach across various dimensions, including usability, system complexity, development, 
testing, evolution, project management, business strategy, viability, and community engagement. Some of 
the pros and cons are intuitive, while others are completely non-obvious for those without experience in 
designing and deploying modular systems. The following categorized list provid es a comprehensive 
overview of the trade-offs inherent to modular systems. The emphasis is placed on the individual points 
rather than the categories themselves, as some points may naturally fall within multiple categories.  
Customization and Flexibility 
✓ Ability to offer mutually exclusive features through different modules. 
✓ Coverage of the full spectrum of user needs and specification requirements is possible. 
✓ Ability to optimize for to any specific user need by creating a module optimized for that need, rather 
than having to reengineer or redesign the entire system.  
✓ High degree of customization by module addition, subtraction, and substitutions within seconds. 
✓ Users can upgrade their system by replacing modules, rather than replacing the entire system. 
✓ User can personalize the aesthetics of their system by swapping modules. 
✓ Converting a modular system to an interconnect one is easy, simply be removing the interfaces 
between modules and any redundancies that exist across modules. 
57 
 
 A modular system cannot be as optimized as an equivalent interconnected system in terms of 
space/capacity/cost/size because there is always going to be some overhead (hardware or software) 
required for the modular architecture. 
 More potential failure points, especially at the interfaces between modules. 
 Larger size, mass, component count, and cost due to module interfaces and enclosures. 
 A modular system may compromise sleekness, which can impact aesthetics and user experience. 
 
System Complexity 
✓ Highly complex systems become simple to understand, as the complexity is hidden within modules 
that have simple interfaces, and what needs to be understood is only the architecture and interfaces. 
✓ Complexity becomes manageable because it is isolated and discretized. 
✓ Greatly reduced design-parameter cycling. 
✓ The complexity catastrophe is avoided. 
✓ Main changes become simple from a design standpoint, as a result of the reduced complexity. 
✓ Highly complex systems can be created even without anyone’s full understanding of the system.  
 Modularity demands a specific thinking approach that many engineers are not trained in. 
System Development 
✓ Rapid experimentation with new designs and features is possible once the architecture has been set. 
✓ Development time is greatly reduced due to the system’s simplicity and isolation of complexity. 
✓ There is built-in tolerance for change and uncertainty in subsets of the overall system. 
✓ Modules can be developed completely asynchronously. 
✓ Failure to solve problems in one module does not hinder progress in another. 
✓ Subcomponents are decoupled, allowing for the best design outcome to be chosen for each module. 
 There is a need to predetermine a set of design rules to which each module should conform. 
 Once the modular architecture has been set, changing it becomes very costly and disruptive. 
 Architectural decisions made early-on may prove to be sub-optimal later and new better ones may 
emerge, leaving one stuck with suboptimal design. 
 Design-rule flaws are revealed only late in the process.  
 Work on individual modules may go smoothly for months, and flaws may be revealed only at the 
end during integration or testing. 
 Knowledge must be built up through experience with artifacts or through iterations. 
 Going from a in interconnected design to a modular design is difficult; the reverse is easy. 
System Testing and Troubleshooting 
✓ Testing becomes possible as soon as a module is developed, long before the full system is developed. 
✓ Rapid prototyping-testing cycling, allowing many module design alternatives to be tried quickly. 
✓ Troubleshooting and servicing can be done very rapidly without any technical expertise simply be 
swapping modules to identify the one containing the problem. 
✓ Faults are isolated inside modules and do not propagate to other modules.  
✓ Problems can be rapidly found, because the simple act of identifying the problematic module 
drastically reduces problem search space to only that which is contained within the module.  
 Testing can be costly because a modular system can have many different configurations to test. 
 Heavy reliance on system-level testing inhibits the development of modular designs. 
System Evolution and Branching 
✓ Incremental evolution is possible within existing modules and by creating new modules. 
✓ Evolution can happen asynchronously and in parallel in different parts of the system. 
✓ Backward compatibility is ensured as module designers must conform to the set architecture. 
58 
 
✓ Individual modules from one system can be reused in the design of another system. 
✓ The underlying architecture of one system can be reused when designing a totally different system. 
✓ Evolution is possible without the need of the system architect after the design rules have been set. 
✓ New features can be added via new modules that the system architect never imagined. 
✓ Evolution can happen in completely unplanned and decentralized yet highly coordinated ways. 
 The architect needs to foresee the system’s long-term evolution to define a modular architecture 
and design rules to enable that evolution to take place without requiring a complete redesign. 
 The architect must identify the entire design space of future possibilities, foreseeing what problems 
and opportunities might arise in the future, so that the system can be designed in a way that those 
future changes can occur at the modular level without requiring a fundamental redesign. 
 Loss of the ability to explore some parts of the design space that have been made into design rules. 
Project Management 
✓ Different parts of the system can be designed by different teams working completely independently 
without any meetings or coordination between them by simply adhering to the design rules. 
✓ Work can happen in parallel across different teams working completely asynchronously. 
✓ The project can evolve in a completely decentralized way, without any need for a project manager 
enabled by simple adherence to the design rules of the modular architecture set by the architect. 
✓ Different parts of the system can be designed asynchronously and in parallel. 
✓ Module designers need expertise related only to the module they are developing and don’t need 
expertise or familiarity about to the overall system or about any other module within the system. 
✓ Once the architect’s job is complete of creating the modular architecture and setting the design 
rules, then less experienced designers can take over the system’s development and evolution. 
✓ Failure to resolve issues in one module of the system does not jeopardize the system as a whole. 
✓ Design rules serve as a substitute for ongoing communication and coordination, leading to fewer 
meetings, higher team productivity, minimal ambiguity, and lower likelihood of poor design choices. 
 A significant amount of time, effort, and resources must be invested into a system architect who can 
devise a sound modular architecture and codify a set of design rules.  
 If the architecture is not well designed, and not future-looking to accommodate new modules that 
may come later in the future, it can be very costly if architectural changes must be made later. 
 Changes to the modular architecture are extremely disruptive for module designers and users. 
 Strict partitions of knowledge and effort are predetermined that cannot be easily altered later. 
 Mere mental decomposition of an artifact into modules is not enough. The system architect must 
also understand fully the nature of the underlying interdependencies.  
 There may be prolonged periods of time during which the fundamental design does not appear to 
be changing very much, since knowledge must be built up through experience with real artifacts or 
via multiple iterations. 
 The expertise required to design modular architectures is highly specialized, creating a knowledge 
gap between architects and module designers. 
Strategy and Business 
✓ The system can be kept confidential even from the people building it, since only the system architect 
needs to know what the system does or what it is, but module designers don’t. 
✓ Greatly reduced time and costs for upgrades, as only some modules may need to be upgraded. 
✓ A modular system can meet different customer needs and budgets, lowering the barrier to entry. 
✓ Opportunity to create a diversified product line consisting not only of a single platform device but 
many different modules that users can choose to purchase.  
✓ Opportunity to continuously release new products in the form of not only upgrades but entirely new 
modules and to have repeat customers rather than one-time customers. 
59 
 
✓ Ability to rapidly convert one product line to another in case of changing demand. 
✓ Reusability of existing modules and design rules to create entirely new products. 
✓ Options for mass customization and high variety at a cost comparable to mass production. 
✓ Drastic time savings enabled by concurrent design of independent modules. 
✓ Customers can take advantage of new hardware as soon as an upgrade to a module is introduced. 
✓ Customization is possible to each user’s needs and budget through module exclusion and 
substitution. 
✓ Once a user is satisfied with a certain modular ecosystem, they tend to stay within that ecosystem 
and make upgrades to it rather than moving to an entirely different one. 
✓ An interconnected design delivers one option, while a modular design delivers many options. 
 Establishing the modular architecture and design rules is a sunk cost investment that takes a 
significant amount of time and resources to get it right. 
 During the design rules identification phase, progress is difficult to demonstrated by the architects 
to external parties, and to those not directly involved it may appear that no progress is being made. 
 Module designers with knowledge of the design rules could become defectors and find their own 
company that specializes in making a better module for the overall system. 
 The right to determine what is and what is not part of the system passes from the system architects 
to the module designers. Ironically, the better the architect, the sooner they become unneeded. 
Sustainability 
✓ Upgrades do not require replacement of the entire system extending the product lifespan. 
✓ Minimal e-waste creation as only modules go obsolete, but not the entire system. 
✓ Reusability of modules, interfaces, and architectures in entirely new systems reduces the need for 
design iteration and experimentation, which itself is a very wasteful process. 
 Additional interconnects and components are needed to create the module interfaces. 
Community 
✓ External contributions can be managed much more efficiently in a modular design than an 
interconnected design, because there are much less dependencies that could be broken. 
✓ Contributions can happen asynchronously and without central coordination, as long as there is a 
strict adherence to the design rules set by the architect. 
✓ Entirely new modules can be created by the community and integrated into the modular platform 
without requiring any effort on the part of the maintainers and without any disruptions. 
✓ Interested technical contributors don’t need to learn all the details about the system to be able to 
contribute but should learn only the details relevant only to the module they want to improve. 
✓ Modular systems are far more conducive to community contributions than interconnected ones as 
they simplify the process for both making and receiving technical contributions, for contributors and 
maintainers, respectively. 
 
 We will come back to the topic of Modularity in 9: , where I will discuss in depth the role of 
modularity across all the core projects covered in this thesis, cases where modularity emerged and others 
where it was preplanned, how modularity drove the innovation and evolution the different projects and 
ecosystems, the potential of modularity as a future-proofing product design strategy against obsolescence, 
as well as the impact of modularity on the users  on users and contributors as a means of means of 
promoting understanding, participation, and feedback. 
 
60 
 
2.7 Utilizing this Ecosystem Framework Model in Practice 
This framework model presented in this chapter can be used both as a powerful conceptual tool 
for both analyzing existing development platforms and for creating & deploying new platforms. In its role 
as an analysis tool, it can help uncover shortcomings, oversights, and areas for improvement in platforms 
that are already developed and deployed. In its role as a development & deployment tool, the framework 
can serve as a recipe or a guide on what steps to take and how to take those steps at each point along the 
way when embarking on the journey of delivering an impactful new development platform to the world. 
 
2.7.1 As a tool for analyzing platforms and their ecosystems 
Let’s explore how we may analyze a popular hardware development platform like Arduino through 
the lens of this ecosystem framework model, and what findings it may help us uncover.  Arduino is an 
open-source development platform for building electronic projects and embedded systems, consisting of 
both hardware and software components. And it is one of the most popular development platforms in the 
world and serves as a good reference example. 
 
1. Arduino: Platform Technology Package 
✓ Hardware & Software: A complete package that includes both. 
✓ Complete Software package: Full software stack with many libraries, APIs, and an IDE. There is 
also support for multiple programming languages.  
✓ General-Purpose: The platform is not limited to a narrow set of user needs. 
✓ Modular: The core device is monolithic, but different modules (shields) can be connected to it. 
✓ Expandable: Anyone can freely create their own new Arduino shields without any difficulty. 
✓ Configurable: Various shield configurations are possible, including stacking of multiple shields. 
✓ Value for Novices:  Novices use it because it significantly reduces the barrier to entry. 
✓ Value for Experts: Experts use it because it allows them to prototype and iterate rapidly. 
✓ Unique Name: Nothing with similar name existed at the time of its launch to create confusion. 
✓ Sticky Name: The name is relatively easy to remember upon hearing it for the first time. 
 Relatable Name: There is no obvious connection between the name semantics and the product. 
 Name pronunciation: The name does not roll off the tongue easily. 
 Reconfigurable: Configurations cannot be changed dynamically on-the-fly. 
 Hardware Specifications: Some of the hardware specifications are limited, especially for experts 
and those interested in more complex tasks. 
2. Arduino: Community & Interactions 
✓ Users: Millions of people have used the Arduino platform 
✓ Contributors: Tens of thousands of people have been contributing to Arduino, including libraries, 
documentations, and many other forms of contributions. 
✓ Workshops: Arduino devices are frequently used in workshops and hackathons 
✓ Conferences: There is an annual event for the Arduino community called Arduino Day. 
✓ Institutions: R&D labs, university lab, and companies commonly use it for rapid prototyping of 
new projects or for creating testing and characterization setups of other ongoing projects. 
✓ Educators: Professors, Teachers, and Lab Instructors use Arduino en masse for education. 
✓ Collaborations: The core developers often collaborate with the community on new features. 
✓ Interactions: Countless opportunities exist to find and interact with other users both in person 
and online, especially because this platform serves the needs of many different communities. 
✓ Diversity: The community is global and comprised of people of all backgrounds and all ages. 
3. Arduino: Content & Resources 
61 
 
✓ Applications: Arduino has been used in nearly every relevant application scenario imaginable. 
✓ Project Videos: Tens of thousands of videos of Arduino-based projects can be found on YouTube. 
✓ Examples: Thousands of hardware and software examples are available online, some provided 
by the core developers within the official libraries, and most provided by the community.  
✓ Tutorials: Entire instructional tutorials are also readily available on how to utilize various 
features of Arduino and how to create many different projects from start to finish. 
✓ Documentation: Vast amounts of resources are available on how to do almost anything with 
Arduino and how to fix almost any problem that one may encounter in the process. 
4. Arduino: Online Community Platform 
✓ Website: There is not just one but many different websites where the community can be found: 
✓ Forums: The official Arduino website is no longer needed for reaching the global community, 
because there are many other places where questions and discussion online can happen. 
Nevertheless, it does still offer forums. 
✓ Chats: There is a Discord server for the Arduino community with nearly 60,000 members. 
 Posts: People cannot directly post their projects on the official Arduino website, but they can 
submit projects to be featured. 
 Groups:  There was an Arduino User Groups initiative for people to get together, though it 
appears to now be dormant. 
5. Arduino: Deployment model 
✓ Access: The hardware is low-priced and accessible for just about anyone. 
✓ Availability: Arduino is readily available for purchase worldwide. Additionally, countless 
institutions and individuals have the platform. 
✓ Supply-demand match: The supply meets the demand, except for occasional supply shortages. 
✓ Distribution network: Arduino is sold by countless online and physical stores worldwide, and 
entire companies like Adafruit and Sparkfun make products for Arduino-compatibility. 
✓ Financial Viability: The deployment model has worked fairly for keeping the company financially 
viable for many years. 
 Incentives: Arduino clones have been a problem because they take revenue away from the original 
creators. This is caused by the fact that Arduino has been traditionally  open-source, and that 
model does not offer sufficient incentives to prevent copycats.  
6. Arduino: Ecosystem Feedback Loops and Symbiotic Relationships 
✓ Community => Content: Most of the content available is community-generated, and it includes 
projects, examples, videos, tutorials, documentations, books, etc. 
✓ Content => Community: The vast amount of content makes the platform more well-known and 
appealing for more people, leading to growth in the community of users and contributors. 
✓ Community => Platform: Anyone can make a contributor to the development of the platform, 
and much of the software component of it are the result of community contributions. 
✓ Platform => Community: Since the platform has become extremely versatile and general-
purpose, that has made it suitable for more projects, leading to growth in the number of users. 
✓ Platform => Content: The evolution of the platform has led to more content about it. 
 Content => Platform: It is not clear to what extent Arduino’s evolution has been directly 
influenced by projects people have published that are based on Arduino. 
✓ Website => Community & Interactions: The website provides means of facilitating interactions 
by offering forums, groups, and a Discord server. 
 Community => Website: Users and external contributors do not have the ability to publish 
directly to the website or to influence changes to it.  
62 
 
✓ Deployment Model =>: The deployment model does facilitate all other parts of the framework. 
✓ => Deployment Model: Over the years, the deployment model has evolved and changed in 
response to various input factors from within and outside the ecosystem. 
The Arduino Platform is one of the most successful and impactful platforms, and very few areas of 
potential improvement were identified when analyzed through the lens of the framework model, as one 
would expect. One can perform the same analysis for many o ther platforms to find potential areas of 
improvement hindering their impact. 
2.7.2 As a tool for creating & deploying new platforms and ecosystems 
Similar to how the framework model was used for analyzing existing platforms and ecosystems, it can 
also be used to guide the process of creating and deploying an entirely new platform from scratch and 
establishing a whole supporting ecosystem around it to achieve maximum impact. One would look at each 
of the components and subcomponents that are part of the Framework figure, go through them one by 
one, and create a plan for realizing each one of them. They would go through the same points that were 
used in the analysis of Arduino, convert each of those statements into questions, and then come up with 
answers and plans for addressing every single question. In the upcoming chapters of this thesis, it will be 
shown how this framework was a tool guiding the development and deployment of several modular 
hardware development platforms. 
Additionally, at any point in the platform development and deployment lifecycle, this framework can 
be used to evaluate, reevaluate, and find areas for improvement. This model is valuable in the pre-ideation 
phase, brainstorming phase, prototyping phase, MVP testing phase, deployment phase, operation phase, 
maintenance phase, and even years after the platform has already been deployed. No matter where on 
their platform development & deployment journey a person or a company is, they would find insights for 
achieving more impact by going through this framework model.  
 
2.8 Chapter 2 Conclusion (Continued in Chapter 4) 
This chapter presented a framework model for how to design and deploy development platforms 
in order to reach a wide impact. It established the need for an ecosystem that a platform must be a part 
of and discussed a few of the advantages and drawbacks of joining an established ecosystem or creating a 
new one. Then it focused specifically on creating new ecosystem s rather than joining existing ones and 
provided a framework with five distinct components that need to be present in the technological 
ecosystem around the platform, along with dozens of subcomponents. An overview was provided of each 
one, and the role it plays in the ecosystem. All the symbiotic relationships, feedback loops, and 
interdependencies between the five different components were also identified and mapped. Section 2.5 
provided in-depth coverage of practical considerations related to the first component of the model – the 
technology package – and each of its subcomponents. The importance of modularity, configurability, and 
expandability wa s addressed in extensive depth; and a comprehensive list of advantages and 
disadvantages of modular design approach were also provided. A practical guide was offered on how to 
come up with a name, why the name must be sticky and unique, and why those facto rs are even more 
important than having a name that is relatable. Finally, the last major section of the chapter covered how 
this ecosystem framework model can be used as a valuable tool for both analyzing existing platforms and 
ecosystems and for creating new ones, providing a case study example of analyzing the Arduino 
development platform through the lens of this framework.  
While in -depth practical considerations were provided only for the first component of the 
Ecosystem Framework model in this chapter, the remaining four components are also discussed in just as 
much depth and detail in  4: , which goes into the aspects of content & resources, community & 
interactions, community website, and viable deployment model. The importance of each of those 
63 
 
ecosystem components is presented in depth, practical guidelines are provided, and a case study is shown 
of how this framework and each of those components were put into practice in the development of the 
SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem. Nestled between this cha pter and Chapter 4 is Chapter 3 about the FlowIO 
Platform, which serves as a case study of applying the principles discussed in this chapter, namely in 
component #1 of the Ecosystem Framework, technology package. After Chapters 3 and 4, the remaining 
chapters are all practical manifestations of this model as well. One can view the current chapter as the 
guide for all the remaining chapters in this thesis.

3: FlowIO Platform 
 
 
Figure 3-1. FlowIO Platform, the suite of all FlowIO component modules, and the FlowIO graphical user interface. 
This is the first of several chapters related to the FlowIO Platform and its impact. This chapter 
provides motivation & overview of the platform, technical description of each hardware module that is 
part of the platform, and comprehensive description of the FlowIO software stacks with detailed coverage 
of each stack layer. The current chapter focuses exclusively on the technical aspects of FlowIO, while 
subsequent chapters will show how this platform has been deployed as part of a larger ecosystem, 
application and use cases enabled across fields, and case studies related to it.  
 
3.1 FlowIO Motivation and Prior Works 
Many electronics project s that three decades ago may have required advanced engineering 
expertise, and a week of work in a lab with expensive equipment, today can be done within a weekend, 
by a high -school student, in his/her living room!  Today, prototyping in soft robotics and programmable 
materials has high barriers to entry and is just as inaccessible to most people as electronics prototyping 
was a few decades ago. In recent years, projects such as PneUI [97], aeroMorph [98], MorphIO [99], Bubble 
[100], Auxetic Breath [101], and numerous others, have demonstrated a growing interest in soft robotics 
Chapter  3 
66 
 
from HCI researchers, students, artists, designers, and makers. However, a challenge faced by nearly 
everyone working on pneumatically actuated projects is that one typically must build their custom driving 
systems from the ground up and dedicate sometimes  most of their time to developing pneumatics, 
electronics, and software, instead of developing novel soft actuators, applications, or user experiences.  
This challenge creates significant time inefficiencies, stifles innovation, and even discourages the pu rsuit 
of ideas requiring more sophisticated drive electronics or software. 
While many electronic platforms are readily available for purchase , fluidic platforms are almost 
nonexistent. Pneuduino [102], Programmable Air [103], and Soft Robotics Toolkit (SRT)  [104] were the 
three most well -known pneumatic toolkit attempts  prior to the introduction of FlowIO . However, their 
large sizes, limited sensing & control capabilities, and lack of integrated pressure/power  sources made 
them impractical for most real -world wearable projects. SRT is a DIY solution with off -the-shelf parts. 
Pneuduino is smaller than SRT, has custom modular hardware, and comes with an Arduino library; 
however, it still requires external pumps and power sour ces. Programmable Air provides similar 
capabilities to Pneuduino while using more affordable parts a nd integrating the pumps into the device 
itself. However, the base version has only a single pneumatic I/O port (with the possibility to add more), 
an external high -current power source is still required, and the pumps cannot be changed easily to 
accommodate different pressure or flowrate needs. All the aforementioned toolkits also lack any GUIs and 
are natively compatible only with Arduino IDE, making them inaccessible to nonprogrammers.  
FlowIO addressed all the aforementioned limitations of prior systems. The goal of FlowIO is to be 
a truly general-purpose and highly accessible miniature, pneumatics development platform. FlowIO was 
inspired out of pure necessity when project Bubble was be ing developed, which required a miniature 
fluidics driver with 5 pneumatic ports, but nothing existed to satisfy that need. This led to a multi-year 
quest resulting in over 20 prototype iterations to create the world’s smallest, wearable, full -featured, 
pneumatics development platform. Further discussion about Bubble and its impact on FlowIO is provided 
in Chapter 7. Continuous feedback from diverse users helped FlowIO become not just a toolkit but a true 
general-purpose platform technology with both hardwa re and software, capable of satisfying the needs 
of nearly all users working on wearable or non-wearable small to medium-scale pneumatics projects, while 
also being highly user-friendly for both novices and experts and compatible across all operating systems. 
FlowIO was designed using a first-principles approach rather than being based on any earlier work. 
The pneumatic and system architectures are therefore drastically different from prior works, as the FlowIO 
design was motivated by the challenges and unmet n eeds faced by the HCI, soft robotics, and maker 
communities, and especially those working on wearable projects. 
The FlowIO Platform was published at CHI’21 [105], where the paper also won the first-place award 
at the CHI’21 Student Research Competition. Since its publication, the FlowIO Platform has won multiple 
major research, design, and engineering awards including the Hackaday Grand Prize [ 106], Tech Briefs 
Create The Future Grand Prize [107], and iFDesign Talent Award [108], among others.  
 
3.2 FlowIO Platform Overview 
FlowIO is the first fully integrated and general -purpose, miniature pneumatics development 
platform for actuation, sensing, and control of pneumatically driven projects. FlowIO is to pneumatics 
prototyping what Arduino is to electronics prototyping. It is a general -purpose toolkit with modular 
hardware and full -stack software designed for the project needs of researchers, makers, students, 
designers, and artists working on projects involving fluidic actuation. As indicated in Figure 3-2, FlowIO 
features 5 programmable pneumatic I/O ports, where each port supports the actions of inflation, vacuum, 
release, hold, pressure sense, and flow -rate variability. The FlowIO hardware is wearable & battery -
powered, fully untethered & remotely contro llable, and feature-complete to suit nearly any pneumatic 
67 
 
control need within the supported pressure and flow ranges. No external pressure or power sources are 
required during use.  
 
 
Figure 3-2. The FlowIO device with the Small Pump module and an overview of key device features. 
Modularity and reconfigurability are core characteristics of the FlowIO Platform. A family of 
magnetically connected modules enables reconfigurability of the FlowIO system within seconds — to 
satisfy different pressure and flowrate needs and specific size and weight constraints that users may have 
for their projects. The suite of pump modules makes FlowIO capable of offering relative pressures ranging 
from -12 to +63 psi and variable flowrates up to 3.4 L/min. There are four categories of modules ( Figure 
3-3 and Figure 3-16) including Controller modules with up to five I/O ports each, Pump modules  of 
different sizes with different pneumatic characteristics, Expansion modules that provide additional sensing 
and actuation capabilities, and Accessories for mounting, wearability, and reconfigurability.  
 

68 
 
 
Figure 3-3. The growing family of modules comprising the FlowIO Platform hardware today. 
In addition to hardware, FlowIO also includes a software suite with multiple layers of abstraction 
including C++ libraries, a command-based Interface, Bluetooth control options, JavaScript APIs, and web-
based GUI dashboard. Just like the hardware, the Flow IO software was designed to be general purpose, 
modular, highly user-friendly, and suitable for people from any technical and non-technical backgrounds. 
The web-GUI runs directly form the web -browser, requires no downloads or installations, and enables 
control of FlowIO form any Bluetooth-enabled device and any operating system that supports modern web 
browsers. The GUI can simultaneously connect to 7 FlowIOs enabling control of up to 35 fluidic I/O ports. 
For more advanced users, object-oriented C++ libraries and JavaScript APIs are provided that include built-
in control algorithms, error-checking features, numerous functions and methods, and many examples.  
 
 
Figure 3-4. FlowIO Software Overview 
3.3 FlowIO Pneumatic Architecture and Configurations 
FlowIO was designed with a first -principles bottom-up approach, where the first part of FlowIO 
ever to be designed was a highly general-purpose pneumatic circuit. Then over the course of a few years 
and dozens of prototyping iterations, various hardware an d software augmentations were developed 
around the core pneumatic architecture to make it  more usable, user -friendly, reconfigurable, and 
expandable.  Gradually, additional modules also were developed to add new features and functionalities 
to FlowIO. Initially, there was just one monolithic device, then it became 4 modules, then 12 modules, and 
now there are nearly 20 different modules.  

69 
 
Using FlowIO for basic pneumatic control applications does not necessitate an understanding of 
its pneumatic architecture. FlowIO was made user -friendly and intuitive so that anyone can begin using 
FlowIO within seconds without the need to study it in depth first. The hardware component arrangement, 
the enclosure designs, the cover markings, and  the graphical user interface were all designed to make it 
self-evident for people how to use FlowIO. Some users have completed entire research projects with 
FlowIO without understanding its internal workings.  Understanding the FlowIO pneumatic architecture 
becomes important for more advanced applications, for knowing why certain features are present, and for 
why specific hardware and software design choices were made. 
One of the foundational questions explored during the FlowIO design process was: what 
pneumatic architecture would offer the most features? This question emerged naturally because the 
design intent of FlowIO was to be a truly general -purpose pneumatic control platform. FlowIO had to be 
able to support – at each port but not simultaneously – the actions of: 
1. inflation,  
2. vacuum,  
3. release,  
4. pressure hold,  
5. positive and negative pressure sensing on each port with a single device sensor,  
6. ability to maintain different pressures at different ports, and 
7. ability to hold those different pressures even if the device powers off.  
Pneumatic architecture from the literature were explored, however, none of the architectures 
encountered in prior related works were able to offer all the technical properties listed above. For example, 
Figure 3-5 shows two pneumatic architectures frequently encountered in the literature, both of which 
appear to be highly versatile at first glance. Moreover, even all manifold valve manufacturers also design 
their manifold valves specifically for use with this type of dual-manifold architecture due to its versatility. 
However, they both fail to meet several of the desired requirements. Specifically, they cannot maintain 
differential pressure at the different ports, cannot maintain the ports pressurized when the syste m is 
powered off, and a single sensor cannot be used for both positive and negative pressure sensing. 
(Discussion about the advantages and drawbacks of these architectures are provided in Chapter 8, where 
a case study is discussed of a user who adapted a FlowIO device to use an alternative architecture.) 
 
 
Figure 3-5. Two of several pneumatic architectures that were initially considered but that did not meet the 
desired characteristics for a truly general-purpose pneumatic system. 
Given that even this highly versatile architecture employed in most prior works and by 
manufacturers still failed to meet the 7 desired requirements, it was unclear whether an architecture that 
meets all requirements was even possible. However, after an extensive design and brainstorming process 
with many variational trials, a suitable pneumatic architecture was eventually devised that offered all the 
desired characteristic features. The result is shown in Figure 3-6 below, which became the basis of FlowIO. 
 

70 
 
3.3.1 General (+/-) Configuration – FlowIO’s Default Architecture 
 
Figure 3-6. Pneumatic architecture used in FlowIO in the General (+/-) pneumatic configuration.  The left side 
shows the pneumatic circuit while the right side shows the corresponding FlowIO implementation of that circuit.  
The FlowIO pneumatic architecture features seven identical 3 -way valves with a single manifold 
channel between them, two pumps in opposite orientations, and a pressure sensor directly connected to 
the manifold. Five of the valves are designated as I/O port  valves, and two are for inlet and outlet. The 
normally-closed inputs of the valves are connected to a manifold channel (shown in green). The normally-
open inputs of the 7 valves are permanently blocked, thus effectively converting the valves from 3-way to 
2-way valves. The reason for using 3 -way valves rather than 2 -way valves is because all valves on the 
market that meet the size, power, and pneumatic properties are 3-way valves; thereby leaving no choice 
but to use 3 -way valves and convert them to 2 -way normally-closed valves. In the General (+/ -) 
configuration, there are two pumps; one for positive pressure (P1) whose output port is connected to the 
inlet valve, and the other for vacuum (P2) whose input port is connected to the outlet valve. The (+/-) 
notation means (P1 = + / P2 = - ), referring to P1 being used for positive pressure and P2 for negative. 
The input of Pump 1 and the output of Pump 2 are unconnected and open to the environment; 
ambient air is used as the flow medium in the pneumatic circuit. The most unconventional aspect about 
the General (+/-) configuration is that the positive pressure source (P1) is connected to the output port of 
the inlet valve. Thus, effectively we are using the inlet -designated valve in reverse of how it is normally 
used. Since this configuration goes counter to the manufacturer specification, this could explain why such 
configuration was not seen in the prior literature surveyed. Nevertheless, this is partly what enables this 
configuration to satisfy all the requirements of interest given the valves available on the market today, and 
what ultimately enabled the existence of FlowIO.  
Enabling different pressures at different ports  necessitated that the manifold channel be 
connected to the normally-closed input-side of the 3 -way valves. Thus, when converting them to 2 -way 
valves, they had to be converted to the normally-closed type. The choice of the normally-closed manifold 
is also what enables maintaining different port pressures even when the system is powered off.  
While inflation and vacuum rely on the activation of the pumps, release is passive and does not 
require pump activation. “Release” refers to both passive deflation when air is exiting from a connected 
pressurized chamber, and passive inflation when air is entering a connected vacuumed chamber. In both 
of those cases the pressure in the chamber is passively equalized to atmospheric pressure. The pressure 
release operation cleverly exploits the fact that diaphragm pumps behave as one -way valves when they 
are off – allowing airflow from a pump’s inlet to the pump’s outlet but not the other way around. Thus, if 
a pressurized chamber is connected to port N, the pressure can be released just by opening port N and 
the outlet valve and letting the air escape freely through Pump 2 without activating the pump. Conversely, 
if the connected chamber is under vacuum, the vacuum state can be released just by opening port N and 
the inlet valve, letting air enter the system freely through Pump 1 without activating the pump, t hus 
bringing the chamber pressure up to atmospheric pressure level. 

71 
 
The pressure sensor, being directly connected to the manifold line, enables continuous pressure 
monitoring during a pneumatic action on the active I/O port(s). For example, suppose there is a 4-legged 
soft robot connected to ports 1,2,3,4, and each leg needs to be inflated / deflated to specific pressures to 
make the robot walk. While inflating port 1, the pre ssure sensor reading corresponds to the pressure at 
port 1; while vacuuming port 3, the pressure sensor reading corresponds to the pressure at port 3; and so 
on for any of the pneumatic ports. In this way, a single pressure sensor is able to monitor the pressure at 
any of the five pneumatic ports, whenever a port is active. If two ports are active simultaneously, then 
then the pressure between them woul d equalize and the sensor’s reading would correspond to the 
pressure at both ports. 
Another feature of this architecture is the ability to perform the same action on multiple ports 
simultaneously. For example, simultaneous inflation on ports 1 and 3 can be achieved by opening the 
inlet, port 1, and port 3 valves and then starting Pump 1. Similarly, it’s also possible to do the other 
pneumatic actions such as vacuum, release, and pressure hold synchronously on multiple ports. However, 
the standard configuration does not allow simultaneous different actions on multiple ports. For example, 
inflating port 1 and vacuuming port 4 is possible only sequentially but not simultaneously with this 
architecture. Nevertheless, simultaneous different actions are possible when FlowIO is configured in the 
split configuration, discussed below, or when two FlowIO devices are used synchronously.  
The last key feature of this pneumatic architecture is flow-rate variability of any of the pneumatic 
I/O ports. The flowrate at a port is a function of the type of pump used, the voltage at which the pump is 
operated, the pressure losses within the system, the number of other ports that are open, and whether 
anything is connected to the open ports. Thus, programmatical and dynamic flowrate control is made 
possible by driving the pumps using PWM signals at variable duty cycles. A second way to have flow-rate 
control is by utilizing the unconnected ports to modulate the flowrate at a connected port. Since all 5 ports 
are supplied by the same pressure source, the flowrate at a port is a function of how many ports are open. 
If two ports with nothing connected to them are open, then the air flow is split between those two open 
ports. If a c hamber connected to port 1 is being inflated, and port 2 is opened momentarily during the 
inflation process, that will momentarily reduce the flowrate on port 1. Moreover, it is also possible to go 
one step further and connect a “pneumatic resistor” to port 2 to reduce the flowrate on part 1 by a specific 
amount. This is analogous to adding a resistor in parallel to the load in an electrical circuit to reduce the 
current through the load. The simplest form of a pneumatic resistor would be just a simple pneu matic 
tube. The pneumatic resistance of the tube would be proportional to its length and inversely proportional 
to its cross-sectional area. The narrower and longer the tube, the greater the tube’s pneumatic resistance.  
 
3.3.2 Split (+/-) Configuration 
Achieving different actions on multiple ports simultaneously is not possible with the General 
configuration, unless two FlowIO devices are used synchronously. However, two FlowIO’s may be cost -
prohibitive to many users. Moreover, the majority of user projects require far fewer than 10 pneumatic 
I/O ports. What if there was a way to achieve simultaneous different actions in a single FlowIO device? 
 
72 
 
 
Figure 3-7. FlowIO Split (+/-) Pneumatic Configuration 
The Split (+/-) configuration makes that possible by effectively splitting a single FlowIO device into 
two smaller FlowIOs, each with one pneumatic I/O port. As indicated on Figure 3-7, the left and right side 
of the split device, each has its own inlet, outlet, and port valve. The two pumps are connected to the two 
inlet valves and the two outlet valves. With this configuration it is possible to perform one action on port 
5 and another same or different action on port 1, simultaneously and independently. The main limitation 
of the Split (+/-) configuration is that there are only two programmable I/O ports. The second potential 
drawback is that pressure monitoring is possible only on port 5, but not on port 1. Nevertheless, if the 
devices connected to the two ports are identical, then it’s poss ible to have effective pressure control on 
both ports by considering the time it takes for the right side to reach a certain pressure and then 
controlling the left side based on this computed time which serves as proxy for the pressure.  The main 
advantage of the Split configuration is ultimately that it eliminates the need for a second FlowIO device 
when only two I/O ports are required to deliver different pneumatic actions simultaneously.  
 
3.3.3 Series (+/+) and Series (-/-) Configurations 
In the General (+/-) configuration, Pump 1 is used for inflation and Pump 2 for vacuum. However, 
many applications do not require vacuum, and only inflation and release are sufficient. There are also 
other applications where only vacuum and release are sufficient. In both of those cases, only one of the 
two pumps is used and the other lies dormant. But in many instances, higher pressures are more desirable.  
The Series (+/+) configuration, Figure 3-8, uses both pumps for inflation to double the output 
pressure. Going from General (+/-) to Series (+/+) requires disconnecting the tube between Pump 2 and 
the outlet valve and instead connecting a tube between the output of Pump 1 and the input of Pump 1 to 
put the two pumps in series. This configuration supports inflation and pressure release but not vacuum. 
 
 
Figure 3-8. FlowIO Series (+/+) Pneumatic Configuration 
The Series (-/-) Configuration, Figure 3-9, enables both pumps to be utilized for vacuum to achieve 
greater negative pressure. Series (-/-) requires disconnecting the inflation tube, keeping the vacuum tube 

73 
 
connected, and having a tube that connects the output of one pump to the input of the other to put them 
in series. This configuration supports vacuum and passive inflation but not active inflation. 
 
 
Figure 3-9. FlowIO Series (-/-) Pneumatic Configuration 
In the physical FlowIO implementation, the pump modules enclosures have built -in channels 
designed specifically for making it easy to switch between the General and the Series configurations. The 
switching of configurations on the physical device involves adding an external tube on the outside of the 
pump module, passing around the backside of the module, and connecting it between the two pumps. 
 
3.3.4 The Parallel (+/+) and Parallel (-/-) Configurations 
While the Series configurations allowed higher pressures compared to the General configuration, the 
Parallel configurations allow for higher flowrates by connecting the two pumps in parallel. In the (+/+) 
variation, the pumps are parallel connected as inflation pumps ( Figure 3-10); while in the (-/-) variation, 
the pumps are parallel connected as vacuum pumps (Figure 3-11). The Parallel configurations are suitable 
for applications requiring only inflation & release or only vacuum & release, but not both inflation & 
vacuum.  
 Reconfiguring FlowIO from the General to either of the two Parallel configuration 
requires only an external tube and a 3-way T splitter or Y splitter. The Parallel (+/+) variation requires 
connecting one end of the splitter to the output of Pump 1 and the other ends to the output of Pump 2 
and the Inlet valve, respectively.  
 
Figure 3-10. FlowIO Parallel (+/+) Pneumatic Configuration 
The Parallel (-/-) variation requires connecting one end of the splitter to the input of Pump 2 
and the other ends to the input of Pump 1 and the Outlet valve, respectively.  
 

74 
 
 
Figure 3-11. FlowIO Parallel (-/-) Pneumatic Configuration 
3.3.5 The XtraSense Configurations 
As previously discussed, a single pressure sensor internally-connected to the manifold is used for 
monitoring the pressure on any pneumatic I/O ports when any port is active. This time -multiplexed 
pressure monitoring with a single pressure sensor suffices for the majority of applications, but not all. For 
example, if there is a connected pneumatic project to one of the ports and the pressure in the chamber 
changes while the port valve is closed (e.g. due to external leakage or due to external squeeze), the 
integrated pressure sensor will not detect this change of pressure until the corresponding port valve is 
opened. The XtraSense configurations were designed to solve this limitational problem, and to also allow 
simultaneous pressure monitoring on multiple ports, without the necessity of opening any of the valves. 
 
 
Figure 3-12. FlowIO XtraSense (+/-) Pneumatic Configuration 
As shown in Figure 3-12, the XtraSense (+/-) configuration adds 5 additional pressure sensors 
which are connected to the 5 pneumatics I/O ports, respectively. Thus, anything connected to the I/O ports 
is always in direct contact with these sensors without any valves or other components in -between. This 
makes it possible to monitor pressure continuously and simultaneously in up to five connected chambers 
– regardless of what actuation action is happening and even when FlowIO is idle.   
The implementation of this configuration in the physical FlowIO hardware is done using an 
expansion module called XtraSense, which goes underneath the Controller module. Since XtraSense does 
not require any modification to the underlying architecture itself, it can be used in conjunction with any 
of the previously discussed configurations, such as the Series or the Parallel configurations. 
One of the unique features afforded by this is the ability to make measurement comparisons and 
to measure differential quantities as well. Moreover, the specific sensors used are capable of measuring 
not only air pressure, but also water pressure as well as temperature. This enables FlowIO to be used as a 
sophisticated pneumatic and hydraulic measurement tool.  
 

75 
 
3.3.6 The Compressed Air Configuration 
In cases where pressures significantly higher than what is possible even with the Series (+/+) 
configuration are required, a compressed air tank with a pressure regulator can be used as a replacement 
for the pump module, as shown in Figure 3-13 below.  The pressure in the tank may be an order of 
magnitude greater than the maximum pressure that FlowIO’s Inlet can handle, which could destroy the 
device and also pose danger to the operator handling it. Thus, it is imperative to have a pressure regulator 
connected to the compressed air tank that brings the pressure down to a safer level below 6 bar. Then the 
output of that pressure regulator is connected to the Inlet valve.  
 
 
Figure 3-13. FlowIO Compressed Air Configuration. 
There are two limiting considerations that must be considered with this configuration. The first is 
that there is a fixed amount of air in the tank, and thus the tank needs to be refilled or replaced once the 
air runs out. The second consideration is that there is no vacuum option in this case, only passive pressure 
release. Nevertheless, in case vacuum is needed, it is possible to connect a vacuum pump to the outlet 
valve in conjunction with the compressed air tank. Though a single -pump FlowIO module has n ot been 
made yet. Further details about this FlowIO configuration are provided in Chapter 8. 
 
3.3.7 Closed-System Configurations 
All the configurations discussed so far have been open -loop, where the ambient environment is 
used as either the source and/or sink of air. However, it’s also possible to configure FlowIO as a fully-closed 
system where the ambient environment serves neither as a source nor a sink. Converting FlowIO to a 
closed-configuration system is achieved by connecting one or more accumulators to the open ends of the 
pumps and potentially to some of the ports as well. At least three closed configurations are possible by 
connecting either one, two, or three accumulators. The figures below show two of those variations. 
 
 
Figure 3-14. FlowIO Closed (+/-) Configuration 

76 
 
 
Figure 3-15. FlowIO Closed2 (+/-) Configuration 
The closed -system configurations are especially well -suited for applications such as buoyancy 
control, hydraulic lifts, or when handling specialized gases such as for chemical reaction control. The 
closed-system configurations also enable FlowIO to be used with clear liquid media such as water or 
alcohol instead of air while keeping the liquid inside the system. 
 
3.3.8 Modularity and Pneumatic Configurations 
In all the Figures above, the left side shows the pneumatic circuit, and the right side shows a 
cartoon representation of that pneumatic circuit resembling how it looks in the implemented physical 
FlowIO device. In all of those cases, there is a strict sep aration between the upper part containing the 
valves and sensors (which we call Controller Module) and the lower part containing the pressure sources 
(which we call Pump Module). Those separations are necessary in order to be able to change between all 
of these configurations as well as to be able to swap one module for another of the same type. Modularity 
in this case emerged as consequence to meet reconfigurability. However, some of the configurations were 
also discovered only as a consequence of the system’s modularity. Thus, the need for configurations led to 
modularity, and then the modularity itself led to new configurations being found.   

77 
 
3.4 FlowIO Hardware Modules 
A modular design philosophy was followed when developing the FlowIO Platform, to make it highly 
reconfigurable and suitable for different applications just by swapping plug -and-play modules within 
seconds. In addition to the general advantages that modular ity offers, as discussed in Chapter 2, FlowIO 
specifically necessitated a modular design in order to be suitable across scales and across fields. For 
example, in the case of wearable applications – small size, long battery life, and low pressures are needed. 
But in the case of desktop of automotive applications – high pressures are often valued while size and 
power optimization are not critically important. Only a design that is modular and reconfigurable can meet 
both sides of this spectrum of needs with different modules for those different application requirements.  
As the spectrum of application needs and the overall diversity of applications created with FlowIO 
expanded over the past years, so did the number of hardware modules in the FlowIO Platform to  meet 
those diverse user needs. Currently there are 18 different modular components comprising FlowIO, which 
are grouped into the 4 categories shown on Figure 3-16.  
 
 
Figure 3-16. All FlowIO Hardware modules grouped into four categories: (a) Controller Modules, (b) Pump 
Modules, (c) Expansion Modules, and (d) Accessories. 
 
  

78 
 
3.4.1 Controller Modules 
The Standard Controller module incorporates all the manifold-connected valves and the manifold-
connected pressure and temperature sensor from the pneumatic architecture shown on Figure 3-6 of 
Section 3.3.1, as well as many other electrical and mechanical components including power electronics, 
microcontroller, battery, connectors of several different types, enclosures, buttons, LEDs, sensors, and 
more. An exploded view of the Standard Controller is shown in Figure 3-17 along with a description of all 
the major components contained in the module.  
The Standard Controller module contains 7 valves in a manifold configuration, where the valves 
have been converted from 3-way to 2-way normally-closed valves. On top of the valves is a custom-made 
driver PCB with power electronics, a 4 -pin magnetic connector, power button, reset  button, battery 
connector, 14-pin expansion port, and another 14-pin port for power distribution to the valves. Behind the 
valves is a second custom PCB to which the valves are mounted, and which contains the integrated 
pressure and  temperature sensor. Underneath the valves is a 600mAh battery which is also custom -
manufactured to fit the module. Between the valves and the battery is a spacer and a layer of Kapton tape 
to thermally isolate the battery from the valves, as they tend to become warm when used continuously 
for a long time. Above the driver PCB lies a microcontroller board (Adafruit Feather Sense) with Bluetooth 
capabilities and integrated sensors. Additionally, the Controller module includes an integrated battery 
charger and a USB port through which the onboard battery can be charged. The Standard Controller 
module integrates more than 10 sensors which can measure: Air Pressure, Water pressure, Temperature, 
Barometric pressure / Altitude, Acceleration, Magnetization, Proximi ty, Battery -level, Sound, Light 
intensity, Color, Humidity, and Gestures (left to right, right to left, up to down, down to up). Many of those 
sensors are integrated on the microcontroller board itself, while others are on the custom-made PCBs. 
 
 
 
Figure 3-17. Exploded view of the Standard Controller module from different angles. 
The only difference between the Standard, Split, and Reduced Controller modules is in how many 
valves they contain and how those valves are arranged. The Standard Controller contains the full set of 
seven valves. The Split Controller contains six valves and is for use with the Split (+/ -) Pneumatic 
Configuration discussed previously where the left and right sides are internally divided. The Reduced 
Controller has fewer than 5 pneumatic ports, which is mainly for cost -reduction and/or mass-reduction 
benefit since the valves are the most expensive of the internal components.  

79 
 
3.4.2 Pump Modules 
There are different size pump modules available for FlowIO, with the main difference between 
those modules being their pressure and flow-rate capabilities. A pump module contains two pumps that 
were shown in the pneumatic configuration diagrams in Section 3.3. There are many other components 
within a pump module besides the pumps, however. Figure 3-18 shows an exploded view of the Small 
Pump module. The module contains two identical pumps facing in the opposite direction, custom driver 
board with power electronics, LiPo battery, integrated battery charger with LED charging indicator, 4-pin 
magnetic connector, 3D printed snap-fit enclosures, and spacers within the enclosures. 
  
 
Figure 3-18. Exploded view of the Small Pump Module from different angles 
Additionally, there are two silicone tubes connected to the pumps, whose other ends are for 
connection to the Controller module. The pump module enclosures also have external channels for the 
silicone tubes to keep them neat and organized. Moreover, there are not only forward-facing channels for 
the General (+/-) configuration, but also backward -facing channels for reconfiguring the device for the 
Series and Parallel configurations. Since most users only use FlowIO in the default General (+/ -) 
configuration, and are unaware of the other configurations, they often don’t realize that the backward -
facing channels also have a purpose until they learn about the different configuration options available.  
Three different mechanical approaches are used to hold a Pump Module connected to a Controller 
module and to establish a mechanically -reliable connection between them. Those include a magnetic 
connector and a mechanical mating notch on the back and two silicone tubes on the sides of the modules. 
In this way, the modules are held together from the top, bottom, left, and right sides. 
Each Pump Module itself is a complete system that is totally power independent of the Controller 
Module, having everything it needs for operating on its own accord just by receiving a control signal 
through the 4 -pin port. The magnetic port only provides s ignaling but no power; no electrical power is 
transferred between the Pump and Controller modules. The four pins are 3V control pins and they include 
Enable, Pump1 ON, Pump2 ON, and GND. The Enable pin is for activating the pump module. The Pump1 
ON and Pump2 ON pins are for switching ON the corresponding pumps when 3V signals are applied to 
those pins. The pump pins can accept a 3V constant or PWM signal. The Controller module of FlowIO 
specifically provides PWM signals to the control pins, so that the flowrates of the pumps can be controlled 
dynamically by modulating the duty cycle of the PWM signals. 

80 
 
The Pump modules were deliberately designed fully power independent, so that they don’t have 
to be voltage-matched to the Controller module. Moreover, the pump driver boards were also designed 
to operate over the 3V to 16V range to support a wide variety o f DC pumps. Ultimately this is to enable 
users to create their own pump modules using any pump of their choice up to 16V if the ones included 
with FlowIO do not satisfy their specific needs. Some users have done just that as discussed in a later 
chapter. However, there is a minor drawback to having the pump modules fully power independent of the 
controller modules. In the words of one user, “charging a single module would be more convenient than 
having to charge two separate modules ”. A solution to this would be possible, at least for the 3V Pump 
modules, if the LiPo battery charger that is in the 3V Pump Modules is moved to the Controller module, 
such that the controller module charges the Pump Module which would still contain its battery.  
The five different Pump Modules are called Small, SubMedium, Medium, Large, and Large (DC), 
abbreviated S, SM, M, L, and L(DC), respectively. The first three use 3V pumps and are powered by built-
in LiPo batteries. The Large module operates on 6 volts and has a Battery version that integrates two 3.7V 
600mAh LiPo batteries and a DC version that uses external power. Identical batt eries are used in the 
Controller, SubMedium, Medium, and Large modules. The battery-powered Large module contains a driver 
circuit with advanced switching capabilities and two integrated battery chargers. The driver establishes 
series connection between the two batteries during use to achieve the required 6V, and it breaks the series 
connection during charging, connecting the two batteries to their respective onboard LiPo charters. This 
circuit was specifically designed to enable charging this module with an ordinary 5V USB charger. There is 
also an integrated mechanism that prevents a user from accidentally con necting a charging USB cable 
while the batteries are still connected in series. The charging cable can only be connected when the 
batteries are disconnected from their series connection.  
The combination of a Controller & Pump module is called a FlowIO Device. Figure 3-19 shows the 
five different FlowIO devices enabled by the five different pump modules. In the case of the S, SM, and M 
pump modules, one pump faces the left and the other pump faces the right side, and a Controller Module 
mounts in front of those three pump modules. In the case of the L and L(DC) pump modules, both pumps 
face forward, and the Controller Module mounts on top of the two pumps contained in those modules.  
 
 
Figure 3-19. Five FlowIO devices with five different Pump Modules – S, SM, M, L, and L(DC). Each device is in its 
default General (+/-) pneumatic configuration.  

81 
 
3.4.3 Expansion Modules 
The Controller Module has a 14 -pin expansion port which exposes GPIO pins, analog input pins, 
power pins, as well as pins for the SPI, I2C, and UART protocols. This expansion port enables various other 
sensors, actuators, and external control devices to be physically connected to a FlowIO device. Expansion 
modules, as the name suggests, expand the capabilities of a FlowIO device . Currently there are five 
different expansion modules within the FlowIO Platform suite. Four of them connect physically via the 14-
pin expansion port, and one of them – XtraSense – connects to FlowIO via Bluetooth instead.  The existing 
set of expansion modules emerged gradually in response to various application needs and feature requests 
beyond those offered by a core FlowIO device. Such new features were initially made application-specific; 
and subsequently they were generalized and packaged as expansion modules. The five FlowIO Expansion 
Modules in existence today are described in detail below. As more needs arise from the user community, 
additional expansion modules may be added to the FlowIO suite. An OLED display for FlowIO is a new 
module currently in development that will be added to the suite as well. 
 
3.4.3.1 Breakout Module 
The most versatile expansion module is a breakout board for the 14 -pin expansion port. Despite 
its simplicity there are some intriguing features to this module. There are 2 identical 14-pin FFC ports on 
opposite ends and opposite sides of the board to allo w daisy-chaining multiple expansion modules. The 
board was designed to be highly breadboard friendly, as narrow as possible to not obscure breadboard 
holes, and with pins distributed evenly on both sides. The Breakout module was also made to be usable 
even without a breadboard, via jumper cables connected directly to the PCB headers – which can be male 
or female and on either side. The pins are marked on both sides of the PCB to aid usability. Finally, the pins 
were carefully arranged in groups, such that I2C pins, UART pins, and SPI pins are in distinct groups next 
to one another, and those groups are themselves distinctively marked on the silkscreen.  
 
 
Figure 3-20. Breakout module for FlowIO 
3.4.3.2 XtraSense Module 
The XtraSense configuration, presented in Section 3.3.5, is made possible with the XtraSense 
Expansion Module, which provides five additional pressure & temperature sensors connected in parallel 
with the five I/O ports of FlowIO as was indicated in Figure 3-12. The XtraSense module was designed as a 
complete standalone system with its own microcontroller, driver board, battery, battery charger, and 
Bluetooth capabilities. Unlike other expansion modules which connect to the 14 -pin expansion port of 
FlowIO, this module does not make an electrical connection with the FlowIO device. Instead, the XtraSense 
module makes a direct connection to the FlowIO web-app via Bluetooth Low Energy (BLE). In addition to 
extra pressure & temperature sensors, the XtraSense module has the entire suite of sensors that are also 
in the FlowIO Controller modules, including an IMU, magnetometer, humidity sensor, altimeter, 
microphone, motion, gesture, and light sensor. 

82 
 
There are two benefits of having the XtraSense module as a completely independent system. First, 
this module can be used without a FlowIO device, such as if one already has an alternative pneumatic 
control system and they need extra pressure sensing capabilities. Second, XtraSense does not occupy the 
FlowIO expansion port, leaving it available for other expansion modules; thus, XtraSense can coexist with 
other expansion modules. There are also slight disadvantages to having XtraSense as an independent 
system. Since the module has its own microcontroller, an integrated battery, and power electronics – this 
makes it more expensive and thicker than it could’ve been if it was instead designed as a wired PCB that 
connects to the FlowIO expansion port.  
 One way to use the XtraSense module is by placing it directly under the Controller module and 
then connecting the five sensors in parallel with each of the FlowIO ports using T or Y splitters. However, 
since the modules are not electrically connected to each other, it’s also possible to place XtraSense to the 
side or even farther away from FlowIO and still make the pneumatic connections in the same way. A third 
way to use XtraSense is to connect it not to FlowIO but to the pneumatic devices being actuated.  Finally, 
since the five pressure & temperature sensors used in XtraSense are hydraulic sensors, it’s also possible to 
use XtraSense to measure temperatures and pressures in hydraulic systems, if liquids are not leaking out 
of the tubes. Figure 3-21 shows how XtraSense connects to the FlowIO ports. 
 
 
Figure 3-21. XtraSense module for FlowIO. 
3.4.3.3 Buttons Module 
 One of the most highly appreciated characteristics of FlowIO is the user -friendliness of setting it 
up and using it with the web-GUI just by pressing buttons on a screen. The Buttons module was designed 
to make controlling FlowIO even without a screen but through physical buttons, by providing users with 5 
programmable buttons and LEDs. The buttons can be programmed to do various actions or sequences of 
actions – from basic to sophisticated. In addition to the buttons, there are also five LEDs, one next to each 
button, and the LEDs are independently programmable as well. Thus, it is possible to control complex state 
machines and even navigate menus by using the buttons as inputs and LEDs as indicators.  
 
 
Figure 3-22. Buttons module for FlowIO. 

83 
 
3.4.3.4 AnalogIn Module 
The 14-pin expansion port of FlowIO has 3 analog input pins. But what if a project requires more 
than three analog inputs? This was the problem faced by the OmniFiber project (Chapter 7), which 
required 16 analog input pins for 16 strain sensors. The AnalogIn module was developed to address this 
need by providing 16 analog inputs split into 4 groups of 4 pins. The module connects to the 14 -pin 
expansion port, and it features three multiplexers along with electronics for signal conditioning to give 
users 16 analog input channels to their disposal. A software driver was also developed for the AnalogIn 
module, which eliminates the complexity of users having to think which multiplexers have to be switched 
in what way. Instead, users simply need to specify which ports are being used and the driver takes care of 
all the switching operations. The AnalogIn module is designed for placement above or below the FlowIO 
Controller module. As such, it requires a 14 -pin FFC cable with opposite-side connectors (not with same-
side connectors). Nevertheless, it is also possible to use the module with 14-pin FFC cable with same-side 
connectors if the bottom-sided FFC connector on the module is replaced with a top-sided FFC connector.  
The OmniFiber paper [109] is one instance showing how this module was used in practice. 
 
 
Figure 3-23. AnalogIn module for FlowIO. 
3.4.3.5 VibroTT Module 
 Many FlowIO applications to date have been in the realm of haptics. The VibroTT module was 
developed to extend the haptic feedback capabilities of FlowIO, allowing it to control up to 5 vibrotactile 
actuators. VibroTT is based on the DRV2605L chip from Texas Instruments which can control both Eccentric 
Rotating Mass (ERM) vibration motors and Linear Resonant Actuators (LRAs). The chip integrates a library 
of 117 signal waveforms such as “Sharp Click”, “Soft Buzz”, “Transition Ramp”, “Double Tick”, and dozens 
more; the full list of all actuation waveforms contained in the library can be found on pages 63 and 64 of 
the DRV2605L datasheet [ 110]. There are five DRV2605L chips in VibroTT to enable fully independent 
control of five vibrotactile ERM or LRA actuators. The VibroTT module is controlled via I2C protocol. It was 
used in the SleeveIO Platform [111], presented in Chapter 5. 
 
 
Figure 3-24. VibroTT module for FlowIO 

84 
 
3.4.4 Accessories 
The final component category in the FlowIO Platform suite is accessories for mounting, 
reconfiguration, and personalization of a FlowIO device. Accessories don’t contain electronics, but they 
augment the possibilities for where or how a FlowIO device can be used. Or in the case of personalization 
accessories, they also change the look and feel of FlowIO and how the device appeals to each user’s tastes 
and preferences. 
 
3.4.4.1 Configuration Tubes and Splitters 
Changing the pneumatic configuration of FlowIO from General (+/-) to Series (+/+) or Series (-/-) is 
achieved by first connecting the two pumps of any pump module in series with an auxiliary tube; and then 
connecting either the inflation-side to the inlet valve for the (+/+) variant or the vacuum-side to the outlet 
valve for the (-/-) variant. In both of these cases only a single auxiliary tube is required.  
Changing the configuration from General (+/-) to Parallel (+/+) or Parallel (-/-) requires a 3-way 
splitter (Y or T type) and two auxiliary tubes. For the (+/+) variant, the inflation ports of the pumps are 
connected to the splitter, and the splitter is connected to the inlet valve. For the (-/-) variant, the vacuum 
ports of the pumps are connected to the splitter, which is then connected to the outlet valve.  
Finally, changing the configuration from General (+/-) to Split (+/-) requires two auxiliary tubes 
and two splitters. Both the inflation and the vacuum ports are each connected in parallel to the inlet and 
outlet valves, respectively, using two splitters 
 Figure 3-25 demonstrates how FlowIO with the Large pump module can be reconfigured for each
