of the six pneumatic configurations. The same can be achieved with any of the other pump modules using 
auxiliary tubes and splitters of appropriate sizes and lengths. 
 
 
Figure 3-25. Demonstrating six pneumatic configurations for FlowIO with the Large pump module through the 
use of auxiliary cnfiguration tubes and Y splitters. 
Auxiliary configuration tubes and splitters are also required in the case of the XtraSense pneumatic 
configuration for establishing the parallel connections between the FlowIO ports and the pressure sensors 
on the XtraSense module. This was previously shown in Figure 3-21.  

85 
 
3.4.4.2 Wearable Straps 
 FlowIO was designed as a wearable -first platform, and thus making the device body -mountable 
was of high priority - especially for applications related to haptics and assistive technologies. The Controller 
module enclosure was designed in a way where different straps can be attached to it, enabling FlowIO to 
be worn on the wrist, upper arm, ankle, leg, torso, or other regions of the body. There are integrated 3D-
printed tabs on both sides of the Controller module, so that a strap can be mounted with a metal rod on 
each strap end, connecting it to the enclosure as shown on Figure 3-26. The design allows wearability just 
like a wristwatch, which is especially valuable for assistive applications. The spacing between the tabs can 
be adjusted arbitrarily, thus nearly any strap from a wristwatch can be used with FlowIO. While the default 
spacing between the tabs is set of a specific inexpensive strap that was readily available on Amazon, there 
is no need for a user to purchase that specific strap if they already have one from an old watch for example 
or if they want something more personal for their unique design taste.  To use FlowIO with a strap of their 
choice, the user simply needs to adjust the tab spacing accordingly before 3D printing the enclosure. 
 
 
Figure 3-26. Strap connection mechanism on FlowIO.  
3.4.4.3 Magnetic Base Cover 
 In addition to wearable applications, FlowIO has also been used for a variety of nonwearable and 
desktop applications. Thus, having a way to mount FlowIO to surfaces is as important as being able to wear 
it on the body. A magnetic base cover was created fo r this purpose to allow FlowIO to mount both 
horizontal and vertical surfaces. The base cover contains magnets on the four corners. It goes over the 
bottom-side enclosure and latches onto the same metal rods that are used for wearable straps. The 
magnets are circular inside a housing. The outer diameter of the magnetic housing is just the right size to 
fit into the base holes, so that the magnetic base cover adds only 1mm to the device height. The same 
latching mechanism can also be used for the creation o f other nonmagnetic stationary mounts including 
ones that accept screws for mounting to an optical table, or ones that that mount to various equipment. 
 
 
Figure 3-27. Magnetic Base Cover for FlowIO 

86 
 
3.4.4.4 Wheels and Axles 
Besides Wearable and Non-wearable applications, there is another application category for FlowIO 
– locomotion – especially in the case where FlowIO is used as a control system for soft robots. In the case 
of locomotive applications, it’s important for the device to be able to move with the soft robot for a fully 
untethered operation. One way to do this is by having the FlowIO device carried onto the body of the 
robot. However, this requires the robot to be fairly strong and sturdy to support the weight of the device. 
Another approach is to have the FlowIO device towed by the robot, which could be more power efficient 
in some cases. Therefore, wheels and a detachable press-fit axle mount were developed as an accessory 
for FlowIO specifically to facilitate towing in untethered locomotive soft robotic applications. The wheels 
are currently passive. However, if there is interest from the community, it is also possible in the future to 
add small motors allowing FlowIO to self -locomote and navigate using the on -board power and 
microcontroller for driving the wheels. 
  
 
Figure 3-28 FlowIO with Wheels 
3.4.4.5 Alternative Enclosures 
 The FlowIO enclosures are 3D printed, thereby offering a high degree of customization in terms of 
design, function, and color .  Enclosure personalization was not recognized as a major advantage until 
different users started expressing interest in having Fl owIO in alternative colors better suited for their 
applications or personal preferences. It was observed that multiple users printed the enclosures in colors 
other than white either for personal appeal reasons or because they wanted to camouflage the device by 
making it the same color as the project they were controlling with FlowIO.  
 Besides alternative colors, enclosures with alternative functional characteristics have also been 
created. One such example is a Lego-compatible enclosure base that makes FlowIO capable of integration 
with construction kits such as Lego. Moreover, if a user wants to have FlowIO mounted to any objects (e.g. 
helmet, bicycle, etc.) or to mount objects to FlowIO (e.g. wheels, straps, etc.), they have the freedom to 
change the enclosure deign itself and reprint it to meet their specific needs.  
 
 
Figure 3-29. FlowIO with clear enclosures.   

87 
 
3.5 FlowIO Software 
The FlowIO suite of hardware modules is only one half of the FlowIO Platform. The other half is the 
software suite , which includes a complete device stack  & web stack  with libraries, APIs, command 
interfaces, Bluetooth-control interfaces, firmware, drivers, graphical user interface, task scheduler tool, 
interactive animated graphics, and dozens of programming examples. The FlowIO software suite makes 
the platform accessible, user-friendly, and suitable for both experts and novices alike. There is a device 
software stack with multiple layers of abstraction and a web stack wi th graphical and programming 
interfaces in different programming languages. A user -friendly GUI allows people from any technical 
background to operate FlowIO within seconds via a web -app just by opening a web -page and without 
requiring any downloads or ins tallations. Intuitive APIs and libraries enable programmers to write their 
own apps or firmware for FlowIO in either Arduino C++, JavaScript, or Python. There is also a command -
execution interface providing users with the opportunity to take advantage of programming instructions 
without having to write entire programs.  
The FlowIO software suite was designed to serve users across fields, backgrounds, and 
programming skills. That is partly why different levels of abstraction were created, so that both novice and 
advanced users can find an abstraction layer suitable for their needs and preferences. For instance, novice 
users can choose to use FlowIO at an abstraction layer in which there is low complexity and fewer control 
options features, while experts may choose to work with FlowIO in an abstraction layer where there is 
high complexity and maximum control, or they can even mix features from different layers of abstraction.  
 
3.5.1 FlowIO Software System Architecture 
 
Figure 3-30. FlowIO Platform high-level system architecture. Overview of the FlowIO Device Stack, the FlowIO Web 
Strack, and the connection between them.  
The FlowIO software system architecture, shown on Figure 3-30, consists of a device stack that runs 
on a FlowIO device, and a web stack that lives on a web server and enables web browsers on devices like 
laptops, phones, and tablets to connect to and control FlowIO simply by visiting an online dashboard.  
The device stack  contains a set of object -oriented C++ libraries defining many properties and 
methods of a flowio object and providing various algorithms for controlling a FlowIO device. The libraries 
have three layers of abstraction – a Driver layer, an Activities layer, and a Command layer. Above those 
layers there is a firmware layer that features a state machine and Bluetooth service and characteristics 

88 
 
that allow full controllability of a FlowIO device over Bluetooth Low Energy protocol. This lays the 
foundation for being able to control FlowIO through various Bluetooth -enabled devices that can access 
the web stack. 
The web stack is the higher -level part of the software suite which enables devices like phones, 
laptops, and tablets to connect to and have full control over FlowIO including the ability to send commands 
and receive information from the device in real-time without any perceptible latency. Thanks to the recent 
development of WebBluetooth, web-pages can connect to Bluetooth devices directly through the web -
browser in which they are running, eliminating any operating system or device dependency. This 
technology is the foundation of the FlowIO web stack and the reason a web-based approach was chosen 
– so that any Bluetooth-enabled device with any operating system that supports a modern web browser 
can be used for controlling FlowIO without having to develop or maintain device -specific or operating 
system-specific software.  
Prior to creating the web-stack, native apps were developed for Windows and iOS, which required 
not only separate maintenance and development cycles, but also device -specific and hardware-specific 
development paradigms; and providing Linux and Android support would have required yet another two 
native applications. The creation of the web stack completely eliminated the need for any native 
applications, and it allowed universal device and operating system compatibility – reducing development 
time and complexity by as much as 80%. Furthermore, an added benefit of the transition from native apps 
to a web dashboard was that users no longer had to download or install anything during set up, and no 
need to check if their software was up to date or to download new updates; all users had to do now was 
just open a webpage with their preferred web browser .  
The web -stack includes the softrobotics.io website where users have to register and log in. 
Additionally, users also have to register their FlowIO device, and then they are provided with a unique URL 
specific to their device with a dashboard personalized f or their account and with access to the features 
they have signed up for. Once a user opens their unique URL, it takes them to the FlowIO dashboard page 
which offers access to both the FlowIO Web -GUI and the underlying FlowIO Web -API. The GUI allows 
controlling FlowIO through a user -friendly interface with buttons and sliders, while the API allows 
programmatic control by entering JavaScript instructions using object-based dot notation.  
In the following subsections, each abstraction layer of the device stack  and the web stack  is 
presented in extensive detail. A comprehensive description is provided of how the software system was 
developed, what are the unique features and characteristics of each abstraction layer, what methods and 
properties are contained in the libraries, and  how to program the device. Also included in the last 
subsection is a specific example demonstrating and comparing how a certain task can be performed using 
any layer of the two stacks. Finally, a short subsection is provided discussing ongoing work towards the 
development of a Peer-to-Peer networking layer enabling FlowIO devices to communicate with each other 
over the internet. 
In addition to the device stack and the web -stack, there is also additional software developed for 
the FlowIO expansion modules. Those include drivers for the Buttons module, VibroTT module, XtraSense 
module, etc. as well as graphical and web -based control interfaces for some the expansion modules. 
However, those module -specific software drivers are not yet integrated with FlowIO’s software 
architecture in user-friendly or intuitive ways. Thus, software details about the expansion modules are not 
discussed in this chapter. Nevertheless, some additional details about the expansion modules can be found 
in specific projects that make use of FlowIO expansion modules, which are discussed near the end of this 
dissertation and also in the corresponding research papers about those projects.  
 
89 
 
3.5.2 FlowIO Driver and Activities Layers of the Device Stack 
The first two layers of the FlowIO software device stack are a Driver layer and Activities layer, which 
are implemented in an object -oriented style and abstracted as Arduino -compatible C++ libraries for 
FlowIO. There is a FlowIO() constructor with multiple properties and methods. Additionally, the FlowIO 
libraries define three new data types called Configuration, Unit, and Component.  
Variables of data type Configuration hold the pneumatic configuration of the FlowIO device, and 
such variables can have one of the following 8 -bit values: GENERAL_IO, SERIES_II, SERIES_OO, 
PARALLEL_II, PARALLEL_OO,  and SPLIT_IO. The ending two letters (IO), (II), and (OO) denote Inlet 
and Outlet connection of the pumps and corresponds to the configurational notations (+/-), (+/+), and (-
/-) respectively. The data type Component defines flowio primitive components, which are named PORT1, 
PORT2, PORT3, PORT4, PORT5, INLET, OUTLET, PUMP1, PUMP2, LEDRED, LEDBLUE, and SENSOR. Finally, 
the data type Unit defines the pressure units that FlowIO supports, namely PSI, ATM, MBAR, KPA, and PA.  
 
 
Data Types 
enum Configuration: uint8_t{ 
GENERAL_IO, SERIES_II, SERIES_OO, PARALLEL_II, PARALLEL_OO, SPLIT_IO}; 
enum Unit: uint8_t{ 
PSI, ATM, MBAR, KPA, PA}; 
enum Component: uint8_t{ 
PORT1,PORT2,PORT3,PORT4,PORT5,INLET,OUTLET,PUMP1,PUMP2,LEDRED,LEDBLUE,SENSOR}; 
 
The FlowIO constructor takes one argument of data type Configuration and, depending on 
which pneumatic configuration is chosen during the object instantiation, it determines the properties and 
behaviors of the instantiated flowio object. If no configuration is explicitly set, GENERAL_IO is assumed 
by default. Additionally, there are getters and setters that allow checking what the current configuration 
is and changing it even after a flowio object has already been created (including dynamically at runtime). 
 
 
Constructor 
FlowIO(Configuration config); 
 
Getter & Setter 
void setConfig(Configuration config); 
Configuration getConfig(); 
 
3.5.2.1 FlowIO Driver APIs 
The Driver layer is the lowest abstraction layer in the FlowIO software device stack. It contains 
properties, methods, and functions for monitoring and controlling individual functional elements within 
FlowIO including pumps, valves, sensors, LEDs, battery level, and all other device indicators. Driver APIs 
are grouped into categories: Pressure-sensing, Power Control, LED Indicators, Error Handling, Hardware 
Status, and Pneumatic Elements. This categorical distinction applies not only on a conceptual basis,  but 
also in the how the FlowIO libraries are split into distinct CPP files. In other words, the same modular 
design philosophy that guided the hardware design also guided the software design to make the FlowIO 
software stack easily understandable, maintai nable, and extendable. This modularization approach was 
used not only for the driver but also for all the other layers of the device stack and the web-stack.  
 The Pressure Sensing group controls all aspects of the integrated pressure sensors. The functions 
drastically simplify the processes of establishing the I 2C connection to the pressure sensor, sending a 
90 
 
request to the sensor to read the pressure, retrieving the pressure value, converting the value to a desired 
unit of choice, and even checking for errors that may occur due to sensor faults or scripting mistakes.  
The onboard pressure sensor is also a temperature sensor, however methods for temperature 
measurement have not yet been implemented in the Driver, mainly because the software development is 
guided by user requests and project needs, and so far, no projects  have yet required the use of 
temperature readings from the internal sensor. Should such user needs arise in the future, then additional 
functions will also be added to the driver for temperature reading.  
 
 
Pressure Sensing 
bool activateSensor(); 
Initializes I2C communication with the on-board pressure sensor and checks its status byte. 
RETURNS:  true if status byte is ok; false otherwise, with error code 222. 
void setPressureUnit(Unit pUnit); 
Sets the unit for pressure values reported by getPressure(void). Possible arguments are 
PSI, ATM, KPA, PA, MBAR. This method is used only if you want to change the default PSI unit. 
float getPressure(void); 
Gets a new absolute pressure reading from the on-board sensor and the value in PSI units, 
unless an alternative pressure unit has been set previously using setPressureUnit(). 
float getPressure(Unit pUnit); 
Gets a new absolute pressure reading from the on-baord sensor and returns the value in the unit 
type specified in the argument. Possible arguments are PSI, ATM, KPA, PA, MBAR.  
 
NOTE: If getPressure returns 888.8, it means the sensor has not been activated. 
 
The Power Control APIs provide power handling features for prolonging FlowIO battery life as 
much as possible, since battery life is a critical characteristic of any wearable device. The FlowIO Controller 
module has advanced power electronics circuitry allowing complete shutdown of the system, so that no 
power is consumed in the off state, with programmatic control that enables the system to be shut down 
through a code-based instruction or for a timer-based shut down after a period of inactivity.  
Included in the driver are intuitive APIs for checking battery level, for powering off the device, and 
for optimizing power consumption. The driver considers the fact that actuating a valve requires a stronger 
magnetic field than merely holding it in an al ready actuated state and implements a power -saving 
algorithm based on this device physics, which can reduce power and extend battery life of FlowIO by over 
50% as will be shown in the device characterization section at the end of this chapter. 
 
 
Power Control 
void powerOFF(); 
Shuts down the FlowIO device by setting pin 16 to HIGH.  
void optimizePower(uint8_t holdPWM, uint8_t thresholdTime); 
Reduces FlowIO power consumption by as much as 50% and prevents device overheating by using 
a valve holding voltage lower than the valve switching voltage. The first argument sets the holding 
voltage. The second argument sets for how long the higher switching voltage should be applied 
after a valve actuation before reducing the voltage to the holding voltage.  
SUGGESTED USE: Put this as the last instruction inside the Arduino main loop, with 1st argument 
set to 150 and 2nd argument to 200, such as : flowio.optimizePower(150,200); 
uint16_t getBatteryVoltage(); 
Returns the battery voltage in millivolts provided that the analog reference voltage is properly set. 
 
91 
 
There are four LEDs on the FlowIO controller module, including three small yellow, green, and blue 
LEDS, as well as a larger more powerful RGB LED. The yellow LED is a battery -charging indicator, which 
turns ON while the device is charging and turns OFF th en charging completes or when a charger is 
disconnected. (Yellow LEDs with the same functionality are also present on all the battery-powered pump 
modules.) The red LED is connected to the same pin that controls device shutdown, and it turns ON only 
momentary during power cycling of the FlowIO. The blue LED serves as an indicator for the Bluetooth 
connection state the FlowIO device is in. The RGB LED serves as an indicator of various device states and 
error states. Of those four LEDs, only the Blue and RGB LEDs are intended for programmability by users, 
and therefore corresponding programming interfaces for those two LEDs are provided in the LED indicators 
Driver APIs group.  
 
 
LED Indicators 
void blueLED(bool power); 
Turn on / off the blue LED. Set argument to 0 or LOW for off, 1 or HIGH for on. 
void pixel(uint8_t red, uint8_t green, uint8_t blue); 
Controls the color and intensity of the onboard RGB led. 
 
 The Hardware Status  APIs provide real -time status monitoring of all components in a FlowIO 
device including LEDs, pumps, valves, and sensors. There is an internal 16 -bit flag where 12 of those bits 
correspond to the on/off state of 12 components. The remaining 4 bits are una ssigned and reserved for 
future use. Every time a component changes state, the corresponding bit in the status flag also toggles. 
Methods are provided for viewing the state of each component separately and all components at once. 
The status flag is protected from being changed by the user. Only system functions that control device 
actions can change bits in the status flag. Therefore, the Hardware Status methods only include getters 
and no setters. In addition to checking the current state of a hardware component, these APIs also allow 
one to check the exact time in milliseconds at which the last activation of that component took place, 
thereby enabling a user to easily create programs based on timed actuation or to actuate ports or 
components for specific durations of time. 
 
 
Hardware Status 
uint16_t getHardwareState();  
Returns a 16-bit value, where the lowest 12 bits map to the on/off states of hardware components.  
The mapping follows the enumeration order of components in the Component enum definition.  
bit 11 – SENSOR bit 9 – LEDRED bit 7 – PUMP1 bit 5 – INLET bit 3 – PORT4 bit 1 – PORT3 
bit 10 – LEDBLUE bit 8 – PUMP2 bit 6 – OUTLET bit 4 – PORT5 bit 2 – PORT3 bit 0 – PORT1 
bool getHardwareStateOf(Component ComponentName);  
bool getHardwareStateOf(uint8_t bitNumber);   
Takes as argument either Component type (top) or component bit number (bottom). 
Returns true if that component is active / on, otherwise it returns false. 
uint32_t getStartTimeOf(Component ComponentName);  
Returns the actuation start time of a specified valve or pump component (items 0 to 8 in the table). 
If the specified component currently inactive, the return value will be 0. Otherwise, the start time 
will be reported based on the output of the Arduino  millis() function. 
 
The most critical methods in the Driver layer are those that control actuation and sensing of 
FlowIO. The challenge in developing the foundational APIs for the pneumatic components was in deciding 
what should be the primitive actions, and how should primitives be packaged in such a way that all higher 
abstraction layers can be developed entirely based on those primitives without loss of generality. Another 
92 
 
vital point of consideration was how to create the primitives in a way that optimizes execution time and 
microcontroller resources while keeping the interfaces intuitive and user friendly. Through an iterative 
design-thinking, implementation, and testing process, it was established that: 
• the primitives should be actions turning components on and off. 
• each primitive action should create a status changed event and update the status byte. 
• each primitive action should create a timestamp for the component it acts on.  
• the primitives would have no return value.  
• individual bits are to be used for the representation of the ports for resource optimization. 
• full bytes are to be used for the representation of the pumps, inlet, and outlet for user friendliness. 
There are methods for turning the pumps on/ off, for opening/closing the inlet and outlet valves, 
and for opening/closing the ports. It was desirable to be able to control multiple ports simultaneously with 
just a single instruction. Thus, a single byte is  used for port control: the lowest 5 bits correspond to the 
on/off states of the 5 FlowIO ports: (Bit0 – port1, Bit1 – port2, Bit2 – port3, Bit3 – port4, Bit4 – port5). 
The upper 3 bits of the argument are irrelevant and have no use. 
 
 
Pneumatic Components 
Whenever any of these methods is invoked, it also acts on the corresponding bit in the status flag. 
Moreover, each method also leaves a timestamp indicating when the invoked action took place.  
void startPump(uint8_t pumpNumber,uint8_t flowRate=255); 
Starts the specified pump number (1 or 2) with specified flow rate (between 0 to 255). The pump 
with connectors on the right side is pump #1 and the pump with connectors on the left side is #2. 
The second argument is optional, and if not explicitly specified, maximum flow rate is assumed. 
NOTE: Once a pump is started, it stays on until the stopPump function is invoked. 
void stopPump(uint8_t pumpNumber); 
Stops the pump number (1 or 2) specified in the argument. If the specified pump is already stopped 
or not active, this instruction will just be ignored and won’t throw an error. 
void openInletValve();  
void closeInletValve();  
Methods for opening and closing the rightmost valve (inlet valve). 
void openOutletValve(); 
void closeOutletValve(); 
Methods for opening and closing the leftmost valve (outlet valve). 
void setPorts(uint8_t ports); 
A bit set to 1 opens  the corresponding port, and a bit set to 0 closes  the corresponding port.  
void openPorts(uint8_t ports); 
A bit set to 1 opens  the corresponding port; a bit set to 0 has no effect  on the corresponding port.  
void closePorts(uint8_t ports); 
A bit set to 1 closes  the corresponding port; a bit set to 0 has no effect  on the corresponding port.  
 
NOTE: While setPorts() acts on all 5 ports, openPorts() and closePorts() methods act 
only on the specified ports while leaving other ports unchanged. The latter two methods are for 
setting ports in a more targeted manner while preserving the state of other ports. For example: 
➔ setPorts(0b00010111);   - open ports 1,2,3,5 & close port 4.  
➔ openPorts(0b00010111);  - open ports 1,2,3,5 & preserve the state of port 4. 
➔ closePorts(0b00010111); - close ports 1,2,3,5 & preserve the state of port 4. 
 
 
 
93 
 
 The final part of the Driver is the Error Handling API group, which contains flags and methods to 
help with debugging and with development. There is a flag for storing error codes, as well as methods for 
reading the flag, clearing the flag, and writing user -generated error codes into the flag. The flag st ores a 
single 8-bit value, allowing error codes between 0 and 255 to be stored within it. Currently, the FlowIO 
system can self-detect a number of errors and raise error codes alerting the user if there is a problem. The 
table below shows the current list of error codes, and the user can also add additional codes to that list if 
they are writing software for FlowIO or developing a particular application of their choice. 
  
 
Error Handling 
uint8_t _errorCode; 
Private flag storing an 8-bit error code. The flag is used by the system to indicate if any of the 
errors listed in the table below has occurred. It can be accessed only via the getter and setter 
below, allowing a user to check for errors or to raise a custom error code. 
void raiseErrorCode(uint8_t code);  
Sets the value of the internal  _errorCode flag to the 1-byte code specified in the argument. 
NOTE: Always ensure that a code you raise is different from those listed in error code table below.  
void clearErrorCode();  
Resets the value of the internal _errorCode flag to 0. 
uint8_t getErrorCode();  
Returns the value of the _errorCode flag. The table below lists all existing error codes defined 
in the FlowIO libraries and firmware. 
0 No error or error code cleared. 
2 Sensor not connected. (Usually happens after a reset) 
102 notify02flag set to true in an unsupported state. 
200 Cyclic Redundancy Check (CRC) failed. 
201 Sensor not initialized. 
222 Bad status byte received during initialization; sensor is probably disconnected. 
223 Initialization flag has not been set; activateSensor() was probably not invoked. 
Error thrown by requestPressure(). 
225 Initialization flag has not been set; activateSensor() was probably not invoked. 
Error thrown by _getStatusByte(). 
 
 
3.5.2.2 FlowIO Pneumatic Activities Layer 
 The second layer of abstraction in the FlowIO device stack is called a Pneumatic Activities layer. 
This layer contains methods and functions that control entire groups of primitive functional elements to 
create activities involving more than one component. For instance, the activity of “inflation” requires at 
least three functional elements – a pump, an inlet valve, and a port – or up to 10 functional elements if 
the inflation is happening on all five ports, if both pumps are used, if pressure is being rea d during the 
process, and if the LEDs are also being triggered. Thus, the activities layer allows a user to simply invoke a 
desired activity, without having to individually control each of the underlying primitives that are required 
for the chosen activity to occur. Moreover, the sequence in which the specific primitives are triggered also 
plays an important role – (e.g. during inflation what should be the order in which the inlet valve, the port 
valve, or the pump be activated, which is first and which is last) – and this triggering sequence is also 
something the user doesn’t need to worry about if they are working with the Pneumatic Activities layer.  
 Identifying and deciding what is the minimum complete set of primitive pneumatic activities for 
FlowIO was a significant conceptual challenge, because this set had to encompass all allowable pneumatic 
actions that FlowIO can do in a way that is completely  general while also being intuitive. Moreover, the 
implementation of the set had to be user friendly and resource-optimized. Another objective that also had 
94 
 
to be met by this layer was exclusion and prevention of unallowed behaviors or sequences that could 
potentially damage FlowIO. One example of unallowed FlowIO behavior would be to start a pump without 
opening any valve. Another example would be to start bo th inflation and vacuum in a pneumatic 
configuration that doesn’t support simultaneous different actions. A search process was undertaken, 
which revealed that there needs to be only four primitive activities, which were later given the intuitive 
names startInflation, startVacuum, startRelease, and stopAction. Four methods with those same names 
were defined, which take as arguments the ports on which they should act in a bit form and the flow-rate 
as a PWM value between 0 and 255. 
Finally, the Activities API also had to be configurationally -aware, meaning when an activity is 
invoked, it should know what pneumatic configuration the FlowIO is in, without the user having to 
explicitly specify the configuration each time they invoke tha t activity. Moreover, if the configuration 
changes, the activity should also adapt to that change. This meant that a function like 
startInflation() should behave differently depending on what pneumatic configuration the FlowIO 
device is in. For example, in the General (+/-) configuration this function should act only on the inflation 
pump, but in the Series (+/+) or Parallel (+/+) configuration this function should act on both pumps as both 
are used for inflation, while in any (-/-) configuration – be ignored.  
 
 
Primitive Pneumatic Activities 
void stopAction(uint8_t ports) 
Stops all pumps, closes the inlet & outlet valves, and closes the ports specified in the argument.   
To stop all components, use an argument value whose last 5 bits are 11111, such as 0xFF .  
void startInflation(uint8_t ports,uint8_t flowRate=255)  
Stops any ongoing actions at the specified port(s), then starts inflation with all  configuration-
defined inflation pumps, with the PWM-specified flow rate in the optional second argument.  
Supported configurations: GENERAL_IO,  SERIES_II,  PARALLEL_II,  SPLIT_IO 
void startVacuum(uint8_t ports, uint8_t flowRate=255)  
Stops any ongoing actions at the specified port(s), then starts vacuum with all configuration-
defined vacuum pumps, with the PWM-specified flow rate in the optional second argument. 
Supported configurations: GENERAL_IO,  SERIES_OO,  PARALLEL_OO, SPLIT_IO 
void startRelease(uint8_t ports) 
Stops all ongoing actions at the specified port(s), then opens both the inlet and outlet valves to 
bring the pressure at the specified ports to atmospheric pressure. Regardless of whether the 
specified port is at high pressure or vacuum, it will be released down or up to ATM pressure.  
 
NOTE:  When startInflation or startVacuum are invoked in unsupported configurations, 
they are simply ignored and don’t cause any actuation action. They also don’t throw any errors. 
 
 Since the startInflation() and startVacuum() functions act on both pumps in the (+/+) 
and (-/-) configurations, there could be some cases where a user may choose to have only one pump 
contribute to an inflation or vacuum activity – even when the pumps are already connected in series or 
parallel. This would offer a secondary approach for controlling pressures and flowrates, in addition to the 
PWM-based approach. For this reason, two additional methods were added to the Activities API for those 
more specialized activities that use a single pump in a series or parallel configuration. For these purposes, 
secondary definitions were created for the startInflation() and startVacuum() functions that 
include two additional boolean arguments allowing the programmer to choose whether to use one or 
both pumps and which of the two pumps to use in the parallel and series configurations.  
95 
 
 
Specialized Pneumatic Activities 
void startInflation(uint8_t ports, uint8_t flowRate=255,  
bool pump1=true, bool pump2=true);  
Same as the original startInflation() function but with two extra arguments, used during the 
Series(+/+) and Parallel(+/+) configurations for specifying whether to use pump1, pump2, or both.  
Supported configurations: SERIES_II,  PARALLEL_II 
void startVacuum(uint8_t ports, uint8_t flowRate=255,  
bool pump1=true, bool pump2=true);  
Same as the original startVacuum() function but with two extra arguments, used during the  
Series(-/-) and Parallel(-/-) configurations for specifying whether to use pump1, pump2, or both.  
Supported configurations: SERIES_OO,  PARALLEL_OO 
 
 There is feedback control API group allowing for inflation/vacuum up -to/down-to a specific 
pressure value as measured by the internal pressure sensor. It includes methods that take in as arguments 
the desired pressure at the desired ports(s) and then take underlying actions until the pressure goals are 
reached. The functions also allow monitoring of how much time the operation takes.  
 
 
Feedback Control 
Supported configurations: GENERAL_IO,  SERIES_II,  PARALLEL_II,  SPLIT_IO 
bool disableInflateP; 
 Public flag used by the functions below to disable them after they complete an operation.  As a 
public flag, it can be reset by the API user with flowio.disableInflateP = false. 
uint32_t inflateP(uint8_t ports, float pMax,  
   Unit pUnit, uint8_t flowRate=255);  
Inflates the specified port(s) to pressure pMax in the specified units at the desired flow rate. This is 
a non-blocking method designed to be invoked in each iteration of the main loop. Once pMax is 
reached, the disableInflateP  flag is set to true and the inflation action stops. 
NOTE: To reinvoke this function, either at the same or at different port(s), you need to 
reset the disableInflateP flag to false again. 
RETURNS: 32-bit integer each time this method is invoked. While inflation is ongoing, it returns 1. 
On the cycle when the pMax is reached and inflation stops, it returns the time duration (ΔT)  in (ms) 
from the start to end of the inflation operation. After inflation has completed, it returns 0.  
NOTE: ΔT is relative to inflation start time of the specific port(s) used, regardless of whether 
the inflation was started by inflateP() or another method in the stack. Say port(1) has 
already been undergoing inflation for 100ms then InflateP(port1) in inv oked without any 
stop in-between. Then ΔT will include the duration of InflateP(port1) plus the extra 100 ms. 
But ΔT will not include the 100 ms if there is a stop in-between or if a different port is used.  
uint32_t inflateP_diff(uint8_t portsA, float pMaxA, uint8_t portsB, 
   Unit pUnit, uint8_t flowRate=255);  
Inflates two sets of ports to two different pressures pMaxA and pMaxB in such a way that portsA 
and portsB start inflating simultaneously until pMaxA is reached, then portsA closes and portsB 
continues inflating until pMaxB is reached. This method is still in the development phase. 
----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- -- 
There are also vacuum feedback control flags symmetrical in their definitions to those above. 
Supported configurations: GENERAL_IO,  SERIES_II,  PARALLEL_II,  SPLIT_IO 
bool disableVacuumP;  
uint32_t vacuumP(…);  
uint32_t vacuumP_diff(…);

3.5.3 FlowIO Command API 
What would be the simplest and most efficient instructional commands through which any feature of 
FlowIO can be controlled? The quest to answer this question led to a surprisingly simple answer. Every 
allowable FlowIO command can be expressed as an answer to a set of three questions:  
(1) What primitive action should FlowIO do?  
(2) Where should the action happen in terms of ports and primitive components?  
(3) How should the action be carried out?  
And then as far as what FlowIO returns back to the command sender, the same three questions can be 
asked about the information flowing the other way:  
(1) What should flow return?  
(2) Where should the data be returned to?  
(3) How should it be returned? 
Since only three questions are needed to express any FlowIO instruction, it follows that a 3-byte instruction 
protocol can offer a viable, highly-optimal, and intuitive solution for FlowIO control where bytes (1, 2, 3) 
encode for the (what, where, how) about any action of interest. Thus, a function defined in the following 
way could potentially offer total control of everything FlowIO can do and everything it can possibly return: 
void command(uint8_t whatAction, uint8_t whereAction, uint8_t howAction,  
  uint8_t whatReturn, uint8_t whereReturn, uint8_t howReturn) 
While this kind of function would offer full generalizability, a slightly more restricted definition of the 
function can be just as valuable but significantly simpler to use and implement. In particular, even though 
there are many kinds of values that FlowIO can return including pressure, temperature, time, as well as 
outputs from the various other integrated sensors, most users are only interested in the pressure as a 
return value. Thus, we can restrict the output to be just the pressure value, and then significantly simplify 
the function by having it directly return the pressure value as a float, rather than having the return 
parameter specified in the arguments. It’s important to recognize that this in no way limits the API users 
about what they can or can’t do with FlowIO as far as retur n values are concerned. It only means this 
particular method command()is limited to specified type of return value, but users can still invoke other 
functions and methods with conjunction with command() to have any type of return value they wish in 
addition to pressure. Two other simplifying assumptions can also be made for the context of fluidic actions, 
namely that the “where” question refers to port(s) on which the action is to take place, and the “how” 
question refers to the flowrate at which the action is to be carried out. Furthermore, for some actions of 
interest there is no “how” parameter associated with them; therefore, the third argument should not 
always be required. With all those simplifying assumptions, the simplified method definition becomes: 
float command(uint8_t whatAction, uint8_t ports, uint8_t flowRate=255); 
Having established the simplified definition of command(), the next task is defining a notation for all 
the commands that may be passed to the function.  In principle, there is a 24 -bit space of 16,777,216 
possible commands, which is the same as the number of colors in the RGB color space. However, argument 
3 is the flowrate, which needs no special notation but numbers. Argument 2 is ports, for which a notation 
was already established in the Driver layer. Th us, notation is needed only for the “whatAction” byte, 
narrowing the problem to just 256 possibilities. The  problem can be further simplified by requiring the 
action notation to be user-friendly; thus, it should be limited to keyboard characters so it can be easily 
entered by just pressing a keyboard key or two. And finally, it should be intuitive and memorable. This 
leaves only a few dozen possibilities of keyboard characters that can be used for action notation. Based on 
these considerations, the following character-to-action mapping representation was defined:  
(+) = inflate;  (-) = vacuum; (^) = release; (!) = stop;  (?) = get;  
(o) = open;  (c) = close; (t) = toggle;  (n) = ON; (f) = OFF,  
97 
 
While second and third arguments will be ports and flowrate in most cases, there are also cases where 
those two arguments need to represent something different. Thus, in the final definition of the function, 
the second and third arguments are treated more generally as parameters about the action specified in 
the first argument. This offers much greater flexibility to create additional 3-byte commands later or user-
defined commands including ones that may intentionally defy the question set order (what, where, how) 
when such an order change would make the user experience more intuitive and memorable. For example, 
in the case of the “get” action, the second and third arguments should be viewed as parameters of the 
action, rather than the “where” and “how” of the “get” action. The box below shows the current definition 
of the Command method and a summary of everything the Command Control API offers. Additional 
commands may be added to the API in the future according to project needs and user requests. 
 
 
Command Control 
float command(uint8_t action, uint8_t param1, uint8_t param2=255); 
Executes the action  in the first argument with corresponding parameters  in arguments 2 and 3. 
The action is specified as an ASCII character. All allowable actions are listed in the table below.  
RETURNS : Pressure value when applicable, or the value 999.9 or 888.8 when not applicable. 
 For Pneumatic actions : 
- param1  corresponds to a ports  byte specifying “ where” the action applies. The last 5 bits 
represent the 5 FlowIO ports (bit0 = port5, bit1 = port4, bit2 = port3, bit3 = port2, bit4 = port1).  
- param2 is optional and corresponds to “ how” the action applies, namely the flowRate  if 
applicable for the action, expressed as a PWM value (0 to 255) with default set to the max 255. 
‘+’, ports, flowRate .startInflation(ports,flowRate); 
‘-’, ports, flowRate .startVacuum(ports,flowRate); 
‘^’, ports, n/a .startRelease(ports); 
‘o’, ports, n/a .openPorts(ports); 
‘c’, ports, n/a .closePorts(ports); 
‘!’, ports, n/a .stopAction(ports); 
For all other actions : 
- param1  and param2 are general parameters serving as qualifier s about to the action. For 
some actions there is only one required parameter while others require both parameters. 
‘f’, ‘f’ [OFF FlowIO] .powerOFF()  
‘f’, ‘b’ [OFF blueLED] .blueLED(LOW) 
‘n’, ‘b’ [ON blueLED] .blueLED(HIGH) 
‘t’, ‘b’ [Toggle blueLED] .blueLED(bool) 
‘?’, ‘@’, ‘p’ 
[Get @-current-state the pressure] 
.getPressure(); 
Returns the pressure. (Returns 888.8 if sensor error.) 
‘?’, ports, ‘p’ 
[Get on-specified-ports the pressure] 
.stopAction(0xff); 
.setPorts(ports); 
 delay(10); 
.getPressure(); 
Returns the pressure. (Returns 888.8 if sensor error.) 
‘?’, ‘@’, ‘t’ [Get @-current-state the temperature] - TODO
