3.5.4 FlowIO BLE Services and Characteristics 
The next abstraction layer in the FlowIO device stack is one which encapsulates nearly all the features 
of FlowIO into a set of Bluetooth Low Energy (BLE) services and characteristics, with different properties 
and permissions. Before delving into the BLE layer of the FlowIO stack, it’s important to first discuss some 
foundational concepts about Services , Characteristics, Attributes, and other notions defined in the 
Bluetooth Core Specification [112]. 
 
3.5.4.1 What are BLE Attributes 
All data on a BLE server is stored in an attribute table, where an attribute is the basic data unit and 
it is defined in the Attribute Protocol (ATT) of the Bluetooth Core Specification [ 112]. An attribute has a 
handle, a type, a value, and permissions. An attribute table can be thought of as a spreadsheet with each 
attribute corresponding to a row, a handle to a row-number, a value to a cell, and permissions to access 
restrictions of the cell. The Handle field contains a 16-bit value serving as the attribute’s address in the 
attribute table. Attributes in the table are ordered by ascending handle number, though gaps are 
permitted. In other words, handle numbers of neighboring attributes may differ by more than one. The 
Value field can store a discreate data value of arbitrary size – from 1 byte to hundreds of bytes. 
 
 
Figure 3-31. Logical representation of an Attribute 
The Type field holds the attribute’s UUID (Universally Unique Identifier), which can be either 16 -bit 
Bluetooth predefined UUID, or 128-bit custom UUID. Each predefined UUID comes with certain roles and 
expectations that the attribute must meet. For example, if th e UUID 0x2902 is used, then the attribute 
must meet the specification for a Client Characteristic Configuration Descriptor Attribute. Or if UUID is 
0x2803, then the attribute is a Characteristic Declaration Attribute. Thus, unless an attribute conforms to 
a predefined set of behaviors and expectations from the Bluetooth core specification, it should never be 
declared with a 16-bit UUID but with a custom 128-bit UUID instead. 
The Permissions field specifies parameters for how a connected client accesses the contents of the 
Value field. The permissions also dictate whether the attribute’s value should be sent over an 
authenticated and/or encrypted link. The permissions themselves can be viewed by a client, even if the 
value is protected. The permissions field controls four permission types:  
(a) Access type:    [Read]; [Write]; [Indicate]; [Notify] 
(b) Encryption required:  [Yes]; [No]  
(c) Authentication required: [Yes]; [No]  
(d) Authorization required:  [Yes]; [No]  
Access permissions define the behaviors about how an attribute displays and presents the data it 
contains. In addition to or instead of Read and Write access, a characteristic can send its value in one of 
two unsolicited ways called Notify and Indicate. They both send the value to a client without the client 
requesting it, with the difference being that Notify sends only the value, while Indicate sends the value 
plus a request for the client to acknowledge (indicate) that it received the value. Notify and Indicate 
provide a more efficient way of sending values to a client without the need for a read request. Notify is 

99 
 
especially well suited for rapidly sending sensor data to a client, and is the approach used by FlowIO to 
send sensor data in real-time continuously throughout the duration of any pneumatic operation. 
 
3.5.4.2 What are BLE Characteristics 
A characteristic in the BLE specification is a data container encapsulating at least two attributes – a 
Characteristic Declaration and a Value Declaration attribute. Optionally, a characteristic can also include 
one or more Descriptor Declaration  attributes. Figure 3-32 shows a logical representation of a 
characteristic. 
 
Figure 3-32. Contents and structure of a BLE Characteristic and a Service. Characteristics and Services are defined 
in the Generic Attribute Profile (GATT) of the Bluetooth specification.  
A Characteristic Declaration Attribute contains required metadata about the characteristic. It always 
has the UUID 0x2803 in its Type field as predefined by BLE GATT. Its Permissions are always Read-only 
access with no authorization and no authentication. Its Value field contains the UUID (16-bit or 128-bit) 
and Value Handle (16-bit) of the Value Declaration Attribute of the characteristic, as well as characteristic 
properties (8-bit). Therefore, the Value field contains either exactly 5 bytes or exactly 19 bytes.  
 
Figure 3-33. Characteristic Declaration Attribute. 
Each bit in the characteristic properties byte represents a specific property as described below. In 
addition to Read, Write, Notify, and Indicate, there are also other properties as listed in the table below, 
including the ability to add additional properties in a CEPD (Characteristic Extended Properties Descriptor). 
Bit 7 Bit 6 Bit 5 Bit 4 Bit 3 Bit 2 Bit 1 Bit 0 

100 
 
Extended 
Properties 
Authenticated 
Signed Write 
Indicate Notify Write Write w/o 
Response 
Read Broadcast 
Figure 3-34. Bit field definitions of the Characteristic Properties byte. If the Extended Properties bit is 
set, additional properties can be added in a CEPD, which needs to be created in that case. 
It may seem redundant having characteristic properties when the Value Declaration Attribute already 
has its own access permissions. However, as clarified in [113] by Nordic, the manufacturer of the nRF52840 
chip: the properties for the characteristic value are only  guidelines or clues to what the client can expect 
from the Value Declaration attribute. Attribute permissions always overrule characteristic properties. 
The Value Declaration Attribute  is the main data container of a characteristic, and it holds the 
characteristic’s value  in the attribute’s Value field. This attribute comes immediately after the 
Characteristic Declaration Attribute in the table of attributes. For example, the Value field may hold a 
temperature value, a state of an LED, a spatial coordinate value, etc. The UUID field of this attribute is set 
to the characteristic UUID specified in the Value field of the Characteristic Declaration Attribute.  
The Descriptor Declaration Attribute(s) are optional and store descriptive information about the 
characteristic’s value. Descriptor attributes come immediately after the Value Declaration Attribute in the 
table of attributes. The UUID of the descriptor specifies what kind of descriptor it is. For example, if the 
Value field of the descriptor holds the units [ºC], one may call this a “units descriptor”, and if the Bluetooth 
specification provides a predefined 16 -bit UUID for a descriptor that holds units, th en that predefined 
UUID number should be used for the UUID field. Otherwise, if this kind of descriptor is not predefined in 
the BLE specification, it would be considered a custom descriptor requiring a 128-bit UUID in its UUID field. 
The BLE predefined descriptors include CEPD with UUID (0x2900), CUDD (0x2901), CCCD (0x2902), SCCD 
(0x2903), CPFD (0x2904), and CAFD (0x2905).  
For instance, the Client Characteristic Configuration Descriptor (CCCD) allows a connected client to 
configure the Notify and Indicate behaviors of the characteristic by writing to the CCCD’s Value field which 
is a 16-bit value, where Bit0 is for Notify, Bit1 for Indicate, and the upper 14 bits are unused. The CCCD’s 
permissions must be set to Read & Write with Authorization. By default, the CCCD’s Value field should be 
set to the 16 -bit value 0x0000. Then Notify=0x0001, Indicate=0x0002. The Notify or Indicate bits of the 
descriptor only matter if the characteristic’s Properties byte (located in the Characteristic Declaration 
Attribute) has its own Notify or Indicate bits set to true. In other words, the descriptor cannot enable 
Notify or Indicate unless they are already enabled in the Characteristic properties.  
The Server Characteristic Configuration Descriptor  (SCCD) behaves similarly to CCCD, with the 
difference being that bit0 of its 16-bit value controls the Broadcast behavior of the characteristic while the 
upper 15 bits are unused. Thus, the default is 0x0000 and Broadcast is 0x0001. And again, this only has an 
effect (and can only be set) if the Properties byte of the characteristic has its own Broadcast bit set to 1.  
The final descriptor relevant to FlowIO’s stack is the Characteristic Presentation Format Descriptor 
(CPFD), whose Value field defines the format of the characteristic’s value. The CPFD’s Value has 56 -bits 
representing the following categories in little-endian order: Format (8-bit), Exponent (8-bit), Unit (16-bit), 
Name Space (8-bit), and Description (16-bit). The Format holds data type (e.g. int, double, bool, struct, 
etc.) where each data type is represented by an 8 -bit number from the Bluetooth Specifica tion’s list of 
Assigned Numbers [114] (e.g. 0x01 = “boolean", 0x0A = “uint64”, etc.). Unit, Name Space and Description 
are also represented by means of assigned numbers for those specific categories found in the same PDF 
document. The Exponent contains a numerical value which represents (Characteristic Value * 10Exponent). 
 
3.5.4.3 What are BLE Services 
A service is a group of characteristics that define a specific feature or function as represented on 
Figure 3-32. In addition to the characteristics contained within it, a service also has a Service Declaration 
Attribute with UUID 0x2800 in its Type field and Permissions that are Read-only access requiring no 
101 
 
authentication and no authorization. The Value field contains another UUID, called Service UUID, defining 
what kind of service is being defined, which is either a 16-bit value for services predefined in the Bluetooth 
specification or a 128 -bit for custom -defined services. It is also possible for a service to include no 
characteristics or to include other services (that don’t circularly reference the original service). A device 
would typically have multiple services and may even have multiple services of the same type (same Service 
UUID). All attributes on a server must contain a service declaration or exist within a service definition; 
there cannot be attributes or characteristics that are not part of a service. In the attributes table, a service 
definition begins with the Service Declaration Attribute and ends before the next Service Declaration 
Attribute or when the maximum Attribute Handle in the table (0xFFFF) is reached. 
Fundamentally, a BLE server device only has a flat table of attributes serving as containers. Services 
and characteristics are just conceptual constructs defined by the contents of the attributes and 
representing relationships between attributes. Attributes with the UUID 0x2800 mark the start of primary 
services, and attributes with UUID 0x2803 mark the start of characteristics. There can be multiple 
attributes in the attributes table with the same UUID, but each attribute has a unique Handle number. 
The Bluetooth SIG has defined several services and characteristics for common use cases, as well as 
profiles which define the behavior of not only the server but also the client [ 115]. Whenever possible 
predefined profiles, services, and characteristics should be implemented for compliance with the standard 
and for reduction of packet size (16 -bit as opposed to 128 -bit UUID); custom ones should only be 
implemented when predefined ones do not exist. In the case of FlowIO, it implements some predefined 
services including Battery Service and Device Info Service with their corresponding characteristics.  
 
3.5.4.4 FlowIO Services and Characteristics 
 FlowIO implements 12 Primary Services with over 20 different Characteristics in total. Four of 
those services are based on the BLE standard and include the GAP Service, GATT Service, Battery Service 
and Device Info Service, along with their corresponding required characteristics. The remaining 8 are 
custom services with 128-bit UUIDs, and each of them has its own set of custom characteristics with 
their own 128-bit UUIDs. The custom FlowIO services are named Power Off Service, Indicator Service, 
Config Service, Control Service, Pressure Service, GPIO Service, AnalogIn Service, and Vibration Service. 
As their names suggest, these services define the various features offered by a FlowIO Device and its 
Expansion Modules. 
The FlowIO Battery Service provides information about the state of the battery within a FlowIO 
Control module. The UUID for the Battery Service is 0x180F and it has one characteristic called Battery 
Level, with UUID 0x2A19. The characteristic holds the current battery level (in 1 -byte), represented as a 
percentage between 0 and 100. The characteristic’s property byte has its Read and Notify bits set to 1 and 
all others in the properties byte are 0. Thus, a connected client can obtain the battery level by ei ther 
reading it or by subscribing to receive it as a notification, but the client cannot change the value of the 
characteristic, nor does it have to acknowledge that it has received the value. FlowIO checks its internal 
battery voltage once every 5 seconds, converts the voltage reading to a battery percentage, and compares 
that value to the value stored in the characteristic. If th e new percentage is different by more than 2% 
from the stored percentage, the characteristic value is updated to the new percentage. If the battery level 
goes below 10%, then the characteristic begins sending its battery level to a connected client as a 
notification, without waiting for the client to read it. The characteristic sends a notification with the battery 
level value also  when a client establishes a connection to the FlowIO device. The Battery Level 
characteristic also includes Client Characteristic Configuration Descriptor (CCCD) with the UUID 0x2902 
and Read & Write permissions, enabling a connected client to write to th e descriptor in order to control 
the Notification and Indication behavior of the characteristic. The FlowIO Battery Service is based on the 
guidelines defined in version 1.0 of the Bluetooth -SIG Battery Service Specification [ 116] which permit 
only one characteristic in this service. However, with the new specification version 1.1 published in 2023 
102 
 
[117], there can now be up to 12 different battery-related characteristics with associated behaviors. Some 
of the new optional characteristics include: Battery Critical Status, Battery Health Status, Battery 
Information, Battery Time status, Battery Model Number String, Battery Serial Number String, and more. 
Future versions of the FlowIO Battery Service may also implement some of these new characteristics that 
are now supported in the most recent Bluetooth Specification for this service. 
The FlowIO Device Info Service  (UUID=0x180A) exposes information about the hardware and 
software on the specific FlowIO device unit itself. Three standard characteristics are implemented within 
this service named System ID, Serial Number String , and Firmware Revision String with standard UUIDs 
0x2A23, 0x2A25, and 0x2A26, respectively, where the first two hold a 16-byte value and the last one holds 
an 8-byte value. Each of the characteristics has Read-only access permissions, no security restrictions, and 
no notification or indication behaviors. There are also no descriptors within any of the characteristics. The 
standard specification [118] for this service defines 0 mandatory and 10 optional characteristics, and the 
FlowIO implementation of this service offers 3 of those 10 in compliance with the standard. The primary 
utility of the Device Info Service in the context of the FlowIO stack is  for the Web-GUI to know firmware 
version a FlowIO device has, to show features that are compatible with that firmware, and to alert the 
user if a FlowIO firmware update is available for them to download. 
The FlowIO Power OFF Service is a custom-defined service (with a 128 -bit UUID) for exposing and 
controlling power-related features of the FlowIO device. The service allows a connected client to turn off 
the device, set a power -off timer, check the timer status, reset/remove the timer , as well as to specify 
whether power-optimization mode should or should not be used by the FlowIO device. All of those 
features are achieved with just a single custom characteristic (with 128-bit UUID) that has read, write, and 
notify properties. FlowIO has an auto-off timer that by default is set to turn off the device after 5 minutes 
of no activity. A connected client can write different values to the characteristic to control power-related 
behaviors of the FlowIO device. For example, writing 0xFF disables the auto-off timer, 0x00 – powers off 
the device immediately, and 0xNN sets the timer to NN minutes, where NN is any two digit hex number. 
The client can also read the characteristic to find the current power state of the device, check how many 
minutes remain on the auto-off timer, or to check if the timer is disabled. Additionally, the characteristic 
also notifies the client automatically once the timer reaches 3 minutes, 2 minutes, and 1 minute before 
automatic power off occurs. The characteristic also includes a descriptor (CCCD type) with read & write 
access permissions through which the client can control the characteristic’s notification behavior. 
The FlowIO Control Service is another custom service and arguably the most vital one in the FlowIO 
stack, because this is the service through which connected clients can control all pneumatic actuation 
features of FlowIO. This service has two custom characteristics – a Pneumatic Command Characteristic 
with a Write-only property and Hardware Status Characteristic with Read and Notify properties, where 
both characteristics have 128-bit UUIDs.  
The Pneumatic Command Characteristic accepts 3-byte or 2-byte values that are then translated into 
pneumatic actuation instructions executed by the FlowIO device.  A connected client can only write but 
not read the characteristic value. The value written to the characteristic is processed by  the FlowIO 
firmware, and if it meets certain validity criteria, it is fed into the command() method discussed in the 
previous layer of the stack. The same byte order and notation is used as in the previous layer; bytes 1,2,3 
correspond to the 1 st,2nd,3rd arguments, respectively. Moreover, just as the 3rd argument of command() 
was optional, byte 3 of the characteristic value is also optional. However, there are also some important 
differences. The first difference is that, unlike the method which provides a return value, the characteristic 
does not provide any value to be read, nor does it offer th e read permission. Second, the Pneumatic 
Command characteristic accepts only pneumatic actuation commands as suggested by its name but no 
other actions. For example, control power, pressure, indicators, and other non-pneumatic actions that are 
allowed by the command() method are not allowed by the Pneumatic Command characteristic . If an 
103 
 
invalid action is passed to the characteristic, the FlowIO firmware will ignore it and not send it to the 
command() method. 
These restrictions on the characteristic were intentionally imposed, in order to follow the philosophy of 
the Bluetooth specification, which recommends that distinct functionalities should be in different services.   
The Hardware Status Characteristic allows a connected BLE client to see the real-time status of each 
internal component within a FlowIO device.  The characteristic stores a 16 -bit value that mirrors the 
hardware-status flag of FlowIO. Each of the lowest 12 bits is mapped to a particular in ternal component 
and the value of a bit represents the state of the corresponding component. The bit -to-component 
mapping is the same as given in the discussion of the Hardware Status API group earlier in this chapter. 
The upper four bits don’t map to any component and are reserved for future use. The characteristic has 
the Read and Notify properties and includes the CCCD descriptor. The value of the characteristic updates 
automatically whenever the state of any valve, pump, LED, or other hardware component changes, and 
any changes in the value are sent as notifications to the client, without requiring the client to read the 
characteristic to find the current hardware state. This characteristic plays a vital role in the Flow IO GUI 
implementation, because the interactive animation prominently displayed on the Dashboard page is 
enabled by this characteristic. It is thanks to this characteristic and its notification behavior that the 
animated graphic can display a visual representation of hardware status changes in real-time.  
There are seven more services also present in this layer of the FlowIO device stack, which will be 
covered only in brief in the remainder of this subsection. Indicator Service controls the state and behavior 
of internal and external indicators on the FlowIO device.  Internal indicators include error reporting and 
system status and there is a custom Error Characteristic for those. External indicators are the LEDs on the 
device and those are controlled through another custom characteristic called LedStates.  Config Service is 
used for changing the pneumatic configuration of FlowIO. It is a custom service and implements its custom 
Config Characteristic.  A custom Pressure Service with one standard characteristic called PressureValue 
and two custom characteristics, called MaxPressureLimits and MinPressureLimits, controls aspects and 
behaviors of the device related to pressure sensing.  
Two mandatory standard services are present on the device called GAP Service and GATT Service. 
The GAP Service (0x1800) implements the BLE standard characteristics of: Device Name (0x2A00), which 
holds the name of the FlowIO device;  Appearance (0x2A01), which holds a 2 -byte assigned value 
corresponding to an icon or a string;  Peripheral Preferred Connection Parameters  (0x2A04), specifying 
FlowIO connection parameters ; and Central Address Resolution  (0x2AA6), specifying where there is 
support for privacy wit h address resolution. The GAP Service also supports characteristics called 
Resolvable Private Address Only  (0x2AC9), Encrypted Data Key Material (0x2B88), and LE GATT Security 
Levels (0x2BF5) which are used for encryption and security though are not implemented currently as such 
features have not yet been requested by any FlowIO users but may be added in a future release. The GATT 
Service (0x1801) in FlowIO implements only one standard characteristic, Service Changed (0x2A05), which 
is a control -point attribute used to indicate to connected clients that the services definitions in the 
attributes table have been changed. The purpose of this characteristic is to tell a bonded client that the 
data in its cache about the service of the device it is bonded with is no longer relevant. Other 
characteristics that are defined in the specification of this service, but not implemented in FlowIO, are 
Client Supported Features (0x2B29), Database Hash (0x2B2A), and Server Supported Features (0x2B3A). 
Then there are three custom services that are specific to respective expansion modules of FlowIO. 
Analog Service exposes the functionality and features of the AnalogIn module. It has two characteristics 
called AnalogValue and AnalogValue16. VibrationService is implemented for use with the VibroTT 
expansion module and has only a Vibrate characteristic through which all features of the module are 
exposed. And the final custom service is GPIO Service  that enables control of the GPIO pins that are 
exposed to the 14-pin expansion port. This is by far the most complicated of the different services on the 
FlowIO device since GPIO pins have many different primary and secondary roles, some offer analog inputs, 
104 
 
PWM outputs, digital inputs, digital outputs, and some are also serving as interfaces for protocols likes SPI, 
I2C, and UART. Moreover, certain pins also have internal pullup resistors that can be enabled or disabled. 
This service needs to provide all those capabilities and there is a separate custom characteristic for each 
GPIO pin number, named Pin## Characteristic where ## is the number of the pin the characteristic 
corresponds to. The GPIO service is still in an alpha phase of development.  
 
3.5.5 FlowIO Firmware 
The firmware for FlowIO is Arduino based. It creates a Peripheral GAP (Generic Access Profile) role 
for FlowIO giving it the ability of sending advertising packets, responding to connection request from 
devices with a Central GAP role, receiving commands from connected devices, and sending data to 
connected devices. Once a GAP Central device makes a connection request, the FlowIO device decides 
whether to allow or disallow the connection based on the parameters sent in the connection request from 
the Central device. The firmware gives FlowIO the role of a GATT ( Generic Attribute Profile) Server and 
implements an attributes table with the GATT Services and Characteristics discussed in the previous 
subsection.  
All the dependencies required by the BLE services and characteristics are also provided in the FlowIO 
firmware. For instance, PowerOff Service depends on there being a power-off timer on the device, which 
is implemented in the firmware. The Control service requires a parser to process commands written to its 
Command characteristic and to filter out invalid commands. This parser and filter are also part of the 
firmware layer. Similar kinds of dependencies apply for nearly all the custom services along with most of 
their characteristics. 
The firmware also controls the power -on and power off sequence of the device, what is in the 
advertising packet it broadcasts, the advertising time interval, the transmission power used, what happens 
when it disconnects from a Bluetooth client, how self -checks for hardware problems are performed, the 
functions of buttons, and many other features and properties of the FlowIO device and its expansion 
modules. For instance, when the device is powered up it performs a quick self-check for errors and missing 
components and, if no problems are found, it goes into General Discoverable mode where it sends 
advertising packets every 25ms indicating readiness to connect to a Bluetooth client. If no device connects 
within the first 15 seconds, then FlowIO starts adverti sing less aggressively with 150  ms advertising 
interval. And if no connection is made in five minutes, then FlowIO automatically turns off to save battery 
power. The LEDs on the device also serve as indicators of the processes that are happening on FlowIO.  
In addition to the Standard Mode of operation provided by the firmware, which offers all the standard 
functionality, features, and behaviors of FlowIO, the firmware also includes an entirely separate mode of 
operation as well, called Test Mode. The two modes have their own separate state machines. Whenever a 
FlowIO device is powered on, it always goes into the Normal Mode state machine and stays on that state 
machine during any typical application use. Most users will never have to leave the Normal Mode state 
machine. The Test Mode of the firmware is effectively a service-menu for troubleshooting FlowIO, which 
is accessible by pressing a hidden button with a thin metal rod such as the end of a paperclip. When the 
button is pressed, the device switches to the secondary state machine that controls the Test Mode; one 
can switch back to the primary state machine by power cycling the device. The Test Mode can also be 
accessed by connecting FlowIO with a USB cable to a computer, opening a serial monitor on the Arduino 
IDE, and following the instructions that are automatically sent by the device. The Test Mode of FlowIO 
provides a sequence of self-tests through which a user can go to inspect and troubleshoot any problems 
with their FlowIO device. For instance, there are tests for checking the connections to sensors, valves, and 
pumps. There are also tests that check if each of the valves is opening and closing properly, tests for leaks, 
tests for pressure levels, a battery test, and also a power controller test. The s ystem makes clever use of 
integrated actuators and sensors to be able to perform these and other tests all by itself, requiring almost 
105 
 
no actions from the user. The user simply supervises the FlowIO device as it goes through its sequence of 
self-tests, by monitoring whether each self-test passes or fails. During the tests, the FlowIO device uses its 
onboard RGB LEDs as information indicators giving feedback to the user that a test (a) is in progress, (b) 
has succeeded, or (c) has failed – indicated, respectively, by (a) dynamically changing, (b) solid Green, or 
(c) solid Red color of the RGB LED. For users who assemble FlowIO by themselves, Test Mode provides the 
critical role of allowing them to quickly validate whether they have completed the assembly successfully. 
 
3.5.6 FlowIO Web Stack 
Having covered the entirety of the FlowIO Device Stack in the previous subsections, this section 
now starts exploring the right-hand-side of Figure 3-30  - the FlowIO Web Stack.  The web stack runs on a 
web server and is integrated into the SoftRobotics.IO website, enabling devices such as phones, laptops, 
and tablets to operate as BLE clients capable of connecting and controlling a FlowIO device within range 
through a Bluetooth connection. To access the FlowIO Web -App for the first time, users simply need to 
visit the softrobotics.io website with any web browser on any device, register their FlowIO device after 
creating an account on the website, and then just open the FlowIO Dashboard page. No downloads or 
installations are required at all, because the Dashboard page itself is the FlowIO Web-App, allowing users 
to connected to their FlowIO device(s) directly from their web browser with the click of the button – and 
having full access to the Web-APIs and the Web-GUI environments for controlling local FlowIO devices 
entirely from within the web browser. Moreover, after having gone through the registration process once, 
the next time a user decides to connect to their FlowIO device, they would directly open the Dashboa rd 
page / Web-App and connect to their FlowIO device within seconds.  
The foundation of the web stack is the SoftRobotics.IO website which serves and provides access 
to the Web -App. The basis for the Web -App is the Web Bluetooth protocol , enabling Bluetooth 
communication between a web browser and a BLE peripheral device such as FlowIO. Web-Bluetooth is a 
recent technology first introduced by Google in 2014 and subsequently added to their family of Google 
Chrome web-browsers. Then, in the early 2020’s other web browsers started integrating Web-Bluetooth 
capabilities, and as of today Web-Bluetooth is supported by all major web-browsers and operating systems 
across mobile and desktop host devices. 
Web Bluetooth is provided as a JavaScript API that allows web developers to create websites that 
interact directly with BLE devices. This is done by the browser gaining access to the BLE chipset that is 
embedded in the device the browser is running on, which could be a  smartphone, a tablet, a PC, or any 
other device that can run a modern web browser. Some of the key benefits of Web -Bluetooth include: 
alleviating the need for native apps; multiple operating systems support; multiple host devices support; 
same level of security as the web browser offering the BLE capability; security updates handled by the 
provider of the web-browser, not by the application developer; HTTPS security; users always having the 
latest web-app version when they visit the website of the web-app; and no downloads or installation needs 
from users, thereby not only adding convenience but also eliminating many potential security risks related 
to downloading and installing files from the internet. Some of the limitations of Web -Bluetooth include: 
more limited functionalities and capabilities than native BLE applications; support for Central role only and 
no Peripheral role on the host device; discovery and connectivity must always be user initiated and 
approved every time rather than being automatic; and support for a maximum of 7 simultaneous 
connections. However, the benefits far outweigh any limitations. 
 Web-Bluetooth exposes JavaScript APIs allowing developers to interface with the BLE chipset on 
the device. The APIs rely on the JS concept of Promises and provide functionality for interacting with BLE 
devices including: device discovery, connecting with devices, service and characteristic discovery; and 
performing different operations on characteristics including reads, writes, and notifications.  
106 
 
The underlying steps for connecting to FlowIO via the web browser are as follows. The browser is 
first instructed to scan for devices that meet specific criteria, such as filtering for specific device names or 
filtering for devices that support specific services. A dialog box is then displayed to the user providing a list 
of all discovered BLE advertising devices that meet the filtering criteria. The user is prompted to select a 
device from the list and connect to it by pressing a Connect button. After this , the browser finds the 
services and characteristics of interest. Finally, the browser performs read, write, or subscribe to 
notification operations on those characteristics. During any steps of this process the browser or the FlowIO 
device may terminate t he connection for a number of reasons, which may include: a characteristic of 
interest was not found on the device; the device was not registered with the web-app; an error occurred 
with the FlowIO device or the web -app; unstable connection; or the device turned itself off due to low 
battery. Once a connection is established successfully, the web-app has full control of FlowIO.  
 
3.5.6.1 FlowIO Web-API 
The Web -API implements the client -side component of all the FlowIO BLE services and 
characteristics that were described in section 3.5.4. It provides a way to connect to one or more FlowIO 
devices via BLE, to interact with the services and characteristics on the device(s), and to be able to read, 
write, and subscribe to notifications from the characteristics, thereby allowing full control over the 
connected FlowIO device(s). The Web -API is implemented in JavaScript using an object -oriented design 
approach where the names of objects, methods, properties, and functions are identical to the names used 
in the device stack. For instance, the sam e methods that were implemented on the device stack, like 
startInflation(), startVacuum(), startRelease(),  etc. are also implemented in the Web -
API and given the same names. One major difference is that this is now JavaScript code rather than C++ 
code and thus JavaScript syntax rules apply.  
An even more important difference is that the Web -API is not limited to controlling just a single 
FlowIO device, and thus there is not one flowio object but a flowios[]array of objects. The Web-API 
allows the web-browser to establish Bluetooth connections to as to as many as 7 local FlowIO devices and 
to control them all simultaneously, synchronously, or asynchronously. Figure 3-35 shows how more than 
one local FlowIO device can connect to the same host. This is the reason for using an array of flowio 
objects, where each object in the array represents a different FlowIO device. Whenever a new device is 
connected, its corresponding object is appended to the end of the array; and the object is deleted when 
the device disconnects. If there is only one connected FlowIO device, its JavaScript object representation 
would be flowios[0]. If a second device is connected, it would be represented as flowios[1], and so 
on. Thus, to request a pressure reading from device #1, the Web -API code for this would be 
flowios[0].getPressure(). There is always an index that needs to be specified with the object.  
The Web-API was designed to provide the same level of control features that the device stack 
offers without any loss of generality. And additionally, it also adds many new features like controlling 
multiple devices, interacting with the web browser, adding and removing devices, storing device data, data 
processing, debugging, task scheduling, con trol of animated interactive graphics, advanced power 
management, control algorithms, and more.  
107 
 
 
 
Figure 3-35. Visual representation of two local FlowIO devices simultaneously connected via Bluetooth to same 
mobile or desktop host through the Google chrome web browser. The Web-API is hosted on a server. It gets loaded 
onto a mobile / desktop device when that devi ce visits the softrobotics.io website and navigates to the FlowIO 
dashboard page. 
Another major advantage of the Web -API is that it allows externalization of computationally 
demanding tasks. Tasks that would be too slow or impossible to perform on the embedded system can be 
externalized into the web-browser to be performed on the phone or laptop host which has much greater 
computation power. Furthermore, for extremely demanding tasks, they can even be offloaded by the 
browser into the cloud for even greater computational resources should such a need arise. Thus, with the 
Web-API, the computational capabilities of the FlowIO Platform are practically unlimited. 
There are also some disadvantages to the Web -API. It requires that an uninterrupted Bluetooth 
connection be always present between the FlowIO device and the Bluetooth device host, thus the FlowIO 
device(s) must be within reasonable range of the host device. Moreover, if too many other Bluetooth 
devices are nearby, then connection issues may arise due to interference. Another drawback is that the 
Web-API cannot update or change the fi rmware of the FlowIO device. One cannot simply compile the 
JavaScript code based on the Web-API and upload it to the device, nor can they download a new firmware 
version and upload it through the web-browser. Such operations involving firmware changes still require 
the Arduino toolchain and knowledge of the C++ based device stack. 
The design principles of Modularity and Generalizability that guided the hardware development 
of the platform were also followed for the design and implementation of the Web -API, and all technical 
decisions were made accordingly. For instance, Each BLE service is isolated into a separate JavaScript file 
and there are no dependencies between them. This allows services, and therefore features, to be added 
or removed simply by adding or removing JavaScript files, in a direct correspondence to the physical 
process of attaching or detaching hardware modules to add or remove hardware features. The web-API is 
significantly more complex than the entire device -stack, but this modular design approach allows that 
complexity to be isolated into separate and independent JavaScript modules. Thus, even though the entity 
as a whole is complex, modularity makes it simple to understand, debug, maintain, and extend with new 
features. If there is a problem that needs addressing, it is isolated and easy to track down. Or if a new  
feature is needed, only a specific module file would need to be changed while keeping all other parts 
unaffected. Moreover, it was modularity that enabled the Web -API to be developed in the first place. A 
nonmodular design would have quickly grown to become overly complex, intractable, and unmanageable. 
The modular design prevented the system from succumbing to the complexity catastrophe.   
The most straightforward and quickest way of using the FlowIO Web-API is to first open the FlowIO 
Dashboard page, then launch the web-browser’s Developer tools, and then opening the browser’s Console 
too. In the Console, one can directly start typing JavaS cript code. One can also use the console in 
conjunction with the FlowIO Web-GUI, to modify or argument its features and capabilities. A future goal 

108 
 
is to make the Web-API accessible even without the need of opening the browser’s Console, by having a 
text field directly in the Web-GUI Dashboard which can accept JavaScript code or JavaScript files directly. 
 
3.5.6.2 FlowIO Web-GUI Dashboard 
 The FlowIO Dashboard is simply a graphical layer on top of the FlowIO Web-API, allowing users to 
connect to and control FlowIO devices without writing any code. The primary benefits of the GUI are the 
convenience, speed, and user -friendliness it offers com pared to the Web -API or any layers within the 
device stack. The layout, functionalities, graphics, and overall appearance are designed to make the GUI 
highly intuitive, so that learning to use it takes only a minute and requires no prerequisite knowledge.  
The GUI, shown in Figure 3-36, was specifically designed to be not only functional but also highly 
intuitive to learn and friendly to use. Three distinct design approaches were employed to make the 
learning curve as flat as possible and to allow one to begin using it within seconds as  soon as they see it 
for the first time, even without any prior background knowledge and without having to read any 
documentation about it.  
 
 
Figure 3-36. FlowIO Web-GUI Dashboard 
The first approach was the graphical design of the icons and other visual elements. All buttons and 
graphics were custom drawn (as vector graphics in Krita) to provide a visual representation of the purpose 
and function of each button. Significant effort was put into the design of the icons to make them as simple 
as possible while still accurately depicting what is happening. Figure 3-37 describes what each button 
graphic represents and what it does.  
The second design approach is by providing a tooltip for each control. Thus, if a control feature is 
not clear from its icon graphic, a user can simply hover over the icon with their mouse cursor and read the 
corresponding tooltip offering a description about what the control is and what it does.  
The third method through which the GUI makes itself easy to learn is immediate visual feedback 
in the form of an interactive animation that represents what is happening on the FlowIO device. This allows 
the user to observe the causal relationship between a  control and its effect on the internal components 
of FlowIO in a salient way.  

109 
 
The Web-GUI dashboard is separated into four sections by the three gray vertical lines splitting 
the interface into a sidebar, a Config / Control section, a Scheduler section, and a Log section. There is 
also a tab interface at the top indicating that all four sections are part of a single tab, and that new tabs 
can be created using the plus icon to the right of the active tab.   
Sidebar section - provides basic device information and control features not related to actuation. 
There is a connection icon  which serves as a connect/disconnect button and as a connection status 
indicator. This is the button that a user needs to press to establish a Bluetooth connection with a FlowIO 
device when they first launch the GUI. The same button is used for disconnecti ng from a FlowIO device. 
The icon itself changes between three states: “connect”, “connecting”, and “disconnect”. Thereby it also 
serves as connection -status-indicator for the user. The second icon on the sidebar is a device error 
indicator. A green checkmark indicates normal operation. If any error is raised by the FlowIO device, the 
icon will change to a yellow warning sign or a red x, depending on the severity of the error. Hovering over 
the icon provides a tooltip showing the error code and its description. It should be noted that this indicator 
is only for errors with the FlowIO device itself and not for errors with the web-stack or web-browser. The 
next icon is a battery level indicator. The icon visually shows the amount of battery life remaining using 
an interactive graphic with four dynamic bars. Moreover, when the battery level drops to one bar or less, 
the icon changes color from green to red. The icon also includes a dynamic toolti p providing the 
percentage of the battery level remaining. The next control in the sidebar is a power button. Pressing the 
button will shut down the FlowIO device and Bluetooth connection to it will be lost. However, to the right 
of the power button is a dropdown menu through which a user can control the auto-off timer. By default, 
the timer is set to 5 minutes of inactivity. One can set this time to a different number of minutes, or they 
can also d isable the auto -off completely. Finally, right below the power off button is a time-indicator, 
which is a countdown timer showing the time until auto -power-off if no activity occurs during that time. 
If any activity takes place, the timer resets to the value set by the user from the drop-down menu next to 
the power button. And if the auto-off timer is disabled, then this countdown timer is not shown at all. 
Config / Control section - offers interfaces for controlling actuation and pneumatic configuration 
parameters, buttons for instantaneous actions, pressure level indicators, and an interactive graphic 
showing the real-time status of each internal component of the connected FlowIO device. To the left of 
the graphic there are six configuration buttons for setting the pneumatic configuration of the device. The 
software-selected pneumatic configuration should always be matched to the pneumatic configuration of 
the hardware. Above the FlowIO graphic are four action buttons for inflation, vacuum, release, and stop, 
in that order.  The two horizontal sliders above them control the flowrate by changing the PWM duty cycle 
of the signal driving each pump. Underneath the device graphic are five pressure gauges, each gauge 
showing the last known pressure at its corresponding port. Whenever a pneumatic action is ongoing, the 
pressure is continuously monitored by the device and is reported and displayed back to the GUI. For 
example, if inflation is happening and ongoing on port 1, then pressure gauge 1 would be showing the 
current real-time pressure of port 1 and updating it continuously. Once the operation on port 1 stops, the 
corresponding pressure gauge will stop updating the pressure, but it will keep displaying the last known 
pressure value it reported. Therefore, each gauge always reports the last known pressure at the port the 
gauge represents. Figure 3-37 shows all action and configuration buttons. 
Right above the 5 ports of the FlowIO device graphic is a square checkbox for each of the five 
ports. The action buttons require a user to first select a port or ports on which to act via checkboxes. Then 
the action buttons operate only on the checked ports. The action buttons operate using the press, hold, 
release operations of a left mouse button, as well as cursor move. Pressing and holding on an action button 
causes the action to start and keep going for as long as it remains pressed. Releasing the butt on causes 
the action to stop. This choice of making the buttons behave in this way was made because, in some 
applications, users want to be able to start and stop an action within a very short time, and the more 
110 
 
conventional alternative of having to press two separate buttons and move the mouse between them 
would take far too long. 
   
 
Figure 3-37. Configuration and Action icons custom designed for the FlowIO GUI. (A) For the Configuration icons, 
a green rectangle represents the FlowIO Controller module while a circle with an arrow through it represents a 
pump; the arrow direction shows whether the pump is used for inflation (if it points in) or vacuum (if it points out). 
Each icon depicts the configuration it represents. (B) For the Action icons, a hollow square represents an inflatable 
air chamber, and an arrow piercing the rectangle represents wh ether air is flowing into or out of the chamber 
based on the direction of the arrow. Hence the inflate button has an inward piercing arrow, the vacuum button – 
outward, and the release button is shown with two opposing arrows indicating that air can flow in or out.   
The Action buttons also have an interaction mode where an action can keep going even after the 
button is released. This can be done when the mouse press occurs over the button, the cursor is moved 
outside the button boundary, and the mouse release occurs. Only if the mouse release also occurs over 
the button will  the stop command be triggered on release. Although this was added as a shortcut for 
keeping an action going, it can be confusing to users initially and may even be misinterpreted as a bug 
rather than a feature, as shown from the case studies. Therefore, this feature will be removed in the next 
release, and mouse release  will trigger stop regardless of where the mouse cursor is positioned. An 
alternative functionality will also be added to be able to keep the action ongoing, by introducing a toggle 
switch that allows the user to choose between sticky or non-sticky action buttons. The default option will 
remain as non-sticky buttons where mouse press starts the action and mouse release stops it. But if the 
sticky buttons option is chosen, then a mouse press on the first click will activate the action button, and a 
mouse release on a second click will deactivate the action button.  
The most prominent item in the Config / Control interface is the FlowIO device graphic, which is 
fully interactive and animated, and it shows what is happening to each internal component of the 
connected FlowIO device. The graphic is vector -based and composed entirely of SVG code modules 
embedded within CSS / JavaScript code. The graphic ca n show valves opening and closing, pneumatic 
configurations changes, starting and stopping of pumps, and pressure changes. The behaviors of the 
interactive graphic are no t triggered by the buttons and controls on the screen, but by changes in the 
FlowIO hardware itself. Because the graphic shows actual device status, changes in the device status, even 
if they are not initiated from the Web -GUI, will also be reflected and s hown on the GUI. The signal flow 
path for how the device graphic updates is as follows:  
Web-GUI → Web-API → Web-Bluetooth → BLE Service on FlowIO device → FlowIO status flag 
change → Send BLE notification → Web-Bluetooth → Web-API → Interactive device graphic on GUI. 
 Task Scheduler section – allows the creation of a sequence of time-based actions to be sent to the 
connected FlowIO device. The task scheduler can have any number of rows, each with four fields. The first 
field, labeled t0 (ms) is the start time of an action in milliseconds. The start time is with respect to the 
pressing of the Play button at the bottom of the task scheduler. A value of 0ms will trigger the action as 
soon as Play is pressed; a value of 1000 would correspond to a delayed start by 1s. The second field is the 
Action field, which offers a drop-down list of actions: Inflate, Vacuum, Release, Open, Close, Stop. The third 
field, PWM, controls the average voltage applied to the pump(s) involved in the action in terms of PWM 
value between 0 and 255. The last field, ports, contains checkboxes used for choosing which port(s) the  

111 
 
action is to be applied to. The scheduler always uses the pneumatic configuration selected from the 
configuration buttons to the left of the device graphic.  
If a user wants to do action X for 2 seconds followed by action Y for 1 second, then they need to 
enter the sequence: [0ms, X] -> [2000ms, stop] -> [2000ms, Y] -> [3000ms, stop]. The start time of each 
subsequent action should always be greater than or equal to that of the previous, and a stop must always 
be included as well. It has been observed that users sometimes confuse the start time field thinking that 
it is a duration field, in which case they may instead incorrectly enter: [2000ms, X] -> [1000ms, Y]. An error 
checking feature is implemented to detect such errors, to now allow a sequence to run when t0 values are 
not in ascending order, and to prompt users to correct their schedule of execution tasks.  
To ensure that a schedule always terminates with a stop, the last row of the scheduler is hard -
coded to be permanently set to the stop action with all ports selected and only the t0 field being editable. 
This not only ensures that the device is always left in a safe state, but it also serves as a reminder for the 
user that the first column represents start times rather than duration times. 
One limitation with the task scheduler currently is that it cannot be exported to or loaded from a 
file. Another limitation currently is that the scheduler only supports one connected device. Both of those 
features are fairly straightforward to implement and a re intended to be added in future releases along 
with other features li ke pressure-based control, ability to input a schedule in a text format, and more 
actions.  
 Log section – displays messages, notifications, events, and other information of relevance to the 
user that is generated by either the connected FlowIO device or by the Web -App. For instance, when a 
Bluetooth connection is established with a FlowIO device, informatio n about the connection and the 
device – including firmware version, device properties, battery level, error codes – is displayed in the log. 
Whenever the FlowIO device generates any alert notification – such as a low battery alert or auto-off timer 
alert – the alert is shown in the Log box. The box is resizable and there is also Clear Log button below it. 
Tabbed Interface – up to 7 FlowIO devices can be simultaneously connected to the Web -App. 
Whenever a new device is added, a new tab is created with controls specific to that device. Each connected 
device has its own designated tab. The user can create up to 7 tabs, corres ponding to the maximum 
number of simultaneously connected FlowIO devices possible. When a device is disconnected and is no 
longer needed, the tab corresponding to that device can be closed and deleted by the user. 
 
3.5.6.3 FlowIO Peer-to-Peer control over the internet 
Given that a FlowIO device has BLE rather than WiFi interface, it may seem unlikely at first that 
FlowIO devices can connect to other FlowIO devices over long distance via the internet. However, since a 
FlowIO device can connect to a web browser, it can also leverage all the features that a web browser can 
offer, including the ability to connect to the internet. Therefore, FlowIO can connect to the internet via the 
web browser as a proxy. Moreover, if there is a second FlowIO device somewhere else on the in ternet, 
then it’s possible to have the two devices interacting with each other over the internet. This can enable 
many new classes of applications like physical telepresence and remote telecollaboration, among others. 
There are at least three ways of achieving FlowIO control over the internet: 
{-1-} The first and simplest way, without any programming required, is through a remote desktop 
application that creates a P2P network between two computers on the internet. Then a connected device 
can be controlled from either the local computer or from the remote computer.  
{-2-} A more elegant approach is to have remote control capabilities implemented within the 
FlowIO Web-Stack, requiring no special permissions, no remote desktop software, and no downloads or 
installations. Such a solution is currently in development using the peer-to-Peer WebRTC protocol. 
WebRTC is a technology introduced in 2011, and it allows the establishment of secure peer-to-peer (P2P) 
connection between two or more web browsers without an intermediary node and without the need of a 
native app. A signaling server is needed only in the beginning to allow the two parties to securely connect 
112 
 
with each other; and afterwards, the signaling server is no longer needed as data is exchanged directly 
between the two browsers without passing through the signaling server. Figure 3-38 shows a diagram of 
how such a P2P connection would look between two FlowIO devices on the internet. 
 With the WebRTC protocol, the two computers are linked directly to each other, and information 
is not relayed via the server. The server is used only for loading the FlowIO Web -App on each browser 
initially and to put the two devices in contact with one another. After those initial steps, the server has no 
role. Implementing a WebRTC P2P network requires nothing more than plain JavaScript and a signaling 
server; and to use this P2P network, the user does not need to download or install any software or plugins. 
 
 
 
Figure 3-38. Peer-to-peer internet connection between two FlowIO devices. 
{-3-} A third way of achieving internet-based FlowIO control is by using a microcontroller with WiFi 
capabilities, such as the ESP32. This would allow the Controller module itself to connect to a WiFi network 
and to the internet, eliminating entirely the need f or an intermediary device with a web -browser. 
Transitioning from a Bluetooth-based to a WiFi-based FlowIO would require minimal hardware changes, 
because there is an ESP32 board with the same pinout as the board currently used by FlowIO.  Software 
wise, this switch would require all the Bluetooth APIs to be ported to WiFi APIs which may take some time. 
However, the main drawback of this approach is that WiFi requires far more power than BLE. Furthermore, 
a FlowIO directly connected to the internet may be a security vulnerability. The safer approach is {-2-}. 
 
3.5.7 FlowIO Basic Example – demonstrated with each layer of the stacks 
Let’s consider an example of some basic action one may wish to execute with FlowIO and explore 
how this action can be implemented and executed using any layer of the device stack or web stack. 
Suppose the desired action is to perform inflation on port 3 for 2 seconds, check the pressure, then stop 
the action. For simplicity, assume maximum flow -rate and the General (+/-) configuration are used, and 
that a blocking function like delay() is also allowed in this example. Furthermore, assume all objects, 
parameters, and required variables have already been created, and focus only on the example essentials. 
With all of these assumptions in mind, there are 7 different ways this example can be realized, using each 
layer of the device stack and web stack would be as follows: 
 
 
Figure 3-39. Graphical representation of the example to be implemented. 

113 
 
 
3.5.7.1 Solved using the Driver Layer: 
 Using the lowest layer of the device stack, we would have to specify the actions of each individual 
component while also paying attention to the sequence in which the actions are invoked. Notice that the 
instruction to start the pump comes before the instructions for opening the valves, and this is because the 
actuation speed of the pumps is slower than the actuation speed of the valves. However, when we are 
issuing the stop commands, the order of the commands is not simply inverted but quite different instead. 
 
 
flowio.startPump(1,255); //pump1, max flow rate 
flowio.openInletValve(); 
flowio.openPorts(0b00000100); //port #3 in binary notation 
delay(2000); 
float p = flowio.getPressure(); 
flowio.closePorts(0x04); //port #3 in hex notation 
flowio.stopPump(1); 
flowio.closeInletValve(); 
 
3.5.7.2 Solved using the Activities Layer 
 The Activities API greatly reduces the complexity of performing the same task, reducing the 
number of methods invoked and reducing the number of FlowIO instructions from 7 to just 3 compared 
to the previous solution. This approach also eliminates the complexity of deciding what is the most optimal 
order in which pneumatic components within activity should be triggered, because the Activiti es layer 
already takes that into consideration internally. 
 
 
flowio.startInflation(0x04, 255); 
delay(2000); 
float p = flowio.getPressure(); 
flowio.stopAction(0x04); 
  
3.5.7.3 Solved using the Command API 
 By using the Command API, one can further reduce the complexity to just a single method invoked 
three times with different arguments corresponding to different 2-byte commands. Since the example is 
to be done at maximum flowrate, the third byte of the command becomes unnecessary. 
 
 
flowio.command(‘+’,0x04); 
delay(2000); 
float p = flowio.command(‘?’,’?’); 
flowio.command(‘!’,0x04); 
 
3.5.7.4 Solved using the BLE Services Layer: 
 Any BLE host device that can establish a connection with FlowIO can control it by writing values to 
its characteristics. For instance, a general -purpose Bluetooth control app like NRF Connect would be 
suitable for this purpose. Once connected to the FlowIO device, one would go into the Control Service >> 
Command Characteristic and then change the value to correspond to the 3 -byte command for start 
inflation on port 4 at max flowrate . The Pressure Value characteristic of the Pressure service would 
automatically start sending the pressure value continuously as a BLE notification several times per second. 
114 
 
After a period of two seconds, one would then write the 2-byte stop command to the Control Service >> 
Command Characteristic >> Value field. 
 
 
Figure 3-40. Implementation of our example by interacting directly with the BLE services and characteristics on the 
FlowIO peripheral. The order of instructions is from top to bottom. The first write command starts the action and 
the second write command stops the ac tion two seconds later. Notifications containing the changing value are 
sent continuously during that interval. 
3.5.7.5 Solved using the Web-API 
 One can implement this example using either the writeCommand() method with different action 
argument or the startInflation() and  stopAction() methods. Assuming only one FlowIO device 
is connected to the Web -APP , the Web-App will create a new object at index 0: flowios[0]. Then the 
JavaScript code would simply invoke the appropriate methods on the object at index 0. 
 
 
flowios[0].startInflation(0x04,255); 
//pressure values are automatically returned and captured by an event listener  
setTimeout(flowios[0].stopAction(0x04), 2000); 
 
3.5.7.6 Solved using the GUI Dashboard Buttons: 
 One would first check the box corresponding to port number 3, to select it as the active port. They 
would then press the Inflate button, hold it for 2 seconds, then release it. During that 2 second period 
while inflation is happening, the interactive graphic will show that pump 1 is on and that the inlet valve 
and the port 3 valves are also on. Moreover, the pressure gauges underneath the interactive graphic will 
also show the pressure value continuously as it changes. The last measured pressure value just before the 
button was released will still be shown on the gauges even after the inflation action has stopped. 
 
 
Figure 3-41. To inflate port 3 for 2 seconds and obtain the final pressure: (1) Put checkmark in the port 3 checkbox; 
(2) press the Inflate button, hold it for 2 seconds, then release it; (3) Check the value shown on pressure gauge 3.  

115 
 
3.5.7.7 Solved using the GUI Task Scheduler: 
 Create a schedule with two rows where the first row would start the inflation process, and the 
second row would stop the process 2 seconds later. The pressure value will be automatically reported 
continuously during the inflation operation on the gauges, and the last measured pressure will continue 
to be displayed after the operation completes. The number of rows in the scheduler should always be 
equal to the number of tasks to be executed, if there are more than two rows those should be deleted 
using the buttons above the scheduler. However, by default, the scheduler displays two rows when the 
GUI dashboard is first opened. Assuming the inflation is intended to start as soon as the Play button is 
pressed, the first rows should be populated with t0 set to 0 ms, Action set to Inflate, PWM set to 255, and 
Ports set to port 3. The second row is also the last row of the scheduler, and therefore it is always set to 
the stop action and all ports are check marked. The only field that can be changed is the action start time, 
which should be set to 2000 ms for this example. Pressing the Play button will execute the scheduled tasks 
and automatically display the pressure value on the port 3 gauge. 
 
 
Figure 3-42. To inflate port 3 for 2 seconds using the task scheduler, (1) create an Inflate task at t=0 and a Stop task 
at t=2000 ms (2) then press the Play button at the bottom. (3) The pressure will be displayed on pressure gauge 3. 
3.6 FlowIO Additional Features and Design Considerations 
3.6.1 Power Efficiency Features and Design Choices 
As a wearable-first battery powered device, FlowIO was designed to be highly power-efficient and to 
achieve high performance in a miniature and lightweight package. On a single charge, the current version 
of a FlowIO Controller module can last approximatel y 80% longer compared to some of the earliest 
iterations of that device. This drastic improvement in the battery life of the Controller was achieved over 
the course of many design iterations, where some of the steps taken towards this goal included: (1) 
Ordering a custom-made battery designed specifically for the FlowIO dimensions that offers 20% higher 
capacity compared to the closest off -the-shelf battery; (2) using Bluetooth Low Energy (BLE) instead of 
Bluetooth Classic or WiFi for communication; (3) Sel ecting a power -efficient microcontroller; (4) 
Implementing dynamic valve current control in the hardware and developing corresponding control 
features in the firmware to achieve more than 50% reduction in power reduction per valve; (5) Integrating 
a power-control chip on the driver board that can fully power off the device; and (6) developing an auto-
off timer in the firmware that turns off the device after five minutes of inactivity.  
The first three steps above required only swapping components or choosing one type of technology 
over another one. This also extended to the choice of valves being used in FlowIO, where they had to be 
not only miniature, but also low-power and low-voltage, so that they could be powered from a single LiPo 
battery. The latter three steps required entire new features to be designed, developed, and implemented 
in the hardware and the firmware; each of those features will be described in more detail below. 
 
Dynamic Current Control 
By using dynamic current control for the valves, power consumption during operation can be reduced 
by over 50% without a negative impact on the performance of FlowIO in normal use cases. This was 
achieved by recognizing that solenoid actuators need high cu rrent only during the actuation phase but 
significantly less current during the holding phase. Thus, hardware and software features were 

116 
 
implemented into the FlowIO power electronics and firmware to deliver high current initially during 
actuation and then reduced current during hold. Dynamic current control was implemented to be 
independent for each valve, and the specific current control p arameters – such as actuation period and 
current amplitude – can be programmatically controlled in the firmware. 
Various measurements and tests were conducted to set optimized parameters for actuation state, 
holding state, and transition time between states in order to achieve high power reduction without loss of 
performance. Implementing programmatic control over th e timing interval for each valve is vitally 
important because on/off times specified by the valve manufacturer are not completely true. Using FlowIO 
across many different applications and over wide pressure ranges has demonstrated clearly that the on/off 
times of the valves are pressure dependent. At higher pressures, the valves take longer time to actuate, 
and moreover, the open / close times also depend on the direction of the pressure gradient. Thus, if there 
are two or more chambers connected to FlowIO , and if they are at vastly different pressures, the 
corresponding port valves would need slightly different times to open / close. Thus, having programmatic 
control enables the pressure dependence to be taken into account when selecting how long to stay in the 
actuation current regime before transitioning to the holding current regime. Moreover, this can be done 
on each valve independently. In addition to more than doubling the battery life, this power -saving 
algorithm also keeps the device temperature low and greatly reduces the risk of potential overheating. 
 
Power Controller 
A dedicated power management IC chip was added on the FlowIO driver board to achieve a true 
power-off state, where the microcontroller and other electronic components are functionally fully 
disconnected from the battery and not drawing any parasitic current when FlowIO is off.  
Prior to the addition of the power management IC, the device could only be put into deep sleep mode 
but not fully powered off unless a physical toggle switch was used. The power controller IC enabled FlowIO 
to have a standard on/off push power button on the device rather than a toggle switch and to allow power-
off features to be added to the firmware and software, including auto-off via timer or on task completion. 
 
Auto-Off Timer 
An auto-off feature was implemented into the FlowIO firmware, such that the device can power itself 
off after a period of inactivity. The firmware monitors each device event and how much time has elapsed 
since the last event. If the time since the last device event reaches five minutes, the device turns itself off. 
A device event, for the purposes of the auto-off timer, is any pneumatic actuation event and also any BLE 
connect / disconnect event. Conversely, actions such as sending and receiving data over BLE, changing the 
state of an LED, or obtaining a new reading from a sensor do not reset the auto -off inactivity timer . 
Additionally, the device sends a notification after each minute of inactivity, returning a value equal to the 
number of minutes remaining until auto-off. This notification is captured by the FlowIO GUI and displayed 
to the user.  While the auto -off timer Is set to five minutes by default, this timer can be changed by the 
user at runtime and set to any number of minutes from the GUI, or it can be disabled entirely. Additionally, 
since the auto -off feature is programmatically controllable, one can program the FlowIO device to turn 
itself off after a particular operation completes, when a specific event occurs, or when set of scheduled 
tasks completes.  
 
Connectivity Options 
FlowIO was designed to over many connectivity options in order to serve many different needs. Thus, 
the expansion port was designed to offer access to all the free GPIO on the microcontroller and all power 
pins. Careful considerations were made about how t o design FlowIO such that there would be as many 
free pins as possible. Earlier versions of FlowIO had a 12 -pin expansion port, and the current version has 
a 14 -pin expansion port as clever opportunities were found to free up more microcontroller pins. 
117 
 
Additionally, the choice of which pins to use for FlowIO’s internal operation, and which pins to leave free 
was based on careful planning, where the guiding principle was to first use -up the pins with the least 
features while leaving the pins with most features free and accessible to the user via the expansion port. 
Thus, the most versatile of the microcontroller’s GPIO pins – including analog pins, SPI pins, I2C pins, UART 
pins, and PWM pins – were left unused whenever possible and less feature-rich pins were used instead. 
In addition to the connectivity provided via the expansion port and the protocols exposed through it, 
FlowIO also offers wired connectivity via the USB port and remove connectivity via Bluetooth Low Energy.  
 
Sensing Capabilities 
 The FlowIO controller integrates a large number of sensors that can measure the following 
quantities: Fluid Pressure, Barometric pressure (separate sensor), fluid temperature, ambient temperature 
(separate sensor), 9 DoF motion (Accelerometer/Gyroscope/Magnetometer), Proximity, Light, Color, 
simple gestures (left to right, right to left, up to down, down to up ), Sound, Humidity, and Battery level. 
Additionally, because multiple FlowIO devices can be synchronized, differential quantities can also be 
measured by pairing two identical sensors on different devices. In this way, one can measure differential 
pressures, differential temperature, and direction of motion with a pair of proximity sensors, and even 
flow dynamics with a combination of pressure sensors. 
  
118

3.7.2 Pressure and Flowrate  
There are four different kinds of pumps that are used across the FlowIO Pump Modules. And each 
module supports five pneumatic configurations. Thus, many different pressures and flowrate 
measurements can be made across all the different modules and configur ations. Moreover, it makes a 
difference whether the measurement is taken directly at a pump module or at one of the ports on the 
Controller module, because the valves act as resistors. Additionally, four different types of valves can be 
utilized in FlowIO – type A, B, C, D – each with different maximum pressure and flow-rate ranges. Therefore, 
to not introduce this extra set of variables caused by the valves, flowrate measurements were done 
directly at the Pump modules and not at the Controller ports. Pressures were measur ed using the 
integrated sensor inside the FlowIO controller, while flowrates were measured using pneumatic flowmeter. 
The following two tables summarize the results of all those measurements.
