These values can vary depending on the specific batch of components used for making FlowIO. That’s 
because even identical pumps from the same manufacturer have slightly different performance 
characteristics, and the same applies for all the other components within FlowIO. 
 
3.7.3 Battery Life and Charging 
Battery life and charging time measurements were performed for the Controller module and for each 
Pump module under different operating conditions. Parameters for the tests were chosen to closely 
resemble how people typically use the device modules. 
In the case of the Controller module two tests with slightly different conditions were conducted to 
measure how long the device lasts on battery under typical operations. Starting with a fully charged 
battery, valves and LEDs were being turned ON for 3 seconds then OFF for 3 seconds repeatedly until the 
battery was drained. Two type C valves and one LED were actuated for the first test, and three type C 
valves and one LED – for the second. The battery was fully recharged between tests. The power -
optimization feature was also applied during the tests. To measure the impact of the power-optimization 
algorithm, a third test was also conducted with parameters identical to those of Test 2, except that the 
power-optimization feature was turned off. The charging ti me was also measured. Charging current can 
be increased to achieve faster charging time if needed, though slower charging is better for the longevity 
of the battery. The results are summarized in the table below. The data indicates the critical role played by 
the power-optimization feature in extending the battery life from 2.5 hours to 6.5 hours in the 3 -valve 
toggle test. Additionally, with power-optimize ON there was barely any temperature increase of the device. 
 
Test Power Optimize Controller Module 
Battery Life, Toggle 2 valves ON 11h 
Battery Life, Toggle 3 valves ON 6h30min 
Battery Life, Toggle 3 valves OFF 2h30min  
Battery Charge Time (adjustable) - 2h30min  
Table 4. Battery life and charging characteristics for the Controller Module under different test conditions. 
In the case of the Pump modules, similar tests were performed for the Small, SubMedium, Medium, 
and Large (Batt) pump modules – 3 seconds ON, 3 seconds OFF repeatedly – first with only one of the 
pumps and then with both pumps contained in the module under test. The tests were conducted at 
maximum flowrate and zero pressure, with both ends of the pumps open to the e nvironment. The table 
below summarizes the results. The charging times for the pump modules were on par with the charging 
time for the Controller module, as expected, since they use the same type of batteries and chargers.  
 
Test S Module SM Module M Module L (batt) Module 
Battery Life, Toggle 1 pump 9h 4h30m 3h 2h30min 
Battery Life, Toggle 2 pumps 5h15m 2h30min 1h30min 1h40min 
Battery Charge Time (adjustable) 2h 2h 2h 2h30min 
Table 5. Battery life and charging characteristics for the Pump Modules under different test conditions. 
3.7.4 Sound and Noise Levels 
FlowIO produces some amount of audible noise s when the pumps are on and when the valves are 
opening or closing. Measurements were performed using a spectrum analyzer with a microphone placed 
exactly 100cm horizontally away from the noise source in a quiet living room with no background noises 
and a noise floor of 20dB. Each module being tested was placed on a padd ed cloth to minimize noise 
amplification from surface vibrations. For the Controller module, the noise test was done when 3 valves 
120 
 
were being simultaneously actuated, while for the Pump modules, both pumps were being actuated 
during the measurements. 
 
Quiet Room Controller S Pump SM Pump M Pump  L Pump 
20dB 26dB (peak) 36dB 34dB 36dB 35dB 
Table 6. Typical sound pressure levels produced by each module at a distance of 1 meter from the source.  
The decibel scale is logarithmic, thus e ach time a sound’s decibel level increases by 10, its intensity 
multiplies by 10. Therefore, a 10 dB sound is 10 times more intense than a 0 dB sound, a 20 dB sound is 
100 times more intense, and a 30 dB sound is 1,000 times more intense.   For comparison, a normal 
conversation at home is at 45-55dB, moderate rainfall – 50db, alarm clock – 80dB, hair dryer – 90dB. Thus, 
the noise produced by FlowIO is relatively low and tolerable  for most application cases considered . 
Furthermore, the sound can be significantly dampened even further by placing the device in a sound -
isolated container such as a box with thick padding around its interior. It is also possible to create 
alternative enclosures specifically designed for sound isolation if both miniaturization and sound level are 
of critical importance such as during musical performances.  
 
3.7.5 BLE Connection and Range 
Two connection parameters of high importance to the user are latency and range. A latency test was 
not formally conducted, because there is no human -perceptible lag when the device is controlled from 
the GUI; the device response seems instantaneous to a user, suggesting that the latency is not more than 
a few milliseconds when the device is in close proximity to the BLE central that is controlling it.  
Range tests were conducted to determine the maximum distance from which FlowIO can be operated 
remotely via a laptop over BLE. The range tests were performed in an environment similar to the type of 
environment where FlowIO is typically used, namely a living room or a lab with furniture and many objects 
around rather than an unrealistic environment free from obstructions. Both the control features and the 
device’s ability to connect and disconnect at a distance were tested. The usable range was around 14 
meters. The device could be controlled even from a greater range, however, the connection reliability 
decreased significantly after the 14 meters range. If an application requires FlowIO devices to be at greater 
ranges than this, then they should be controlled in a wired way rather than through Bluetooth.  
 
Test Condition Useful Operating Range 
Remote control a connected FlowIO device 14m 
Initialize a connection to a FlowIO device 14m 
Table 7. Operating range of FlowIO over Bluetooth Low Energy 
3.7.6 Mechanical Connection Between Modules 
Another important characteristic is how strong and secure the connection is between a Controller 
module and a Pump module. For the Small, SubMedium, and Medium modules the mechanical connection 
to the Controller module is supported by the magnetic connector, a 3D printed notch, and two silicone 
tubes. For the Large Pump modules, it is only the magnetic connector and t he notch contributing to the 
strength of the mechanical connection. While the smaller modules offer additional mechanical stability 
via the silicone tubes, the large modules provide added stability by creating a right angle into which the 
Controller module sits. In the smaller pump modules, the strength of the mechanical connection was 
optimized for stresses experienced during wearable applications, while in the large pump modules – for 
stressed in desktop applications. The magnetic connector also offers the special feature of automatic 
alignment, making the process of connecting the modules quick and effortless.

4: SoftRobotics.IO Ecosystem 
 
 
Figure 4-1. Visual overview summary of the SoftRobotics.IO Ecosystem. 
SoftRobotics.IO is an ecosystem around FlowIO and other platforms inspired by it. This ecosystem 
extends beyond technology to encompass other impact -related factors, including deployment, content, 
community building, publicity, collaborations, workshops, and fostering new interactions. SoftRobotics.IO 
follows the framework established in Chapter 2 and serves as practical manifestation of that framework 
and an opportunity to put the framework into practice. 
Chapter  4 
124 
 
SoftRobotics.IO is an ecosystem for prototyping, innovation, and creative expression with soft 
robotics and actuated materials, which are often used in domains such as haptics, HCI, robotics, art, 
interaction design, and many other fields. Its goal is to provide makers, designers, and researchers with 
everything they need to bring their creative project ideas to life quickly and without frustration. At its core, 
general-purpose development platforms such as FlowIO, HydroIO, ThermoIO, and others discussed in this 
thesis serve as the foundation of the ecosystem, enabling projects with diverse actuation approaches to 
be brought to life and programmed effortlessly. The ecosystem also includes user-generated projects, 
videos, and tutorials about the platforms; a community website featuring blogs, forums, chats, and user 
pages where members can share their work, seek help, and find answers; and a growing global community 
of 1000+ registered members and tens of thousands of visitors which have contributed hundreds o f 
questions, projects, and tutorials. Additionally, a novel deployment model is introduced that allows people 
to access expensive hardware even if it’s beyond their financial ability. As it continues to grow and evolve, 
SoftRobotics.IO aims to become a fully self -sufficient and self -sustaining ecosystem that meets nearly 
every hardware, software, service, equipment, development, or information need that users may have.   
A subset of this ecosystem is the SoftRobotics.IO website. To distinguish between the website and 
the ecosystem, references to the website will use the term “SoftRobotics.IO website” or “SoftRobotics.IO 
online community platform”, and references to the broader ecosystem will use the term “SoftRobotics.IO 
ecosystem”.  SoftRobotics.IO alone would refer to both website and ecosystem together. 
This chapter has a two-fold purpose. First, it shows how the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem was created 
and everything within it. Second, it distills lessons learned from that process to offer recommendations 
and guidance for other platform creators seeking to optimize for impact. Many lessons and 
recommendations are presented, including how to choose what type of platforms to create, how to start 
leveraging user interest from the beginning of the platform design process, how to choose which users 
and projects t o target first, how to avoid feature creep, and how to develop only features that will be 
actually used by users. Other lessons and guidance are distilled for content creation, publicity, deployment, 
and optimizing for impact. There are several strategy-related sections, such as in what order content and 
publicity should occur for maximum impact, where the traditional open-source model fails, how to release 
a project preserves the benefits of open -source while many of its drawbacks. This chapter will also 
introduce a new open-source deployment model called “earned open-source”, which eliminates some of 
the pitfalls of the traditional open -source model, stimulates continued growth and development of the 
platform, and offers a greater long-term impact potential than the traditional open-source model.  
The following sections will provide an in -depth exploration of each aspect of SoftRobotics.IO 
ecosystem depicted in Figure 4-1 above, across the five different categories. Moreover, the relationships 
between those categories and the various positive feedback loops within the ecosystem will also be 
explored in separate sections. Readers are encouraged to refer to the ecosystem diagram frequently while 
reading this chapter, as nearly all discussions tie back to it. 
 
4.1 Development Platforms 
Although pneumatics has been arguably the  most common soft robotics actuation approach for 
the past decade, hydraulic, tendon-based, thermal, electromagnetic, electrostatic, and chemical actuation 
are also used – especially in adjacent fields such as haptics, programmable materials, and tangible 
interfaces. Therefore, other development platforms besides FlowIO are also needed for the 
SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem to truly be able to meet all the actuation (and sensing) needs of users working 
with dynamic soft and programmable materials across many fields. 
 Identifying what other platforms are needed can be done either by (1) evaluating prior literature 
and talking to researchers, interaction designers, and makers to see what actuation methods they have 
used and are interested in using, or by (2) considering  what actuation approaches are possible from a 
125 
 
physics perspective and then aiming to provide solutions for all the possible approaches. The physics -
based approach offers a more complete set of results including ones that may not be easy to find in the  
research literature, while the literature-based method provides information about the relative importance 
and potential impact of each approach. Combining both investigative methods reveals that the 
SoftRootics.IO ecosystem would need to provide solutions for (1) pneumatic, (2) tendon-based, (3) 
hydraulic, (4) electromagnetic, (5) thermal, and (6) electrostatic actuation and sensing – approximately 
in this order of relevance – to satisfy the broader range of actuation and control needs of the community 
and to also inspire a far wider diversity of novel project ideas in users . Those may be named FlowIO, 
TendonIO, HydroIO, MagnetoIO, ThermoIO , and ElectroIO, respectively. Each one of them can be a 
separate platform, but an even better approach is having them as different modules of a single , highly 
generalized, modular platform sharing the same core hardware and software architecture agnostic to the 
type of actuation approach. 
 The FlowIO platform was developed to serve the first of the six enumerated actuation needs, both 
in terms of hardware and software, as described in the previous chapter. The reason for starting with a 
pneumatic as opposed to another kind of platform was because the need for pneumatic platform was 
most salient, and because this kind of platform was expected to have the highest impact across fields 
compared to any other platform; this expectation has come to fruition as discussed in this dissertation.  
 The software architecture and much of the electronics of FlowIO are agnostic to the specific type 
of actuation used. Therefore, it is possible to quickly adapt the FlowIO electronics and software to create 
other non-pneumatic platforms. For instance, going from FlowIO to HydroIO requires almost no change, 
except larger batteries since more energy is needed to move liquid through the system than to move air. 
Similarly, going from FlowIO to MagnetoIO also requires minimal changes because the valves and 
pumps used in FlowIO are already electromagnetic, and all it takes is to replace them  with different 
electromagnets. if higher magnetic field strength is needed, then a 2 -battery setup or an external power 
supply can be used to achieve higher voltage and current. Those types of circuits – for two-battery setup 
with integrated charging and for an external power supply – are already in the Large Pump Modules. Thus, 
MagnetoIO can be built by reusin g what already exists in FlowIO’s hardware. Software wise, no 
architectural changes are needed either, and the only changes needed are refactoring the names of 
functions and methods. 
Going from FlowIO to ThermoIO requires simply replacing the valves with thermal actuators and 
adding more temperature sensors. Slightly more effort would be needed on the software side to 
implement thermal actuation feedback loops, because unlike fluids, which can be contained, heat escapes; 
and thus, more complex control loops that continuously compensate for heat losses would be needed. 
Going from FlowIO to TendonIO or ElectroIO would require more substantial hardware changes 
but even then, around 50% of the FlowIO hardware can still be repurposed. However, if those two 
platforms are to be developed last, then features not only from FlowIO but also from HydroIO, ThermoIO 
and MagnetoIO can be repurposed towards developing TendonIO and ElectroIO. Ultimately, realizing the 
vision of having a complete suite of platforms for all actuation and sensing needs is largely an exercise of 
repurposing what has already been FlowIO and making small modifications along the way.  
 The reason these other platforms can be created easily from FlowIO today is not a coincidence, 
but the result of deliberate design choices made during the iterative design engineering of FlowIO software 
& hardware. Those include making it general-purpose, using a modular design philosophy, designing the 
electronics suitable for diverse actuation approaches and operable over a wide voltage range, and 
architecting the software stack in a way that is largely agnostic to the specific type of actuation used.  
However, the long-term goal is not to have 6 different platforms with different software for each – 
even if they are similar. Rather, the goal is to have a single universal actuation development platform with 
modules for each actuation approach, allowing users to change between actuation approaches within 
seconds by swapping modules, combining multiple modules, and having a universal software interface 
126 
 
automatically adaptable to the type of modules connected. Part of this vision is already possible with 
FlowIO expansion modules as the chapter on SleeveIO will indicate where different types of actuators and 
different types of actuation approaches can be combined and/or swapped on the fly.  
 
4.2 Community & Interactions 
Chapter 2 presented an argument for why building a community and fostering interaction are vital 
for the deployment and adoption of any general-purpose development platform, and it also presented the 
various feedback loops, interactions, and dependencies that exist between the community and all other 
parts of the framework. This section explores how those abstract ideas from the framework chapter were 
implemented, and how they manifested in practice during the development and deployment of FlowIO 
and the building of the overarching SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem. Four different methods and approa ches 
were used for building the community aspect of the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem and for enabling and 
fostering interactions within that community: 
{1} Collaborating with FlowIO users on their own projects,  
{2} Organizing Workshops and providing resources for other workshop organizers,   
{3} Developing an online community platform, and 
{4} Establishing a hardware sharing program for FlowIO.  
Each of those approaches are discussed in the next four subsections, fostering and growing a 
community around FlowIO, how community interactions have shaped the platform development 
trajectory, and how this has led to new interactions and new collaborations between people within 
SoftRobotics.IO community, who otherwise may have never known each other. The discussions that follow 
also provide guidelines on specific approaches for how other platform creators can engage with users early 
in the development proc ess, how to foster and grow a community around a platform or within an 
ecosystem, and how to leverage the symbiotic relationships and positive feedback loops that the 
community enables. Moreover, this discussion will also reveal how the other remaining components of 
the ecosystem – Content & Resources, Online Community Platform, and Deployment model – arise as a 
natural consequence of trying to meet the needs of all members in the community.  
 
4.2.1 {1} Community Building through Collaborations 
FlowIO has gone through numerous prototyping iterations, with a strong emphasis on community 
engagement from the outset, which has significantly influenced how FlowIO evolved and what design 
decisions were made. The first prototype was developed to address a specific need for project Bubble (to 
be discussed in Chapter 7 ), and the device was monolithic, non -modular, and nearly twice the size of 
today’s version. Even as that initial prototype was being developed, multiple people expressed interest in 
using it for their own projects. Given that the platform was intended to serve a broad range of user needs, 
the decision was made to engage others in all development cycles – leveraging external interest and to 
consider the needs of those already drawn to the project. 
One option to leverage community input could have involved surveying a large number of people 
about their needs  and preferences and then building a solution accordingly. While such multiattribute 
approaches are commonly used in market research and in engineering and entrepreneurship programs, it 
is questionable how well they work in practice. In the build up to his theory of Lead Users [119], Eric von 
Hippel lists several studies in [ 135] supporting his arguments that in high-tech industries “[typical] users 
are not well positioned to accurately evaluate novel product attributes or accurately quantify familiar 
product attributes that lie outside the range of their real -world experience.” His proposed solution is to 
focus on Lead Users instead – who “are familiar with conditions that lie in the future for most – and, so, 
are in a position to provide accurate data on needs related to such future conditions.” By contrast, regular 
users are often not even aware they may be facing a problem or that there is potential for improvement, 
127 
 
let alone being able to suggest a possible solution to a problem they don’t realize they are having. Many 
only realize a need after encountering a product or feature that fills it.  Anyone can think of personal 
examples where a new technology or feature became an indispensable part of their productivity, yet prior 
to its introduction, they didn’t even know they needed it. One recent example of this ChatGPT.  
Another option could have been to apply Von Hippel’s approach directly and focus on Lead Users. 
However, as he also points out in his book [135], “The needs of today’s lead users are typically not precisely 
the same as the needs of the users who will make up a major share of tomorrow’s predicted market. Indeed, 
the literature on diffusion suggests that in general the early adopters of a novel product or practice differ 
in significant ways from the bulk of the users who follow them.”  
For these and other reasons, a very different community engagement approach was taken when 
developing and iterating on new version of FlowIO. In each iteration phase only 1 to 2 interested people 
were selected to use FlowIO, collaborating closely with them on their own projects. This ensured the 
platform met the needs of those selected projects. Working closely with just 1 to 2 users at a time  and 
being involved in their projects allows a platform designer to not only gain in -depth familiarity with 
problems faced by users, but also to experience challenges directly from a user’s perspective. By limiting 
the focus to just two users at a time, the platform designer can focus on implementing the hardware and 
software features that are actually needed by real people, without being overwhelmed by user requests 
or not knowing where to focus their development efforts. After each user -focused iteration cycle, the 
updated platform was shared to another 2 users, whose needs prompted additional hardware and 
software features to be added to the platform. This highly -focused user-centered process was repeated 
multiple times – engaging with just 1 to 2 peo ple at a time, working very closely with them on their 
projects, figuring out what new features they may need, translating those application -specific feature 
needs into general -purpose features implementations, and then augmenting the FlowIO hardware and 
software accordingly. Some of the changes and new features that arose from these focused interactions 
include the transition from a monolithic to a modular hardware architecture, the development of most 
modules that exist today, the introduction of countles s software features, and the development of a 
graphical user interface.  
Choosing which 1-to-2 users  to engage with in each iteration is a critical decision in this 
development approach. A practical strategy is to select user A whose project needs are fully met by the 
current version and user B  whose needs are only partially met. In this way,  user A validates existing 
features while user B helps motivate  the development of new features. Especially during early iteration 
cycles, even the needs of user A would often reveal opportunities for minor improvements to the platform, 
while the needs of user B would require entirely new features or major changes to be made. That’s also 
why it is important to not take more than two simultaneous projects at a time, since fully meeting the 
needs of just the two strategically-chosen users A and B  would already require a significant amount of 
engineering effort. And it is important for the platform designer to ensure the needs of the selected users 
are robustly met in order to also gain reputational benefits and positive publicity for the platform from 
those collaborations. 
To maximize generalizability, it is also essential to diversify the types of projects and users selected 
in each development iteration round. For instance, if one cycle involved uses A and B with projects in 
haptics and locomotion, the next  round might focus on another set of users A and B doing assistive 
technology and submersibles.  Collaborating on projects in diverse categories and with users of diverse 
backgrounds allows the platform designer to better design a solution or a feature in the most general way 
rather than in a way that only serves a limited user base or a limited set of needs. It may also enable the 
designer to see opportunities for generalizing features they have already previously developed. One of the 
key roles of the platform designer / architect / developer is to translate specific needs into general-
purpose features, and to then implement general -purpose solutions that meet a whole class of related 
needs rather than the specific needs of a single user. The process of designing a new platform  
128 
 
 
 
Figure 4-2. Depiction of the user-focused approach to collaborating with just two distinct and strategically chosen 
users in each iteration development cycle. User A validates the ecisting version while user B requires a new feture 
that motivates the next development cycle. A different set of users A and B are chosen after each iteration cycle. 
There are many advantages and benefits to the collaborative user-focused approach compared to 
alternative ones. Through this approach, the platform designer can truly understand the needs of users 
because the designer adapts the perspective of each user during the focused collaborations,  rather than 
simply relying on requests and feedback from other users. This approach does not rely on users knowing 
what exactly they need, their ability to articulate the problems they are facing, or their ability to imagine 
a solution or a feature. Since users often express needs in application-specific terms, direct collaboration 
allows the designer to generalize solutions effectively.  Additionally, when the platform designer is 
collaborating on the actual applications for which the platform is being used, they receive immediate 
feedback on every step – and this feedback doesn’t have to come from another person but comes from 
the actual project being developed. This allows rapid iterative design cycling as it eliminates reliance and 
waiting for a user to submit a feedback report. Consequently, features and improvements can be made 
rapidly and generalized effectively. Miscommunication is also eliminated when the architect becomes the 
user, and there is less room for confusion about what needs to be implemented and how.  
There are also many intangible benefits to both the platform designer and the users. The platform 
creator gains opportunities to contribute to diverse research, design, and art projects, potentially leading 
to co-authorships on publications which otherwise may not have occurred. Meanwhile users also benefit 
significantly from working directly with the platform creator, having access to unreleased new features, 
accelerating their progress and enhancing their projects. Moreover, these collaborations can also lead to 
new future opportunities, increased recognition for their work, and new professional connections. 
The approach presented here also promotes and increases user innovation, consistent with the 
conclusion from The Sources of Innovation – Chapter 7 [135] stating, “product manufacturers who wish to 
increase user innovation affecting their products might offer free equipment or design help to the 
innovating users they want to encourage. Or, if they want to decrease user innovation, they could refuse 
to service products users have modified, seal the product physically to hamper user access, or refuse user 
requests for technical help, circuit diagrams, software source codes, and so on.” 

129 
 
 
4.2.2 {2} Community Building through Workshops 
A second way of developing the community & interactions aspect of the SofRobotics.IO ecosystem 
has been by organizing and running workshops, as well as by enabling others to use the tools and resources 
within the ecosystem when running their own workshops. Those include FlowIO hardware and software, 
soft robotics fabrication content, design resources, CAD models, code examples, assembly tutorials, and 
technical documentation. Some of those workshops will be discussed in this section. 
 
4.2.2.1 How to Make Almost Anything – Wildcard Week Soft Robotics Workshop 
The first workshop where participants used the FlowIO platform was during the wildcard week of the 
MIT graduate course How to Make Almost Anything  in which I led a week -long session on soft robotics. 
More than half a dozen graduate students and some undergraduates participated in the soft robotics 
workshop sessions, where they made various individual projects which were controlled by an early version 
of the FlowIO device. Full details about this workshop, the composition of participants, and the various 
projects made – some of them shown on the figure below – are presented as one of the FlowIO case 
studies in a later chapter.  
 
 
Figure 4-3. A sample of the soft robotic projects made during the workshop.

4.2.2.2 MIT Edgerton Center Workshops in Spain and Italy 
Another workshop in which participants had access to FlowIO was a multinational hands-on-learning 
workshop organized by MIT Edgerton Center in collaboration with two technical high schools in Ferrara, 
Italy and Barcelona, Spain. High -school seniors in Ferrara and Barcelona were paired with engineering 
students from local universities to work on creative engineering projects, wh ile MIT graduate and 
undergraduate students served as mentors for the groups and taught relevant skills remotely and in -
person by traveling to Italy and Spain. The projects ran for multiple months and included a highly intensive 
final week. With dozens of participating groups of students, there was a wide diversity of projects including 
amphibious hovercrafts, water-powered rockets, a motorized skateboard, a musical glove, and various 
other interactive projects. Further context about this event can be found  in a news article published by 
the MIT Edgerton Center in 2021 [120]. 
 
 
Figure 4-4. Workshop in Barcelona, Spain 
FlowIO was one of the technologies made available to the workshop participants, after some groups 
showed interest in working on soft robotic projects. One of the student projects that emerged from this 
was a glove with soft silicone actuators for assistive grasping or rehabilitation, called SoftGlove, and some 
images of it at shown on Figure 4-5. The students involved in that project also posted a replication tutorial 
on Instructables.com [121], which subsequently was featured in a Hackaday article referencing their post 
[122]. 
 
 
Figure 4-5. SoftGlove project in Ferrara, Italy by France Bonde et. al. 
In subsequent years, the MIT Edgerton Center has obtained five more FlowIO devices which were 
then used in other workshops, both at MIT and internationally. Some of the devices at the Edgerton Center 
have also been borrowed and used by MIT and Harvard students working on various class projects during 
the school year. In this way, the MIT Edgerton Center has served as a hub, enabling many students to have 
access to the FlowIO Platform technology. Two of the current projects intended to be made with those 
FlowIO devices include a submarine with buoyancy control and a remote-handshake telepresence system.

4.2.2.3 Workshop at CHI’22 on Actuated Materials and Soft Robotics for HCI Design  
 As part of the Community & Interaction efforts of the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem, I have co -
organized two workshops at ACM conferences. The first of those was titled “Actuated Materials and Soft 
Robotics Strategies for Human-Computer Interaction Design” [123], and it took place during the premiere 
international conference on Human-Computer Interaction, CHI’22 in New Orleans, USA (Figure 4-6). This 
workshop brought together nearly 30 people from academia and industry to identify challenges and 
opportunities in soft robotics and adjacent domains, and to explore how to make research, prototyping, 
and innovation related to soft robotics and dynamic materials  more accessible to HCI researchers and 
designers. This was a full-day event featuring three keynote talks, two brainstorming sessions, hands-on 
activities, as well as project demos and short presentations from selected participants.  
 
 
Figure 4-6. Workshop summary page hosted on the SoftRobotics.io website at www.softrobotics.io/chi22 
Interested participants were asked to submit short papers two months prior to the workshop, 
demonstrating challenges and new opportunities in the design, fabrication, implementation, or 
deployment of actuated materials for interaction design. After a review process, 17 papers were accepted 
to the workshop. Authors were asked to incorporate the changes requested by the reviewers and submit 
the camera-ready version of their papers two weeks prior to the workshop. Some of the topics explored 
included novel fabrication methods, commercial utilization of soft robotics, soft and programmable 
materials in HCI, tools and resources, applications, and diversity and inclusion. Figure 4-7 below shows the 
list of accepted papers which include title, name of primary author, and their affiliation. The accepted 
papers are open-access and published on the workshop website at softrobotics.io/chi22. 
The workshop included 6 organizers, 4 invited guests, and some 20 other participants from diverse 
fields including robotics, haptics, art, design, human -computer interaction, education, mechanical 
engineering, and electrical engineering. Approximately half of the participants joined in-person while the 
rest joined remotely. The keynote speakers were two researchers from Meta Reality Labs and Toyota 
Research Institute and one professor from Carnegie Mellon University. Each keynote talk lasted 
approximately 30 minutes covering a breadth of topics and projects including haptic feedback, tangible 
interaction, robotic sensing & manipulation, digital fabrication, digital materials, and interactive structures. 
There were also short presentations from selected participants talking about the papers they submitted.  
 

132 
 
 
 
 
Figure 4-7. Accepted papers for the CHI’23 workshop on soft robotics and actuated materials. 
In addition to keynotes and presentations, there were also two brainstorming sessions, show-and-
tell demo session, and a hands-on activities session. During the brainstorming sessions participants were 
asked to envision the future of soft robotics and programmable materials as it relates to fabrication, tools, 
applications, commercialization, art, learning, and prototyping. The first brainstorming sessions was 
organized to follow a top-down approach – starting with a vision 10 years into the future and then going 
backwards from it to identify a path towards that vision. The second brainstorming session took a bottom-
up approach – considering challenges that exist today, envisioning solutions for addressing those 
challenges, and then arriving at a future enab led by those solutions. Dozens of ideas were generated 
during the two sessions, and some participants also indicated that the workshop helped them identify 
research projects for their master’s and PhD studies. The show-and-tell part included a 30-minute session 
where participants could demonstrate their projects and walk around the room to see projects from other 
participants. Some of the demos brought to the workshop were pneumatically actuated and thus the 
FlowIO Platform was made available to those participants who needed it to drive their demos. The hands-
on activities session was led by an invited host from Fabricademy who demonstrated and taught some 
rapid fabrication approaches for pneumatically actuated soft robots. The FlowIO platform was also used 
during this session of the workshop for the actuation, control, and testing of those rapid prototypes.   
 
 
Figure 4-8. Photos from the day of the workshop at CHI’22 in New Orleans, USA.  

133 
 
 The workshop website was created on the SoftRobotics.IO online community platform under the 
URL www.softrobotics.io/chi22. This choice offered several mutual benefits to both the workshop and to 
the SoftRobotics.IO website. The long -term goal of SoftRobotics.IO is to serve as a general -purpose 
resource for all needs of the soft robotics community, and that also includes having various informational 
and educational resources, materials for running workshops, as well as content generated during 
workshops. One goal is to have a growing collection of workshops and workshop-materials on the online 
platform, allowing visitors to not only learn from those materials but to also be able to use those materials 
for running their own workshops. As of today, websites from four prior workshops are hosted on 
SoftRobotics.IO, which also adds value to this online platform – making it more well known, bringing more 
traffic to it, and helping establish is more firmly within the academic, maker, and tangible art communities.  
Workshops also receive significant mutual benefit from being on the SoftRobotics.IO website since 
the website is highly ranged by Google and other search engines and receives many organic visitors. This 
significantly helped with recruitment efforts, publicity, and reaching people from highly diverse fields and 
backgrounds when organizing the CHI’22 workshop. Finally, hosting workshop websites on the 
SoftRobotics.IO website also helps with preserving the workshop content online long -term and making 
the resources from the workshop easy to find by people searching the web. This is important as indicated 
by the fact that people are still visiting www.softrobotics.io/chi22 regularly and downloading the papers 
from the workshop even several years after the event. 
This workshop was made possible thanks to some of the earliest outreach with the FlowIO 
platform. Nearly the entire organizing team consisted of people who reached out to me at different points 
in time to express their interest in the FlowIO Platform. This  illustrates how one form of community 
engagement leads to new opportunities, which then lead to other forms of community engagement, and 
that itself then leads to further unexpected opportunities. Specifically, several new collaborations and 
publications followed as a direct conscience of this CHI’22 workshop. One of those collaborations is the 
organizing of another related workshop at DIS’23 discussed next. 
 
4.2.2.4 Workshop at DIS’23 on Soft Robotics and Programmable Materials for HCI 
The success of the CHI’22 academic workshop on soft robotics prompted the creation of another 
academic workshop the following year titled “ Soft Robotics and Programmable Materials for Human 
Computer Interaction” [ 124] during the ACM Conference on Designing Interactive Systems (DIS’23) in 
Pittsburgh, USA at Carnegie Mellon University. Several of the participants from the previous year’s CHI’22 
workshop were now part of the organizing team of the DIS’23 workshop. Moreov er, the know-how and 
feedback from the CHI’22 workshop was used to make this workshop significantly better and even more 
diverse. For instance, the type of submissions was expanded to include not only papers but also videos 
and tutorials from participants. This workshop also added audience choice awards for the best submission 
in each category as well as modest awards for the winners from sponsors. Moreover, rather than a single 
hands-on session, there were two parallel tracks of hands-on activities based on submitted tutorials. 
A stronger emphasis was placed on brainstorming sessions, and a longer time was provided for 
discussing ideas, challenges, and opportunities in the field and then formalizing and presenting some of 
the best ideas. There was also a single opening keynote of longer duration rather than three shorter 
keynotes compa red to the previous year’s event. The knowledge and resources from the previous 
workshop significantly helped with the organization, preparation, structuring, recruiting, and running of 
the DIS’23 workshop. The website for this workshop was also hosted under SoftroRotics.IO with the URL 
softrobotics.io/dis23 and featured on the softrobotics.io home page, offering the same mutual benefits to 
the workshop and to the SoftRobotics.IO platform as discussed in the preceding subsection. A screenshot 
of the workshop website with summary details is shown in Figure 4-9. 
 
 
134 
 
 
 
Figure 4-9. Workshop summary page hosted on the SoftRobotics.io website at www.softrobotics.io/dis23 
Holding this workshop at another academic conference compared to the prior one allowed 
reaching people from other communities and other fields. In the case of the DIS conference, this meant 
more participants were expected to be from design, interaction, art, and material fabrication backgrounds. 
The Soft Robotics and Programmable Materials workshop at DIS’23 also drew significantly greater in -
person participation compared to the CHI’22 workshop, partly due to the lifted international travel 
restrictions. With the organizers included, approximately 25-30 people attended the workshop in total, of 
which the vast majority joined in-person. Most participants were graduate students and there were also 
some assistant professors and industry professionals. The total number of participants was similar to that 
of the CHI workshop, despite the fact that DIS is a much smaller conference than CHI. Conversations with 
participants during the event revealed that more than half of all participants had come to Pittsburgh 
specifically and only for the workshop. In the case of the CHI’22 workshop, there were also a few 
participants who had come just for the workshop, but the majority were there for the CHI conference first 
and the workshop second. But by contrast, in the case of the DIS’23 workshop, most participants were 
there for the workshop first and the DIS conference second. Some said that they had come just for the 
single day of the workshop and did not plan to stay, nor did they register for the conference itself at all. 
This was an unexpected observation, indicating the importance and emphasis people placed on this 
workshop and the value that they saw in it, given that some considered it more important and impactful 
to them than the conference itself. 
One of the factors most appreciated by participants and instrumental to the success of this event 
was that multiple types of submissions were accepted - papers, videos, and tutorials. In workshops where 
paper submissions are the only ones allowed, such as our prior-year CHI’22 workshop, there is a selection 
bias towards a narrower set of works suitable for papers and narrow composition of participants who are 
willing, able, and motivated to write papers. However, by allowing submissions other than papers, t his 
selection bias is eliminated, enabling wider expressivity, a greater breath of projects to be shared, people 
from more diverse backgrounds to be present, a wider pool of ideas to be presented and generated, which 
ultimately results in a wider impact – the factor of most relevance for this thesis.  
 

135 
 
 
Figure 4-10. Photos from the day of the workshop at DIS’23 in Pittsburgh, PA at Carnegie Mellon University. The 
center photo shows one group of participants making soft biofilm fibers, while the rightmost photo shows 
another group making a robotic strap like the one used in the ModiStrap project discussed in a later chapter. 
The call for participation invited submissions of papers, videos, and tutorials on topics related to 
challenges and opportunities in soft and programmable materials being more accessible; lowering the 
barriers to creativity, prototyping, and innovation; and creating and deploying new tools, applications, and 
fabrication methods. Figure 4-11 lists the wider range of topics that were of interest to the workshop. 
 
 
Figure 4-11. Topics of interest and relevance to the workshop discussed during the brainstorming sessions. 
Participants were invited to submit papers, videos, or tutorials related to any of these topics. 
While paper and video submissions were limited to a length of 5 pages and 3 minutes, respectively, 
tutorial submissions had no length limitations. For the case of tutorial submissions, authors were asked to 
show the detailed steps needed to replicate a pro ject or a process. Tutorials had to follow a specific 
structure that provides references to all required prerequisite topics, a bill of materials, detailed visuals, a 
list of possible variations, difficulty level, duration time, and a list of post-requisite topics to take the reader 
to the next level beyond the tutorial. Participants were given a link to a previous tutorial that they could 
use as an example template [ 125]. They were also granted account -access to the SoftRobotics.IO 
community platform, where they could create and post their tutorials using the blog -authoring tools on 
the website. 
All paper, video, and tutorial submissions were reviewed before acceptance, and the authors were 
expected to incorporate any feedback from the reviews into their final submissions that were due one 
week before the workshop. Additional support was provided to tutorial authors to prepare them to lead 

136 
 
hands-on sessions based on their tutorials. The accepted submissions included five papers, seven videos, 
and three tutorials – as listed in Figure 4-12. Some participants also made submissions in multiple 
categories. All submissions are open -access and posted on the workshop website at 
www.softrobotics.io/dis23.  
 
 
Figure 4-12. Accepted Papers, Videos, and Tutorials for the DIS’23 Workshop on Soft Robotics and Programmable 
Materials for Human Computer Interaction. 

137 
 
Submissions included robotic textiles, generating soft robotic concepts using AI, a starter kit for 
soft robot prototyping, making fibers from biofoam, tactile braille displays, and various more research, 
design, and art projects. During the workshop, each  author had 3-5 minutes to present their paper, play 
their video and talk about it, or give an overview of their tutorial. After the presentations in each category, 
attendees were given a few minutes to rate every submission / presentation for its clarity, personal appeal, 
effort, and value & relevance  using a 5 -point scale on each of those four metrics. The rating was done 
anonymously using an online voting/rating system called Mentimeter. A rating approach was chosen as 
opposed to voting to eliminate some of the drawbacks associated with the traditional voting approach 
where a person only gets to cast their vote for their favorite submission. A rating approach does not restrict 
one to a bin ary decision and to just a single choice unlike a voting approach d oes. Moreover, it greatly 
eliminates the impact of a person voting more favorably/critically for their own submission. At the end of 
the workshop, the submissions with the highest overall rating scores were announced as the audience -
choice award winners and their authors received modest prizes. 
 
4.2.2.5 Additional Workshops at Universities and FabLabs 
Community engagement through workshops also includes workshops in which I have not been 
involved in organizing. Several such workshops have taken place organized and led by others which have 
utilized various SoftRobotics.IO resources including hardware and software, contents on the website, 
tutorials, prior workshop agendas, design files, tutorials, and the SoftRobotics.IO site itself for hosting their 
workshop sites.  
Maker workshops have also used the hardware, software, content, and service of SoftRobotics.IO 
at FabLabs and hackathons such during the Fab23 conference in Bhutan.  
Furthermore, during Fab24 in Puebla, Mexico, the organizers of the Democratizing Soft Robotics 
Prototyping workshop based it a style similar to that of the CHI’22 and DIS’23. The website for that 
workshop was also created on SoftRobotics.IO under the URL www.softrobotics.io/fab24. 
 
 
Figure 4-13. Workshop overview page from www.softrobotics.io/fab24 

138 
 
A few university workshops have also utilized the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem including at Rhode 
Island School of Design, ETH Zurich, Stanford, and other universities. One example is a month -long 
workshop that took place during the January 2024 term at Harva rd University, details of which are 
discussed in the case studies chapter later in this dissertation.

4.2.3 {3} Community and Interactions Online 
In addition to direct collaborations and workshops, the community & interaction  aspect of the 
SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem has also been pursued through various forms of online discourse including via 
emails, messaging apps, and the SoftRobotics.io website. Interactions are vital for the creation, prosperity, 
and survival of a creative ecosystem, especially when the community is dispersed geographically. Thus, 
significant effort has been invested in building infrastructure that enables and fosters interactions online.  
Early in this project’s history, communication occurred through already established channels such 
as emails. However, as the community grew and volume of communication increased, ecosystem-related 
engagement had to be separated from all others, and a dedicated email and Slack channel were 
established, which streamlined communication and reduced the risk of overlooking important requests.  
However, managing even a small online community via Slack and email proved challenging and 
there were many inefficiencies with such approaches means: 
• Organizing and browsing past discussions on platforms like Slack and Discord is difficult and 
inefficient, and important information is often buried under a flood of messages. 
• Discussions naturally evolve from one topic to another, yet email and platforms like Slack do not 
offer any branching mechanisms  or the possibility of continuing an older discussion without 
disrupting the main conversation flow. 
• It is  difficult or impossible, especially for new members, to determine whether a question of 
interest to them may have already been asked and answered by someone earlier. 
• Redundant discussions and questions reoccur, leading to extra work and more information noise. 
Recognizing these communication limitations, community infrastructure was added to the 
SoftRobotics.IO website, including user accounts, forums, chat, messaging, blogs, and groups. These 
ultimately transformed SoftRobotics.IO from an information website into an online-community platform. 
To maintain a high-quality and spam-free community, a manual verification and vetting process 
was introduced. Only users who could be verified and who provided their real names and affiliations were 
allowed to create accounts on the website, giving them access to certain member-only pages, such as the 
FlowIO BOM, assembly instructions, control dashboard, and als o allowing them to post on forums, to 
publish blog posts, to message other members through the website, to use the chat, or to join groups.  
Forums provided a solution to the aforementioned problems. Elements were added to most pages 
on the website nudging users to ask their questions on the forums rather than via other more direct 
channels. Even when questions were received via other channels, users were encouraged to repost them 

139 
 
on the forums so that other visitors may also benefit in the future. It was observed that people would 
usually formulate their questions better and add additional context when posting them on the forums, 
therefore increasing the overall benefit to the community even further. Over time, dozens if not hundreds 
of questions were addressed via the forums. This not only allowed keeping questions and discussion topics 
focused, but it also allowed other visitors to quickly find whether a question has already been asked, 
eliminating the redundancy and improving communication efficiency. 
The Blog feature was introduced to allow members to share their projects, discuss their journeys 
of assembling FlowIO, post tutorials, write about technical challenges they faced and how they overcame 
them, or share any other valuable information with the community or with the world. All posts are public 
and indexed by search engines, allowing anyone to view them without making an account. Additionally, 
the blog post feature also allows authors to receive feedback from other members who can leave 
comments under their post.  Tens of posts have been created to date by the community featuring projects, 
assembly stories, troubleshooting advice, and more. Many of those posts have amassed hundreds of 
views, likes, and comments which have sparked further community engagement.  
The Forums and Blogs also play a vital role for the partial fulfillment of the requirements for the 
“Content & Resources ” component of the framework / ecosystem. These features greatly increase the 
strength of the feedback loop between “Community & Interactions” and “Content & Resources”, discussed 
in Chapter 2. Without these features, the feedback loop still exists, as explained in Chapter 2, because 
users naturally would publish their works in research papers, YouTube, and other places online. But having 
the ability to publish also on SoftRobotics.IO lowers the barrier for others to easily find that content – 
thereby strengthening both directions of the positive feedback loop. Similarly, these features also 
strengthen the positive feedback loop between the  “Community & Interactions ” and “ Development 
Platforms” because the posts of projects, questions, assembly stories, challenges overcame, and 
difficulties faced provide feedback about the technology, the user experiences, and they also inspire 
changes or improvements to the platform technologies and the creation of entirely new features.   
The Chat feature on the website has played an important role in the hardware sharing program 
discussed in a later section, helping establish connections between people without the need for them to 
expose their contact information. Members have their contact information protected while still being able 
to reach out to be reached by another member through the website. They can also adjust their settings 
and choose to receive an email notification when someone sends them a chat message on SoftRobotics.IO.  
 
4.2.3.1 Demographics and Composition of the SoftRobotics.IO online community 
Since its inception, the SoftRobotics.IO website has had over 40,000 unique visitors, and some 
1100 of them have completed the registration form applying for an account to access member -only 
content such as the FlowIO assembly instructions and most communi ty features. The registration form 
asks applicants to provide their full name, the name of their institution, position of affiliation, email, city, 
gender, link to a personal website or social media profile, how they first heard about FlowIO, and why they 
are applying for an account. Some of those questions are optional while others are required. 
Approximately 980 of those who applied have been approved for a SoftRobotics.IO account, after a manual 
verification process to ensure that no account holders with  fake names or misleading information are 
allowed in the community. The verification process was done on a case-by-case basis and involved several 
methods of verifying the submitted information for accuracy, including by comparing email host with 
affiliation, comparing city and country to time zone and language of the submission, and also by 
considering the information in the portfolio websites or social media links that people submitted.  
Verified members are based in 67 countries worldwide. One third of all members are US -based, 
another third are in the UK, China, Germany, and France, and the remaining third are from the 62 other 
countries – all shown on Figure 4-15 in a table and a map form. UK and Germany are the countries with 
the highest representation in Europe followed by France and Switzerland. In Asia, most members are from 
140 
 
China and India, followed by Japan and South Korea. This information serves as a proxy for how much 
research, art, and other creative activities related to soft robotics, pneumatic -based haptics, and other 
related research occur in different regions of the world, especially because (as will be shown later) most 
of the community consists of professors, researchers, and students that come from just about every 
research lab around the world doing some work related to some robotics. The regions with the least 
number of members on the SoftRobotics.IO website were South America, Australia, and Africa. 
Since a large part of those registered are based in the US, further classification was performed just 
for US-based members, revealing that they come from 40 different states. Nevertheless nearly 50% of US-
based members are just from the three states of Mas sachusetts, California, and New York in that order. 
The next four states with the highest representation were Texas, Pennsylvania, Michigan, and Illinois. 
Combined, those 7 states comprise 65% of the US based community on SoftRobotics.IO. More detailed 
breakdown by state of the US-based segment of the community is provided in Figure 4-16.

only, and in that case, a slightly more balanced male:female ratio emerged of 7:3. Considering that 
respondents come from over 250 academic institutions and from nearly every soft robotics research lab 
in the world today, this analysis indicates that soft robotics and fields related to soft robotics today are 
highly male-dominated.
