4.2.4 {4} Community Building and Interactions through Hardware Sharing 
The fourth approach used for building a community and fostering new interactions is through a 
hardware sharing program that is part of the deployment model. This program is rooted in the notion of 
a sharing economy and promotes the sharing of FlowIO devices rather than the creation and ownership 
of new devices. Approximately half of all FlowIO devices today are part of this hardware sharing program, 
where approved users are borrowers and keepers rather than owners. Once a user no longer needs a 
device, they are expected to pass it on to another approved borrower.  
 
 
Figure 4-20. Locations of FlowIO devices. 
On the SoftRobotics.IO website, there is a public list and a map showing approximately 50 institutions 
and private individuals around the world who are currently in possession of one or more FlowIO devices. 
They are located in more than 15 countries on fou r continents. New users interested in FlowIO are first 
asked to check this list and see if there may already be a FlowIO device in their city or country that they 
could potentially borrow, before making a request for a new device or before embarking on the  long 

144 
 
arduous journey of replicating FlowIO themselves, which would take them months just to procure all the 
required parts. If there is a FlowIO device near them, then the one seeking the device is connected to the 
current keeper of that device and they coordinate between them whether the device can be borrowed or 
used at that time. The list does not include details or the full names of the users / keepers, therefore 
connection between seekers and keepers are done manually either via email if both parties are 
comfortable sharing their emails, or via the website’s chat feature if they want to keep their emails private. 
Over the past years, dozens of such connections have been made between existing FlowIO users and 
FlowIO seekers in neighboring locations. Some of t he cities where such connections and subsequent 
exchanges have occurred include Berlin, Cambridge, San Francisco, London, Ithaca, Providence, Paris, 
Stockholm, Zurich, Boulder, and Dresden. There have also been several exchanges across neighboring cities 
(e.g. San Fransico to San Jose; Boston to Providence) or across countries in close proximity (e.g. Sweden 
to UK; Netherlands to Switzerland). The list on the website is regularly updated to reflect those exchanges 
and to indicate the latest locations of FlowIO devices that may be available for sharing or borrowing.  
The community-building impact of this program is manifested in several different ways. The FlowIO 
hardware is expensive, assembling one can take months just to obtain the 100+ parts in the bill of 
materials, and the demand for FlowIO is significantly highe r than the supply available. This program 
enables people to have quick and free access to hardware that is expensive, in limited supply, and that 
they may otherwise not be able to access at all. The sharing program also lowers the total number of 
devices required to meet demand by allowing multiple people to use the same resource, thereby also 
reducing the carbon footprint of FlowIO. Interactions within the community are also fostered in this way 
by establishing connections between people in the same geographic locations who are working on related 
projects but who may otherwise never know each other. Some of those connections then grow into 
collaborations and friendships and lead to new opportunities for new possibilities as has happened 
multiple times since the inception of SoftRobotics.IO.  
There are also numerous second and third order benefits not only for those directly involved but also 
for other members of the community and for the entire ecosystem. When more people have access to the 
hardware, there are more projects made, resulting in more content, publications, and resources for 
everyone. These additional resources enable new people to learn about FlowIO, to be inspired to think 
creatively, to pursue difficult projects that FlowIO makes easy, to contribute with feedback and new 
features, and to get involved in the ecosystem. As the number of users and contributors grows, the 
symbiotic relationships within the ecosystem also grow and become stronger, benefiting everyone and 
everything within the ecosystem and the world at large through t he ideas, research, and technology 
output from all projects.  
While some have assumed that the Hardware Sharing Program is analogous to how a library operates, 
this analogy is not correct because there are at least four major differences compared to a library: 
 
 Public Library Operation vs. Hardware Sharing Program Operation 
 
 
 1. Centralized.  1. Decentralized and distributed  
 2. Resources are property of the library.  2. Resources do not belong to a single 
entity. 
 
 3. Borrowers are only users.  3. Borrowers are not only users, but 
also trustees and nodes.  
 
 4. Borrowed resources must be 
returned to the central lending entity. 
 4. Borrowed resources can be returned 
to any node or passed on to users. 
 
 
145 
 
The distribution system is one where devices are located around the world with users who are 
entrusted to be keepers of the devices in their possession allowing others to borrow from them. Thus, the 
borrowers are not only end users but also nodes in the community network. And they can either lend the 
device to someone else temporarily or pass it on ward to other users who would then become the new 
node for that device. If someone no longer needs a FlowIO device, they can return it to any keeper or they 
themselves can continue being a keeper of the device until someone else requests it from them. The only 
centralized component of this program is the coordination and the tracking of all these activities, which 
happens via the SoftRobotics.IO website.  
Some FlowIO devices are assembled by the users themselves for them to own. Therefore, not all 
FlowIO devices are part of the hardware sharing program. However, even those users who own their 
FlowIO devices are also encouraged to allow others to borrow thei r hardware, because ultimately that 
benefits the entire ecosystem and those benefits trickle down back to them as well through the 
introduction of new features, new software versions, additional documentation, more support tickets on 
the forums, more people who can offer support, and faster development cycles. Plus, by allowing others 
to use their devices, this may also lead to various social and interpersonal benefits to them including new 
connections and possible collaborations and co-authorships. For this reason, the institutions and persons 
who have assembled FlowIO devices themselves are also included in the list of nodes. 
The running, management, and coordination of this program has been done manually, and the 
program has also evolved over time. Especially during the initial phases, there was exclusive reliance on 
an honor system and trust in users. This was motivated by th e success of other trust-based systems and 
endeavors such as Wikipedia, Linux, and various other community-based projects and programs. The vast 
majority of FlowIO users have followed the honor system of sharing the hardware with others and passing 
it on o nce they no longer need it. However, about 5 -10% of users misappropriate the hardware after 
borrowing it and become unresponsive to any communication or to requests to share it with others. As a 
result, the sharing program has evolved over time to counter the effects of such bad actors. A stricter 
vetting process was introduced requiring a video call before one is allowed to borrow any hardware. The 
video also adds a human element to the interaction, reducing the likelihood of theft. Additionally, a 
requirement was introduced to fix the length of time prior to the exchange. Technological measures were 
also added to address the problem of bad actors. For example, previously, anyone in possession of a 
FlowIO device could use all features that come with the Flo wIO platform; but more recently, additional 
infrastructure was developed to require device registration and an account in good standing for a user to 
access the latest FlowIO software features and the GUI control dashboard. Those 5-10% of bad actors all 
have been university affiliates – postdocs, professors, and students. Because all these offenses have been 
committed by university affiliates, and because universities have more resources than private individuals, 
another change implemented in the program is to have higher contribution requirements from university 
affiliates than from private individuals. 
The hardware sharing program has resulted in significant benefits to the ecosystem, growth of the 
community, increase in content, improvements to the website, and improvements to the technology itself. 
Many of the features in the FlowIO Platform and in the  SoftRobotics.IO website – as well as some of the 
online posts, collaborations, and scientific papers that have resulted – would not have come to fruition 
without the hardware sharing program. Therefore, moving forward, the intent is to continue this program 
and to expand it to all other products envisioned in the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem. Some changes are also 
planned to improve the efficiency, efficacy, and impact of this program. Stronger measures and incentive 
structures may also be added to mitigate r isk from bad actors, such as requiring a refundable security 
deposit prior to borrowing any hardware. Some additional infrastructure would also be developed for the 
website to automate the borrowing process including the making of requests, establishing of connections, 
tracking the hardware exchanges, and managing the collection and refunding of security deposits. The 
ledger on the website where exchanges are currently recorded manually by the administrator may also be 
146 
 
replaced with blockchain technology, which will also be used for managing how and when security 
deposits are withheld and refunded. Ultimately, the program would continue evolving and various changes 
would be introduced gradually to fix management problems , mitigate risks, improve efficiency, and 
increase impact across fields and communities. 
 As more platforms and tools are added into the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem, they would also be 
added to the hardware sharing program. One of the long-term goals is to have FlowIO and the other future 
platforms in all locations around the world where they are needed. Therefore, nearly anyone interested in 
using the hardware & software resources within the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem would have the access to 
those resources. The hardware sharing program has been instrumental to the early success and broad 
reach of FlowIO, and it would also play an equally important role especially in the early deployment phases 
of all the other platforms to make them more well-known, accessible, and impactful.  
 The cost of FlowIO is quite significant, in the high hundreds of dollars, and therefore the hardware 
sharing program offers a way for people to obtain and use FlowIO without having to spend this amount. 
This ultimately enables greater impact. Further details about the hardware sharing model, the philosophy 
and inspiration behind it, and the benefits and outcomes of this approach will be discussed in the 
Deployment Model section of this chapter. 
 
4.3 Everything else follows from Focusing on the Community  
This short subsection explores how the three remaining categories of the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem 
follow as a direct consequence of the community aspect through the process of deductive reasoning. Let 
the driving question be: how to meet the needs of the community that the platforms are aimed at and 
how to enable more people to have access to the development platforms? Those driving questions reveal 
the following needs that need to be met in order to achieve the desired impact goals: 
- Ease of discovery of the technology’s existence. 
- Digestible tutorials on how to utilize and operate the platforms. 
- Inspiring examples of what is possible with the tools. 
- Providing software updates to existing users. 
- A process of obtaining the technology directly or by replicating it. 
- Ability to make effective use of the technology. 
- Online town square where questions can be asked. 
- Public places where users can share what they have achieved. 
- Means for fostering interactions between users and contributors. 
- Processes that derisk the likelihood of the ecosystem dying and leaving users stranded. 
- Ability to collaborate with other contributors and/or users. 
- Enabling people across diverse backgrounds to have access to all resources. 
The list of needs and requirements for the prosperity and growth of the platforms, the community, and 
the ecosystem can be grouped into three categories, and those correspond to the remaining three 
components of the ecosystem framework, namely: 
❖ Content and Resources 
❖ Online Community Platform 
❖ Viable Deployment Model 
Thinking about how to meet the needs of the community and taking a long-term perspective 
naturally leads one to deduce these remaining three components of the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem, 
which are discussed more extensively in the remainder of this chapter.  
 
 
147 
 
4.4 Informational Content & Resources 
Chapter 2 made the argument that content & resources are essential for the deployment, growth, 
evolution, and sustainment of a development platform and the community around it. This section explores 
what roles content and informational resources play in th e deployment of the FlowIO platform and the 
building of the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem. It also offers a conceptual framework that distributes content 
and resources into three buckets based on the role each content bucket serves and the order in which it 
is consumed, along with an impact optimization strategy for what order to fill those buckets in. 
 
4.4.1 (A) Discovery Content 
 One role that content plays is for people to discover the existence of the platform technology. 
Initially, this discovery can happen by a word of mouth, but that approach would generally have a narrow 
reach geographically and in terms of field, which was also the case during the early phases of FlowIO when 
only a few dozen people within the HCI and robotics community knew about it mostly in MIT and Harvard. 
By contrast, there are now tens of thousands of people who have heard about FlowIO thanks to various  
content about FlowIO including YouTube videos, academic publications, citations, competitions, news 
articles, magazines, blogs, posts, and on the SoftRobotics.IO website. There have been over 40,000 unique 
visitors to the SoftRobotics.IO website since its  inception, many LinkedIn posts and YouTube video 
mentioning FlowIO which have garnered well over 100,000 views, as well as news articles of projects 
featuring FlowIO.   It’s due to this content and its diversity across various online platforms that people from 
all walks of life were able to discover the existence of this platform.  
The role of discovery content is simply to make people aware of the platform, excited, and curious 
to learn more about it. It is not to teach them in detail what it does, how to use it, or how it works. 
Discovery content may be just a photo, a short paragraph, a brief mention in a video about another project 
that makes use of FlowIO, etc. While some discovery content about FlowIO has been accidental and 
unexpected – such as news outlets or bloggers writing about it unprompted – the majority has been the 
result of highly intentional effort. This effort has included not only building the SoftRobotics.IO website, 
but also establishing collaborations, organizing workshops, publishing papers, asking users to post projects 
on SoftRobotics.IO, speaking at events, applying to competitions and entrepreneurship programs, making 
posts on LinkedIn, and winning popular awards which generate publicity. 
 
4.4.2 (B) Overview Content 
After the initial discovery process, content needs to serve the roles of providing overview 
information, inspiring ideas, and enabling action . After hearing about FlowIO, those who are intrigued 
would generally seek to find more information about it. This is where additional content and resources are 
needed to provide the necessary information that satisfies the curiosity of those intrigued. Th is type of 
content should provide a more focused overview about the technology, what it can do, who it is for, as 
well as what applications, projects, and outcomes it has enabled in the past. There should be sufficient 
information to satisfy the intellectual curiosity of the reader / viewer, to let them decide if the platform 
technology is something they are interested  to learn more about, and to also inspire them to think 
creatively about what they can do with the platform. In the case of FlowIO, this was achieved in various 
ways including: (1) having graphics, animations, videos, and overview descriptions the SoftRobo tics.IO 
home page; (2) having FlowIO overview content on high -traffic websites including the MIT Media Lab’s, 
on Hackaday, and on websites of popular competitions that FlowIO has won; (3) publishing tens of projects 
on the SoftRobotics.IO website enabled b y the FlowIO platform; (4) and creating infrastructure and 
incentive structures that enable FlowIO users to posts their own projects in which they use FlowIO.  
 
148 
 
4.4.3 (C) Detailed Content 
If the platform or other part of the ecosystem has significantly piqued one’s interest, and if they 
have decided to either use the technology in their own project, join the community, or contribute to the 
further growth and development, then what becomes n eeded next is detailed descriptive content, 
documentation, information on how to obtain it, as well as additional resources such as examples , 
tutorials, bill of materials, and other related resources. In the case of FlowIO, this is manifested through 
research papers, this dissertation, documentation pages on the SoftRobotics.IO website, forums on the 
website where one can find answers to many questions or ask a question themselves, detailed examples 
on how to operate the FlowIO or program it, bill of materi als, replication instructions for FlowIO, as well 
as information for how the platform can be obtained. 
 
4.4.4 Summary of Content Types Needed 
 
Discovery Content Overview Content Detailed Content 
Widely distributed and merely 
makes people aware of the 
platform’s existence 
Basics about the platform, its 
features, capabilities, and 
examples of what it enables 
In-depth description, docs, 
examples, tutorials, instructions, 
price, business model 
 
Without these three types of content, a development platform cannot reach its full impact 
potential. It is through content that people become aware of the platform, get inspirations for new ideas, 
become interested in using the technology, learn what the p latform can do and how to use it, become 
motivated to contribute to the further improvement of the platform and the overall ecosystem around it. 
Therefore, these same varieties of content and resources would also be needed in the future for each of 
the other platforms envisioned within the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem to facilitate their deployment and 
impact. That would entail retracing much of the same steps that were taken with FlowIO to ensure that 
discovery content about each of the platforms is available at dozens of different places including news 
websites, social media platforms, YouTube videos, magazines, academic papers, competition websites, and 
of course also on SoftRobotics.IO, and that there is also overview and detailed content and resources ready 
for those who become intrigued to learn more. 
 
4.4.5 Order of Content Creation and Content Publishing  
The order in which content and resources are created is also vitally important for the impact and 
wide reach of a development platform. Academic projects usually follow a sub -optimal order, which 
typically starts with a publication, then some news outlets may write about that publication, and then, if 
there is sufficient interest, a website may be created later by the researchers. Not all projects take this 
path, but the incentives of publishing quickly encourage exactly this sub-optimal path, which significantly 
stifles impact and often leads to the death of most projects post publication. This standard order of content 
creation in academia has many pitfalls. After someone reads a news article about a new technology, they 
may get excited about it, be interested to learn more, to follow its progress, and to use it when it becomes 
available. However, having only a news article and a publication leads them to a dead end, and then all 
the excitement the readers had about the technology would quickly vanish or be  forgotten if there is 
nothing else for them to follow related to the technology to nurture and to maintain their interest. 
Moreover, in most cases of academic technology projects, the publications are not even accessible to 
people due to paywalls, thus the news articles themselves are the dead ends for most readers.  
A more optimal order for content creation would be one where there is first a project website, an 
option to subscribe for updates, and documentation with examples about the project ready to be launched 
publicly prior to the time of the publication. Thus, when someone reads the published paper or a news 
149 
 
article about the new technology and they get excited about it, they are not faced with a dead end but 
have somewhere to go to satisfy their curiosity, to channel their excitement, to nurture their interest, to 
follow updates about the project, to find other people who share their excitement, to leave questions and 
comments, and to indicate if they wish to contribute to the project. Thus, by having a project website 
along with an online community platform ready before anything else, then research publication and news 
articles naturally serve as funnels leading people toward the project’s website. 
Thus, the optimal order in which content should be created is opposite to the order in which it 
would be consumed. Detailed content and overview content must be ready before starting any initiatives 
that create publicity and generate discovery content. 
 
 
Figure 4-21. Optimal order for content creation is opposite to the order in which content is typically accessed. 
This optimized order of content creation was used with the FlowIO platform, where the 
SoftRobotics.IO website was developed prior to any academic publications. Then the website was 
launched just prior to the paper publication date. Major traffic upticks were observed on the website each 
time a FlowIO-related paper was published. Moreover, whenever there was a news article about FlowIO 
or about a project featuring FlowIO, significant increases in traffic were observed on the SoftRobotics.IO 
website, as well as increases in the number of people signing up for accounts to join the community. Some 
of those major events included: the winning of the Hackaday Grand Prize and FlowIO being featured on 
the homepage of Hackaday.com, the publication of OmniFiber being featured on the cover of MIT News, 
the appearance of FlowIO in a TechBriefs magazine article, and publications of SleeveIO and other projects 
enabled by FlowIO. Had the SoftRobotics.IO online community platform not been there when those major 
publicity events occurred, most of the community that is now part of the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem would 
not be there today. Figure 4-22 shows the number of unique daily visitors on the SoftRobotics.IO website, 
and the spikes correlate with dates when FlowIO received some external publicity. Those publicity events 
serve as major sources of organic discovery content, which then brought new visitors to the website 
curious to find overview and detailed content there. 
 
 
Figure 4-22. Unique daily visitors on SoftRobotics.IO from January 2021 to January 2024. 

150 
 
4.4.6 Discovery Statistics and Recommendations for Impact 
The account registration form on the SoftRobotics.IO website includes the optional question, “How 
did you find about FlowIO? Name the person, site, paper that led you to it ,” which people can answer in 
their own words. Of those who have submitted the registration form, 803 have provided a valid response 
to this question with enough information to be suitable for classification. Analysis of the responses 
revealed that there are three distinct ways in which people learned about the FlowIO Platform: through 
research papers , through  interpersonal communication , or through online sources.  Additional 
subcategories were identified within those macro categories with the breakdown being as follows:

Table 8. Category breakdown for how members first heard about FlowIO. 
 
Figure 4-23. Visual Representation of how members first heard about FlowIO. 
 
Figure 4-24. Visual Representation of how only the university-affiliated members first heard about FlowIO. 
When this data is represented visually as in Figure 4-23, several intriguing observations can be 
made. Despite there being more than 60 research papers that have used, featured, or cited FlowIO, only 

151 
 
6% of people found the platform from research papers. And this is despite that the vast majority of those 
who registered on the website and completed the survey question are academics (university affiliates). 
Even more striking is that even when filtered for academics, as shown in Figure 4-24, research papers still 
contribute only 8% of the publicity within academic audiences too.  
A surprising result from these figures is how strong an importance interpersonal communication 
plays in the discovery process – well over a third (37.2%) of all respondents, and almost exactly half (49.7%) 
of academic-affiliated respondents – professors, postdocs, university students. This result shows that even 
in the information -age of today, interpersonal communication has as high of an impact as all other 
information sources  (research papers, news articles, search engines, social media, project website,  
YouTube videos) for the popularity of a platform, especially in academic circles. This has strong implications 
for how an impact-oriented platform designer may approach their deployment efforts. It further bolsters 
the case for why focusing on building communities and fostering interactions is vitally important for the 
impact-success of a platform and its ecosystem.  
At a first glance, it may appear that research papers have only a very minor role to play in the 
platform discovery process. However, that is not quite the case, because the categories are not 
independent of one another. Even though interpersonal communica tion is the predominant form of 
discovery, research papers and online sources played an important role in enabling this information spread 
through interpersonal communication. That’s because those who spread and disseminate the news about 
the platform to o thers often first learned about the platform through online sources or papers. This 
important part of the discovery process is one not captured in the data and the graphs above. Research 
papers and online articles are vital for raising awareness among spreaders, and for making it possible for 
them to quickly and conveniently share what they learned with their network as a link or an attachment.  
Another type of categorization that could also be extracted from certain of the responses is 
whether people learned about FlowIO directly or through projects based on the platform. Many said they 
found FlowIO through child-projects, but some even said they found it through grand-child projects, such 
as through Corsetto, which was based by OmniFiber, which was enabled by FlowIO. One response read: “I 
found this kit from searching Corsetto -> OmniFiber -> FlowIO Flatform”. Another response was: “Paper on 
pneumatic actuator design led me down a rabbit hole, and I found this project through google.” This also 
has very strong implications for the publicity and impact of a platform. It shows how important it is for an 
impact-oriented platform designer to ensure that their platform technology is placed into the hands of 
researchers, makers, engineers, and artists who then make projects with it, so that when those projects 
are presented or published, they lead people back to the platform that enabled their existence.  
 
Recommendations for Impact 
As evidenced by the results and discussion in this section, interpersonal communication has an 
outsized importance in the platform discovery process, especially within academia. Thus, it is important 
for a platform designer to fucus their deployment efforts on community and interactions. Furthermore, a 
large number of people find out about the existence of a platform not directly but through projects that 
the platform has enabled in the past when they hear or read about those projects. Thus, it is also important 
for the platform designer to provide their platform as early as possible to people who can then make 
valuable projects with it that would be exhibited or published, and those projects should span a broad 
diversity of fields rather than be concentrated in one field if the goal is to reach wide impact. Ultimately, 
the more people learn about the existence of a platform, especially if it is through peer communication or 
projects, the higher its potential for impact is. That’s because interpersonal communication also serves as 
social proof  while published projects provide indisputable evidence that the platform is valuable, 
functional, and useful.  
152 
 
4.5 SoftRobotics.IO Website 
The SoftRobotics.IO website is the central hub for everything within the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem. 
It provides information and documentation about the hardware development platforms it features, serves 
as an online community platform for users and contrib utors, enables the discovery of other community 
members and interactions between them, allows users to post projects they have created related to soft 
robotics and dynamic materials, and offers opportunity for people to share ideas about projects or about 
the progress of the development platforms within the ecosystem. SoftRobotics.IO is not a website about 
FlowIO, but a website about the broader ecosystem and everything in it.  
The necessity for a website and an entire online community platform was already well-established 
in the prior sections of this chapter  as well as in chapter 2, and some parts of the website have already 
been discussed at a high level earlier in this chapter, such as the workshop pages. Therefore, this section 
will explore the specific structure of the SoftRobotics.IO website, the content & resources it features, and 
the tools the website offers for nurturing and fostering community & interactions. Some future goals for 
the website will also be discussed.  
Currently there are over 100 pages on the softrobotics.io website; the home page is shown on 
Figure 4-25. Most pages are publicly accessible, some require a free account to access, and a few are 
reserved only for contributors. There are four different types of pages – (a) static informational, (b) 
community engagement, (c) account setup and configuration, and (d) interactive FlowIO device control.  
 
 
Figure 4-25. SoftRobotics.IO home page with a nav-bar at the top from which users can access all other pages log 
in to the members area. 

153 
 
4.5.1 Static Informational Pages 
Most of the website is comprised of static information pages, which include documentation 
resources, instructional tutorials, code examples, project pages, and blog posts, as well as pages such as 
Home, About, Terms & Conditions, and Contribute.  
One section of the website is for FlowIO documentation, and this includes everything that a user 
needs to set up, understand, start using their FlowIO device, and troubleshoot any problems they may 
face. The documentation section also includes informational resources about the FlowIO Arduino libraries, 
JavaScript API, Graphical User Interface, and special features of the hardware and software. 
Another section is for hardware replication, which includes a 17-chapter guided tutorial on how to 
replicate FlowIO (Figure 4-26). The first chapter of the replication tutorial is a bill of materials listing not 
only all the components that go into the making of FlowIO but also all the tools, equipment, and 
consumables required to assemble, test, program, and troubleshoot the hardw are. The bill of materials 
contains over 150 items including links to the suppliers that sell each item; and for some items, that are 
frequently out of stock, multiple supplier options are listed. The other chapters provide detailed 
instructions on how to prepare, assemble, program, and test each subassembly and each module of 
FlowIO. 
 
 
Figure 4-26. FlowIO Replication - guided tutorial series overview. 

154 
 
The entire replication process consists of approximately 200 steps, and each of them is 
documented in exquisite detail in the replication chapters. An instance of this is provided in the figure 
below, showing some of the initial steps documented in Replication -Chapter 7. All chapters follow the 
same format for consistency, with text on the left and a corresponding image on the right for each step. 
The replication tutorial series has evolved and matured over the course of dozens of revision cycles 
inspired by requests for clarification as people embarked on the replication journey. Whenever anyone 
raised a question about a particular step, the answer was provided to them by means of modifying the 
tutorial instructions and images to clear their confusion, whic h typically meant writing additional details 
to an existing step or splitting a step into two or more smaller steps, hence the large number of ~200 steps. 
 
 
Figure 4-27. A screenshot demonstrating how the content in the replication chapters is structured. Each step has 
a text description and one or more photos. A sidebar shows the list of all chapters and highlights the active one.

Other sections of the website that contain static info pages include: an about section that contains 
pages related to the project’s history, deployment model, and contributors; a legalese section with pages 
for privacy policy, terms and conditions, disclaimers, and other policies; and a soft robotics section which 
includes various soft-robotics resources including recent review papers, a page with resources on modular 
soft robotics, a list of soft robotics software tools, a list of other websites and databases, etc. The various 
workshop pages that were discussed in the community & interactions section of this chapter earlier are 
accessible also from the Soft Robotics menu of the site.  
Additionally, there is a page that lists soft-robotics related institutions in the world including within 
both academia and industry, organized geographically, as shown in the figure below. The institutions page 
is one of the two most visited pages of the entire software, and this appears to be the only such list / 
resource on the internet that can be found with a search engine. The value and significance of this page 
was not apparent until various professors and lab leaders started reaching out, pointing out that they also 
had a soft robotics lab and wanted to be added to the list of relevant institutions. A future goal for this 
page is to make it editable by site visitors, just like a Wikipedia page, to ease the process of keeping it 
relevant and up to date.

4.5.2 Community Engagement Pages  
The second types of pages on the SoftRobotics.IO website are those that enable active community 
participation and engagement. They include forums, posts, and member-communication pages.  
Forums provide a place for members to ask questions publicly, share new ideas, request features, 
and find answers. The forums are viewable by anyone, while posting is only available for those who have 
an account. There are six forum categories: (1) FlowIO Features & Capabilities, (2) Projects & Applications, 
(3) Replicate FlowIO, (4) Hardware Sharing, (5) General Discussion, and (6) Soft Robotics for HCI. The most 
active of those is the replication category, which has also helped improve the replication tutorial chapters. 
Blog Posts are an area of the website accessible to all but editable only be registered community 
members. Members have made many posts to date (Figure 4-29) across into three blog post categories – 
Projects, Stories, Tutorials. Project posts provide descriptions of projects made with FlowIO. Story posts 
describe specific challenges that users faced and how they overcame those challenges either with the 
assembly process or when they were developing their personal project. And finally Tutorial posts provide 
detailed instructions for how to do a certain process or how to replicate a certain project.  
 
 
Figure 4-29. Community posts across three categories - Projects, Stories, and Tutorials. 

157 
 
Shown in Figure 4-30 below is an example of a project tutorial post. In addition to providing the 
tutorial steps, authors were encouraged to also provide a list of prerequisite topics that readers should be 
familiar with, a complete bill of materials, the approximate time req uired to complete their tutorial, the 
level of difficulty, and a brief discussion. Finally, authors were also encouraged to include in their tutorial 
posts a section that talks about different variations, alternative approaches to creating their project, and 
additional topics readers can explore on their own for further study. Authors were also asked to review 
some of the tutorials that are already posted to serve as a reference for how they may structure the 
content of their own tutorial. 
 
 
Figure 4-30. Example of a tutorial page and its structure. It contains the sections: Introduction, Prerequisite & 
Background topics, Materials & Equipment, Instructions, Variations to try, and Further topics.
