Member-communication pages are part of the member-only area of the SoftRobotics.IO website. 
Each member has his or her own public profile page, which they can update with details such as name, 
affiliation, a blurb about themselves, a profile photo, and more. There is a members directory page which 
can be viewed only by those who already have an account, where existing members can search for and 
find the public profiles of other members, see posts they have made on the forums or blog, follow them, 
and message them through the website . Shown below is screenshot from the directory page. From this 
page, viewers can also filter the list for specific member type, such as Writer, and see whether a member 
has a FlowIO device or not. This provides a convenient way for prospective users to find and interact with 
existing or former users directly. 
 
 
Figure 4-31. Members directory page, filtered for the category of “Writers” 
From the directory page, one can send a private message to another member, who then receives 
a notification in their email. Another way to have communication with a member is to engage with content 
they may have posted in the forum or blog section of the website and leave a comment at the bottom of 
their post. 
 
4.5.3 Account Setup and Configuration Pages 
Site members also have the opportunity to set various settings and preferences for their accounts. 
For instance, they can control whether their profiles are public or private, edit their profile URL, and update 
any information they have shared. They can al so control their notification settings, subscribe to mailing 
lists and much more. Other pages that fall into this category include the sign-up and log-in pages. 
 
4.5.4 Dynamic Device Registration & Control Pages 
The last type of pages are those for registering FlowIO devices and controlling them through the 
dashboard web-app. To access the dashboard, one needs to register a FlowIO device to their account. 
When the user opens the “GUI Dashboard” link, they are prompted to register a FlowIO device by entering 
its unique device ID. A web -based utility is provided that can automatically retrieve the user’s FlowIO 
device ID by the click of a single button named “Obtain Device ID” which establishes a Bluetooth 

159 
 
connection to a FlowIO device and extracts the device’s unique ID. After the user submits their device ID, 
a manual verification process is carried out at the back end, and in the meantime the dashboard page asks 
the user to wait for up to 24 hours until the device registration process completes. If the registration fails, 
the GUI Dashboard page will reset to its original state; if the registration succeeds, the GUI Dashboard 
page will provide the user with a unique access link through which they can access their unique instance 
of the FlowIO web-app to control their registered device.  When a user registers a device, a new instance 
of the FlowIO web-app is created personalized for that user and for their particular device.  
Each user has their unique FlowIO control dashboard page on the server personalized to their 
account and to the uniquely identified FlowIO device they have. 
Then, whenever a user opens the GUI Dashboard page in the future, they always see the link to 
access their device’s unique web-app. A graphical representation of the device registration user experience 
is provided in Figure 4-32 below. 
 
 
Figure 4-32. Device registration process flow and user experience.  
There are many benefits to having unique web-app instances. One of those benefits is a high degree 
of personalization. For instance, if a user requests specific changes to the graphical user interface, or 
certain new features to be added that are specific to their project needs, those customizations can be 
made just for that user’s instance of the web-app, without affecting other users. Another benefit of having 
unique user instances is that updates to the web-app can be rolled out in stages, by pushing updates first 
to those users who have opted into a beta -testing program before officially releasing the features to the 
entire community. A third benefit is access control, which is important for enforcing compliance with 
contribution expectations – whether those are financial, technical, or content-based contributions.  
In the initial phases of deployment, the web -app was openly accessible to all, and no device 
registration was required. However, this proved to be a nonviable strategy for the prosperity of the 
ecosystem because users had no incentive to make any contributi on back to the project, and, 
unsurprisingly, most users did not contribute. However, once registration became required and access 
restrictions were put in place, that created the incentive structure for users to make the contributions they 
had promised in  order to gain and maintain their access to the software. This experience showed that 
providing unrestricted access is unfavorable  for the ecosystem’s health, and that access restrictions are 
required as incentive structures, even if they are not strictly enforced. 

160 
 
4.5.5 Site Visitors 
The website has had more than 40,000 unique visitors from 124 countries since it launched. By 
comparison, those who have made an account on the website are from 67 countries. Below is a traffic 
map of the website visitors over the last four years. The top 20 countries from which visits have occurred 
include (in descending order): US, CN, GB, DE, FR, JP , IN, KR, HK, RU, NL, CA, IT, CH, ES, SG, SE, TW, AU, ID.  
 
 
Figure 4-33. Traffic map of site sessions on softrobotics.io by city and detailed views of the three regions with a 
high concentration of visitors.  
There are three regions in the world from which a high concentration of visitors is observed - 
Northeastern US, Western Europe, and East Asia.  Other regions that also have a strong representation 
include the US west coast, southern India, and eastern Australia. By contract, there are also highly 
populated regions but with very low representation on the traffic map, such as central Asia, Africa, South 
America, southern Canada, and the Middle East. 
This information is of high value when deciding where and how to deploy hardware, especially in 
the case of the decentralized hardware sharing program which relies on people to serve as community 
nodes. For example, a shared device in central Europe has a higher impact potential in its lifetime than a 
shared device in central Asia, because more borrowing requests are likely to come from central Europe 
than from central Asia given the traffic map. Thus, for maximum impact potential, it is preferable to give 
higher priority to user requests that come from regions of the world where interest in the project is higher.  

161 
 
This data is also valuable when choosing where to organize in-person events such as workshops, 
with a goal of maximizing attendance. It would again make more sense to organize workshops, hackathons, 
and related events in areas with proximity to the largest number of site visitors as that would make it more 
likely for the events to have higher attendance when people don’t have to travel very far.  
 
4.6 Deployment for Impact and Financial Viability 
For a development platform to achieve real-world impact, there must be a way for people to have 
access to the platform and a means of using it effectively. The subject of deployment can be viewed from 
the top down perspective, but it also arises completely organically form the bottom up as well when 
people interested in using the platform start asking questions like, “How do I obtain the platform?”, “Can 
I build it myself?”, “Can you sell it to me?”, “Where can I buy it?”, “When will it be on the market?”, “Can I 
borrow it?”, etc. All of these are actual questions people have asked about FlowIO before there was any 
means of obtaining it. And it is questions like these posed by interested people that then lead one to start 
thinking about the topic of deployment and how to answer those questions. 
Initially, one may just let people borrow the hardware and use it freely, as often happens in 
academic environments. This is not a problem when the number of requests is low and when the people 
making the requests are local. But when requests start to grow in number and when they come from 
different cities or countries, then this approach no longer works, especially when the technology is 
hardware-based, time-consuming to replicate, and expensive. A need then arises for a systematic solution 
to be devised to address the incoming requests from people. And this was what happened with the FlowIO 
project, where initially the requests for it were local and few and therefore manageable; but then they 
quickly grew in number and location, prompting the need for stra tegizing how to handle, manage, and 
fulfill those requests.  
This is where the deployment challenges unique to hardware-based projects became very evident. 
In the case of software, the cost of distributing it to five people or to five hundred people is the same; 
there are no logistical challenges, no components need to be ordered to make additional copies, no time 
needs to be spent on replicating it, there are no shipping costs for  obtaining raw materials, no shipping 
fees when sending finished products, no time and resources required for packaging, and the distribution 
of updates is just as easy.  
But in the case of hardware, none of those conveniences apply. Each additional hardware unit 
costs extra to make and distribute, requires time to produce, and resources are also needed for packaging 
and shipping, many logistical challenges exist when shipping abroad, and even updates are subject to the 
same challenges. The overall time and cost of deployment is orders of magnitude higher than a purely 
software-based project, thus making hardware-based projects far more difficult to scale. Furthermore, the 
more expensive the hardware is to replicate, the more difficult it is to scale it.  Additionally, all the 
challenges that exists with software distribution also exist in the case of hardware-development platforms, 
because as was established earlier – such platforms also include software in addition to the hardware. 
Thus, scaling a hardware project presents significant barriers in both time and finances, especially when 
replication costs are high . Gershenfeld notes that the distribution and replication of phys ical products 
introduces numerous constraints absent in digital technologies, underscoring the need for new models in 
hardware innovation [126]. 
Given the disparity between the distribution and software versus hardware, one may start 
exploring whether there is a way to remove some of the obstacles associated with hardware and make its 
distribution more similar to that of software. The fundamental difference is that software is composed of 
information while hardware is composed of matter; one is made of bits, the other is made of atoms. 
Information can be copied and transmitted freely over the internet, while matter cannot be copied or 
transmitted freely over long distances. Nevertheless, there is an information component in hardware 
162 
 
projects too, and this component is the process or recipe of making the hardware. Thus, by distributing 
that informational recipe rather than the physical hardware itself, it becomes possible to have some of the 
benefits associated with software distribution to extend to hardware. Pearce [127] argues that by sharing 
design files and fabrication instructions for open -source hardware online, it becomes possible to reduce 
production costs and increase accessibility, particularly in underserved regions. This supports the idea that 
distributing the 'recipe' for hardware can emulate the scalability benefits of software. 
 
4.6.1 Deployment objectives for SoftRobotics.IO 
The SoftRobotics.IO core objectives include democratizing emerging fields, reducing barriers to 
creativity and innovation for people of all backgrounds, and eliminating technological, educational, and 
monetary constraints. The aspirational goal was to make  SoftRobotics.IO and all resources within this 
ecosystem meritocratic, directed to those projects and ideas that maximize the  impact-return on 
investment, rather that those who merely maximize the financial-return on investment. Nevertheless, the 
financial ROI was also highly important because the ecosystem needs to be able to sustain itself and grow, 
else it cannot have impact. Furthermore, the democratization objectives also meant enabling people not 
only to use the ecosystem resources, but also to have t he freedom to make modifications, changes, and 
improvements to the hardware, software, and content resources to fit their own unique needs. This 
ultimately required that the hardware and software source files be made accessible to people interested 
in modi fying them or building on top. Meeting all these objectives was highly challenging because 
hardware is expensive and replication takes time and capital resources, yet the goal was to make it 
accessible and available to people irrespective of their financial circumstances. 
The first approach towards addressing these objectives was by providing access to the recipe for 
creating the hardware  so that those interested in it could recreate it themselves. This meant not only 
open-sourcing it but also creating detailed instructions on how to make and replicate everything.  
However, this approach served only a small minority of those interested in utilizing the hardware for a 
number of reasons. That’s because everyone had the skills to replicate it, the components themselves 
were prohibitively expensive for most people, procurement of components was nontrivial, and a few parts 
were custom made and could only be ordered in volume quantities from manufacturers. Ultimately, all of 
those were barriers that required high levels of skills, time, and financial resources to overcome. Thus, 
merely providing the complete informational resources for how to replicate a new platform technology 
did not meet the core objective of lowering barriers and democratizing access to emerging fields, except 
for only a very small number of users. 
Therefore, to meet those objectives more widely, a creative solution had to be found that involves 
physical distribution of hardware and not merely digital distribution of information on how to create the 
hardware of interest. However, this physical distribution approach had to be devised in such a way that it 
would not deplete resources from the ecosystem, but rather bring even more resources into the ecosystem 
to sustain and grow it so it can have even larger impact, while at the same time providing acces s to 
expensive hardware resources to people regardless of their financial ability to pay for those resources but 
based on the potential for positive impact they can bring to the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem and to the 
world at large.  
 
4.6.2 Discussion of Existing Deployment Models and Initiatives 
Many different deployment models, business models, and initiatives that exist today, as depicted 
on Figure 4-34, were studied, considered, and evaluated for suitability with the aspirational objectives and 
goals of SoftRobotics.IO. Several of the models under consideration were not even related to hardware or 
to development platforms, but they were evaluated for the possibility of adapting them to the hardware 
domain and specifically to the domain of hardware development platforms. All models were found to have 
163 
 
certain advantages and drawbacks, and no single model was found to be ideal for the objectives of 
SoftRobotics.IO, nevertheless the Fab Lab model , and its community -driven principles introduced by 
Gershenfeld [128] resonated most closely with the aims of SoftRobotics.IO as will be discussed in this 
section. (Fab Labs are local fabrication spaces democratizing access to digital manufacturing, promoting 
both technological literacy and innovatio n.) Ultimately, this prompted a search for establishing a new 
deployment model better suited for SoftRobotics.IO by mixing and matching  concepts from various 
established existing models and adding a few new ideas as well, as also illustrated on the figure below. 
 
 
Figure 4-34. A list of various deployment models, initiatives, and philosophies from other projects that were 
considered for inspiration in order to find a suitable deployment model for SoftRobotics.IO. 
4.6.2.1 Software & Information Initiatives 
For example, Wikipedia demonstrates how one of the most impactful informational projects today 
was created by leveraging the power of community crowdsourcing even without financial incentives, and 
then providing that community -generated product to the world completely free. The  Wikipedia model 
served as a major inspiration for the deployment and impact objectives of SoftRobotics.IO. However, 
Wikipedia is an exception rather than a norm, because the vast majority of information projects and open-
source software projects do not succeed in being impactful or in attracting contributions. The success 
stories are what everyone is familiar with and hears about, but they are not the norm; they are just popular 
because of survivorship bias. Moreover, even a project like Wikipedia is often struggling to raise funds to 
sustain itself, as evidenced by messaging within the Wikipedia fundraising campaigns that run every year.  
Furthermore, only a tiny fraction of the users who benefit from Wikipedia, Linux, and other open-
source software and information projects contribute anything back to these projects . Raymond [129] 
discusses this phenomenon in the context of open -source software, observing that most projects fail to 
reach critical mass due to lack of participation and support. 
For digital projects low community contribution  is less of  a problem, because users utilizing 
information products without contributing back to them do not deplete resources from the projects, nor 
do they prevent others from accessing the same resources. However, the same cannot be said for 
hardware projects, where every non-contributing user of a physical resource is a financial burden to the 
project, and their usage of the resource supply  leaves fewer resources remaining for others. That is one 
reason why contribution is far more critical for physical projects than for digital projects. 

164 
 
4.6.2.2 Open-Source Hardware Initiative 
Open-source hardware projects offer a natural place to look for an answer, especially open-source 
hardware development platforms such as Arduino, Adafruit Feather, as well as other projects like RepRap 
for 3D printing. Successful open-source hardware projects operate in ways similar to a traditional hardware 
business – selling products, services, or licensing their technology – with the main exception that their 
designs are open and that that in some cases the community is also involved in the design and 
development process.  However, one major difference with FlowIO compared to platforms like Arduino is 
that FlowIO is more than an order of magnitude more expensive than Arduino, thus a deployment model 
that works well for a low-cost product like Arduino cannot be expected to work equally well for a high-cost 
product like FlowIO due to the cost being prohibitive for most users.  
Furthermore, many open-source projects fail to reach wide adoption, to attract contributors, or to 
survive. The open-source hardware model has failed many projects because of  competitive entities that 
take advantage of the open designs for profit and undercut the original creators, often without 
contributing back. Projects that have succeeded under the traditional open-source hardware model have 
been the exception . Several well-known projects, including early 3D printer companies like MakerBot, 
began as open -source ventures but transitioned to closed -source models once business survival was 
threatened by unregulated replication and competition. 
The traditional open -source hardware model also does not address the question of how to give 
access to a physical hardware resource to people who can and who cannot afford the physical hardware. 
It discriminates between people based on their financial resources, dividing people into haves and have-
nots, exclusively favoring those who can meet the price threshold of a hardware resource and completely 
excluding those who cannot meet that price threshold. While anyone may download design files, few have 
the means—technical, financial, or logistical—to build sophisticated hardware systems.  
 In the case of affordable open-source hardware projects, this favoritism is not strongly apparent 
because the price is low enough to be within reach of most people. But in the case of expensive projects 
like FlowIO, most people interested in obtaining hardware resources cannot meet the price threshold of 
over $1,000 per unit.  
As a result, the standard open -source hardware model falls short of meeting the core values of 
SoftRobotics.IO—namely, equitable access and the democratization of advanced technologies. When 
access is contingent on financial capacity, it introduces systemic inequality, reinforcing the divide between 
those who can afford to participate and those who cannot. A different model is needed—one that enables 
access based not on purchasing power, but on contribution, collaboration, or potential for impact. 
 
4.6.2.3 Sharing Economy Initiatives 
The final category of deployment models and initiatives explored were ones involving the sharing 
of physical resources—examples such as public libraries, Airbnb, Uber, and Fab Labs.  These examples 
involve the sharing of resources that may be difficult to obtain such as old books in the case of public 
libraries, or prohibitively expensive such as houses in the case of Airbnb, cars in the case of Uber, and 
fabrication facilities & equipment in the case of Fab Labs. And thus, all four of these examples strongly 
resonated with the objectives of SoftRobotics.IO, in different ways, but especially Neil Gershenfeld’s 
philosophy of Fab Labs including the values they embody, their mission, the communities they serve, the 
way they operate, the way they offer access to expensive resources even free of charge, and the way they 
are able to achieve financial viability even when having free offerings. 
Fab Labs are spaces for community engagement, creativity, entrepreneurship, play, learning, 
mentoring, and inventing. Fab Labs provide access to a physical environment, advanced technology, 
materials, and people with skills who can support anyone to make almost anything. There is a network of 
over 1,750 Fab Labs across the globe in over 100 countries, and a global creative community of fabricators, 
artists, scientists, engineers, educators, amateurs, and professionals [ 130]. Fab Labs have the goal of 
165 
 
democratizing the tools for digital fabrication and technical invention, and they are comprised of off-the-
shelf industrial grade fabrication equipment and electronic tools wrapped in open -source software and 
programs written by MIT researchers.  
All Fab Labs share a common set of equipment and materials; therefore, a project that can be made 
in one Fab Lab can be replicated in any other one anywhere in the world, enabling people to transfer 
physical projects digitally between Fab Labs by means of sharing the design files and detailed replication 
instructions. This idea of transferring a physical project digitally between Fab Labs was the inspiration for 
the FlowIO replication recipe tutorial series discussed earlier. The Fab charter specifies that for an 
institution to qualify as a Fab Lab, it must meet four requirements:  
(1) Be open to the public for free or in-kind service / barter at least part of the time each week. 
(2) Support and subscribe to the Fab charter. 
(3) Share a common set of tools and processes as other Fab Labs as listed in the Fab Lab BOM. 
(4) Participate in the global Fab network and being part of the community. 
The first and last of the requirements above had a strong influence and played a vital role in shaping the 
eventual development of the SoftRobotics.IO deployment model.  
 
 
4.6.3 “Earned Open-Source” – a new deployment model 
After evaluating the limitations of traditional deployment models —including those drawn from 
commercial, open-source, and sharing economy paradigms—a new approach was formulated specifically 
for hardware-oriented development platforms  – which I call  Earned Open Source. This model evolved 
gradually through trial-and-error and iterative experimentation with the FlowIO community over a period 
of 3 years, and it is currently being used in the deployment of FlowIO. Before describing the Earned Open-
Source model, I discuss some of the deployment challenges that had to be addressed, which necessitated 
the need for a new deployment model for open-source hardware. 
 
4.6.3.1 Going Beyond Financial Transactions 
One of the issues with financial transactions is that they are geographically discriminatory, due to 
wide income and wealth gaps between different countries and even different regions within countries. 
This gives an advantage to potential customers in high-income geographies and disadvantages customers 
in low-income geographies. Furthermore, it also disadvantages businesses, especially those producing 
more expensive products, because they would have more difficulty finding customers in lower -income 
regions who are able to buy their products. Thus, offering products in exchange only for monetary 
payments is disadvantageous for both the producers and the potential consumers. And this problem is 
exacerbated for higher -priced items. By simply extending the range of payment methods to include 
service-based payment methods  in addition to financial methods of payment for a product, those 
disadvantages can be largely eliminated, thus benefiting both the business and their customer base in the 
process. 
A gadget priced at $100 is something almost everyone would be able to afford regardless of where 
they are in the world if they really want it. While a $1,000 gadget would be out of reach for many in certain 
parts of the world even if they have a strong need for it . Moreover, many of those who can comfortably 
afford a $1,000 product may still have hesitations as to whether to purchase it or not. However, when a 
$1,000 product can be obtained in exchange for a service contribution of similar value, it becomes equally 
affordable to everyone irrespective of their financial circumstances. Moreover, even for those who can 
comfortably afford the product, paying $1,000 is more uncomfortable than making a skill -based 
contribution of the same value. Thus, having a service-based payment method is beneficial even for those 
not financially constrained. Moreover, the business  providing the product benefits even more than the 
166 
 
customers because it can sell significantly more products in this way while still receiving the full value – 
for those extra product sales – in the form of services received. 
The range of tasks that a business typically needs help with is vast, and thus every potential 
customer may be able to offer some valuable service to the business. An engineer may help with product 
development, a developer may fix a bug, a designer – create a graphic, a writer – generate content, an 
entrepreneur – advise with strategy and consulting, and even those without a specialization can help with 
fundraising, testing, reporting and triaging bugs, moderating forums, providing support to customers, etc. 
Nevertheless, accepting and coordinating service -based contributions is not as simple as accepting 
payments and requires significantly more effort upfront to create the necessary automation infrastructure, 
contract agreements, and management processes. 
For example, similar to a bug tracker in the case of software, there could be a list of open tasks 
that customers and contributors can browse through to find something of interest and relevance to their 
skills and interests. Each task could have a certain point value, and upon successful completion of the task, 
the contributor would earn that number of tokens redeemable for products or services. Those points could 
be similar to how hotels, airlines, and other businesses offer points and miles. Some examples where such 
methods of payment are used include hotels and airlines, nevertheless, in those cases the points / miles 
are earned not for performing services for the businesses but for engaging in prior financial transactions 
with those businesses or their partners. A slight better example where points are actually earned for tasks 
is Microsoft Rewards, though the tasks there are trivial, such as utilizing Microsoft products for online 
search, and thus of negligible value to the business. Thus, all of those e xamples are inadequate for the 
objective of democratizing access. An example, where one can purchase a product by performing a service 
that is materially important for the business, is difficult to find in practice or doesn’t exist. 
Alternatively, contributions can be tokenized on a blockchain and then be used as a cryptocurrency 
in the full range of ways that cryptocurrencies offer including as a store of value, as a medium of exchange, 
and for value appreciation. Creating the infrastructure for tokenized contributions and a cryptocurrency 
based on this idea is one of the areas under consideration for future work. 
A business needs to have cash for operating expenses, for purchases from its suppliers, and for 
paying taxes. Therefore, service-based payments cannot be used for all sales, and the majority of sales 
should still be made with monetary transactions to gener ate the cash flow the business needs for its 
operations. This begs the question then, what is a reasonable limit on the sales that can be made with 
service-based payments . The lower -bound on the limit for service -based sales is equal to what the 
business would otherwise spend anyways on employees or contractors doing the tasks that can now be 
offloaded to customers. For example, if the business can save $10,000 on contractors in this way in a given 
period, then in that same period it can sell (for service-based payments) products with a total production 
cost of $10,000, without affecting the free cash flow of the business. Furthermore, assuming 30% profit 
margins, the business can then receive services worth $13,000 in exchange for those products. The upper 
bound on the limit for service -based sales would depend on the free -cash flow of the business. If the 
business offers – in exchange for services – products whose production cost is greater than what the 
business would otherwise spend on employees / contractors to complete those same services in the same 
period, those additional production costs would deplete some of the free cash flow in the business, as the 
value received in exchange for the products sold is non-cash generating. Thus, the upper-bound on service-
based sales should be one that has only negligible impact on the available cash in the business.  Such 
transactions also have tax-advantages in some jurisdictions if their value is below a certain threshold. 
Finally, there are also significant non-financial and intangible positive outcomes for all participants. 
When a customer makes service-based contribution – such as improving a feature, fixing a bug, writing an 
article, editing a graphic, helping with fundraising, etc. – they are engaging not merely in transactional 
exchanges but also in acts of gifting; gifting not to the entity that makes or distributes the product but to 
the community and users that benefit from it  – which also includes the contributor hi mself or herself.  
167 
 
Some contributors may feel a sense of accomplishment, fulfilment, and enjoyment from leaving their mark 
on a project and making a tangible impact. However, even if one is not motivated by any of those factors, 
but purely by personal gain, they would still have an  incentive for them to contribute – as long as they 
have the capacity and mental clarity to  recognize and realize that their contribution is also benefiting 
themselves either directly or indirectly (through trickle down effects), or both. 
Furthermore, multiple research studies have shown that gifting generates many positive outcomes 
for the giver. Stephen Post found that altruistic behavior and volunteering are associated with physiological 
well-being and physical health benefits – producing a sense of joy and emotional reward [131]. Dunn et. 
al. produced a highly cited experiment published in the journal Science, which clearly demonstrating that 
“Spending money on others promotes happiness”, which is also the title of their paper [132].  Adam Grant 
also showed that helping others enhances the helper’s own sense of competence and fulfilment [ 133]. 
Importantly, these positive outcomes for the giver manifest themselves regardless of their original 
motivation. Even if they were motivated by some self-interest, and not by any altruistic tendencies, they 
still receive the intangible emotional, social, and health benefits associated with giving.  
Additionally, as Charles Isaacson argues in his book Sacred Economics [134], there are societal-
level benefits in gift economies that are largely absent in monetary economies. While monetary exchanges 
are transactional and often terminate the relationship once completed, gift -based exchanges create 
enduring social bonds and mutual obligations that strengthen communities over time. Gifts foster a sense 
of unity, reciprocity, and belonging, as contributors and beneficiaries remain connected through ongoing 
relationships rather than discrete transactions. Eisenstein emphasize that, “in a money economy, there is 
no necessity for ongoing relations hips. You pay for something and that’s it —no ongoing obligations, no 
ongoing ties.” Additionally, he notes that money turns relationships into transactions between strangers, 
where the only obligation is to get the best deal, not to care about the other party . “ Money creates 
competition among its users. In a market transaction, your gain is my loss .” I n a typical financial 
transaction, the buyer wants to pay less, and the seller wants to earn more, which naturally puts them in 
adversarial roles. By contrast, gift-like transactions crate camaraderie and unity, mutual gratitude and a 
sense of shared purpose. “Gifts create ties between people, while money tends to dissolve them.”  
Thus, service-based contributions not only provide individual emotional and health benefits but 
also contribute to building more resilient, interconnected communities that are characterized by trust, 
mutual support, and collective flourishing.  Importantly, the societal benefits produced by acts of gifting 
are largely independent of the giver’s original motivation. Whether a contribution is motivated by self -
interest, a desire for reputation, or a genuine sense of altruism, the resulting effects—strengthened social 
bonds, enhanced reciprocity, and greater community cohesion—remain consistent. Eisenstein emphasizes 
that it is the act of giving itself, rather than the purity of intent, that generates the circulation of value and 
the reinforcement of communal ti es characteristic of a gift economy. Even self-interested contributions 
serve to weave individuals more tightly into the fabric of a community, fostering connections that 
monetary transactions typically erode. Thus, service -based contributions not only ben efit the individual 
contributor but simultaneously cultivate the relational structures that underpin resilient and flourishing 
communities. 
Therefore, it follows that introducing additional incentives  and systems in place to encourage 
greater giving and contribution would further amplify the positive outcomes for both individuals and 
communities. Since the societal benefits of gifting manifest regardless of the contributor’s initial 
motivation, creating environments that lo wer barriers to contribution or offer modest incentives can 
enhance participation without undermining the positive outcomes. Such incentives need not be monetary. 
The critica l factor is maintaining the relational essence of the exchange, ensuring that contributions 
continue to build trust, gratitude, and social cohesion rather than reverting to purely transactional 
interactions. Thoughtfully designed incentives, when aligned w ith the values of service and reciprocity, 
can thus strengthen rather than diminish the foundational dynamics of a gift-oriented system. 
168 
 
 
These dynamics are observable in the success of large -scale collaborative projects such as 
Wikipedia, open-source software communities, and open innovation platforms. Wikipedia, for example, 
operates almost entirely on voluntary contributions, with individ uals motivated by a combination of 
altruism, personal interest, reputation, and intrinsic satisfaction. Despite the varied motivations of 
contributors, the cumulative effect is the creation of a resource that benefits all users and strengthens a 
global kno wledge community. Similarly, open -source software projects rely on voluntary developer 
contributions, where acts of coding, debugging, and documentation function as gifts to a broader user 
base, including the contributors themselves. In many cases, contributors directly ben efit from the 
improvements they help create, as they are users of the very products, services, or communities they work 
to enhance. Open innovation models, which invite external participants to contribute ideas, solutions, or 
improvements, similarly leverage the emotional and social benefits associated with contribution, while 
often supplementing them with modest incentives such as recognition, access to communities of practice, 
or opportunities for learning. These examples demonstrate tha t systems based on voluntary service 
contributions can achieve remarkable levels of innovation and collective value creation when the relational 
and community aspects of gifting are preserved, even when self -interest and external incentives play a 
role. They provide living proof of the principles articulated by Eisenstein and by other research on prosocial 
behavior, showing that fostering environments of trust, mutual benefit, and gratitude leads to thriving, 
self-sustaining ecosystems. 
 
4.6.3.2 Pitfalls of Open-Source as it is Practiced Today 
 Countless digital products that are used regularly by nearly everyone today, often without users 
realizing it, were created by communities of contributors as gifts to the world, open -sourced, and made 
freely available for anyone to use, extend, build -upon, and remix – from operating systems and libraries 
on which most of the internet runs, to consumer software and applications. This also applies to 
information products like Wikipedia and countless other websites that offer valuable content freely, often 
times without even generating any advertising revenue. All these free and open -source products have 
costs associated with their creation, maintenance, and the continuation of their existence. And the vast 
majority of users are not bearing any of those costs. Only a small fraction of those who benefit from free 
and open-source products make any contribution back to them to help cover the operational expenses.  
 The standard open-source model suffers from the free-rider problem in economics – where those 
who benefit from the resources do not pay for them. The open -source model that exists today does not 
offer any incentives for people to contribute back (monetarily or otherwise) to the resources they are 
benefiting from, and instead it relies on people’s cooperation with goodwill. For open-source projects that 
have very large communities of users, this tends to not be a problem because even if only 1% of users 
contributes back to the project, that may be sufficient to cover the operating costs for developing, 
maintaining, and sustaining the project. For digital and information projects a small number of 
contributors can support a much larger user base, because the costs are the same whether a particular 
software is used by 10 people or by 10,000 people. However, for small-scale projects or ones that are just 
starting up, free ridership can be a significant problem, because there may not be a sufficiently large pool 
of contributors to cover the development and operational costs. Nevertheless, the existing open -source 
model does not offer practical tools for incentivizing users to give back and for disincentivizing free-riding. 
 A second major problem with open-source deployment as practiced today is the risk of intellectual 
property theft . Open-sourcing of designs, schematics, and replication instructions allows anyone to 
replicate the product. This creates a risk that others may profit unfairly, potentially harming the original 
creators financially and even forcing them out of business. The open-source model has some measures to 
mitigate this risk by providing various types of licenses that creators can choose– from permissive licenses 
like the MIT license, to more restrictive ones like GPL that require all derivatives to follow the same license, 
169 
 
to more nuanced ones like Creative Commons and all its variations. Those licenses do offer some 
protection for the creators, but the creators first need to have high familiarity with various licenses and 
with copyright law just to ensure that they choose the appropriate license or potentially different licenses 
for different kinds of digital content. Even with the protections that licenses offer, if there is a violation of 
those license terms, there are not tools for creators to find licensing violations, unless they somehow on 
their own accord find out about it. And even if they do find violations, there is no mechanism to stop such 
violations; they would have to start litigations against the violators, which in most cases would be 
prohibitively expensive and therefore impossible for a small or emerging open -source project to do 
anything about stopping the violators. 
 Open-source hardware in particular is also at higher risk of supply-chain scarcity than closed-
source hardware. Certain electronic components can often be in low stock or out of stock, and sometimes 
for extended periods of time. When the designs and bill of materials for a popular hardware product are 
openly available, there would be mor e buyers of those same components, therefore exacerbating the 
shortage problem and making it difficult or potentially even impossible for the product designers to 
continue making their product in a period of extended chip supply shortages. Moreover, even those who 
are working on completely unrelated projects, if they see a particular chip being successfully used in an 
open-source hardware project, they would be more likely to choose that chip than to go with an alternative 
lesser-known chip, especially if the open hardware project also includes a driver for that chip. This type of 
scarcity problem was especially apparent in the early 2020’s global chip shortage when certain 
components were out of stock for over a year, while others had temporary price hikes of over 1,000% due 
to their extremely limited availability.  
 Other strategic pitfalls with open-source projects include potential loss of control and community 
division. While one of the benefits of open-source is that it allows others to build upon a project and even 
create their own unique versions of a project, this is also a risk because the original author may lose control 
of their project to a forked version, and it may also cause confusion in the community leading to division 
and potentially even the demise of both the original and the forked project if neither of them has sufficient 
community support to cover the costs of the project. This is less dangerous for well -established projects 
with large communities, but it could be fatal for new and emerging projects that have not yet become well 
known or have not yet established a strong brand recognition.  
 There are many good reasons for deploying a project as open -source, but pitfalls like those 
mentioned above and others discourage individuals and companies from open-sourcing their products to 
protect themselves and their products from such problems. There are also many instances where products 
start as being open-sourced and then they transition to closed-source when the companies behind them 
start encountering those challenges to protect themselves from going out of existence. One example of 
this was the 3D printer company MakerBot and more recently Arduino. When companies make this 
transition, they face a significant backlash from their users for being greedy as well as accusations that 
they started with an open-source product merely to deceive users and earn their trust. When in reality, it 
is the pitfalls of the standard open-source model that often leaves companies with no other choice but to 
close-source their products or cease to exist.  The underlying problem is that there are only two options 
to pick from – open-source vs. closed-source. There is a need for a model that maintains the principles 
and offers the same advantages as the existing open-source model but without its pitfalls.  
 
4.6.3.3 Earned Open-Source Model 
 The Earned Open-Source model retains the core principles of openness: enabling replication, 
fostering community development, and allowing source access. However, it introduces the concept of 
conditional access —that is, source files and design resources must be earned through meaningful 
contribution, not granted by default. 
170 
 
The core principles of open source include giving people freedom to understand how a product 
works, enabling individuals to replicate the product and modify it for their own needs, and allowing an 
entire community of people passionate about a project or an initiative to participate in its development. 
However, upholding these principles does not necessarily mean that sources files, designs, and schematics 
must be placed in a public repository where everyone can just access them without any expectation on 
their part. Open-source licenses like GPL and others all put obligations on the users, but those obligations 
only deal with what the user does with the source files after they are already in possession. The earned 
open-source model retains those core principles of the open -source philosophy but introduces the 
concept of earned access – requiring one to first earn their right to access the source files  through 
meaningful and progressive contribution. In this model of deployment, source code, design files, and all 
other relevant resources can still be accessed by those who want them, and the community can still 
participate in the development, but that access privilege must first be earned rather than granted by 
default. Moreover, this privilege cannot be bought either – it must be earned.  
 
 
Figure 4-35. Visual representation of the Earned Open-Source Model, shown as a series of stages and steps that a 
user must take to gradually earn their access privileges to the source files. 
In most aspects of life, privileges are either bought or earned, and the earning process generally 
happens in stages, where expectations of commitment and service must be met. Yet, in today’s standard 
open-source model, the concepts of earned privilege and access levels do not exist. Furthermore, when 
something is given freely to everyone without them putting any effort into earning it, people often do not 
cherish or value it. This behavior is also evident in open-source communities, where users of open-source 
products often feel entitled to the source code / design files / schematics / instructions, rather than 
viewing their access to such resources as privilege. The access privilege ceases to be special when 
everyone has that access freely. Earned open source  simply takes these concepts of gradually earned 
privilege through commitment and service that are found in nearly all other aspects of existence and 
adapts them to open -source as well, and in this way rectifying many of the deficiencies and pitfalls th at 
currently exist today.  
The specifics of how access is earned can be up to the discretion of project owners, but the 
following general guidelines may be considered. Full access should not be granted after a single 

171 
 
contribution but rather earned in stages. Some parts of the source code or designs could be open by 
default for everyone, and each access tier would unlock more of the code / design files, while requiring a 
greater level of contribution and commitment. Contributions, especially for full access, should be service 
based rather than financial, to demonstrate commitment to the project. Those who genuinely need the 
source code / design files are, by definition, going to have technical skills, or else they have no legitimate 
reasons to need such access  to make legitimate use of them . Therefore, the project authors can even 
require the contributions to be technical to screen for legitimacy and to advance the project’s growth. 
Earned open-source offers many advantages compared to the traditional open-source model.  It 
addresses all the major pitfalls of open source discussed previously as well as the problem of entitlement 
in users. This approach largely eliminated the free-rider problem; there is an incentive within the model 
itself for contribution and not merely a reliance on people’s goodwill. The likelihood for theft of intellectual 
property is also greatly reduced because only a limited number of people would have access to  the 
designs. This risk is not completely eliminated as there could still be a bad actor that may go through the 
process to gain access, though this process deters all but the most determined ones and even they would 
be slowed down significantly. A similar point is also argued by Eric von Hippel in his book “The Sources of 
Innovation” [135] where on page 54 he says, “Methods for protecting trade secrets need not be foolproof 
to be effective; they simply have to raise enough of a barrier in a given case to create an unattractive cost-
benefit equation for would-be imitators in that case.” 
License violations can also be identified significantly more efficiently when the people with full 
access privileges are limited in number. The problem of artificially induced supply chain scarcity would also 
be greatly reduced, as only those with earned access privileges would know the bill of materials. Creators 
can maintain stronger control over their project; they can exercise leverage over contributors until they 
earn full access privileges. Finally, when people earn something through hard work, they r espect it and 
value it more and are more likely to maintain it better. 
There are also second-order advantages for the entire open-source community at large. With the 
current status quo, even businesses and individuals who resonate with the open-source philosophy often 
choose closed-source because they face only a black and white dichotomy between open and close, and 
they wish to avoid the pitfalls that come with open source. Earned open source provides them with a third 
much safer option that embodies the same open -source philosophy. Those who are inclined to open -
source their products would be far more likely to do so, if they had an established model to follow on how 
to open-source their products without taking tremendous risks. Thus, if this idea of earned open source 
becomes widely known, it is possible to see an entire wave of hardware and software products become 
more open in this way. The dream of the open -source community is to have every product they use be 
open-source, and this model of earned open source makes that dream much more plausible, ultimately 
benefiting the entire open-source community.  
The primary disadvantage with earned open source lies in community management. Managing 
the community around an open-source project is already challenging and time-consuming as it is. Adding 
processes for deciding who has access to what, when access is given, and what contributions are needed 
to earn what level of access creates an entirely new layer of complexity which could be a full -time job. 
Doing this efficiently requires setting up an entire infrastructure system just for this management. 
However, this is where the connection comes with the earlier idea of payment through service -based 
contributions, because that requires the same type of management infrastructure. Therefore, a single 
solution addresses both the financial transaction problems and the pitfalls of traditional open-source. With 
this infrastructure in place people can (1) purchase products and (2) earn access privileges, both through 
service-based contributions. 
There are also other challenges that come with the earned open -source model. One of them is 
ensuring fairness in evaluating contributions. This could be mitigated with a tokens-based approach as 
described previously where different tasks have predetermined point/token value which the contributor 
172 
 
earns upon successful completion of the task. By restricting access, innovation could slow down as fewer 
people have the opportunity to experiment, tinker, or build upon the project. This model would also deter 
some people, nevertheless that is part of the goal, and the project creators can decide the extent to which 
they wish to deter people, by either increasing or lowering the friction for obtaining access. If they lower 
the friction to none, then then that takes them to the traditional open -source; and if they increase the 
friction to infinity, that takes them to closed -source. From this perspective, traditional open -source and 
closed-source can be viewed merely as special cases of the earned open -source model, which serve as a 
generalization, and servic e-contributions are simply a dial that take a user from one side of the 
open/closed spectrum to the other – progressively and in stages. 
The FlowIO platform was initially deployed according to the traditional open-source model where 
everything was openly accessible to anyone visiting the SoftRobotics.IO website. But soon afterwards, it 
became evident that many of the pitfalls of open-source discussed earlier started emerging with FlowIO. 
Some users made contributions while others were free riders.  The content on the website was licensed 
under the Creative Commons Attribution Share-Alike license, and a few people failed to comply with the 
licensing terms, by not giving attribution credit for example. And possibly there are many others who just 
haven’t been noticed even. There were also other problems encountered that led to financial loss for the 
FlowIO project. All of those challenges prompt ed the need to seek an alternative to the open -source 
model, that would not take away the freedom of people having access to the FlowIO source code and 
design files, but at the same protect the project from the pitfalls that come with open-source deployment. 
This is what ultimately led to the idea of the earned open-source model, which has evolved gradually and 
iteratively over the course of two years in which many different strategies were tried with the FlowIO 
community, seeing how FlowIO users respond to them. The model has also undergone further refinement 
and even name changes until eventually the name earned open source was coined. 
Both for obtaining FlowIO and for accessing certain subsets of the source code / designs, p eople 
are expected to make some contribution to help th e project survive, grow, and evolve. Example 
contributions include hardware and software development, content creation, video preparation, donation, 
graphic design, hardware assembly, writing blog posts, etc. The requirements are lower for individuals 
than for institutions, as I nstitutions are expected to also include a financial contribution for their access 
privilege. 
 
4.7 Chapter Summary and Future Goals for SoftRobotics.IO 
This chapter covered the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem and every aspect of it was explored in depth 
and from many different angles. The range of topics discussed was vast and diverse. Therefore, to form a 
cohesive picture of how everything fits together, and the role played by every topic that was part of the 
discussion, a summary list is provided here with everything that was covered in this chapter. 
 
SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem 
❖ Development Platforms - for Actuation & Sensing 
• 1(Pneumatic), 2(Tendon-based), 3(Hydraulic), 4(Electromagnetic), 5(Thermal), 6(Electrostatic).  
• FlowIO can morph into any of those six types of platforms with relatively few modifications only.  
• Future goal: unify all platforms into one with different modules for the different actuation types.  
❖ Community and Interactions 
• Community Building through collaborations. 
o Focused strategic collaborations with 1-to-2 users at time, where user #1 needs only the 
existing platform features and user #2 requires some new features to be added.

o Additional Workshops at Universities and FabLabs. 
o Providing online resources that enable others to run their own workshops. 
• Community and Interactions Online. 
o Community management challenges. 
o Community oriented features – user accounts, forums, chats, blogs, groups. 
o Vetting process and verification.

▪ Account holders.

• Community Building and Interactions through Hardware Sharing.

▪ Geographically distributed and decentralized. 
▪ New connections formed and collaborations established between users. 
▪ Higher resource utilization and greater impact per hardware unit. 
▪ Symbiotic relationships and positive feedback loops as users are required to 
make some beneficial contribution back to the ecosystem and/or community. 
▪ Eliminating financial barriers by focusing on service-based contributions. 
❖ Content and Digital Resources 
• Three types of content needed: 
o Discovery content – making people aware of the platform’s existence. 
o Overview content – basic description for curiosity satisfaction and exciting examples. 
o Detailed content – docs, instructions, tutorials, and other in-depth information. 
• Strategizing the order for content creation and publication. 
o Content creation order: Detailed → Overview → Discovery. 
o Content consumption order: Discovery → Overview → Detailed. 
o Content publication order for FlowIO.

o Word of mouth remains the primary way through which the discovery process (learning 
about a platform’s existence) happens within academic communities especially. 
o Projects built with the platform play a major role in the discovery process. 
o Recommendations for platform designers to maximize the impact of their platforms – 
give it as early as possible to people who can projects with it that they publish.

❖ Deployment for Impact and Financial Viability 
• For a platform to have impact, there must be a way for people to access it and use it. 
• Deployment objective for SoftRobotics.IO  
o reducing educational, financial, and technological barriers to creativity for people of all 
backgrounds, democratizing fields, and maximizing impact. 
• Discussion of Existing Relevant Deployment Models and Initiatives. 
o Software & Information oriented initiatives and models. 
o Open-Source hardware initiatives and models. 
o Sharing-oriented initiatives and models. 
• Earned Open-Source Model for deployment. 
o Going beyond financial transactions and incorporating service-based transactions. 
▪ Benefits they bring to both businesses and customers. 
▪ Implementation challenges and recommendations. 
o Pitfalls of open-source as it exists today: 
▪ free-rider problem. 
▪ risk of intellectual property theft. 
▪ exacerbation of supply-chain scarcity. 
▪ loss of control and community division. 
▪ community expectations and entitlement. 
o Earned Open-Source Model 
▪ The right to view and access the sources must be earned. 
▪ Tiered approach to earning different access levels. 
▪ Service-based contribution requirement for full access to all sources. 
▪ Advantages: addresses all the pitfalls of the traditional open-source model. 
▪ Challenges: added complexity for community management. 
▪ Second order advantages and disadvantages of earned open-source. 
▪ How FlowIO’s deployment helped formulate the earned open-source model. 
There is future work associated with each of the five top-level constituents of the SoftRobotics.IO 
ecosystem. Some of those future works include continuing the technical development of hardware and 
software to bring about each of the 6 different types of platforms described in detail, and to eventually 
merge them into a single general purpose development platform supporting all the varieties of actuation 
approaches through swappable modules. On the content and digital resources front, the goals are to 
expand the type of projects, examples, resources, and documentation that are currently available for 
FlowIO. And after that, to repeat the same for each of the other platforms in the ecosystem – creating 
discovery content, overview content, and detailed content for all of them. On the community & 
interactions front, future goals continuing the community engagement in all ways discussed as well as in 
creating additional in-person and online opportunities such as organizing a hackathon for instance. The 
website a nd online community platform still have significant room for improvement to streamline all 
aspects of the earned open-source deployment model, and to automate many of the processes that are 
currently done manually including the hardware sharing program, the management of service -based 
contributions, earning tokens from contributions, transferring and trading such tokens on a blockchain , 
and streamlining access control management. Finally, on the deployment model front, long -term goals 
175 
 
include going beyond platforms and focusing also on providing similar access to other equipment, services, 
and materials that help make prototyping and innovation more rapid and more accessible for everyone – 
by enabling all members of the community to offer digital and physical resources related to soft robotics 
and programmable materials on the website. One possible avenue is to foster stronger relationships with 
FabLabs around the world, since the vision of SoftRobotics.IO is largely based on the same philosophy from 
which FabLabs were born.

P h. D. D I S S E R T A T I O N  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
5: SleeveIO: Modular and Reconfigurable Platform for 
Wearable Haptic Feedback Interaction 
 
 
Figure 5-1. Example multimodal haptic feedback interactions enabled by SleeveIO: (a) Two vibrotactors, two 
bellows, two muscles, and a quad -chamber module on a knitted sleeve substrate; (b) Bellow modules on two 
knitted-band substrates with a wearable pneumatic control system; c) Suction cups, muscles, and bellow modules 
connected to three knitted-band substrates. 
This is the first of two chapters that demonstrate instances of tools making tools, and in particular 
how one development platform gives rise to another development platform, which then gives rise to new 
classes of applications. Specifically, this chapter covers the SleeveIO Platform which on the one hand serves 
as an instance of a platform enabled by the FlowIO Platform. And on the other hand, it serves as one of 
the core platform projects in this dissertation ( independent of its relationship to FlowIO) de veloped in 
accordance with and guided by some of the lessons captured within the D&D Framework. 
SleeveIO is a modular and reconfigurable hardware platform for rapid prototyping of multimodal 
wearable haptic feedback interactions. SleeveIO features engineered machine -knitted sleeve and band 
substrates, and five categories of haptic feedback actuator modules incl uding vibrotactors, bellows, 
muscles, suction/puffing cups, and quad-chamber actuators. A universal magnetic attachment mechanism 
unifies the different types of actuators, enabling countless multimodal haptic experiences involving 
combinations of different actuator types in different configurations. SleeveIO makes use of the FlowIO 
Chapter  5 
178 
 
platform’s hardware and software for actuation and sensing , which enables individual control of each 
haptic actuator and makes the system battery-powered and untethered. This paper presents the SleeveIO 
platform in detail along with replication resources, a novel generalized approach to making different types 
of haptic actuators modular and interoperable, new application possibilities enabled by SleeveIO, and a 
pilot assessment of the viability of the platform as a whole and each module individually.  This work was 
done at Meta Reality Labs in collaboration with Amirhossein Memar, Mengjia Zhu, and Nicholas 
Colonnese, and it was published at UIST’23 [136]. 
 
5.1 SleeveIO Introduction 
Modern audio/visual interfaces and electronics development platforms feature impressive 
characteristics of modularity, interoperability, ease of use, and plug -and-play capabilities. These 
affordances and capabilities enable interaction designers, applicati on researchers, and prototypers 
working with those technologies to seamlessly combine different hardware and software, electronics, and 
devices to rapidly test application ideas and to unlock new interaction possibilities. For instance, VR 
headsets, hand t rackers, motion sensors, hardware development platforms, and various software 
frameworks - can rapidly be integrated with one another, allowing designers and researchers to then focus 
most of their effort on building new applications, without spending much time on the integration aspect 
of those technologies. 
However, those high-level interoperability and plug -and-play characteristics that are common in 
the audio/visual and electronics domains are hard to find in the haptics domain currently. As a result, 
haptics researchers and haptics interaction designers oft en face far greater challenges when trying to 
combine different haptic technologies in order to create a new application. They often have to spend the 
majority of their time just on the integration aspect of the technologies they wish to combine, before 
being able to start developing the applications. They may have to find custom connectors, create new 
coupling mechanisms, develop new communication protocols, or may even have to modify from the 
ground up the technologies they wish to combine, just to make them interoperable and compatible with 
one another. 
Conducting haptics research often requires creating “full stack” systems composed of custom -
made components which are oftentimes incompatible with other projects. For instance, from 2017 to 2020 
several haptic sleeve or wristband projects were published re ndering similar haptic stimuli , like 
HapticClench [137], hBracelet [138], Affective Sleeve [139], Tasbi [140], Wrap [141], a forearm device for 
creating lateral motion illusion [ 142], Bellowband [143], and PneuMod [144]. However, despite their 
similarities, all these research projects were constructed, implemented, and controlled in different ways – 
representing a substantial duplication of effort and making collaboration between different research 
teams difficult. 
Inspired by the interoperability, modularity, and plug-and-play capabilities of consumer electronics 
and hardware development platforms, and by witnessing and experiencing the hardware -integration 
struggles faced by haptics researchers and designers, we be gan exploring the possibility of creating a 
standardized modular approach to wearable haptics that enables the seamless combination, 
reconfiguration, and adaptation of many different kinds of haptic actuators and actuation approaches. We 
wanted to provide an approach or a standard to wearable haptics so that haptics researchers have a way 
of adapting various past, present, and even future kinds of haptics actuators to be compatible with one 
another. And we also wanted to enable haptics interaction designers  to be able to seamlessly create 
multimodal haptic user experiences with many different kinds of actuators without having to dedicate 
much effort to the integration aspect of the hardware and software and be able to focus on the 
applications instead. Thus, in this chapter I present a new standardized approach to wearable haptics that 
addressing those goals. We also created a new haptics development platform called SleeveIO built in 
179 
 
accordance with these objectives, while being untethered, modular, reconfigurable, and supporting 
combinations of many different types of haptic actuators. SleeveIO serves as both a demonstration of our 
proposed approach to interoperability that can be ada pted by other researchers, and also as a design 
canvas that can be used by interaction design researchers to rapidly prototype novel user experiences or 
to test new combinations of actuators and placements of those actuators. 
The key contributions of this work are as follows: 
• A novel modular and reconfigurable approach to wearable haptics, that enables plug-and-play 
combination and reconfiguration of various new and existing types of haptic feedback actuators. 
• A full-stack development platform and toolkit for wearable haptics that is modular, 
reconfigurable, conformable, and self-contained / untethered. 
• An example set of five types of plug-and-play haptic feedback modules demonstrating how all of 
them have been adapted to use our unified magnetic interface. 
• A repository of resources for replicating the SleeveIO system and each type of haptic module. 
• Pilot assessment of the viability of SleeveIO and each type of module, and haptic experience 
comparison between modules. 
 
5.2 Addressing the Interoperability Problem in Wearable Haptics 
To address the problem of interoperability between different types of haptic actuators we 
identified four characteristics that had to be met: modularity, reconfigurability, support for many types of 
actuators, and support for multiple actuation approaches. 
Modularity: Baldwin & Clark [ 145] define modularity as “Independence of structure and 
integration of function” – the structural units of a system are independent of one another, but they work 
together. The ideal type of modularity is full plug-and-play capability. 
Reconfigurability: the capability for repeated structural and functional transformations. The most 
relatable example of reconfigurability is a LEGO set. Reconfigurability is an essential feature for enabling 
arbitrary haptic user experiences. 
Many Types of Actuators : To achieve general-purpose interoperability, our approach had to be 
suitable for not only one type of actuator with many variations, but also for many different types of 
actuators with multiple variations of each actuator . We needed to devise a common meth od for 
connection and coupling of distinct actuator types. It also had to allow actuators from prior works to be 
easily adaptable to that common standard. 
Multiple Methods of Actuation : We also must account for the fact that wearable haptic devices 
can be actuated by many different technologies - including electromagnetic, pneumatic, biologic, thermal, 
and others. Thus, if we want to offer a designer the possibility to integrate a wide va riety of haptic 
actuators, they need to have the freedom of using multiple methods of actuation. 
Despite numerous advances in haptic platforms and haptic actuation, the prior works we found did 
not meet all four of our criteria above. Thus, we devised our own approach to satisfy these objectives. 
Inspired by educational pegboards into which pegs of di fferent shapes and functions can be inserted 
anywhere on the 2D board to create art, we asked what if we make a 3D pegboard that is knitted, 
conformable, and wearable, and to which we can attach different kinds of haptic actuators to create 
diverse haptic user experiences. 
Based on this approach, we built a development platform that is modular, reconfigurable, and 
supporting many different kinds of haptic actuators. It has a knitted substrate worn on the forearm to 
which various haptic actuator modules can be attached anywhere using magnetic cap-&-pole connectors. 
To provide multiple methods of actuation, we leverage the FlowIO Platform which offers 
pneumatic/hydraulic sensing and actuation with positive and negative pressures, while its expansion 
modules offer additional capabilities, such as electromagnetic actuation for driving vibrotactors. 
180 
 
 
5.3 Related Work 
This work builds upon prior research in wearable haptic systems, haptic actuators, hardware 
development platforms, modular and reconfigurable toolkits, and digital knitting.
