P h. D. D I S S E R T A T I O N  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
6: ModiStrap Platform 
 
Figure 6-1. ModiStrap Platform – (a) the set of mechanical components; (b) three examples of arm-mounted, 
freestanding, and extrusion-mounted robotic straps, respectively; and (c) three examples of holding a battery, a 
metal box, and a tape roll with the suction, electromagnet, and hook end-effector modules, respectively. 
ModiStrap is a new robotics development platform that features a robotic strap and a set of plug-
and-play component modules connectable to the robotic strap to enable reconfigurability and rapid 
prototyping of numerous application possibilities. This project was inspired by the large-scale robotic strap 
in [ 188] and was done in collaboration with  the paper’s main author  Kentaro Barhydt from the MIT 
d’ Arbeloff lab led by Prof. Harry Asada. The FlowIO device is used for the actuation, sensing, and control 
of the robotic strap and for the  end effectors attached to it. Thus, ModiStrap is both an independent 
platform and an instance of a general-purpose tool enabled by another general-purpose tool. The FlowIO 
platform enabling the ModiStrap Platform is an example of the philosophy discussed in Chapter 1 on how 
tools and building blocks can have unlimited and exponentially growing impact as they give rise not only 
to new applications but also to new types of tools and building blocks, which themselves can then enable 
Chapter  6 
208 
 
even more diverse applications and more tools in completely different domains, resulting in a recursive 
cycle of innovation growth. 
 
Figure 6-2. The complete set of ModiStrap hardware and software components grouped into 6 categories: straps, 
mounts, couplers, end effectors, control hardware, and control software. Most mechanical parts are 3D printed. 
6.1 ModiStrap Overview 
ModiStrap (short for Modifiable/Modular Robotic Strap) is a modular and reconfigurable toolkit for 
rapid prototyping of wearable and nonwearable robotic applications. It consists of a pneumatically 

209 
 
actuated shape-changing robotic strap, a suite of swappable modules, and a miniature control system with 
hardware and software that can actuate both the robotic strap, and any end-effectors connected to it.  
There are six hardware and software component categories within ModiStrap as shown in  Figure 
6-2. The primary component is a robotic strap comprised of 3D printed segments, named tumblers, strings 
that hold them together while allowing rotational motion between them, and up to 3 pneumatic muscles 
running along the top of the tumblers. The robotic strap can be made with any number of tumbler 
segments; thus, the length of the strap can be an arbitrary multiple of a tumbler’s diameter. 
Munts enable the robotic strap to be mounted and operated from various locations and positions, 
as well as to be used as a wearable device. There are U -shaped mounts that allow a robotic strap to 
connect on one end to the mount while the opposite end of the str ap is free -floating. There are also 
constraining mounts that connect to the sides of the strap and serve two roles. First, they constrain angular 
motion between two neighboring segments. Second, they enable the strap to be mounted on its side at 
any two tumblers rather than on its end. The U -shaped mounts come in a wearable and non -wearable 
variant. The wearable variant features a GoPro-style connector for secure attachment to body-worn gear 
like helmets, armbands, and chest-straps; while the non-wearable variant is T-slotted to allow attachment 
and repositioning to aluminum extrusions commonly used in industrial and research facilities. The T -
slotted variation comes in locking type that constrains any rotation and nonlocking type that only allows 
one-way rotation similar to the operation of a ratchet gear.  
Couplers comprise the third component category, and they enable multiple robotic straps to 
connect to each other at various bending and twisting angles - giving rise to numerous configuration and 
application possibilities. Each coupler uses a mechanism that slides into the center of a tumbler and clips 
securely when fully inserted.  The socket of the tumbler has a square cross-section, which allows the clip 
to connect at two different orientations.   
A family of end-effector modules uses the same connection mechanism as the couplers, making it 
possible for a user to rapidly attach, detach, and swap end-effectors to either endpoint of a robotic strap. 
Currently, there are 5 end -effector modules designed for ModiStrap, which include Su ction cup, 
Electromagnet, Hook, Push/Pull module, and Gripper module.  
Finally, the control software and control hardware roles are served by the FlowIO Platform with 
its expansion modules and corresponding software stack –providing actuation, sensing, and control of 
both the robotic strap and all end -effectors. Even though the FlowIO software stack is not specific to 
ModiStrap, it greatly simplifies complexity and reduces time when developing ModiStrap applications.  
 
6.2 ModiStrap Details 
In this section, each of the different types of components that comprise ModiStrap will be described 
in detail, including their function, properties, and behaviors, as well as some of the application possibilities 
they enable. The design process and thought process that led to the specific manifestation of some of the 
components will also be presented.  
 
6.2.1 Robotic Strap 
ModiStrap implements a smaller, more versatile, and more user-friendly version of the robotic strap 
that was initially published in [ 188] and posted on the SoftRobotics.IO blog [ 189] by the paper’s author . 
This smaller scale of the strap also makes it suitable for both benchtop and wearable applications. The 
robotic strap consists of 3D printed tumblers, Kevlar strings that hold the tumblers together while allowing 
them to rotate aro und each other, and commercially available pneumatic muscles connected across all 
tumblers via zip ties. The 3D printed strap components were redesigned from the ground up to optimize 
them for the small scale and to add new features and improvements such as sockets at the center of the 
tumblers, special channels for the zip ties, and special mounting bumps for attachment to the mounts. A 
210 
 
robotic strap can have any number of tumbler segments and either one, two, or three muscles running in 
parallel. The number of tumblers controls the length of the strap, while the number of muscles controls 
the maximum force the device can exert under actuation. The muscles connect to only the top side of the 
tumblers. When a muscle is pressurized / inflated it contracts, thus pulling the tops of the tumblers closer 
together, which ultimately results in the strap bending inward toward the side where the mus cle is 
attached. When deflated, a muscle extends to its original length without buckling, thereby pushing the 
tumbler tops away from each other and causing the strap to return to its unactuated shape – which is 
typically a straight line but can also be oth er shapes. The bending radius is positively correlated with the 
amount of air pressure applied to the muscles, but it is not dependent on the number of muscles. All else 
being equal, for a given inflation pressure, a strap with 3 muscles would bend by the same amount as a 
strap with only 1 muscle. However, the 3-muscle strap would be more powerful and capable of exerting 
more force than the equivalent 1 -muscle strap; this higher power and force capability comes at the 
expense of not only more muscles being needed but also slower actuation time, because three muscles 
require three times as much air as a single muscle to reach the same pneumatic pressure. 
 
 
Figure 6-3. Two 9-segment robotic straps with 3 muscles (top one) and 1 muscle (bottom one). 
In addition to actuators, the muscles also serve as constraints. The muscles are neither stretchable 
nor expandable beyond their unactuated length, thus a muscle that is not under pressure behaves 
analogously to how a rope would behave. Thus, a strap can b end very easily towards the side on which 
the “rope” is attached, but it cannot bend at all toward the opposite side because of tension in the “rope”. 
Moreover, the tumblers also restrict the strap from bending in any of the orthogonal directions. Therefore, 
the robotic strap has a unique anisotropic property of being easily bendable in one direction without any 
resistance, but being rigid and unbendable in any of the other three directions beyond its resting state. 
This bending anisotropy is demonstrated in Figure 6-4. 
 

211 
 
 
Figure 6-4. Demonstrating the compliance of a robotic strap that is not being actuated. (a) The strap bends freely 
towards the side of the muscle. (b,c) However, it resists bending in any other direction beyond its unactuated 
state, which in this case is a line. In image (b) the resistance to bending is due to the tension of the muscle, while 
in (c) the resistance to bending is due to the tumblers.  
While the unactuated or resting state of a strap is typically in the shape of a line (as has been the 
case in all the figures so far), it is not limited to just that shape. The resting shape is materially-programmed 
at the time of assembly when the muscles  are attached to the tumblers. The unpressurized muscle acts 
similarly to a non-stretchable rope, and thus when connected to the tumblers, the tension of the muscle 
keeps the strap in its preconfigured shape. Figure 6-5 shows how a robotic strap can be configured at the 
time of assembly to have various resting shapes and how those shapes are maintained because the muscle 
is attached differently in each case. Namely, when the muscle length between segments is nonuniform, 
the shape is nonlinear. This can be highly desirable for certain applications. For instance, with this 
approach, we can create a strap in which the ending segment is at any angle relative to the rest of the 
strap – even if the rest of the strap has the standard linear resting shape as in the case of Figure 6-5 (a). 
 
 
Figure 6-5. Examples of three different resting shapes for an idle robotic strap under no actuation. 
A strap whose non-actuated shape is line has an actuated shape that is a uniform curve. If the non-
actuated shape of the strap is not a line, then the actuated shape of the strap will also not be a uniform 
ark but rather a shape that is a modified version of the starting shape.  
 
6.2.2 Locking and Nonlocking Mounts 
We wanted to make the robotic strap suitable for mounting at both ends, at various different angles, 
and in both lockable and nonlockable way. Thus, we needed to develop a locking mounting mechanism 
that would constrain any rotation around the mounting axi s, and nonlocking mounting mechanism that 
enables stepwise rotation around the mounting axis, and the two mechanisms had to be compatible with 
each other. Moreover, this mechanism had to be designed in a way that is modular, rapid to assemble and 
disassemble, fully 3D printable, and not requiring any fasteners or other nonprintable components, so that 
it is highly user -friendly. To meet all these requirements, we followed an iterative design approach with 
many cycles of iteration, where each design cycle im proved upon the previous iteration. Ultimately, this 
process led to the two types of mounts shown on Figure 6-6, where each of them is compatible with the 
single type of tumbler that is also shown on the figure. 

212 
 
 
Figure 6-6. Locking mount, non-locking mount, and a tumbler that goes into the mount. 
The tumbler (third image) has 8 bumps on each side which hold it securely in place when inserted 
into either of the two mount types. The locking mount (first image) has channels into which the tumbler 
bumps slides. Once fully inserted, the tumbler can be locked into a position to prevent any rotational 
motion and also to keep it from sliding back out. Since the 8 bumps have circular symmetry and are evenly 
spaced, the tumbler can be inserted in 8 different angular orientations, namely 0, 45, 90, 135, 180, 2 25, 
270, and 315 degrees. The nonlocking mount (second image) features a structure that contains mountains 
and valleys into which the tumbler bumps fit. Each mountain has one shallow side and one steep side.  
This enables the tumbler to rotate in a stepwise fashion when turned in one way but to not be  able to 
rotate at all when turned the other way. The principle is like a ratchet – allowing stepwise rotation in only 
one direction but not the other. The locking mount is suitable for applications where the a ngle needs to 
remain fixed during the entire operation but can be changed before or after the operation, while the 
nonlocking mount is for applications where angular change is desirable in the middle of an operation.  
Each of the two mount types can be used for both wearable and nonwearable applications 
depending on the type of base on the bottom. The locking mount has the wearable GoPro base while 
the nonlocking mount has the nonwearable t-slotted base. Figure 6-7 shows several examples of 
wearable and nonwearable mounting of the robotic strap. 
 
 
Figure 6-7. Examples of the robotic strap being mounted (a) over and (b) below the forearm for wearable 
applications, and (c,d) onto an aluminum extrusion for nonwearable applications. 
6.2.3 Constraining Mounts 
The constraining mounts are used for constraining rotation between neighboring joints, and 
specifically to keep the constrained joints straight during actuation. One utility for these components is in 
applications where certain segments of the strap need to be immobilized and unaffected during actuation. 
The mounts can also be daisy chained; thus, any number of segments can be immobilized. Another 

213 
 
utilization of the constraining mounts is to keep the strap perfectly straight during the assembly process 
itself. A third is to enable an assembled strap to be mounted to its side, such as mounting it to a wall or a 
table. Such mounting approaches would be highly relevant for certain artistic applications and interactive 
demonstrations. Additionally, the constraining mounts can even be used to join two straps together at 
their ends at different angles, nevertheless, this should be done using the couplers instead because they 
offer greater strength and stability. Figure 6-8 shows several scenarios of the use of constraining mounts. 
 
 
Figure 6-8. Example uses for the constraining mounts. 
6.2.4 Couplers 
We needed a universal highly versatile mechanism to enable various modules, end-effectors, and 
robotic straps to be connected to the end of a robotic strap. This mechanism of interest had to meet 
multiple requirements, including: 
- Easy connection and disconnection within seconds. 
- Low force to make a connection; high force to sever a connection.  
- Strong lateral holding force of at least 100N when connected. 
- High tensile strength to resist breaking, bending, or damage.  
- Capable of thousands of connect / disconnect cycles before major wear and tear. 
- High shearing resistance that is on par with the shearing resistance of the strap itself. 
- Firm fit without any wiggling when connected. 
- Ability to connect modules at various angles along two planes of rotation. 
- Fully 3D printable with no fasteners or other non-printable parts. 
- Simple geometry suitable for printing on even a basic 3D printer. 
- Small and compact with a cross-section no greater than that of the strap.  
- Ergonomic for ease of grasping. 
 
Devising a mechanism that meets all the requirements involved many trials and errors until a 
suitable structure was identified; and afterward, this structure was refined through dozens of iterative 
design cycles. The final solution created was a square socket at the center of the tumbler and a 3D-printed 
clip that securely fits into the socket. Figure 6-9 shows how this clip and socket approach works. The clip 
slides into the socket and then latches when fully inserted as indicated in images (a) and (b). Some unique 
design features are indicated in part (c) of the figure. For instance, cutouts were made in to the top and 
bottom sides of the clipping mechanism for easy grasping, pulling, and pushing with just two fingers. The 
top cutout is offset from the bottom cutout to account for the optimal positions of the thumb and index 
finger for comfortability during grasping. The center of the clip was made thicker to serve as a stopper, 
allowing the insertion to be precise and to prevent any motion when snapped into position. Cutouts were 
also added to the stopper on top and bottom, and they also are optimized for comfort and function, hence 
the reason for the inverse symmetry between the top and bottom sides.  
 

214 
 
 
Figure 6-9. Clip and Socket coupling mechanism (a) before and (b) after assembly. (c) Side view of a straight clip. 
Based on the clip and socket coupling mechanism, a family of couplers was designed to enable 
two or more robotic straps to connect to each other at different angles and orientations. There are 2-way, 
3-way, and 4-way, and within each of those classes there are couplers at various different angles including  
Straight, Right-angled, Angled 45°, Twisted 45°.  
Furthermore, in addition to the male -to-male couplers which are for connecting robotic straps, 
there are also male -to-female couplers – Figure 6-10. The male -to-female variations were created 
primarily for the purpose of changing the connection angle of end-effectors. Without such couplers, end-
effectors can only be connected parallel to the strap.  
 
 
Figure 6-10. Male to Female Couplers: (a) straight, (b) 30 degrees, (c) 45 degrees, (d) 90 degrees. 
As a result of this diversity of couplers, there is a high degree of freedom for how components in 
the ModiStrap toolkit can be connected to one another, giving rise to many different possibilities of what 
can be done with ModiStrap. For instance, just with the 2-way Right-angled male-to-male coupler, there 
are 7 different possibilities for how two robotic straps can be connected to each other as depicted in Figure 
6-11. That’s because the square symmetry of the clip mechanism allows for a tumbler to be connected 
either horizontally or vertically to each clip. Moreover, the side on which the muscle is located also matters, 
because of the anisotropy of the strap. A simila r degree of diversity of possibilities is offered by each of 
the other couplers. 

215 
 
 
Figure 6-11. The four couplers and all nineteen connection possibilities they enable for joining two straps. 
 
 
 

216 
 
6.2.5 End-Effector Modules 
We designed a set of 5 end -effector modules that can connect to the end of the robotic strap with 
the same clip-based coupling mechanism discussed above. The set of end-effectors includes – suction cup, 
electromagnet, gripper, hook , and push/pull module. While the current modules are primarily for 
applications related to object manipulation, they also can enable other classes of applications and use 
cases including tangible interfaces, musical instruments, education, locomotion, and more. We chose  
these spe cific 5 end effectors because of their design simplicity, versatility, and actuation approaches 
compatible with FlowIO. However, many other end -effectors can also be added to the family, including 
more sophisticated ones such as tool-based end-effectors. The type of additional end-effectors that may 
be added in the future will be based on the deployment efforts of this project and on feature requests 
received from users (just as was the case with FlowIO when it was initially deployed). Nevertheless, the 
simplicity of the design, the versatility of the FlowIO actuation capabilities, and the open-source nature of 
this work also makes it possible for anyone with access to a 3D printer and a computer to quickly create a 
new type of end-effector by themselves if they need one for their specific use-case needs.  
In addition to connecting mechanically to the strap, the end-effectors also must connect electrically 
or pneumatically to the FlowIO device which actuates them. Both the mechanical connection for stability 
and the electrical / pneumatic connection for actuation are designed to be highly user-friendly and quick. 
Thus, a user can swap one type of end-effector with another within seconds.  
One limitation of the current design of the end -effector modules is that they can only be attached 
tangent to the strap. However, it is often desirable to attach an end effector at an angle, or to even be able 
to change the angle dynamically and programmati cally during operation. We leave static and dynamic 
angular changes of the end effectors for future work. One simple way of achieving this is by having several 
different versions of each end effector for different angles. Though a more elegant solution wo uld be to 
have one version that is capable of being locked at different angles. An alternative option is to make the 
end-effectors compatible with the couplers, such that rather than built-in clips they have built-in sockets.  
 
6.3 ModiStrap Characterization 
The robotic strap component of ModiStrap is a smaller-scale version of that strap described in the 
original paper; otherwise, the operating principle is the same. In ModiStrap, each tumbler has a width of 
32mm, a diameter of 20mm, and a mass of 5 grams. Thus, the length of a strap can be N*20mm where N 
is the number of tumbler segments in a strap. While N can be as large as needed, there is a practical limit 
to how long a strap can be, because after a certain length the strap will not be able to support its  own 
weight. Nevertheless, a strap can have at least several dozen segments, and the actual maximum length 
would depend on what end-effector is attached and what force the strap needs to apply.  
A fully assembled strap with 10 segments and 3 muscles weighs approximately 55 grams. Thus, the 
three muscles, 10 small zip ties, and the threads connecting all the segments together weigh only about 5 
grams. The end effectors have the following masses: Ho ok – 3.1g, suction cup – 4.9g, electromagnet – 
19.7g, push/pull solenoid – 9.7g, gripper – 8.5g. 
The force required to connect an end-effector module to the strap is approximately 0.6N, and the 
force to disconnect an end-effector is approximately 0.8N. Nevertheless, these parameters can be easily 
modified over a wide range by making small changes to t he designs. The higher the force requirements, 
the more heavy-duty applications the strap can be used for, but also the more difficulty it would be for 
users to swap modules. Conversely, having very low force requirements makes swapping modules 
effortless for users but also the range of applications is more limited. Currently, ModiStrap has been 
optimized for the latter, but if subsequent user studies and feedback indicate alternative preferences from 
users, we can increase the force needed to connect/disconnect an end -effector via minimal changes to 
the CAD files. We can also add a locking mechanism to prevent possible disconnection during operation.  
217 
 
6.4 ModiStrap Applications and Use-Cases 
ModiStrap enables the creation of numerous applications related to object manipulation, 
locomotion, shape-changing interfaces, assistive technology, art, interaction design, home automation, 
gaming, productivity, medicine, sustainability, and more. This se ction will explore some examples of 
applications that have been envisioned with the ModiStrap platform. 
 
6.4.1 Pick & Place Cranes 
The most straightforward and simplest -to-implement applications for ModiStrap are those that 
simply take advantage of the included end -effectors for their most straightforward use cases. The 
electromagnet, hook, gripper, and suction cup module can all be used for picking up objects to move them 
from one place to another. In this case, the robotic strap would be mounted to an aluminum extrusion 
that is grounded to a table or a floor. A single robotic strap would be able to move an object only along 
the path of an arc within the plane of bending. In some applications, such as an assembly line, this 
constrained motion along a specific curve may be highly desirable, because the path is “programmed” 
using the mechanics of the hardware itself, without requiring so phisticated software programming. In 
other applications, greater freedom of motion is more desirable, which is also possible by coupling two or 
more robotic straps – in which case the path of motion would no longer be limited to a single arc or even 
to a single plane but can be arbitrary in 3D space. Nevertheless, this would introduce higher software and 
algorithmic complexity for path planning.  
 
6.4.2 Wearable Manipulator 
 One or more robotic straps can be mounted to the forearm of a person and serve as an extra finger 
or even as an  extra hand that can grasp and manipulate objects or that can operate a toolhead. One 
instance where this could be valuable is in electronics soldering jobs where a third hand is often highly 
desirable, especially when soldering wires. Many research projects explored this type of application using 
different implementation approaches. For instance, Sang Leigh created a soft robotic ringer prosthetic 
augmentation kit for the hand [ 190] as well as a Morphology Extension Kit [ 191] serving as a small 
wearable robot that attaches to the forearm featuring various modules of different functions. 
 This type of utility is even more important in cases of assistive technology where one may have 
lost fully or partially their ability to use one’s own hand either due to amputation or a medical condition. 
Having a diverse set of end-effectors is especially important for such cases where a user can simply swap 
one tool head with another as needed for different everyday tasks that require object manipulation.  
 The robotic strap can also be mounted elsewhere on the body such as on the torso, on the head, 
or the leg. Various applications could be envisioned with each of those wearable mounting possibilities. 
The fact that a GoPro mounting clip is used makes this especially convenient as there are already many 
wearables on the market that are designed to accept a GoPro camera or anything else that uses the same 
mounting style – from helmets and wrist-guards to chest straps and armbands. 
 
6.4.3 Locomotion assisted by end-effectors 
When one end of the robotic strap is grounded and the other end has an end-effector connected 
to it, the application is typically object manipulation. But suppose both ends of the robotic strap have end-
effectors on them. In this case, the robotic strap can pull itself or push itself off a surface using those end 
effectors. A choreographed sequence of such pulling and pushing actions would enable the robotic strap 
to move spatially along a one-dimensional path.  
Additionally, if instead of a single strap there are two straps connected at some angle to each 
other, and if the free-ends of the straps have end-effectors connected to them, then actuation of the end 
218 
 
effectors combined with the bending of each strap can enable locomotion in two dimensions. Moreover, 
this can even enable actions such as wall climbing. One way to achieve this is by having two straps 
connected linearly to each other at their base points, and electromagnet end effectors at the free end of 
each strap. The following sequence of steps would then enable this 2-strap assembly to climb or descend 
a ferromagnetic wall such as refrigerator door or magnetic whiteboard: 1) energize one electromagnet to 
connect that end of the dual-strap assembly to the wall. 2) Actuate the strap assembly to bend it until the 
electromagnet on the other end meets the wall. 3) Deenergize the first electromagnet and deactuate the 
straps as well. This will cause the strap to move. 4) Actuate one or both straps of the two-strap-assembly 
until the first magnet contacts another spot on the wall. 5) Repeat this sequence 
There are numerous ways to achieve locomotion on a horizontal, inclined, or vertical surface by 
employing different end -effectors or combinations of end -effectors. The example above that was 
illustrated with electromagnets can also be achieved with the suction cup end-effector module, in which 
case there won’t even be a need for the surface to be ferromagnetic  but would have to be smooth . 
Moreover, if there is a particular surface type for which locomotion is desired, then a specialized end -
effector can be created that is optimized for motion along that surface. One example is moving inside a 
pipe, in which case an end-effector with an inflatable bladder can be used to scale the pile through friction 
holding. Another example is moving along an electrical cab le, in which case gripper end effectors can be 
used to allow the robot to move along the cable. 
Various prior works exist in the literature related to this type of end-effector assisted locomotion. 
For example, SkinBot [186186] is a miniature wearable robot that features a set of small suction cups that 
actuate in sequence to allow the robot to move on the human body. A similar project is Limpet II [192]. 
Numerous inchworm robots have also been developed over the years based on similar principles. Kyung-
min Lee introduced a miniature inchworm robot equipped with claws and driven by an electromagnetic 
oscillatory actuator; the claws serve as end-effectors, providing anchoring points that facilitate the robot's 
locomotion [193]. Jiang Ding presented a soft robot capable of omnidirectional steering by employing 
specialized end-effectors that adapt to different surfaces [194]. Another project is Complibot – a compliant 
external pipe climbing robot using end effectors that conform to pipe surfaces [195]. There are also papers 
that explore the principles of legged crawling in soft robotics, such as [ 196] analyzing the role of end -
effectors in locomotion. Relatedly, Gilbertson and Webster [197] introduced an approach for locomotion 
within tube-like environments by inflation and deflation of its actuators. 
 
6.4.4 Locomotion without end-effectors 
It is also possible to have locomotion even without any end effectors at all, simply by creating 
assemblies of two or more of the robotic straps and then actuating the straps in specific sequences. For 
example, one can create a rolling robot composed of th ree robotic straps connected to a T -slotted 
extrusion – two on one side and one on the other side centrally located. By actuating each side 
sequentially, this robot can move forward by rolling. Moreover, since one side contains a pair of 2 straps it 
is also possible to achieve turning left or right while rolling forward – simply by actuating one of the two 
straps in the pair slightly more relative to the other one.  
Numerous peer-reviewed papers exist in the literature that offer examples of soft robots that 
achieve locomotion without relying on end -effectors, utilizing coordinated actuation of compliant 
structures. Calisti et. al. provides a comprehensive overview of various locomotion strategies in soft 
robotics, discussing how soft robots can move without traditional appendages or end -effectors by 
exploiting their inherent flexibility and deformability [198]. Wen-Bo Li et. al. presented three rolling soft 
robot prototypes  that achieve high -speed locomotion through different rolling mechanisms, 
demonstrating how compliant structures actuated in sequence enable movement without traditional end-
effectors [199].  
219 
 
6.4.5 Artistic applications 
 Numerous interactive art applications are possible with ModiStrap. For example, one can create 
a clock with it. Various dynamic indicators can also be made with ModiStrap including representing 
information flow, representing anthropomorphic characteristics, showing facial expressions, and creating 
alternative forms of information displays.  
 
6.4.6 Object Augmentation 
 A strap can also be embedded into other objects to augment them with programmable 
properties. For instance, embedding ModiStrap into a pillow can allow the user to change that pillow’s 
shape. Another example is embedding it into a lighting fixture to programmatically control where the 
light is pointing. A similar utility is possible for orienting solar panels during the day so that they always 
point directly at the sun.  
 
6.4.7 Medical Applications 
The strap’s compliant property of easily bending inward but resisting bending outward can also be 
exploited in applications that require anisotropic dynamic stiffness control. This would be useful especially 
for partially immobilizing an extremity post fracture during recovery, where there may be some motions 
that are allowed but not others. This would serve as an alternative to current immobilization approaches 
used today that restrict motion in all directions,  even though only partial immobilization is me dically 
necessary in many cases.  
 
6.4.8 Platform Applications 
 One very practical application that can be built with ModiStrap is a Stewart Platform [200] based 
on robotic straps that tilt a planar surface when they are actuated. This would be an example of how one 
platform enables another platform to be created, which then itself enables another platform to be 
created, namely FlowIO → ModiStrap → Stewart Platform.  
 
6.4.9 Musical Applications 
By augmenting ModiStrap with sensors it is possible to create new forms of musical instruments 
with the toolkit. Some simple applications can also be created even without any additional 
augmentation, such as playing other musical instruments like piano or drums.  
 
6.4.10  Educational uses 
Replicating this work requires only a 3D printer, pneumatic muscles (S -MuscleTM)[201], zip-ties, 
thread, and a FlowIO device. And the 3D printable files will be made available for download as well on the 
SoftRobotics.IO website. This makes ModiStrap ideal for educational and classroom use cases – both by 
students and by instructors. Stude nts can try prototyping various creative applications with the existing 
components of ModiStrap. Moreover, students can easily create additional components for ModiStrap, 
come up with alternative designs for a clip connector, and/or create new kinds of end -effectors. For 
instance, in a design engineering course, an instructor can provide ModiStrap to a class of students and 
then ask each student or group to design some additional component for ModiStrap, enabling the students 
to learn and practice various design and project-management skills. Or, the students can be asked to come 
up with and implement a novel application with ModiStrap where that application requires some 
additional components to be designed and integrated with ModiStrap. This can also be used for learning 
about and practicing control theory. 
220 
 
 
6.4.11  Brainstorming tool 
Brainstorming ideas is much more effective when having a physical artifact that enables one to 
visualize and experience the idea being proposed. Not only is a physical artifact beneficial for 
communicating an idea, but it also helps the one who came up with the idea to think more creatively and 
iteratively when they see their idea materialized. ModiStrap can be used as a tool for rapid prototyping 
functional mockups aimed at addressing real problems that a company may be trying to solve. For 
instance, during a brainstorming session, people can use ModiStrap to demonstrate ideas they may have 
and to then inspire other participants in the session to build upon those ideas – not only metaphorically 
but in a literal sense by building their own prototype or augmenting an existing one on the spot.  
 
6.5 ModiStrap Future work 
This project is still in progress and a  substantial amount of work remains before it is ready for 
deployment or before it is in a state where it can be placed in the hands of end -users. The next most 
immediate steps would be to implement some, or all the applications proposed in the previous section. 
This process would help show visually through pictures and videos what ModiStrap can offer, and it would 
help inspire people to come up with even more creative application ideas. Additionally, in the process of 
implementing those applications, new hardware and software features may be added to the ModiStrap 
platform, and then those new features can give rise to even boarder classes of applications subsequently. 
Once all the technical work completes, the next step would be to conduct case -studies, where 
ModiStrap would be given to users for a limited time – which could be from a few hours to several days – 
and observe them how they are using the ModiStrap toolkit, what assemblies they are able to do with it, 
what difficulties they encounter, whether it helps them achieve particular application goals, whether it 
helps them learn new concepts or skills, and/or whether it helps them think more creatively.  
The final set of steps would be focusing on deployment, which would entail creating informational 
resource about ModiStrap including documentation, applications, project examples and tutorials, making 
it available for people to obtain it, creating a community around it, utilizing it in workshops and 
hackathons, and following all the other steps prescribed in the framework fro m Chapter 2, including 
fostering the feedback loops and symbiotic relationships that can help drive the further development  and 
evolution of this platform.  However, ModiStrap can be integrated within the existing SoftRobotics.IO 
ecosystem, leveraging all the resources and infrastructure part of this ecosystem – rather than having to 
build a separate ecosystem specifically for ModiStrap.

7: Research Projects & Collaborations Across Fields 
To date, hundreds of proposals have been received through the SoftRobotics.IO website from 
people requesting FlowIO for various use cases and applications, and many of those have been approved. 
The selection process was done on a case-by-case basis, with an emphasis on diversity across projects and 
diversity across backgrounds of the users. This chapter explores some of the major research projects in 
which I have been directly involved that were enabled by the FlowIO Platform, which have led to 
publications. It also features the initial project, Bubble, t hat led to the development of the platform 
revealing its origins. In total, 6 research projects are presented in this chapter: Bubble, OmniFiber, 
PneuBots, Touchibo, Peristaltic Suit, and Corsetto, which span the fields of Assistive technology, HCI, HRI, 
Soft Robotics, Interactive Arts, HRI, Space, and Performing Arts. The projects SleeveIO and ModiStrap could 
have also been included in this chapter, had they not been covered separately in their own respective 
chapters, thus adding Haptics and Robotics to the list of fields.  
While the projects presented in this chapter are ones where I’ve been directly involved in, the next 
chapter covers a much broader diversity of projects, applications, and alternative use cases of the FlowIO 
platform in which I have not been directly involved, but which serve as case studies of how other people 
have been using the platform, replicating it, and augmenting it for their needs. Thus, both this chapter and 
the next serve similar roles of demonstrating the impact across fields that was achieved by following the 
platform development & deployment framework. These case studies have also reciprocally helped inform 
and refine that framework as well.  A graphical representation of some of the projects made and areas 
impacted is shown on Figure 7-1.
