7.1 Bubble: Soft Wearable Grasping Augmentation Device 
 
 
Figure 7-2. The Bubble device worn on the forearm. A user places their hand over an object and the bubbles 
inflate around the object to grasp it without requiring bending of the fingers.  Demonstration of various objects 
being grasped with Bubble requiring only partial finger bending. 
7.1.1 Origins of Bubble and FlowIO 
Project Bubble holds an important role in this thesis, because it is the project that inspired and led to 
the development of version zero of the FlowIO Platform. The idea for Bubble emerged during an assistive 
technology hackathon back in 2018, and the project continued as a formal research project afterwards.  
Then in 2019, we published this work at CHI’19 [202]. The miniaturized wearable control system developed 
for Bubble generated strong interest from other researchers at the conference and from the soft robotics 
community at large. People began inquiring about using it in their projects, seeking details on how to 
replicate it, asking whether it could be used in specific projects they were working on, whether specific 
features would be added, and some researchers even requested to buy the control system.  
This unprecedented interest and demand for the Bubble control system made it viscerally apparent 
to me that I could multiply my impact by shifting my focus from developing application-specific projects 
towards developing and deploying versatile general-purpose development platforms of high value to other 
researchers. That’s because application-specific work typically leads to one  publication, but a general -
purpose platform can enable hundreds of projects, and then each project itself can lead to publication. 
Furthermore, I could also be a direct collaborator on many of those projects and co -author on the 
publications they lead to – provided that I also created appropriate deployment and incentive structures 
to align with this vision. This ultimately led to a multi-year journey of platform design and deployment that 
began by generalizing, miniaturizing, extending, and modularizing the control system I had developed for 
Bubble, turning it ultimately into what would later become the FlowIO Platform.  
 
7.1.2 Bubble Motivation 
Bubble is a pneumatically actuated wearable device that enables people with hand disabilities to use 
their own hands to grasp objects without fully bending their fingers. Bubble offers a novel approach to 
grasping, where slim, ultra-lightweight silicone actuators are attached to the fingers. When a user wishes 
to grasp an object, the silicone units inflate pneumatically to fill the available space around the object. The 
inflatable units are interchangeable, can be independently inflated, and can be position ed anywhere on 

225 
 
the fingers in any orientation, thereby enabling a wide variety of grasping gestures including palmar grasp, 
pinch, and more.  
The ability to grasp and manipulate objects is essential for humans because our civilization was 
designed around grasping. The basic objects in daily life —such as utensils, pens, and clothing —require 
grasping. However, grasping by default is not a necessity and most animals don’t even have that ability. 
However, it was society that has made grasping essential for people because of past and present design 
and engineering choices that have created a world suitable only for graspers and unsuitable for those who 
can’t grasp. Those who can’t grasp objects face major disadvantages  and struggle with everyday tasks, 
leading to a reduced quality of life or even inability to care for themselves.  
Researchers have explored various wearable assistive grasping solutions. One approach uses rigid 
hand exoskeletons, such as HandSOME [203] or HandEXOS [204], though such devices are heavy and 
complicated to wear. Assistive hand gloves are another solution, either tendon -driven such as Exo-glove 
[205] and SEM Glove [206], or pneumatically actuated [ 207, 208, 209] acting like grippers [ 210]. These 
approaches enable grasping by externally applying force to bend the fingers around the object. However, 
forceful bending of the fingers may be painful for people with certain conditions such as arthritis. 
Moreover, with such systems, smaller objects like pills are more difficult to grasp than larger ones and the 
supported grasping gestures are very few, often limited to palmer pinch and not supporting gestures like 
finger pinch. 
 
7.1.3 Bubble Overview 
Bubble offers a fundamentally different approach to grasping compared to prior works. Rather than 
bending the user’s fingers around an object, Bubble employs ultra -slim inflatable silicone chambers 
attached to the hand that expand dynamically to fill the space between the user fingers and the object 
being grasped. This enables the grasping of various-sized objects, does not require users to fully bend their 
fingers around the objects, and allows for a variety of grasping gestures based on how the inflatable units 
are positioned and orientated on the palm. Bubble consists of four main parts: 1) a set of soft inflation 
units that attach to the hand, made to attach (depending on object size) either to the fingers or the palm, 
2) a control unit powering the system and controlling the behavior of the pneumatic system,  3) an EMG 
sensor (Myo Armband), and 4) a computer working remotely for real-time data collection and processing.  
 
 
Figure 7-3. Block diagram of the Bubble system architecture; the Bubble system with all hardware components; 
an illustration depicting how the components are worn on the arm and hand. 

226 
 
To use Bubble, a user first attaches the EMG sensor to their forearm, and then the inflation units 
to the hand in the desired configuration. The control unit can be attached to the forearm using a rubber 
band or elastic wristband. Users decide which fingers to place units on, the number of units per finger, and 
the position and orientation of each unit. To grasp an object, the user approaches it with their hand and 
then contracts their forearm muscles to trigger the inflation of the chambers. The inflation continues while 
the muscles remain contracted or until a maximum inflation threshold is reached. Once the object is 
properly held, the user can stop the inflation by relaxing their forearm. To release the object, the user 
performs a strong flexion with the  wrist (moving the wrist downwards) which causes all chambers to 
deflate synchronously and the object to be dropped. 
For EMG sensing, the Myo Armband was used, featuring 8 stainless steel pads to collect 8 -channel 
EMG signals at 200Hz sampling rate. The data is sent to a computer via Bluetooth. Custom Python software 
was developed for data collection and signal classification . For EMG signal classification, a convolutional 
neural network was trained with two hidden layers containing 30 and 60 nodes, respectively. The training 
dataset consisted of 6,000 records of 8-channel EMG data for each target muscle movement. The data was 
preprocessed by first converting each data sample into its absolute value, followed by applying a moving 
average to each data channel using a window size of 5. The same preprocessing was applied to real-time 
data before feeding it into the trained model . A randomly chosen 20% of the dataset was reserved for 
validation, achieving a training detection accuracy of 94%. Classification was performed in real time. 
Hardware wise, Bubble used the Adafruit Feather nRF52 with integrated BLE capability. Firmware was 
developed to implement UART over BLE for communication between the controller and the computer. No 
noticeable delay was observed between muscle actuation and device actuation. A pneumatic circuit was 
built which included a micro pump and an array of eight modified two -way valves derived from 3 -port 
valves. Each valve measured 5.9mm in width and weighed 4.5g, enabling a lightweight and compact design 
that improved wearability. A block diagram of the system architecture is given in Figure 7-3.  
 
7.1.4 Fabrication of the Bubble Inflatable Modules 
The soft modules were fabricated using molding and casting process. A mix of Ecoflex 00-50 with SLO-
JO retarder was used to form a silicone material with the desired stiffness and elasticity for the modules. 
The molds were 3D printed from CAD files at a hi gh resolution to create silicone parts with maximally 
smooth faces. A base component and an elevated component were used to create the inflation chamber 
with a channel for airflow. After the uncured silicone was poured into the molds for these two parts an d 
allowed to cure, the products were sealed together using Sil-Poxy. Finally, the sealed structure was placed 
into a larger mold which was then filled with silicone to create an adjustable strap around the structure.  
 
 
Figure 7-4. Casting process for (a) the inflatable chamber and (b) the final wearable unit.  

227 
 
7.1.5 Bubble Evaluation 
To investigate the grasping capability of Bubble, a preliminary test with one participant was conducted 
to grasp objects of different shapes, sizes, and weights categorized into three groups. The participant used 
the device to grasp objects by first putting their hand on an object and then initiating th e inflation. Four 
inflatable units were used in the test, placed on the thumb, index, middle, and ring fingers. Changing the 
position and the orientation of each inflatable unit was allowed if the participant believed it was easier to 
grasp an object in th at way. Overall, the participant was able to grasp most objects (4 out of 5) in each 
category including pen, toothbrush, cup, bottle, ruler, box, and others. Objects that could not be grasped 
included a fork and a smartphone. Further details about the expe rimental procedure and the results are 
provided in the Bubble paper [202].  
One of the main limitations of Bubble is that the process of putting on and taking off the device is not 
user-friendly for a person with a disability, and it may require assistance from someone else to buckle and 
unbuckle the wearable modules. Another limitation is with the durability of the chambers since they are 
very thin and can pop if in contact with a sharp object or if too much pressure is applied by the user when 
they are grasping an object. Some of those limitations can be addressed by iterating on  the chamber 
design, trying different types of materials, and designing a mechanism that allows the chambers to attach 
on and detach off the user’s hand automatically thus eliminating the need for human assistance when 
putting on or taking off the system.  
 
7.1.6 From Bubble toward FlowIO 
The control unit depicted in Figure 7-3 is effectively version 0 of FlowIO, and it served as the starting 
point for building all future versions of the FlowIO Platform. From the picture of the control system, we 
see that there is a single pump to the left side of the valves, a microcontroller a t the back of the valves, 
and everything is mounted to a custom PCB with power electronics. Some of the early modifications made 
to the control system in the next several design iterations included changing the types of valves being used 
to 3V valves, miniaturizing the PCB even further to be no larger than the size of the microcontroller, placing 
the driver PCB on top of the valves and the microcontroller on top of the driver PCB, and moving the 
pumps. Then a 3D printed enclosure was made for it, and that’s how FlowIO version 1 was born. Afterward, 
the following 2 years were spent on writing a complete software stack for that device, making incremental 
modifications and improvements to the hardware, and creating an online community platform around it. 
 
 
Figure 7-5. Version 1 of FlowIO as a monolithic device. 
  

228 
 
7.2 OmniFiber: Strain Tunable Integrated Fluidic Fiber Actuators for 
Designing Soft Robotic Textiles and Movement-Based Interactions 
 
 
Figure 7-6. OmniFiber muscles and examples use cases for OmniFiber related to haptic feedback, skills training, 
interactive displays, toys, and robotic textiles. 
OmniFiber was the first major research collaboration to use the FlowIO Platform, and it was also the 
collaboration that most significantly influenced the evolution of FlowIO itself. OmniFiber was initiated by 
Ozgun Afsar who at the time was a visiting researcher at  MIT. Shortly after starting this project, Ozgun 
learned about the existence of the FlowIO Platform through her MIT colleagues, which at the time was 
still unpublished and in its infancy. This eventually led Ozgun to send a request for FlowIO, and  
subsequently to me joining the OmniFiber project as a main collaborator. My involvement with OmniFiber 
was as a system designer responsible for creating the hardware and software for robotic fiber sensing and 
control. At the start of this project, FlowIO still lacked many of the features required for OmniFiber. Many 
of the modules comprising FlowIO today were not present at the time and were developed specifically to 
address the needs of the OmniFiber project initially, and then subsequently made more general-purpose. 
Similarly, many of the FlowIO software features today were also added because of OmniFiber’s needs. 
Thus, while FlowIO enabled a large part of the OmniFiber project, the OmniFiber project also gave rise to 
a large part of the FlowIO Platform’s hardware and software. OmniFiber was published at UIST’21 [211]. 
 
7.2.1 OmniFiber Motivation and Overview 
OmniFiber is a technology that enables the design of tangible interactions with thin robotic fluidic fibers 
capable of actuation, sensing, and closed-loop control. OmniFiber features ultra-thin (0.6mm to 1.8mm in 
diameter) fluidic artificial muscles with sensing capabilities and reconfigurable actuation behaviors. The 
outer layer of each muscle is a mechanical constraint responsible for defining the behavior of the muscle 
under actuation. Thereby, various kinds of actuation behaviors can be “mechanically programmed” 
through changes in the constraint. Additionally, OmniFiber includes hardware and software control suite 
based on FlowIO, providing tools for actuation and closed -loop control with the robotic fibers, making it 
possible for artists, designers, and technologists to create dynamic tangible interactions with OmniFiber.  
OmniFiber explores how thinness and sensory capability of artificial muscle-based devices can be utilized 
by designers to seamlessly weave them into everyday tangible interactions, such as haptics-mediated skills 
training or actuated tangibles for remote interpersonal communication. 
 
7.2.2 OmniFiber Structure and Behavior 
The muscles of OmniFiber have a multilayered design, depicted in Figure 7-7, comprising of an 
elastomeric microtubule that can be pressurized with air, an integrated soft resistive sensor traced on the 
microtubule, an interlocking outer mesh, and an inextensible mechanical constraint to program the fiber’s 
behavior under actuation.  The integrated soft sensor allows the fiber to capture strain and simple bending 

229 
 
behavior. Two types of fibers can be designed. (1) Contractible-type fibers contract when pressurized, 
exhibiting high force output with low geometric deformation, thus making them more suitable for high -
force applications such as movement assistance, kinesthetic haptics, or rehabilitation. (2) Extensible-type 
fibers elongate when pressurized, exhibiting high geometric deformation with low force output, making 
them suitable for applications such as soft wearable displays, tactile haptics, toys, and expressive robotic 
art. The mechanical constraint on the outer layer enables diverse morphing behaviors, beyond simple axial 
motions, to be achieved with the fibers including bending, coiling, and texturization ( Figure 7-7). These 
five primitive behaviors depicted in the figure can also be combined, thus enabling an OmniFiber actuator 
to exhibit a highly complex compound behavior.  
 
 
Figure 7-7. OmniFiber structure and a set of primitive behaviors possible with the fibers under actuation, 
including (a) contraction, (b) extension, (c) bending, (d) coiling, and (e) texturization. 
The basic motion of the fibers can be mechanically programmed to bend at a certain angle or to 
fit arbitrary linear shapes. Mechanical constraints are added via either (1) an inlaid inelastic thread on the 
outer mesh or (2) a 3D-printed flexible constraint placed on the actuator - Figure 7-8. The two methods 
of mechanically programming the muscles are not mutually exclusive and can be combined, thus making 
it possible to have different behavior not only in different sections of the muscle but even in the same 
section of the muscle. The last i mage in Figure 7-8 is an example of a muscle, programmed through a 
combination of both methods, to take the shape of a heart when actuated.  
 
 
Figure 7-8. (a) A muscle with a strain -limiting inextensible thread inserted parallel to the braided mesh axis for 
mechanical constraint. (b) A muscle with 3D -printed mechanical constraints. (a) A muscle with both, embedded 
inextensible thread and two 3D -printed mechanical constraints, programmed to morph into the shape o f a heart 
when it undergoes pneumatic actuation. 
The two types of mechanical constraints enable a wide space of design possibilities, which is explored 
extensively in the OmniFiber paper [211]. The paper also covers morphing properties, haptic properties, 
fiber structuring techniques, and modular compositions involving multiple muscles each programmed to 

230 
 
exhibit different behaviors and play different roles. Detailed information about the fabrication pipeline of 
the robotic fibers and how each layer is made can also be found in the paper. 
 
7.2.3 OmniFiber System Architecture and use of FlowIO 
The complete system for developing user experiences with OmniFiber includes: 
• Flexible and stretchable fiber-based modular interface,  
• 16-channel analog strain-sensor driver circuit (FlowIO AnalogIn Expansion Module) 
• FlowIO device for open-loop and closed-loop control (FlowIO Controller + XL Pump module) 
• Web-based graphical control dashboard (FlowIO web-GUI),  
• software stack including Arduino and JavaScript libraries and APIs (FlowIO software stack) 
 
 
Figure 7-9. OmniFiber system architecture for closed-loop control.  
An extra-large (XL) pump module for FlowIO with D2028 pumps in series was made to provide high 
pressures and flowrates needed to actuate multiple 1-meter-long fibers. The GUI simultaneously connects 
to multiple FlowIO devices and offers bidirectional data-flow. Each fiber contains 4 analog strain sensors. 
We needed closed -loop control of at least four fibers to enable a sufficiently large enough range of 
applications for the OmniFiber system. Therefore, a sensor input module with 16 analog channels and a 
corresponding software driver for the module were developed. The FlowIO web app is used to receive the 
multichannel strain data from the 16 -pin AnalogIn module based on tangible deformation input by an 
OmniFiber muscle. The data is processed in real time in the web browser and used to control the pressure 
output of the same FlowIO device or another paired FlowIO device. This enables a closed -loop control in 
the same system, or the input from one system to control the output of another system. 
 
7.2.4 OmniFiber Applications 
OmniFiber-based devices can be utilized to develop many novel user interactions. Some examples 
explored included augmenting natural finger movements  when playing an instrument,  recording and 
replaying the respiratory physiology of a classically trained singer, and offering breathing guidance during 
singing or meditation. Other applications explored included line-based interfaces, interactive cables, soft 
on-body displays for sending visual-haptic messages, and robotic jewelry that adapts to its owner's body 
movements. Many textile -based applications were also made with OmniFiber including inlay -knitted 
contractible-bending swatch, stiffness-changing collar, inlay-knitted extensible swatch, shape-locking fiber, 
contractible twisting swatch, and 3D knitted quasi-spacer for out-of-plane feedback.  Some of these and 
other applications are pictured in Figure 7-6 and Figure 7-10, and they are discussed more extensively in 
the OmniFiber paper [211].  

231 
 
 
 
Figure 7-10. OmniFiber in use: (a) An assistive exoskeleton for piano learning. (b) Patchbot. (c) A woven swatch 
with OmniFiber. (d) Finger-worn kinetic jewelry. (e) 30-meter OmniFiber spool. (f) Interactive corset inlay-knitted 
with OmniFiber. (g) Synchronized spine tensegrities with active OmniFiber tendons. (h) FlowIO hardware. (i) 
Wearable sign language. (j) Heart-shaped dynamic ring. 
7.2.5 New FlowIO Features added due to OmniFiber 
The OmniFiber project pushed the FlowIO Platform to new heights in terms of its capabilities and 
motivated the process of making it even more general purpose. For the OmniFiber project, the FlowIO 
Platform was significantly extended and augmented with new hardware and software features to meet 
certain requirements previously outside of its reach. Those included: (1) yielding high pressures up to 
350kPa, (2) offering 16 analog input channels, (3) providing software features for closed-loop control, (4) 
allowing a user to create and execute a sequence of tasks through the graphical user interface, and (5) 
ability for bidirectional communication between two FlowIO devices. 
To meet the higher-pressure requirements, several modifications and improvements were made to 
the pump driver boards to be capable of driving higher -pressure pumps operating up to 18 volts. Before 
this change, the operating voltage limit for the pump module driver was 6V. This then made it possible for 
users to create by themselves an ever-greater diversity of pump modules for FlowIO. For instance, Ozgun 
created an extra-large pump module for FlowIO, for driving OmniFiber, simply by swapping the types of 
pumps being used. The other significant hardware addition to FlowIO resulting from OmniFiber is the 16-
pin Analog Input expansion module. This module was made specifically due to the need for closed -loop 
control with 4 OmniFiber muscles since each muscle has four embedded strain sensors – hence the need 
for 16 analog input channels. Before this addition, only three analog input sensors could be connected to 
FlowIO.  
Numerous software feature that are currently part of FlowIO also have their origin in the OmniFiber 
project. The most notable of those is the task scheduler which is now an integral part of the FlowIO 
graphical user interface. Other major software features added to FlowIO to meet the needs of OmniFiber 
include functions in the Arduino libraries for closed-loop control, the ability to connect multiple devices to 
the web-GUI, and the ability for bidirectional communication between FlowIO devices. There were a lso 
dozens of minor modifications and improvements made to the FlowIO software stack and graphical user 
interface to make the software more robust and more user-friendly.   
  

232 
 
7.3 PneuBots: Soft Robotics Construction Kit for Playful Exploration 
PneuBots is a modular construction  starter kit for the playful exploration of soft robotics  using 
actuators made of sheet materials. The kit consists of seven types of self -foldable segments with high 
tensile strength, three types of pneumatic connectors and splitters, and a custom -designed box. The kit 
enables various assemblies to be made with modular pieces, allowing creators to explore the world of soft 
robotics in playful ways. When combined with a FlowIO device, PneuBots allows seamless programmability 
of the different assemblies, enabling artists, designers, engineers, and makers to create dynamic, shape-
changing, and interactive works that can be used for education, storytelling, dynamic art, and more. The 
project was developed during the COVID pandemic and published at the CHI’22 design research 
competition in a joint paper with Hye Jun Youn as the main author and me as co-author [212]. 
 
 
Figure 7-11. The PneuBots kit features (a) 7 inflatable modules, (b) 3 types of pneumatic connectors, and (c) a 
custom-designed package. Some of the dynamic assemblies made possible with PneuBots, in combination with 
the FlowIO Platform, include: (d) rolling robot, (e, f) various artistic designs, (g) self-assembled ball, (h) haptic 
bracelet, and (i) interactive flower. 
7.3.1 Motivation for PneuBots 
Soft robotics is an emerging and rapidly accelerating field with a high impact potential and of strong 
interest to the Human-Computer Interaction (HCI) and Haptic communities [213,214]. Yet the barriers to 
entry for most researchers and makers are still very high, and those barriers are even greater for people 
who come from nontechnical backgrounds. Learning about soft robotics fabrication techniques became 
even more difficult in the w ake of the COVID -19 pandemic. That’s because most fabrication and 
prototyping processes necessitate expensive materials that are not readily available in most countries, 
space for molding and casting, computer -aided design (CAD) skills, and access to equip ment such as 3D 
printers. Additionally, the actuation and sensing of soft robots require advanced skills in pneumatics, 
engineering, and programming. Websites such as softroboticstoolkit.com, opensoftmachines.com, and 
softrobotics.io offer a variety of res ources related to soft robotics research and prototyping. However, 
while such online resources offer a great starting point, reading articles and watching videos alone does 
not allow people to develop intuition about soft robots and to use the creative learning process of learning 
through doing and playing. A constructive assembly kit in combination with carefully curated informational 
resources could significantly reduce the barrier to entry and allow people to easily get started with soft 
robotics, prototype basic interactions, develop intuition about the behavior and control of such robots, 
and be inspired and motivated to go beyond the offering of a kit. Currently, there are only a few examples 
of such kits related to this field, and most of them are onl y research prototypes, not readily available to 

233 
 
people. Chao Zhang et.al. [215] wrote a comprehensive review paper in 2020 on modular soft robots that 
also lists nearly all known soft robotics construction kits up to that time. Two examples of reconfigurable 
soft robotic kits include InflatiBits [216] and Soft LEGO [217]. 
PneuBots is a novel soft robotics construction starter kit made from durable, low -cost inflatable 
materials. PneuBots includes a variety of building blocks with different actuation behaviors and comes 
with a FlowIO Platform [ 218] that allows even novice users without any programming or electronics 
background to create soft robots with dynamic interactions and bring their creations to life. Moreover, this 
physical prototyping toolkit even allows users to create basic projects such as a hugging robot, an affective 
device that metaphorically expresses emotion through a rhythmic pattern, interactive flowers that can 
serve as gifts for loved ones, or ghostly presence interfaces for those who are far away or are isolated in 
quarantine. Additionally, the PneuBots starter kit can also be used for numerous educational, storytelling, 
or artistic purposes. 
 
7.3.2 PneuBots Self-Folding Design 
The PneuBots kit contains modular self -folding blocks that actuate when air is pumped in . The 
structures were designed and fabricated using a modified version of the process  found in [219] utilizing 
PVC (Polyvinyl Chloride) or TPU (Thermoplastic Polyurethane) sheets and a heat sealer to create hinges 
that fold under inflation. Figure 7-12 shows the four hinge patterns that were created (Linear, Diagonal, 
Narrow Diamond, and Wide Diamond)  to achieve four different bending behaviors (Contraction, Twist, 
Fold, and Half-Fold). The linear and diagonal patterns feature a 5mm gap between each seam line, which 
causes axial contraction during inflation. In addition to the four primitive shapes, three additional 
inflatable PneuBots modules were created – one that combines multiple hinges and two that serve simply 
as a base to which the other modules can be attached. The complete set of pneumatic modules is shown 
in Figure 7-13. Each of the modules can be connected to one or more identical or different modules, thus 
enabling a diverse range of configurations, structures, and applications to be explored seamlessly and 
playfully. Each modular design features small, standardized units that can be independently combined in 
various configurations to create different forms and provide multiple functions. 
 
 
Figure 7-12. Four hinge patterns used in PneuBots and their corresponding action under pneumatic actuation. 
 
 

234 
 
 
Figure 7-13. PneuBots features seven modules including (a) axial contraction, (b) helical twist, (c) bending along 
a single joint, (d) partial bending, (e) bending at two joints, and (f, g) radial expansion modules serving as bases 
connectors for the other modules. 
7.3.3 PneuBots Fabrication Process 
The modules of PneuBots were developed by a heat-pressing fabrication process, where two layers 
of PVC film were sandwiched together and then sealed along specific origami-inspired patterns. The typical 
heat pressing fabrication method is to first laser cut a geometric pattern on a paper, then manually stamp 
it on a semi-flexible film using a heat press. This isn’t convenient because the method creates undesired 
wrinkles, holes, and irregular linear shapes during the manual heat pressing. Therefore, in our mold design, 
we used a 1/2-inch-thick bronze block etched using a CNC mill to a 1/8-inch depth to create metallic molds. 
Then, PVC film was pressed for 5 seconds at 260°C onto the bronze mold. The CNC -milling plus heat -
pressing fabrication method achieves an accurate and smooth geometric pattern without wrinkles. In 
addition, the bronze molds allow consistent patterns suitable for mass production. The versatility of PVC 
film and the smooth finishing of the seam makes the modules resistant to bursting from overinflation. 
 
7.3.4 PneuBots Connectors 
The PneuBots module has one or more ports for inflation / deflation, comprised of so -called “oral 
inflation ports” commonly found on beach balls and swimming tubes. The oral inflation ports provide not 
only holes for air to flow but also structural stability which can be exploited to allow a pair of ports to 
connect to each other using a tubular structure that fits i nside the two ports. This is exactly the method 
we exploit to allow PneuBots modules to connect together. By using Straight, T-Shaped, and Cross-Shaped 
tubular connectors, we can connect two, three, or four modules, respectively; or one side can be left open 
for attaching a FlowIO device to it. Moreover, because of the structural offering of the oral inflation ports, 
some of our modules feature more than  one port, so that there are multiple locations possible where 
modules can connect or allow for separation between ports for actuation and ports for connecting to other 
modules.  
The oral inflation ports have a built-in one-way valve. This is desirable because it allows inflation and 
prevents air from escaping, but that desirable feature becomes a problem when deflation is needed. 
However, when the membrane of a built -in valve is p ressed, then deflation becomes possible. We 
engineered our tubular connectors in a way that achieves exactly this outcome, where the one-way valve 
is disabled and air can flow freely when a tubular connector is connected to the port. This means that 
when two modules are connected to one another via our connectors, there is no barrier for the air to flow 
between the two modules because the one-way valves are disabled. For this reason, it’s also required that 
our tubular connector be used even when a module i s connected to just a FlowIO device for actuation, 

235 
 
because otherwise only inflation and no deflation would be possible if FlowIO is connected directly to the 
port without a tubular connector. Another consequence of this is that any structure built with PneuBots 
can stay inflated or pressurized when a connector is disconnected. Even the individual modules themselves 
can remain pressurized when they are disconnected from other modules and when the tubular connectors 
are removed. 
 
 
Figure 7-14. Three types of connectors for PneuBots – Straight, T-Shaped, and Cross-Shaped. 
7.3.5 PneuBots Applications 
The construction kit was distributed to graduate students from backgrounds in architecture, 
engineering, design, and education who were then tasked with experimenting and brainstorming possible 
uses. For most users, this was their first exposure to soft ro botics, and through it, they learned about 
pneumatic control, hinge patterns, and heat sealing. The students were able to create playful projects 
including a rolling robot, a wearable armband, a hugging robot, and several other artistic creations – Figure 
7-15. The students used the five pneumatic ports on FlowIO and its graphical user interface to generate 
various motion patterns by controlling individual modules or combinations of modules.  
 
 
Figure 7-15. Applications created using PneuBots: a) artistic work, b) underwater rolling robot, c) garden 
metaphor, d) gripper, e) hugging toy, f) interactive flower, g) lifting robot, and h) haptic bracelet. 
 
  

236 
 
7.4 Touchibo: Multimodal Texture-Changing Robotic Platform for 
Shared Human Experiences 
 
 
Figure 7-16. Two form factors of the Touchibo robot, including Touchibo Turtle (a, b) and Touchibo Tree (c, d). 
Touchibo is a collaborative project between Cornell University and the University of Lisbon and is part 
of a larger research theme in human -robot interaction exploring how robots can aid in the education of 
children with visual impairments. Touchibo uses the FlowIO platform to enable all the behaviors of the 
robot. My involvement in this project  includes contributions to the system design, coining the project 
name “Touchibo”, and helping with paper writing. Touchibo was first published in a demo paper at UIST’22 
[220]. A second paper featuring Touchibo was published at HRI’24 [221]. I am a co-author only on the first, 
which focuses more on the system itself to which I contributed. The second paper was about studies 
conducted with Touchibo to which I did not contribute directly, and thus I am not a coauthor on that one. 
 
7.4.1 Touchibo Motivation and Overview 
Touchibo is a modular robotic platform for enriching interpersonal communication in human -robot 
group activities, suitable for children with mixed visual abilities. Touchibo enriches children’s interactions 
by generating physical sensations using texture -changing skins, scents, and lights. Two variations of the 
robot were created — Turtle and Tree — for supporting storytelling activities and mediating group 
conversations between children with and without visual impairment. The goal of this project is to provide 
an inclusive platform for children to interact with each other, perceive their emotions, and become more 
aware of how they impact others. 
Similar to how humans convey their emotions in various ways including gestures, heartbeat, and 
sweat, robots could also display emotions more diversely through physical expressions [ 222,223], 
including through auditory, olfactory, and tactile modalities [224]. In this way, robots also become more 
accessible and easily perceived by people of different ages or various sensory abilities, especially in mixed-
visual ability groups where non-visual feedback is of utmost importance [225,226]. 
While texture change for emotional expression has previously been explored without a robotic 
embodiment [ 227,228], integrating texture -changing modules onto a social robot in a real -world 
application remains mostly unexplored. This work proposes a design space for robots’ emotional 
expressions using multiple modalities, where participants can share multisensory experiences regardless 
of their visual abilities. To demonstrate the flexibility of Touchibo in different contexts two versions were 
created: Touchibo Turtle for storytelling activities and Touchibo Tree for mediating group conversations. 
 
7.4.2 Touchibo Design and Implementation 
Touchibo was developed through a co-design process that involved engaging with four psychologists, 
two teachers, and one blind teenager. The primary objective in developing Touchibo was to have a robot 
that can enhance group activities equally perceptible by children with and without visual impairments. 
 

237 
 
Touchibo integrates dynamic texture-changing skin to enrich the tactile sensation and expressiveness 
of the robot. Each soft -skin module comprises a silicone body and air cavity that can be pneumatically 
controlled via a FlowIO device to generate surface texture changes [229]. Five bio-inspired texture shapes 
were designed (Figure 7-17):  
Wrinkles - to generate haptic tension under vacuum, expressing confusion and concentration.  
Goosebumps – to express excitement,  
Spikes – to display anger and defense with sharp haptic tips,  
Stomata – to simulate breathing of the robot by opening and closing the "pores", and finally  
Tentacles – inspired by sea anemones, to express a sense of liveliness in a robot.  
 
 
Figure 7-17. Five bio-inspired texture-changing skins for Touchibo.  
Each of the textures consists of multiple air chambers and channels for a high degree of 
expressivity even with just a single skin texture. And when multiple skin types are used together, the 
degree of expressivity rises exponentially with each additional skin texture. Figure 7-18 presents some of 
the different surface dynamics possible with the Goosebumps skin via 3 -channel independent control. 
Additional dimensions of expressivity are possible by changing the actuation frequency and amplitude for 
each skill and even for each channel within a particular skin.  
 
 
Figure 7-18. Example surface dynamics of the Goosebumps skin with 3 pneumatic control channels. 
Two sets of the five types of skins were created - one set with rectangular and triangular skinks for 
Touchibo Turtle, and the other set with circular skins for Touchibo Tree. The skins in each set were designed 
to be interchangeable and swappable. For instance, Touchibo Tree has three branches, each capable of 
accepting any of the five skin types. 
In addition to dynamic texture -changing skins, two other forms of expressivity were added to 
Touchibo. (1) Color -changing LED rings were embedded underneath the skin units, exploiting the 
translucency of the skins allowing light to penetrate through them. (2) A scent box was added for smell 
manipulation which contains four mini fans with different pieces of scented cotton in front of each fan. 
The four scents included are campfire, rain, grass, and dust. When a fan is turned on, the wind blows air 
through scented cotton, spreading the corresponding scent around the robot.  
 
 

238 
 
7.4.3 Use of FlowIO in Touchibo 
A FlowIO device with Large Pump module was used to control the dynamic texture movements. In 
the case of Touchibo Turtle, FlowIO was embedded into the body of the robot, whereas for Touchibo Tree, 
FlowIO was connected to the robot externally. Both the Web -GUI and the Arduino libraries were used 
during this project. The Web -GUI served as a testing tool during the development phase of the project. 
Whereas for the creation of specific user experiences and user studies with Touchibo, the FlowIO Arduino 
libraries were used. Five pneumatic channels were controlled by FlowIO to perform inflation, deflation, 
vacuum, and hold. The integrated pressure sensor of FlowIO was also utilized for feedback control. By 
dynamically controlling the pressure, flow rate, and frequency, various textural dynamics were achieved.  
Additionally, a consideration was made whether the light and scent control should also be 
performed by FlowIO. However, those two subsystems were already previously developed, and, for the 
sake of time, a decision was made to keep the preexisting LED and olfaction control hardware and software 
rather than redoing them using FlowIO’s hardware and software. 
 
 
Figure 7-19. Touchibo Tree and Touchibo Turtle side-by-side. A FlowIO device is seen connected externally to the 
Touchibo Tree and being programmed from a laptop. 
7.4.4 Touchibo Applications and User Studies 
Touchibo Turtle was primarily designed to be a storyteller that enacts different physical sensations of 
characters such as happiness, sadness, fear, or anger. Its size allows four children’s hands to play with it 
physically and socially. The use of audio, textures, wind, lights, and scents enables immersive experiences.  
In one storytelling scenario, the robot is hidden inside a cave made of fabric with sleeves, so children don’t 
have visual access to the robot but can interact with it through touch, audio, and olfaction. The background 
music sets the scene and overall mood; lights that change color make the scene dynamic and promote 
children’s curiosity; texture-changing skins convey different feelings; and wind enacts the breathing flow. 
Touchibo Tree was built primarily to serve as a conversation companion. It uses textures and lights 
to convey fear during aggressive conversations, boredom during long monotone monologues, and other 
emotions such as excitement, surprise, and curiosity – so that childre n can learn the impact of their 
behavior on others through the robot’s response. In one demo explored, the robot displays boredom or 
curiosity in response to speaking time, encouraging balanced participation. In another demo scenario, the 
robot expresses fear with intense texture changes in reaction to high-volume speech, promoting respectful 
conversations. 
Formal studies on using Touchibo for educational research were conducted at a later time as a 
separate research project in which I was involved. That research was published in the second paper 
featuring Touchibo at the HRI’24 conference [221].  

239 
 
7.5 Peristaltic Suit: Active bioelectronic intra-vehicular spacesuit for 
microgravity adaptation and cardiovascular deconditioning. 
 
 
Figure 7-20. Images of the Peristaltic Suit worn during a zero-gravity flight, components of the suit, and an 
active-compression sleeve with five bladders worn on the calf. 
This project was initiated by Irmandy Wicacsono, a colleague from our research group at MIT highly 
familiar with the FlowIO platform, and who found FlowIO highly suitable not only for the pneumatic needs 
but also for other system-related needs of Peristaltic Suit, which will be abbreviated as PS Suit. The highly 
versatile microcontroller onboard FlowIO, the many kinds of sensors integrated into the device including 
IMU, and the unique software stack that enables wireless control from a web browser – made FlowIO in 
ideal control system to be used during a zero-gravity flight. PS Suit was the only project to date in which 
FlowIO was even more valuable for its hardware sensing and software communication capabilities than 
for its pneumatic actuation capabilities. This was also the only project to date to have used a FlowIO device 
in a zero-gravity environment. The project was a collaborative effort with more than six people involved in 
it, and my contributions were to the control system and the communication prot ocol.  This project was 
first published in ASCEND’22 [230] and subsequently also in ICES’23 [231]. The first paper focused on the 
design and working principle of the suit, while the second paper covered the development and user testing 
of the suit in a microgravity environment during a ZeroG flight. The project was also discussed in a 2023 
IEEE Pervasive Computing paper with the theme of pervasive computing in space [232]. 
 
7.5.1 PS Suit Overview  
Peristaltic Suit is a wearable telemedicine and real -time intervention system for cardiovascular 
deconditioning and microgravity adaptation. The suit can simultaneously perform (1) multimodal physical 
and physiological sensing and (2) spatiotemporal and peristaltic pressu re exertion – both distributed 
throughout the body – to normalize cardiovascular flow. PS Suit  integrates wireless biosensing and 
pneumatic actuation systems to provide a closed -loop response to the medical needs of wearers. The 
sensing and actuation capabilities of the suit also enable researchers to study the direct influence of active 
dynamic compression on the human body. 
 
7.5.2 PS Suit Motivation and Utility 
Prolonged exposure to microgravity induces acute health risks, including muscular, vestibular, and 
cardiovascular dysfunctions [233]. Without the influence of gravity, hydrostatic pressure gradients in the 
body disappear, and blood distribution shifts from a person’s legs toward their upper body. This can lead 
to complications such as thrombosis and blood clots [234].  Therefore, astronauts and space visitors must 
have continuous medical monitoring and active intervention throughout their journey in microgravity and 
immediately after their return to Earth. Exercising machines, compression systems, and passive skinsuits 
have been used as countermeasures. However, they cannot adapt to physiological and physical changes in 
the body and cannot apply dynamically controllable pressure throughout the body [235].  

240 
 
Peristaltic Suit was developed to provide remote diagnostics for astronauts, better regulation of 
cardiovascular health, physical training augmentation through active muscle compression, and kinesthetic 
haptic feedback, as well as to monitor and prevent other microgravity  adaptation health risks such as 
osteoporosis, skeletal muscle atrophy, spatial disorientation, and motion sickness. The design and control 
framework of the PS Suit is also suitable for other environments and applications where health monitoring 
and intervention are important, including in sports, construction, rehabilitation, diving, telemedicine, 
hypergravity environments, mountaineering, and various high-risk field jobs.  
 
7.5.3 PS Suit Implementation 
The Peristaltic Suit embodies soft, textile-based actuators and sensors into an intravehicular activity 
garment with miniaturized and wireless sensing-actuation control hardware. It consists of an array of air 
pouches that can be individually controlled for dynamic pressure or compression gradients across the body 
through a wearable, compact, and lightweight pneumatic control system. It is also equipped with multiple 
wireless skin -contact compression pressure sensors, inertial -measurement units, and physiological 
sensors that constantly measure heart rate, respiration, blood pulse transit time, temperature, and blood 
oxygenation level of the wearer. Incorporating both sensing and actuation systems enables closed -loop 
intervention. The design and fabrication processes are detailed in the published papers [230,231]. 
Active compression sleeves for the calves and the arms were developed by embedding five 
inflatable pouches into a customized textile structure. The inflatable bladders were fabricated by thermally 
welding two layers of TPU film with an impulse sealer. A tw o-terminal textile pressure sensor for 
quantifying the compression force was developed by sandwiching a piezoresistive knit fabric between two 
conductive knit fabrics and connecting TPU -insulated conductive threads. Each pouch is connected to a 
port on a F lowIO device. The airflow can be programmed to provide controlled compression where the 
chambers are inflated and deflated in a sequence that mimics peristalsis movement.  
 
 
Figure 7-21. Illustration of the active compression sleeve constituents and layers. Demonstration of a peristaltic 
dynamic compression movement ankle-to-knee. 
The final prototype of the suit is illustrated in Figure 7-22. While the vision is to have many 
chambers fully distributed across the body, the current prototype only has five chambers distributed from 
the lower abdomen to the ankle. The prototype also includes a physiological activity sensing system and 
a FlowIO pneumatic actuation system. The PS Suit's outer layer is adapted from a high-performance Nomex 
racing suit (Profox) with a combination of knitted Nomex around the joints and a double-layer quilt woven 
and tailored based on the wearer’s body circumferences. Nomex, found in the outermost layer of 
spacesuits, is a high-performance fiber with excellent temperature and mechanical resistance, serving to 
ensure protection, mobility, and comfortability for the wearer. 
 

241 
 
 
Figure 7-22. Peristaltic Suit concept and the corresponding working prototype. 
 
7.5.4 PS Suit System Architecture 
 
 
Figure 7-23. System Architecture for PS Suit. 
The physiological and activity sensing module, indicated in orange in Figure 7-23, comprises a set of 
skin-contact physiological sensors with their central processing system (Feather Sense nrf52840) 
embedded in multiple locations across the PS Suit. It includes three biopotential and bioimpedance 
electrodes around the upper left chest, right chest, and lower left region of the body to monitor respiration 
and the heart's electrocardiography (ECG). The respiration sensing is done through impedance 
measurement from these electrodes. Two blood -flow sensing nodes are distributed around the left toe 
and right toe. The blood flow sensing mechanism is based on photoplethysmography (PPG), in which a 
light-emitting diode illuminates the skin, and a phot odiode captures the reflected signal and light 
absorption caused by the blood circulation. In addition, blood oxygen saturation level can be derived from 
PPG based on the ratio of red and IR light absorbance. By processing the ECG and PPG data, one can 
measure pulse arrival time that correlates to the blood pressure without any inflation cuff that could 
otherwise interfere with circulation. An inertial measurement unit (IMU) for gravity data analysis and 
physical activity sensing is also integrated in this module. A GUI was developed to visualize all the 
compression, physical activity, and physiological sensor data continuously in real time. The FlowIO system, 

242 
 
shown in blue on Figure 7-23, also uses the same type of microcontroller as the central processing system 
(Feather Sense nrf52840). It controls a set of five air chambers distributed throughout the PS Suit. A fabric 
pressure sensor is integrated into every air chamber for feedback and control of the compression 
mechanism. Based on ADC data of every inflatable pressure sensor, it is possible to estimate a gradient 
compression in the PS Suit of around 18 mmHg in the lower abdomen to 50 mmHg around the ankle. The 
Large Pump module of FlowIO was used to provide flow rates of up to 4.5L/min and a pressure range of -
60 to 150kPa.  
Both subsystems communicate via Bluetooth to a laptop. This communication is largely based on the 
communication approach implemented in the FlowIO software stack. This is made possible in part and 
made easier by the fact that an identical microcontroller i s used in both FlowIO and the activity sensing 
subsystem. For the sake of managing complexity better and making debugging easier, the choice was made 
to use two separate microcontrollers for the two systems.  
 
7.5.5 PS Suit Testing 
A parabolic flight study was conducted to test the functionality of the PS-Suit for wireless physiological 
sensing and dynamic compression in extreme gravity environments. It involved a series of rapid ascents 
and descents, creating periods of zero -G and h ypergravity that simulate the environments astronauts 
experience during space missions. This preliminary study was conducted to provide insight into any 
necessary adjustments needed by our spacesuit design and operations to ensure that the system can 
withstand the rigors of spaceflight and the ISS environment expected during further development and 
testing. Extensive details about the planning, conducting, and outcomes of the study can be found in the 
second paper on Peristaltic Suit [231]. Figure 7-22 features one of the images taken during the parabolic 
flight. 
7.5.6 Influence of PS Suit on FlowIO’s Evolution 
Just as some of the other projects presented in this chapter influenced the evolution of FlowIO, so 
did PS Suit as well. At the time when PS Suit was being developed, only the DC version of the Large Pump 
module for FlowIO was in existence. However, for the parabolic flight, the system needed to be fully 
battery-powered. Thus, a small modification was made to enable the large pump module to be operated 
with a high C-rate externally connected LiPo battery (45C and 7.4V, AWANFI). Drawbacks of this approach, 
however, included the need for the battery to be removed and replaced for charging, the requirement for 
a separate battery charging device, and overall, the system did not look el egant when having a battery 
outside the device. This is what ultimately inspired, at a much later time, the development of the battery 
version of the Large Pump module. It not only includes an integrated 7.4V battery (as two 3.7V LiPo 
batteries in series) but also two integrated battery chargers and a highly sophisticated switching circuit 
that enables charging the module simply from a USB port by breaking the series connection of the 
batteries during charging and then reconnecting them again in series after charging completes. The choice 
to pursue a highly complex power electronics design circuit was largely inspired by the desire to have a 
system that requires nothing more than a USB port for all its power needs.  
 
  
243 
 
7.6 Corsetto: Kinesthetic Haptic Garment 
Corsetto is a project that leverages both OmniFiber and FlowIO, pushes them to the limit of their 
capabilities. Corsetto is a collaboration between multiple universities involving researchers from the 
United States and Sweden. My involvement with this project was in the early phases, where I spent three 
months in Sweden helping prepare 18 FlowIO devices required for the pro ject with project -specific 
hardware modifications, as well as software development assistance about the implementation details of 
the original FlowIO software stack. Other team members then developed a modified software stack based 
on my original one, in order to make it more suitable for the needs of the Corsetto project. This work was 
published at ACM CHI 2023 [236] and holds the record for the most FlowIO devices used in a single project.  
 
 
Figure 7-24. Corsetto garment and hardware: (a) front view with rib and abdominal modules; (b) side view with 
rib, abdominal, and lower back modules; (c) back view with lower back and spinal modules. The Corsetto modules 
are connected to three FlowIO devices via 12 pneumatic channels. 
When attentively listening and watching someone singing, the listener often resonates with or mimics 
some of the muscle movements and breathing patterns of the singer – even without noticing it. Corsetto 
leverages this experience and enriches it – making it fully perceptible, immersive, and delivering the full 
richness of the experience felt by a professional vocalist, enabling the audience to feel and experience the 
natural movements of the singer’s body  in synchrony with their voice. Corsetto is a kinesth etic haptic 
feedback garment for real -time transfer of vocal experience and upper body movements – from a 
professional singer performing a piece, to a listener. Corsetto was developed as a high-fidelity kinesthetic 
wearable platform able to materially emulate and viscerally communicate the nuances of a singing body. 
It consists of a full -stack end -to-end system for upper body haptics featuring a corset -like garment, 
hardware control system, embedded software, graphical user interface, audio recording device s, and 
machine learning algorithms. The garment integrates OmniFiber technology and FlowIO to enable the 
capture of upper -body respiration patterns and the transfer of those patterns to another person via 
kinesthetic haptic feedback. Artificial Intelligenc e (AI) is also employed to automate the real -time 
translation of vocal gestures into haptic gestures during a live performance. 
The system thus far has been used in the contexts of (a) vocal pedagogy and (b) live opera 
performance. However, this system can also be used (c) for helping runners with respiratory support during 
practice or recovery after practice, (d) for supporting emotional regulation with pressure feedback, (e) as 
a mechanical counterpressure suit, (f) for various medical applications, and (g) for gaming applications.  
 
7.6.1 Corsetto Garment  
The garment features machine-embroidered actuated modules with abdominal, lower-back, spinal, 
left rib, and right rib sections – Figure 7-25. A fabric structure with embedded OmniFiber actuators 
provides a rich repertory of soft mechanical stimulation such as vibrotactile feedback, lateral stretch, 
compression, and biaxial push and pull motions.   The Corsetto garment contains 12 muscle actuators, 
grouped into sets of 4 where each group is controlled by a single FlowIO device. 

244 
 
 
 
Figure 7-25. The corset features (a) mechanical adjustments for body fitting and (b) actuated modules including 
(b1) abdominal, (b2) lower back, (b3) spinal, (b4) left rib, and (b5) right rib. 
Connecting the robotic garment three synchronized FlowIO devices enables diverse haptic 
expressions, mediated by the central controller software, and ranging from minute tactile to powerful 
vestibular stimulations like sternum and rib cage vibrations, abdominal compression, and spinal drift.  
The final garment, seen on Figure 7-24, came about in an iterative design process with four iterations 
after testing the technology on 30 people over the course of 12 months. The garment was chosen in the 
style of a singer’s corset due to multiple considerations including comfort, adjustability to different body 
shapes, and ease of donning and doffing it. Finally, six identical Corsetto garments were created to be worn 
by six listeners during a live opera performance; this required an ensemble of 18 FlowIO devices in total. 
 
7.6.2 Corsetto System Architecture 
Figure 7-26 depicts the design of a complete Corsetto system. The hardware stack consists of the 
Corsetto garment with embedded OmniFiber fluidic fabric muscle and three FlowIO Controller modules. 
Rather than pumps, a compressed air tank (1.5 L of air at 200 bar pressure) with a built -in mechanical 
pressure regulator is used to achieve silent actuation, high pressures, and at least 35 minutes of continuous 
usage as needed for the final opera performance. The tank is connected also to an external electronic 
pressure regulator (Festo VEAA proportional pressure control valve, 0-10 bar), set to operate on 0 to 5 bar 
of air pressure enabling varying force feedback and actuation speeds. The output of the electronic 
pressure regulator is connected to the inlet of 3 FlowIO Controller Modules via a four-way splitter.  
 
 
Figure 7-26. System diagram for a single Corsetto System. 
The software stack consists of several interconnected systems and offers a suite of tools for 
controlling the hardware and arranging haptic compositions for Corsetto. The output of the software stack 
is a stream of instructions sent to the FlowIO devices and the electronic pressure regulator. A “Composer” 
tool with a graphical user interface was developed to enable haptic compositions to be entered directly, 
extracted from musical notation, saved, or loaded from a file. Figure 7-27 shows a more detailed view of 
the software stack used for controlling not just one but six Corsetto wearables with 18 FlowIO devices.  

245 
 
 
 
Figure 7-27. System diagram of the software stack used for controlling multiple wearable Corsetto wearables. 
The Composer suite consists of three separate tools: (1) A scheduler allows the creation of 
instructions to be enacted across all 18 FlowIOs to run haptic gestures simultaneously in six Corsetto 
garments. The scheduler is almost identical to the original FlowIO scheduler, with one key difference being 
that it controls not just one but multiple FlowIO devices. (2) A converter enables the conversion of sheet 
music files (in the open MusicXML format) – adorned with haptic scores – into instructions compatible 
with FlowIO’s native protocol. To turn a sheet of music into a haptic score, the sheet music file is loaded 
into the converter and parsed. The user/composer is asked to associate each ’instrument’ with a FlowIO 
device and I/O ports, and to specify how dyna mics are to be converted to pressure regulator controls. 
Then, the user selects the beats per minute (BPM) value for the sheet music used to determine the start 
time of every instruction. (3) A sequencer enables sequencing several individual short schedule s into 
larger compositions.  The creation of this component was prompted by the sheer number of instructions 
required for even a short music score. Even just a 45 -second sequence can contain thousands of haptic 
instructions. By making large sequences from smaller chunks, sequencing and modifying compositions 
becomes easier and less time -consuming for the user/composer. The sequencer also allows the user to 
see which devices are needed for each port of the haptic piece. It also allows saving the complete sequence 
in a JSON file. 
Initially, the software for Corsetto used the default FlowIO software stack without any changes to 
the libraries, firmware, protocols, or connectivity to the web browser – with changes only to the JavaScript 
and higher layers used by the Composer, created in the Elm programming language. Subsequently, certain 
changes were made at even lower layers of the FlowIO stack to accommodate Corsetto-specific application 
needs. A modification was made to the firmware to make FlowIO controlled via USB rather than Bluetooth, 
as USB offers lower latency and higher reliability compared to Bluetooth Low Energy (BLE), and also 

246 
 
because WebBluetooth supports only up to 7 connected devices whereas 18 were needed for the final 
performance.  A central controller was also made in Python to control the FlowIO devices over USB.  
The aforementioned software modifications of the FlowIO stack were made by other users without 
my direct involvement. Thus, I cover that part in more detail in the next chapter on FlowIO case studies, 
where I present the Corsetto-specific software stack as a case study of users modifying / extending FlowIO.  
 
7.6.3 Corsetto in a Live Opera Performance 
Two live opera performances were staged with a professional vocalist and an audience of six guests 
as listeners, each wearing Corsetto. Both performances were based on Feldman’s Three Voices score, and 
the Corsetto Composer tool was used for haptic scoring and orchestration of the garments. Details about 
the haptic scoring system can be found in the published paper. The performances took place on different 
dates, and each performance lasted for 30 minutes. The guests were in terviewed following each 
performance, the findings from which are also covered at length in the Corsetto paper. 
 
 
Figure 7-28. Corsetto garments in use during a live opera performance rehearsal with six audience-members. 
7.6.4 Discussion on FlowIO use in Corsetto  
Since each FlowIO device controlled a group of 4 muscles, only 4 of the 5 available pneumatic I/O 
ports per FlowIO device were connected to a muscle. However, the unconnected I/O port also served a 
very important role!  The unconnected port in each device was programmed as an additional air outlet 
port to enable air release at a rate much faster than what is otherwise possible with just a single outlet 
port. To account for the varying breathing rates of the singer, it was important to have flow rate control  
for both pressurization and depressurization. By having the unconnected port serving as a secondary 
exhaust valve, a much wider range of exhaust rates was possible, ultimately enabling rapid haptic feedback 
transitions and high-frequency haptic response up to 40kHz.  
The Corsetto project pushed the FlowIO platform to the edge of its limit and even beyond. As a 
result, several modifications and developments were undertaken in both hardware and software to make 
FlowIO suitable for the needs of Corsetto. One of them was the need for achieving pressures that are far 
greater than what the pump modules could offer; this resulted in the creation of a compressed air module 
for FlowIO and required a corresponding modification of the main driver PCB; the detail s of this are 
presented in the next chapter as another case study of modifications. Another upgrade was the need for 
Composer software, a specialized graphical user interface (GUI), and the capability to synchronously 
control multiple FlowIO devices from the GUI; a detailed account of those software developments and 
modifications are presented in the next chapter. 

247

8: Case Studies of FlowIO Deployment 
The previous chapter presented case studies of Research Projects Made with FlowIO across fields, 
with my direct involvement in them. This chapter presents four additional classes of case studies on FlowIO 
Deployment: (1) Classroom use cases, (2) Master theses enabled by FlowIO, (3) FlowIO replication cases, 
and (4) cases of users extending FlowIO and adding new hardware and software features themselves. Each 
of those categories covers distinct case instances, limited to three cases per category. One goal of this 
chapter is to show the breadth and the extent of FlowIO’s impact, which has been a recurring theme 
throughout this dissertation. Nevertheless, this is still not an exhaustive list. Additional projects can be 
found on the SoftRobotics.IO website and also by following citations of the FlowIO paper. This
