8.1 Origination of the Case Studies and Lead User Theory 
Eric Von Hippel’s Lead User theory [237][238] is particularly applicable to the  process employed 
when choosing which users to accept in the FlowIO deployment pilot , to the origin of the case studies 
covered in this chapter, and to all the other use-cases and applications developed with FlowIO that are not 
covered in this dissertation.  In [237] Von Hippel defines Lead Users as: (1) Facing needs that will be general 
in a marketplace months or years before the bulk of the marketplace encounters them. ( 2) Being 
positioned to benefit significantly by obtaining a solution to those needs. More specifically, he writes: 
“Lead users are users whose present strong needs will become general in a marketplace months 
or years in the future. Since lead users are familiar with conditions that lie in the future for most others, 
they can serve as a need-forecasting laboratory for marketing research. Moreover, since lead users often 
attempt to fill the need they experience, they can provide valuable new product concepts and design data 
to inquiring manufacturers in addition to need data”  
The formal process of obtaining a FlowIO device or kit requires interested users to submit an 
application (reproduced in Appendix B) that includes – a 300-500 word proposal explaining what they want 
to do with FlowIO, a statement about whether their project or use case is accepting of collaborators, the 
type of contribution they would make in exchange for their access to FlowIO should they be approved , 
and other more basic questions. This application process for obtaining FlowIO was designed effectively as 
a way of identifying lead users, and specifically ones likely to have the highest net benefit for the FlowIO 
project, for the user community, and for the overarching SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem. However, only a 
subset of the applicants whose proposals were approved and who obtained access to a FlowIO device  / 
kit followed through with their originally intended projects or use cases – some dropped their projects 
while others pivoted. Even among those whose subsequent works matched their proposals, only a small 
subset of them were still reachable to be interviewed for the case studies (in some cases two years later). 
In addition to this formalized way of providing FlowIO to lead-users, some of the devices or kits 
were also provided to non-lead users with more basic needs and with lower impact potential proposals, 
especially in cases where the users were deemed highly likely to return the device and do so within a short 
period of time. Even though there was little to be gained in terms of technical advancements to the FlowIO 
project in such cases, non-lead users still brought some gains in terms of publicity for the FlowIO Platform 
– either through content they published online or in papers, or through private interactions they had with 
colleagues – some of whom also then requested FlowIO for their own projects.  
Proximity to a FlowIO device also played a nontrivial role in who was able to obtain FlowIO. If a 
proposal was submitted by someone who was in close proximity to an existing FlowIO device no longer in 
use, then that applicant was often approved to be the next user of that FlowIO device, even if their project 
proposal was not the most impactful among the pool of proposals. For example, loaner units in Germany 
were shared among users in Germany, units in the UK among UK users, and ones in California  stayed in 
California for the most part. Part of the reasoning for this decision was to have a broad geographic 
coverage of devices, especially in areas where future proposals were most likely to come from – regions 
with high density of visitors to the SoftRobotics.IO website. Nevertheless, in certain cases users exchanged 
devices across borders and even across continents – especially in the instances of time-sensitive projects. 
While the original aim was to distribute all FlowIO devices and device kits to lead-users with the 
most innovative ideas, and then do case studies of those lead-users, in practice it often made more sense 
to distribute some of the FlowIO devices also to non-lead users due to the factors mentioned above, even 
when that sometimes came at the expense of not being able to approve the application of a lead-user due 
to unavailability of a device. Ultimately, this enabled a broader diversity of people to have access to the 
platform, and to have a more heterogeneous set of use cases and users . Consequently, the case studies 
presented here include projects from both lead users and non-lead users. Each case category was limited 
to covering just three representative cases – selected for their differentiating characteristics. 
252 
 
8.2 FlowIO in the Classroom 
FlowIO has been and continues to be utilized as a teaching platform by several professors and 
instructors in their course curricula. In this section, I will discuss three cases, demonstrating different 
variations of how FlowIO is used in the classroom by both students and instructors. The specific cases are 
for the courses: (1) “How to Make (almost) Anything ” at MIT / Harvard, (2) “Tangible Interface” at MIT 
Media Lab, and (3) “Inflatable Materials” at Harvard GSD.  In addition to these three instances that will be 
covered here, there are many others where FlowIO is used by students for their coursework. Multiple 
other professors have also obtained FlowIO for their courses at various universities in the US, Europe, and 
Asia. For example, Professor Robert Katzschmann from ETH Zurich has assembled more than 10 FlowIO 
devices that are currently used in a course he teaches. However, an interview with him could not be 
conducted to gather sufficient information for a case study. 
Before delving into the specific case studies, it’s worth noting that there are at least three distinct 
educational utilities that are possible of the FlowIO Platform – a tool for students, a tool for instructors, 
and a subject of study – as described below. 
The most common educational use of FlowIO is as a tool for students for pneumatic actuation, 
sensing, or control in project-based engineering, robotics, and art courses. All the case studies covered in 
this section focus primarily on this type of use case. However, this is not the only way FlowIO can be used 
for educational purposes. There are two other ways in which FlowIO can be used in the classroom.  
FlowIO can serve as a tool for instructors  to build with it and show various demonstrations in 
physics and engineering courses. This is how FlowIO is being used in some soft robotics courses, and this 
utility will also be seen in two of the case studies covered below. Furthermore, since FlowIO can be used 
with not only air but also with clear liquids such as water or alcohol (HydroIO), it also serves as a tool for 
creating dozens of different demos related to fluid mechanics and fluidic systems. Additionally, it can even 
be used for creating demos in introductory physics course where it serves as an actuator source for 
powering various mechanics demos. Nearly half of all the concepts that are taught in an introductory level 
physics course on classical mechanics can be demonstrated with the help of FlowIO and the full family of 
modules in the platform, therefore making this platform a nearly universal tool for the physics classroom. 
Furthermore, since new modules can be created for FlowIO, it is also possible to extend the platform to 
the point where even more physics and mechanics concepts can be demonstrated with FlowIO. 
The third educational use of FlowIO is to have it as a subject of study  in product design and 
engineering courses. A case study of this is covered not in this section but in section 5 on Extending FlowIO, 
case #3 - AirPort. Additionally, many of the people who request FlowIO or sign-up on the SoftRobotics.IO 
website to access the replication tutorial series explicitly state that they are simply interested in learning 
how to assemble a complex device, learning about interfacing between hardware and software over 
WebBluetooth, and other similar requests where people are interested in acquiring new skills from the 
design engineering of FlowIO’s hardware and software. 
The FlowIO hardware is open source and consists of more than 100 components – from miniature 
electronics to pneumatic and electromechanical components, to multiple 3D printed parts and enclosures. 
The design is also fully modular – not only in the sense of  having plug-and-play modules, but also each 
block itself is modular on the inside as well. All these characteristics make FlowIO suitable as a reference 
for teaching concepts related to electronics design, miniaturization, product design, mechanical 
engineering, precision 3D printing, and more topics. What makes FlowIO especially well -suited for this 
purpose is that the designs are open -source and detailed graphical assembly instructions with steps are 
provided on the project website. The open assembly instructions coupled with moderate complexity also 
make FlowIO well -suited for teaching practical skills such as mechanical assembly, soldering, 
microcontroller programming, and debugging. 
  
253 
 
8.2.1 Case 1: “How to Make Almost Anything” course @ MIT / Harvard 
Neil Gershenfeld’s How to Make (almost) Anything is one of the most legendary graduate courses 
at MIT , which has grown to become so widely renowned that it’s now offered across three MIT 
departments and one Harvard department. The course attracts hundreds of student applicants every fall 
semester, of which approximately 80 are accepted. The course is so intense that in addition to the 80 
students, it has an astounding number of as many as 40 Teaching Assistants (TAs) who help students with 
their weekly and term projects.  During the span of a 14-week-long semester, students learn various skills 
related to digital fabrication. Each week covers a different topic like Additive & Subtractive manufacturing, 
Electronics Design, PCB fabrication, Molding & casting, Embedded programming, Sensors & Actuators, 
Interface programming, Networking, and more. Not only are students expected to learn each topic in just 
one week, but they must also come up with a project based on each topic, implement their project within 
seven days, and create a website documenting what they made each week. In addition, there is also a 
term project, where each student must individually make something that integrates all areas covered in 
the course. Students must do a demonstration of their final projects during a full-day-long exhibition that 
is open to the general public and that attracts hundreds of attendees. 
 
8.2.1.1 Wildcard Week 
Despite the wide breadth of topics covered in the course, there are still some additional important 
fabrication-related topics to be covered but for which there isn’t enough time in the semester. To address 
this limitation, there is a wildcard week near the end of the semester, where rather than a single topic, 6 
to 8 different topics being covered in parallel and each student must select one of the available choices. 
During the wildcard week, the topics are taught by TAs or by invited lecturers.  
During the 2019 edition of the course, one of the topics for the wildcard week was soft robotics 
and I was invited to be the instructor for that topic. Approximately 8 students signed -up for the soft -
robotics topic. Similar to the requirements for other we eks, the students were again responsible for 
completing an individual project of their choice based on the topic they had selected, which meant they 
had to make some soft robotic project in just 7 days.  
I delivered a 3-hour presentation plus a workshop on soft robotics design and fabrication. During 
the lecture, I also introduced the students to the FlowIO platform, which at that time I was still developing. 
I also informed them that that a FlowIO device will be available to them should they choose to use it for 
their projects. This was time when I had just finished developing the first version of FlowIO – which was 
monolithic, non-modular, capable of pressures of between -10 to +10psi. Only three such devices existed 
at the time, and the students were informed that they may have to time -share the devices amongst 
themselves should the number of projects be greater than the number of devices. They were also 
expected to return the FlowIO devices after the completion of their final projects. 
 
8.2.1.2 Use of FlowIO 
Four students chose to create soft robotic projects that were pneumatically actuated, while others 
chose alternative actuation approaches. All of those four decided to use FlowIO for the actuation and 
control of their projects. Moreover, three of the stude nts also made their final projects based on soft 
robotics and they again used the FlowIO device for actuation and control. Figure 8-1 shows all the FlowIO-
based projects that the students made during the course – including wildcard-week projects and final 
projects.  
254 
 
 
Figure 8-1. Projects made with FlowIO during the How to Make (almost) Anything course. 
One student created a standalone pneunet actuator for the wildcard week, and later, for her 
final project, she extended that project to create a soft gripper with four of the same pneunet actuators 
and a FlowIO device controlling the gripper. Another student made a buckling actuator with rotational 
motion when depressurized. This actuator required primarily the vacuum feature of the FlowIO device, 
which was a refreshing change as most other projects aren’t vacuum-actuated. For his final project, he 
made another soft-robotics project – a self-stabilizing column - which also used the FlowIO platform. A 
third student made a walking robot constructed with straws and balloons – this was the first instance of 
FlowIO being used for a locomotive application. The final student using FlowIO made was an inflatable 
actuator in the form of fish with buoyancy control, enabling it to go to different depths underwater. She 
also used this for her final project. Details about each project can be found on the corresponding project 
pages created by the students, listed below. 
• Robot: https://fab.cba.mit.edu/classes/863.19/Harvard/people/dgil/14_soft_robotics.html 
• Pneunet: https://fab.cba.mit.edu/classes/863.19/EECS/people/rebei/week14/week14.html 
• Soft Gripper: https://fab.cba.mit.edu/classes/863.19/EECS/people/rebei/final/final.html 
• Fish: https://fab.cba.mit.edu/classes/863.19/Architecture/people/Deb/finalproject.html 
• Column: https://fab.cba.mit.edu/classes/863.19/Architecture/people/Kii/final/index.html

8.2.1.3 Discussion 
This was the first instance of FlowIO being used in a course, and despite it being just an early 
prototype and not feature-complete at the time, it still achieved great success in enabling all the 
students who chose to use FlowIO to successfully control their projects with it.  
Moreover, after the wildcard week, some students even chose to make their final projects also 
based on FlowIO. The fact that students were willing to entrust their final projects to FlowIO came as the 
strongest validation up to that time that FlowIO was extremely useful to people, that they enjoy using it 
not just once but multiple times, and that users find it robust and reliable. This validation came at a time 
when the hardware and the software of the platform were still in their earliest phases of dev elopment, 
when it had less than a third of the features that is has today. The success of this pilot deployment in the 
classroom served as a validation that continuing the further development of the platform was a 
worthwhile effort; and it was a reason for me  to continue developing the platform and to bring it to the 
much more evolved state in which it is today. Had this early pilot not been as successful as it was, the fate 
of FlowIO may have been significantly different.  
Some of the challenges students encountered with the device were related to having to program 
it manually. As this was still in the early days of FlowIO, there was not yet a feature-complete software API 
and library with it. Moreover, documentation was not  yet available about how to use the device. As a 
result, almost all interactions had to be programmed manually using C++ code. This was challenging for 
the students because it required both proficiency with embedded programming and full understanding of 
the internal hardware. As a result, I had to offer a significant amount of help with the programming aspect 
of the device. This experience helped inform what kind of software abstractions were needed, what APIs 
and libraries to develop, what hardware and software documentation was most important and what was 
less important, as well as how to make the entire user experience more user-friendly and accessible.  
The success of this case, the challenges encountered and overcame, and all the knowledge that was 
gained from this small-scale pilot deployment of FlowIO were instrumental to the future development of 
the platform. Moreover, today FlowIO has now become part of the official teaching curriculum, being 
discussed briefly during the lecture on output devices.  
 
8.2.2 Case 2: “Tangible Interfaces” course @ MIT  
Tangible Interfaces is Hiroshi Ishii’s flagship course at MIT Media Lab. Each year, the course 
revolves around one or more themes that the Tangible Interfaces group is researching that particular year. 
Example themes include connected touch, telepresence, shape-changing interfaces, mixed reality, radical 
atoms, and teleabsense. The course consists of a series of talks by invited speakers presenting their 
research related to the theme that semester, as well as workshops led by either the invited speakers or by 
the TAs. The talks allow the students to become familiar with state-of-the-art research and to be inspired 
to come up with their own novel ideas. The workshops teach students practical skills on how to take 
advantage of the latest research technologies and processes related to Tangible User Interfaces (TUIs) and 
Human-Computer Interaction (HCI).  
Students have to develop two midterm projects and one final project during the semester, working 
in groups of 4 to 6 members. Each project must be an interactive interface related to the themes of the 
course and needs to be a system with an input and output. Students must do formal presentations and a 
demonstration of their final fully packaged working prototypes during an interactive session where the 
entire department is invited to attend. The students have approximately one month to come up with viable 
project ideas, procure materials, and complete their final projects.  Additionally, they also have to write a 
report about their projects in the format of the ACM extended abstract. Some students choose to go one 
step further and continue working on their p apers after the course and submit them for publication at 
academic conferences.  
256 
 
8.2.2.1 Student and TA demographics 
The course takes place in the fall semester and accepts approximately 45 students, of which over 
50% are design students from Harvard Graduate School of Design cross registered at MIT. The rest are 
mainly MIT students and there are also usually 1 -3 students from other colleges in the Boston area. The 
gender ratio is well balanced with slightly more female to male students. There is a mix between students 
with engineering skills and with design skills, though the vast majority of students don’t have extensiv e 
technical skills while some don’t have any prior hardware prototyping experience.  
In addition to students, there are also six TAs, half of which are in charge of administrative tasks 
and the other half — in charge of technical tasks such as leading workshops and helping students with 
their projects. The TAs are typically Master students  from the Tangible Media group and also other 
students who took the course in the previous year.  
 
8.2.2.2 Availability of FlowIO to Students 
During the 2022 edition of the course, the course themes were shape -changing TUIs and 
programmable materials. In the second half of the semester, relevant past and ongoing research projects 
from the Tangible Media group were introduced to the students, inc luding the OmniFiber project which 
utilizes a subset of the FlowIO Platform for actuation and control. The FlowIO Platform was introduced to 
the students during a presentation and workshop about the OmniFiber project delivered by Ozgun Kilic -
Afsar. The students were informed that they would have the opportunity to utilize FlowIO in their final 
projects should they choose to, up to the number of devices available. 
The Tangible Media group only had available a subset of FlowIO Controller modules and not the 
complete device with pump modules, hence only controller modules were directly available to the 
students as well as portable compressed air tanks. That’s because in the OmniFiber project, FlowIO was 
used with compressed air tanks rather than with Pump modules. As a result, students also had only a 
subset of the features that the full FlowIO Platform would have otherwise afforded to them. For instance, 
they did not have the vacuum feature or programmable flow-rate variability because those features are 
only possible with the pump modules. Nevertheless, some groups were still able to obtain pump modules 
by borrowing from the wider FlowIO community in the Boston area. Furthermore, even those who had 
only the controller modules were still able to successfully make use of FlowIO for their final projects 
because they did not require the vacuum feature. Other resources provided to the students included an  
Arduino kit with multiple sensors and actuators, soft robotic devices based on heat sealing, and shape -
memory polymer.  
 
8.2.2.3 Use of FlowIO 
The students divided themselves into seven groups, and five of those seven groups expressed 
interest in utilizing FlowIO for their final projects. However, because of the limited number of devices 
available, only four groups had access to FlowIO, and one w as encouraged to pivot to a tendon -based 
actuation for their project instead. Some of the students had also already heard about FlowIO even before 
it was officially introduced to them in the course by either finding it online or through other users at 
Harvard GSD, and they already had plans of using it. The students had one month to come up with viable 
project ideas, procure materials, and complete their final projects – though only the last two weeks were 
available to them to focus on technical execution of the projects.  
Students were encouraged to present their final project ideas before they began working on the 
projects, so that they could receive feedback from the TAs on how to scope their projects such that they 
can complete them on time.  For the groups that chose to use FlowIO, the TAs also provided an additional 
training session specifically about FlowIO’s hardware and user interface. However, no training was 
provided about how to write software for the device. Since the students were not exposed to the 
257 
 
programmatic ways of controlling FlowIO via the Arduino or JavaScript libraries, the student relied 
primarily on FlowIO’s graphical user interface, rather than on the APIs and libraries.  
 
Projects made 
Of the four groups that utilized FlowIO, two wrote blog posts and shared pictures and videos about 
their projects on the softrobotics.io website. The other two groups did not disclose details about their 
works publicly; therefore, they will not be discusse d in this case study. Figure 8-2 shows a screenshot of 
the two final projects that were posted on the blog at https://www.softrobotics.io/posts.  
 
 
Figure 8-2. Two final projects from the Tangible Interfaces course controlled by a subset of the FlowIO Platform. 
The first project by Yubo Zhao et.al is a pneumatically actuated haptic feedback glove that 
simulates tapping in extended reality. When discussing the role FlowIO played in their project, the students 
wrote in their blog post: “ Providing force feedback on glove interface often involves a bulky actuation 
mechanism that contains motors, wires, linkages, ergonomic enclosures, etc., while the integration of 
FlowIO brings a solution with hackable air which aligns with our goals of tan gible immersive experience 
and human -factor needs. Its easy -to-use web -based interface also allows us to rapid prototyping and 
testing our ideas.” The students also expressed intent to improve their project in the future and to develop 
software that allows native control of FlowIO directly form the Unity game engine. The other students 
involved in this work were Davide Zhang, Xiao Zhou, Yuchen Zhao, Yimeng Hou, and Alicia Cheng. More 
details and a video of this work is available at https://www.softrobotics.io/post/p-a-g-e  
The second project by Mengy u Zhao et.al., called 1001 Flowers, is an immersive experience 
allowing visitors to explore a synthetic ecosystem with flowers of various shapes and behaviors made of 
inflatable silicone rubber as well as optical fibers that enable them to glow in different colors of light. The 
FlowIO Platform is used to provide pneumatic actuation that enables the motion of the petals, flowers, 
and leaves. The other team members of this project were Danny Clarke, Nix Liu Xin, Candice Wu, Esther 
Xie, and Hongjin Yang. All team members were from Harvard Graduate School of Design. More details and 
a video of this work is available at https://www.softrobotics.io/post/1001-flowers  
Although details were not provided about the remaining two projects, interviews with the course 
TAs showed that all four projects that utilized the FlowIO platform were successfully completed and 
demonstrated as working prototypes at the end of the semester.  
While this was the first year of the course where FlowIO was included in the official course 
curriculum, this was not the first time in the course where FlowIO was used in a project. In the 2021 edition 
of the Tangible Interfaces course, there were also some projects that were based on FlowIO. However, in 
those cases, it was the students themselves who procured FlowIOs on their own accord rather than having 

258 
 
FlowIO device handed to them by the course TAs. The most notable project of that year was Telecon by 
HyeJun Youn, details of which can be found at: https://hyejunyoun.com/TeleCon  
 
 
Figure 8-3. TeleCon - class project by Hye Jun Youn and collaborators. 
8.2.2.4 Discussion 
Because the students were exposed only to the FlowIO GUI but not to the Arduino or JavaScript 
libraries and APIs, the students did encounter some limitations since the GUI is not as feature rich as the 
APIs. One feature that most groups found limiting in the GUI was the task scheduler, namely because there 
isn’t a way to import, export, or save a sequence of tasks. And hence the set of tasks needs to be reentered 
during each session or whenever the control dashboard webpage is refreshed. The most desirable feature 
was to have a way of saving a schedule of tasks and loading a schedule form a saved file. 
One group overcame some of the GUI limitations in a clever way, by developing an automation for 
the GUI, where they used a software tool that automates mouse clicks on a webpage. This was a new way 
of interacting with FlowIO that had not been considered previously, as typically one would normally work 
with the JavaScript or Arduino libraries instead of the GUI if they have more advanced needs. But by not 
being made aware of those lower-level options, the students inadvertently found a new way of controlling 
FlowIO by building something on top of the GUI itself.  
Another limitation that some of the groups faced was the unavailability of pump modules and the 
use of compressed air tanks instead. The main drawback of this is that no vacuum or flow-rate-variability 
features are available in that case. But this was overcome by using the release function instead of vacuum; 
albeit the fluid outflow happens more slowly in that case and the pressure cannot drop below atmospheric 
pressure. One group wanted to have a variable flow rate, though they had to use a fixed flow-rate because 
of the limited number of pump modules available in the course. Another group that also required features 
only afforded by a pump module was able to obtain one by reaching out to the FlowIO community and 
borrowing a module from an existing user.  
It's amazing that despite the unavailability of FlowIO pump modules and despite not being 
introduced to the programmable aspects of FlowIO – students were still able to develop projects that were 
made possible with this hardware -limited and feature -limited subset of FlowIO. This case study also 
reveals how important it is to have redundancies in a platform, and to have it be functional and useful 
even when not all the hardware is available – which is made possible by modular design approaches. The 
primary benefits that FlowIO brought to the students in this case included significant savings of time and 
complexity, as well as a small form -factor – enabling them to focus during the limited time they had on 
developing user experiences rather than control system s and software. Since most students were from 
non-technical backgrounds, the FlowIO not only enabled them to execute their projects, but also the 
availability of FlowIO is what made it possible for some of the groups to come up with those particular 
project ideas in the first place. Thus, in this case, FlowIO also served as an enabler for creative thinking. 
 

259 
 
8.2.3 Case 3: “Inflatable Materials: Aesthetics of Pneumatic Structures” J-
term course @ Harvard 
During the 2024 January term at Harvard, Hye Jun Youn taught a two-week-long course exploring 
fundamental design principles and hands-on fabrication techniques for developing soft prototypes using 
silicone and TPU. The course was specifically designed to introduce art, design, and architecture students 
at Harvard GSD to fabrication techniques and materials that they otherwise never use in architectural 
projects / mockups which are normally done with rigid materials. The course consisted of four sessions in 
total. Each session lasted three hours, where the first half was a lecture and the second half a hands -on 
workshop activity. The lectures gave students an overview of the field, presented various examples of prior 
works, discussed fundamental concepts, and  introduced tools and online resources. The hands -on 
activities taught the students practical skills on how to design and fabricate inflatable actuators and 
structures. The first week of the course focused on silicone -based projects using molding and casti ng 
fabrication techniques, and the second week focused on TPU -based projects using ultrasonic welding of 
TPU films. Participants had to come up with project ideas individually, digitally design them, and later 
fabricate them using either molding and casting of silicone or by cutting TPU films on a Zünd digital cutter 
machine and then sealing the TPU film using an ultrasonic welder attached to a Zünd Generic Application 
Module. The welded TPU films and the silicone actuators were then pneumatically actuated to observe 
their dynamic movements and changes in shape. 
 
8.2.3.1 Student Demographics 
12 students participated in the course with evenly balanced male to female ratio. All participants 
were first- or second-year master’s students at Harvard Graduate School of Design in their mid -twenties 
to early thirties. The students came from design and architecture backgrounds and none of them had prior 
engineering training or expertise. They also had little to no familiarity with soft robotics, inflatables, or the 
materials and processes taught during the course. 
 
8.2.3.2 Availability and Use of FlowIO 
During the first -class session, the instructor introduced the students to various resources and 
tools, including the SoftRobotics.IO community website with its collection of soft robotics resources, and 
the FlowIO Platform as a tool for actuation and control. The students were also shown the FlowIO overview 
video and some examples of using the hardware with the graphical user interface. The students were not 
introduced to the more advanced hardware or software features such as the expansion modules or the 
programming libraries or APIs. Only the basic device and the GUI were introduced in the course. Only one 
device was available to everyone, which was sufficient as it was needed only for demonstration purposes 
and used primarily by the instructor. 
 
8.2.3.3 Projects Made  
The students made simple silicone-based and TPU-based inflatable art projects that could be done 
in a few hours. They included various animals, furniture mockups, bags, inflatable shoes, and also a Tic -
Tac-Toe game. Almost all the students’ projects had on ly one port, thus they didn’t require advanced 
control systems and most of them could be controlled manually without the need for any electronics at 
all. Therefore, the FlowIO Platform was an overkill for many of the student projects and only the most 
basic features of it were used by the students and only during the final demonstration at the end of the 
course. The platform had much greater utility for the instructor who used it to show the actuation of more 
advanced projects during the teaching process.  
 
260 
 
 
Figure 8-4. Images from Inflatable Materials course at Harvard Graduate School of Design. 
8.2.3.4 Discussion 
Unlike in the previous case studies, where the FlowIO Platform was used primarily by students for 
driving their own projects, in this case, FlowIO was used both by the instructor for demonstration and by 
the students for project realization. Since the proj ects did not require advanced control features, the 
students preferred to use an analog approach such as syringes and pumps which were more user-friendly 
than an electronic device that had to be used either with a GUI or with an API. The students commented 
that they would have liked to be able to use FlowIO in a completely analog way where they could just press 
buttons directly on the device itself rather than having to connect it to a computer or a phone. This shows 
the importance and value of the Buttons module, although this module was not available at the time.  
During a demonstration someone accidentally dropped the FlowIO, breaking the enclosure. Thus, 
a more durable enclosure would be desirable, which can be achieved by 3D-printing it with a semi-flexible 
rather than rigid filament or resin, so that it can deform elastically on impact without breaking. 
Other comments and suggestions received were related to streamlining the process of obtaining 
and using FlowIO. Especially for course projects, students and instructors don’t have the time to assemble 
a FlowIO kit themselves or to repair one if something breaks. Those are the cases where having assembled 
devices readily available is important. There was also a request for fully documented project examples 
with tutorials of how to use FlowIO. Currently the website has tutorials on how to assemble FlowIO, 
documentation about how to program it, and projects demonstrations. However, there are no tutorials on 
specific use cases, and that is something that would be very valuable to both instructors and students.  

261 
 
8.3 FlowIO in Master Theses – Case Studies 
The FlowIO Platform has made possible at least five Master theses – all at different universities in the 
US and Europe – including at KTH in Sweden, ETH Zurich in Switzerland, Weissensee in Germany, and MIT 
and RISD in the US. These theses were all in different fields, namely Art, Design, Human -Computer 
Interaction, Robotics, and Architecture. In all of these cases, the students had the FlowIO device for an 
extended period of at least a few months. Three of these theses will be covered in this section. 
 
8.3.1 Case 1: “Info-Motion”, M.S. Thesis at Weissensee, Germany 
In 2023, Jiawen was a master’s student at Weissensee School of Art and Design in Berlin in her final 
year. She submitted a proposal through the softrobotics.io website to request a FlowIO for her thesis 
project, which she described as being about “giving dynamics to everyday products to convey information”. 
She wanted to enable objects to co nvey information content, reminders, and other forms of feedback 
through movement rather than through pixels or static features. Jiawen’s proposition for the use of FlowIO 
was as follows: “I need 2-3 air ports that can control 2 -3 air pockets on the product respectively, and the 
air pressure is adjustable when each air hole is inflated - for example, rapid inflation with high air pressure 
and slow inflation with low air pressure can shape di fferent dynamics, which are distinguishable to the 
user, making it possible for us to deliver different messages. I want the set to be small enough to fit into 
the size of our daily life supplies, which is why I found FlowIO. Flow IO is  indeed a very useful tool in 
exploring these new approaches to design and interaction.” 
Jiawen also expressed interest in contributing back to the FlowIO community, sharing the device with 
other students at her school who may need it, and posting about her work and use of FlowIO on the 
softrobotics.io website so that this can help other desig ners, artists, and members of the FlowIO 
community. “There are a lot of design students at Weissensee working on related projects, and we are 
involved in projects related to deformable surfaces from a designer's perspective, and we are actively 
integrating technology with psychology and design disciplines, and really designing the usability of 
deformable devices with User-Centered Design in mind, and being able to bring the technology further into 
the daily lives of the users. If we discover interesting applications and issues during our use of FlowIO, we 
would be happy to share them within the FlowIO community. This can help other designers or students in 
art and design schools to further apply FlowIO in their design projects, while also expanding the impact of 
FlowIO in design and art schools. I think that's what we can contribute to the community.” 
Finally, on the question about whether she needed an assembled FlowIO device or a kit of parts to 
assemble for herself, Jiawen indicated that she has prior experience with hardware assembly and that she 
is confident in being able to assemble the FlowIO herself by following the instructions on the website.  
Taking all the considerations about the project proposed, the potential contributions back to the 
community, and the comfortability with assembling the kit made Jiawen a great candidate for receiving a 
FlowIO. After a 30 -minute video call to discuss furthe r details about her project proposal and the 
expectorations for participation in this pilot deployment, Jiawen was approved. A FlowIO kit of parts was 
prepared for her, and she received it within approximately one month after her initial request. Jiawen 
successfully assembled the FlowIO device in two weeks. Details about her assembly experience are 
provided in a later section on case studies of assembling and extending FlowIO.  
 
8.3.1.1 Project Overview and use of FlowIO 
Info-Motion is the project developed by Jiawen for her MA thesis, exploring the question, “How 
to design interaction feedback by plant-inspired motion?” Jiawen first identified all the different types of 
motions that various plants in nature exhibit and then classified each motion type into either structural 
or spatial. Motion such as expansion, rebound, twist, shrinkage, turn, opening, and closing falls under 
the structural category, while rolling, falling, and floating fall under the spatial motion category. She then 
262 
 
created various artifacts capable of exhibiting all the motion patterns in the structural motion category. 
Figure 8-5 shows five such artifacts inspired by flowers, Venus flytrap, tree branches, cactus, and balsam, 
respectively. They all feature a combination of rigid and soft components, where the actuation of the 
soft components causes motion of the rigid components. The soft components are pneumatically 
actuated. Controlling the behavior and the motion of any of the artifacts requires the ability to precisely 
be able to monitor the pressure of each chamber in the artifact, be able to supply or remove air from 
each chamber at different rates, and the ability to change the flow rate of air going into or out of each 
chamber independently. These requirements for bidirectional actuation, continuous pressure sensing, 
and flow-rate control across five different ports – made FlowIO the only option in existence capable of 
meeting all the technical requirements of the project.  
 
 
Figure 8-5. Five artifacts created by Jiawen for her MA thesis project – inspired by and representing flower, fly 
trap, branch, cactus, and balsam. 
Before obtaining a FlowIO kit, Jiawen initially used manually operated syringes for actuation, and 
later she also tried building her own pneumatic system. However, that system (shown on Figure 8-6a) 
lacked many features, occupied a large space, and was neither robust nor portable. Software -wise, it 
required fully manual coding and there were no libraries, abstractions, or error corrections to rely on. This 
made it time-consuming and difficult to program as well as dangerous for the project because any software 
bug could cause the chambers in the artifacts to be overpressured and destroyed.   
After obtaining a FlowIO kit and assembling it, Jiawen transitioned to utilizing the FlowIO Platform 
hardware & software for the actuation, control, sensing, and programming of dynamic motion behaviors 
for the artifacts. In addition to the FlowIO core hardware modules, Jiawen also utilized the expansion port 
and the breakout board to be able to integrate different sensors with her project. To operate the device, 
she relied on the Graphical User Interface and the task scheduler in particular, which enabled h er to 
“program” various behaviors without having to write any code, simply creating a sequence of scheduled 
tasks. Subsequently, after connecting external sensors to the FlowIO device, Jiawen also learned how to 
work with the FlowIO Arduino libraries and to write her own embedded code for the hardware.  
 

263 
 
 
Figure 8-6. Side-by-side comparison of the initial pneumatic control system used by Jiawen and the subsequent use 
of FlowIO with expansion breakout module to achieve the same purpose. The  FlowIO device is shown actuating 
one of the artifacts of the Info-Motion project. Sensors embedded into the artifact are also connected to the FlowIO 
device via the expansion breakout board. 
Jiawen likened the experience of using the GUI’s task scheduler to the experience of editing a 
video in Premiere Pro. “At first, I used syringes to create some basic prototypes. However, as I started using 
more air bladders, syringes became insufficient. I needed more precise control to test and record 
movements in a quantifiable way. That's where FlowIO's visual GUI came in handy. It allowed me to easily 
set up and modify actions and record them in a scheduler. It was like editing a video in post -production 
software. It was very similar to the user experience of editing video s in Adobe Premiere.” She also found 
the modular architecture of the device and the ability to switch modules extremely valuable for her project 
requirements, in addition to the flow -rate control via pulse-width modulation. “It's important for all my 
patterns to distinguish between different airflow speeds so users can read different types of information 
from each pattern. The GUI interface allowed me to achieve this by controlling PWM or by switching out 
different air pump mo dules.” The complete details ab out the Info -Motion project - as well as many 
graphics, images, and videos – can be found in project references [239], [240], and [241].  
 
8.3.2 Case 2: “Self-Shaping Mechanisms”, M.S. Thesis at MIT, USA 
In 2022 Maryam – a second-year Master of Architecture student at the time – requested a FlowIO 
device for her thesis research project at MIT , which explored self -forming deployable structures for 
architectural applications such as emergency shelters. The project u tilized inflatable McKibben muscles 
integrated within knitted textiles to stiffen and shape the textile into adaptable forms. Maryam requested 
a fully assembled device to be used for just a few months, and she expressed interest in contributing to 
the FlowIO project by helping with the assembly process to also learn how the device worked, as well as 
by sharing her use of FlowIO with the community in a blog post.  
Maryam’s thesis titled, “Self -Shaping Mechanisms” [ 242] provides a framework for the 
development of lightweight adaptable systems that are affordable, easy to manufacture with readily 
available components, and rapidly deployable. The thesis investigated the fabrication of responsive self -
actuating composites that are adaptable and responsive to people or to changes in the environment 
through embedded sensors. The composites consist of knitted textiles, pneumatic McKibben muscles 
integrated into the textiles to provide actuation, and capacitive sensors also integrated into the textiles for 
proximity sensing.  Three strategies were explored and tested for integrating a pneumatic actuator into a 
tensile surface including: cutting, weaving, and knitting. Prototypes were developed for each of those 
approaches and eventually, knitting was chosen as the preferred approach. The prototypes are shown on 

264 
 
Figure 8-7. Machine knitting was chosen as the preferred approach, and a small-scale feature-complete 
prototype was developed on a double-bed knitting machine. A functional sensing fiber developed at MIT 
was inlaid between the rows of knit stitches firmly securing it in place to serve as a sensor. The fiber senses 
information from the surroundings, and then the shape of the structure changes in response to the sensor 
input. Although various types of sensing fibers can be embedded into the knitted structure, in this 
prototype, a capacitive sensor was used as the input and then specific pneumatic actions were mapped to 
certain sensor inputs.  
 
 
Figure 8-7. Three approaches for embedding a pneumatic actuator into a textile structure. 
8.3.2.1 Use of FlowIO and Discussion 
A FlowIO device was used as the control system for this research project. FlowIO served the triple 
roles of (1) the input device to which the sensing fiber was connected, (2) the output device that provides 
pneumatic actuation, and (3) the data processing unit that transforms specific sensor inputs into desired 
pneumatic actuation behaviors. A diagram of the system setup is provided in Figure 8-8.  
 
 
Figure 8-8. System diagram showing how the FlowIO Main module and the Expansion Breakout board were used 
in the system for sensing, actuation, and control. 

265 
 
Two FlowIO modules were used in this project: A Controller module and an Expansion Breakout 
module connected to the Controller module. The pneumatic inputs of the knitted structure were 
connected to the pneumatic ports of a FlowIO Controller. The sensor em bedded into the knit structure 
was connected to a FlowIO Expansion Breakout board. No Pump modules were used in this case, but 
instead an external air source was connected to the Controller module. Higher pressures between 30 and 
40 psi were more desirable  in this case rather than portability, and an external air source was readily 
available that could deliver such pressures.  
Initially, the graphical user interface was used to test the actuation of the knitted prototype and 
to create different shapes. Later, the FlowIO Arduino libraries were used to develop custom embedded 
code that could sense inputs from the capacitive sensors and map them to specific pneumatic outputs and 
control sequences.  
When asked about what challenges encountered with FlowIO, Maryam had the following to say: 
“Initial challenges faced were due to the limitations of the FlowIO device as it was not tailored to 
accommodate high PSI requirements. As a result, it couldn’t achieve the necessary PSI to inflate the 
McKibben muscles independently. Therefore, requiring a connection to an external air source. While the 
system remained portable, it sacrificed some of its lightweight, compact, and low-volume attributes which 
were originally sought after.  The adaptability and ‘hackability’ of the FlowIO system emerge as its most 
compelling and beneficial qualities. This enables accommodation for varying levels of expertise and project 
complexities, whether opting for the basic FlowIO in terface for rudimentary tasks or engaging in more 
sophisticated procedures facilitated by the Arduino library. The system's versatility highlights its 
effectiveness across a broad spectrum of applications.” 
  
8.3.3 Case 3: “Koinryō”, M.Sc. Thesis at KTH, Sweden 
Koinryō is an interactive wooden dummy that serves as an autonomous martial arts training 
partner. This was a master’s thesis project at KTH Royal Institute of Technology published by Yoav Luft in 
2023 [ 243,244]. The project was motivated by the conditions of the COVID pandemic to fill the void 
between individual martial arts practice and training with a partner and to bring the experience of outdoor 
training into an indoor setting. 
 
 
Figure 8-9. A wooden dummy used in Chinese Martial arts. The Koinryo prototype hanging from a ceiling. Back -
side of the prototype showing all the electromechanical hardware integrated into the device. The final prototype 
hanging from a tree ready for use in martial arts training. 

266 
 
Koinryō was built as an interactive opponent for practicing martial arts attack and defense 
techniques. The intent was to give agency to traditional passive equipment used in training practice – 
wooden dummies, boxing bags, tree branches, etc. – making them active-capable so they fight back. In a 
martial arts play, Koinryō occupies a role that lies between a passive instrument (e.g. boxing bag) and a 
living training partner. The system is constructed from natural materials, pneumatic muscles, and 
electronics. The system connects to the ceiling and carries its weight via the wooden shaft. Pneumatic 
muscles pull on the shaft, and their actuation enables swinging movement. The main body is made from 
a hollowed wooden log which houses pumps and a FlowIO device. The arms are made from a single forked 
tree branch with minimal processing to retain the natural appearance. The movements of Koinryō are 
controlled by 3 pneumatic muscles, chosen because of their high power density, fast actuation rate, and 
conformance to human levels of force. Additionally, springs were added to increase the effective range of 
the muscles.  
Fragility was an intentional part of the design of Koinryō – aiming to mirror martial arts training in 
the woods where dead branches break and pieces of bark tear off. The training sessions performed with 
Koinryō put considerable strains on the device’s construction – some parts broke, others were pushed out 
of alignment, and some pneumatic tubes became disconnected. The fragility made Koinryō come across 
as a sensitive and precious construction. However, not all kinds of fragility were desirable. Fragility  was 
desirable only in parts that were easily replaceable or fixable, while the trunk and electronics needed to 
maintain their integrity and robustness.  
 
8.3.3.1 Use of FlowIO and Discussion 
This project uses the FlowIO device for both actuation and sensing purposes. Koinryō relies on the 
readings from FlowIO’s internal inertial measurement unit (IMU). Capacitive sensors were also used to 
sense touch and proximity to the human partner. The software is designed as a set of state machines 
mapping sensor data to state transition s that actuate the muscles causing movements of the wooden 
arms. The system relies on several software libraries provided by FlowIO, Adafruit, and the Embedded C++ 
Template Library.   
The author of this work is a highly experienced software engineer and was thus able to take full 
advantage of the low -level libraries for FlowIO, and also to write custom software that utilizes FlowIO 
features not even abstracted into libraries yet. One such example is the use of the IMU. Another project 
that took advantage of several of the built-in sensors was Peristaltic Suit discussed earlier in this chapter.  
This is the highest -stress environment in which a FlowIO device has ever been used in a project, 
where much of the project is supposed to break but the FlowIO device itself housed within the project has 
to remain intact and keep working under the experienc e of high forces and repeated punches, pushes, 
pulls, and kicks. The fact that FlowIO was successfully able to serve the needs of the user even under these 
harsh conditions serves as a validation of its robust construction.  
 
8.4 Assembling FlowIO - Case Studies and Discussion 
8.4.1 Pros and Cons of providing FlowIO fully assembled 
Initially, FlowIO devices were provided to users in a fully assembled, preprogrammed, and tested state 
ready to use right out of the box. Users could start using the device within mere seconds by simply turning 
it on and establishing a Bluetooth connection with the GUI dashboard on SoftRobotics.IO. No download, 
installation, or setup was required. This fully-assembled approach offered several short-term advantages 
for everyone, including:  
• High convenience for end-users as very minimal effort is required on their part to start using the 
device, assuming they receive the fully assembled & tested device rather than the kit. 
• Quick access to feedback form users about the general hardware and software user-experience. 
267 
 
• Rapid feature implementation based on the feedback and requests received from users. 
• Faster diffusion of the technology as users can directly start operating and utilizing it. 
• Higher customer satisfaction with the technology and likelihood that they would be successful in 
utilizing it for their intended application needs. 
• More rapid publicity and content generation resulting from projects completed with FlowIO. 
• Rapid growth of a small user community who can help each other. 
• No need to provide assembly and testing support, but only usability support. 
However, there were also many disadvantages with providing a fully-assembled ready-to-use device. 
Some of those drawbacks were known in advance, while others were much less obvious and only became 
known with time. Obvious disadvantages included: 
• Additional time and resources required from the platform provider for the assembly service.  
• Fewer total number of devices available to people given a fixed amount of time available. 
Preparing a single ready-to-use device takes the same amount of time as preparing 4 or 5 kits.  
• Wider gap between supply and demand, leaving people who want a device unable to obtain one.  
• Underutilization of crowdsourcing in the assembly process.  
• Lack of feedback from the community about improvements to the assembly process. 
• Higher cost per assembled unit produced compared to the cost of producing a kit unit. 
But even more intriguing than those were the nonobvious disadvantages associated with providing 
the platform as a ready-to-use product compared to a kit. Those disadvantages tend to be more long-term 
oriented, and they revealed themselves gradually during the course of interacting with users, noticing the 
challenges and difficulties users encountered, and identifying the root causes of those challenges.  
• A subset of users lacked a proper conceptual model of how FlowIO works internally, even though 
that documentation is available, and even though in some instances they were already 
successfully using the device. They often viewed FlowIO as just a mystery box with ports, without 
concerning themselves about how it works – as long as it satisfied their needs. Or they made some 
wrong assumptions about the pneumatic architecture when questioned about it.  
• For short-term users, who mainly wanted to use the device to enable their demo, the mystery box 
mental model worked well. But for those who needed the device for extended time and  who 
wanted to take advantage of more advanced hardware and software features , they needed to 
have a deep understanding of the internal workings of FlowIO.  
• Even though technical documentation about the platform is available to users, some indicated 
that to fully understand the device, they had to assemble it themselves – or in some cases partly 
disassemble the one they got and then reassemble it. 
• The majority of users significantly underestimated the complexity and time required for assembly. 
Many were surprised to learn that there are over 100 unique parts in the device and that it takes 
multiple weeks just to assemble a single FlowIO device. It was only when users attempted to 
assemble a device for themselves that they fully appreciated the complexity and effort to replicate 
one. As a result, some requested to pay – just to avoid assembling it themselves. Prior to having 
this awareness, they did not have the same willingness to pay. 
• Underestimating the time and effort required to replicate a FlowIO device  leads some users to 
exercise less care in how they handle the product and undervalue it. And even if they have the 
proper awareness, they may still undervalue it. But that changes if they play an active role in the 
assembly process and if they put some time and effort themselves. 
268 
 
The fact that people initially view the FlowIO Platform as much more approachable and easier to 
assemble than it actually is also an advantage, because it proves that FlowIO successfully achieves the 
goal of hiding its complexity and having the aura of being highly inviting and user friendly. 
 
8.4.2 Pros and Cons of providing FlowIO as a kit of parts 
After providing the initial 10 -15 devices  in a fully assembled state, an option was introduced of 
offering FlowIO as a kit of parts to be assembled by the end user.  In the FlowIO application request form 
(Appendix B), applicants were asked to choose between assembled or a kit version. While most applicants 
said they were okay with either option, approximately a third specifically requested the kit, citing a desire 
to better understand the device through hands-on assembly. Those who preferred the assembled version 
primarily cited time constraints as their reason. 
To date, most FlowIO devices in use have been at least partially assembled and configured by users 
themselves by following the detailed tutorials under the Make FlowIO section on SoftRobotics.IO. In some 
cases, users received kits that included preassembled sub-assemblies, and they simply had to put together 
the sub-assemblies. In other cases, users received kits containing the most basic components and they 
had to assemble everything themselves – a long and arduous task. There were also cases where some 
users received partial barebone kits containing only custom -manufactured and most difficult to obtain 
parts – while they had to procure the other parts and 3D print the enclosures themselves.  
Advantages of providing FlowIO as a kit of parts include: 
• The time, effort, and cost are significantly less for preparing a kit than for  a fully assembled, 
programmed, and tested device.  
• This enables more people from the user community to be served, thus narrowing the gap of 
demand outpacing supply.  
• A kit is within financial reach of a significantly greater number of people while an assembled 
device is financially unaffordable to many.  
• The entire community benefits from having more users, who can offer help to others, feature 
suggestions, or direct skill-based contributions to the project and the ecosystem. 
• Users also provide feedback about the assembly and testing process itself, suggestions about 
making the tutorials clearer, and ideas about streamlining specific steps – thus benefiting the 
entire community and the growth of the project. 
• When people put time and effort into making something by themselves, they usually appreciate 
it and value it more than if they did not put any effort into making the artifact. 
• Going through the assembly process also enables people to learn and gain intuition about the 
device, how it works internally, and what features the hardware is capable of even beyond those 
exposed in APIs and documented. 
• This then enables people to be more creative with their own projects. The availability of features 
causes users in some cases to expand their original project ideas and take advantage of features 
they previously did not think they would need. This was one of the more surprising outcomes of 
the pilot – that FlowIO not only enables people to execute ideas they already have, but it inspires 
them to think of more creative ideas. This inspiration aspect is directly correlated with how much 
awareness a user has with all the possibilities offered by the platform. 
• There are also private benefits for the platform creator when they are providing a kit, especially 
if they are also offering support with it. Those private benefits include making connections with 
users which often may lead to  novel project collaborations, co-authorships, and other private 
opportunities for the platform creator. 
 
269 
 
Disadvantages of providing FlowIO as a kit of parts include:  
• A kit is not suitable for everyone, primarily in cases where a user has limited time and needs a 
device as quickly as possible.  
• A kit is also not suitable for those who may not have the experience of tinkering with hardware, 
the appropriate tools, or the patience to go through the process. 
• The assembly and testing process must be documented in exquisite detail and readily accessible. 
This requires a significant investment of time beyond internal use documentation. 
• Having the entire assembly process publicly accessible to everyone makes the project more 
vulnerable to competition, potentially causing it to cease to exist if a manufacturer who can take 
better advantage of economies of scale replicates it and offers it at more attractive terms to users. 
Thus, some measures need to be put into place to find a middle ground between providing the 
designs while still having some way of mitigating such risks from competitive forces. There are 
costs associated with creating, implementing, and enforcing such measures. 
• Not everyone who starts assembling a kit will be able to finish it. They may also become 
dissatisfied with the platform as a result, leading to losses of potential users, publicity, and 
feedback opportunities. 
• Users will typically take several months to complete the assembly of a kit, leading to delays in 
publicity and feedback. 
• Offering more advanced service support about the assembly and replication process itself is also 
needed with the kit option. Even if such service is not officially offered, users would still send 
questions when they run into difficulties with assembly or testing. 
8.4.3 Cases of users Assembling FlowIO 
Everyone who received a kit to be assembled was encouraged to share their story publicly by writing 
a blog post on SoftRobotics.io, documenting their process and focusing especially on things that they 
thought may benefit others who also want to assemble a  FlowIO device. Additionally, a pre -assembly 
survey with questions was sent to participants in this pilot about their assembly expectations. The survey 
questions are provided in Appendix A. 6 people wrote blog posts about their experience, 5 people 
completed the survey form, and two of those people completed both a blog post and the survey. 
Additionally, other users also shared feedback about their process via the forums or via email, often in the 
context of seeking help with specific steps about the assembly, programming, or testing of their devices. 
Ultimately, the feedback collected from people about the assembly of FlowIO came from a mix of blog 
posts by the users, survey questions in a google form, and feedback received via the online forums and via 
email. In total, feedback about the assembly process was received from approximately 20 people. 
The survey’s main aim was to understand the backgrounds and expectations of people interested in 
assembling FlowIO before they do the assembly, while the blog posts mainly aimed to reveal what happens 
during the assembly process and where expectations differ from reality. Of the six respondents to the 
survey, there was a mix of people who had little prior experience with electronics and mechanical 
prototyping, as well as people who had advanced degrees in electrical and mechanical engineering. When 
asked about what they hoped to gain from the process of assembling F lowIO, one person said, “ an in-
depth hands-on understanding of how the FlowIO platform works and how it could be potentially modified 
to better fit the needs of my project”. Another left a similar comment, “gain understanding of why and how 
the FlowIO was designed in the certain way and see the full potential of it .” There were also people who 
saw it as an educational opportunity for learning new transferable skills. One person said, “I mainly want 
to learn how to assemble such a complex device ” and another one wanted “ to learn the communication 
between the hardware and software components (IDE, Bluetooth, GUI, API)”.  
The pre-assembly survey also asked people about how confident they feel about the process after 
having read the full set of tutorials online , and which aspects of the assembly they feel least confident 
270 
 
about. On a confidence scale of 1 to 5 (low to high), one person said 5, while the others said between 3 
and 4. The processes and steps that people expressed most concern about were the surface -mount 
soldering, the high precision required when preparing the smallest 3D printed parts and cutting the 
internal tubes to exact length within 0.5mm accuracy, and finally a fear that they may encounter problems 
when setting up the software toolchain to flash the firmware – mainly due to, as one said, “ unpleasant 
prior experiences using unfamiliar microcontrollers or sensors ”. Another concern indicated was that of 
doing something wrong, “even minor missteps can directly impair the resulting FlowIO’s capabilities, and 
some mistakes require the user to remake entire components - some of which are incredibly time 
consuming,” read one of the responses. 
Almost all respondents said that they did not expect the assembly process to go smoothly, and they 
were expecting to run into challenges. “In past projects, I have rarely, if ever, not encountered issues after 
finishing assembly and extensive troubleshooting was required at times. Seeing as this is a very complex 
device that lacks pre -prepared/standardized parts, and it has not yet received e xtensive testing on the 
mass production side of things, I think it would be quite miraculous if no errors/mishaps occ ur.” 
Nevertheless, they all expressed confidence in being able to overcome any challenges, with response like: 
“I assume that the platform may have some unforeseen issues once the assembly is complete that will 
need to be fixed. Once these issues are addressed, I generally expect that the platform will function as 
reliably as one can expect a research prototype to f unction. It seems as though the platform is generally 
reliable.” 
The most surprising of the expectations people gave was about the time it would take them to finish 
assembling and testing FlowIO. The instructional tutorials inform people they should expect the assembly 
process to take at least 40  hours. Nevertheless, the responses to the question of expected time for 
assembly ranged from 12 hours in the span of just 1 week to 120 hours in the span of 2-3 months. Those 
with little technical experience underestimated the actual time required, while the more experienced gave 
more conservative estimates and wider ranges, with one engineer saying, “By heart I would say 40 hours, 
but I always take my estimate and times 3, and it usually turns out that way ”.  While some kits included 
subassemblies already premade and others only included discrete parts, the participants completed the 
pre-assembly survey with the assumption that they would have to assemble everything themselves, 
before they knew whether there would be any preassembled parts in their kit.  
Although only some of the blog posts were written by the same people who completed the survey, 
they reveal that the more conversative expectations match more closely with reality. For instance, some 
people who expected the assembly process to take just a week or two in reality took several months and 
others did not even find the time to complete it all due to external circumstances. The shortest span of 
time in which a  new user has assembled and programmed a FlowIO has been 2 weeks – when no SMD 
soldering was required as the PCBs were provided to them already soldered. When a user is expected to 
also do the SMD soldering, that typically adds at least another two weeks t o the process. The only 
instances where people have been able to complete the assembly process in a time less than two weeks 
(without soldering again) are when they have already assembled a complete FlowIO device previously and 
are now assembling their second or third one. 
The blog and forum posts that people wrote about their experience making a device are all 
available at https://softrobotics.io/posts and https://softrobotics.io/forum. Some posts revealed instances 
of new assembly challenges encountered by some users not previously documented in the official 
tutorials, and as a result, steps to address those challenges were also subsequently added to the tutorials 
helping the community in this process. For instance, some of the posts suggested how to do certain steps 
using alternative tools, how different 3D printers had to be set  up, where caution should be exercised 
when modifying parts, what alternative adhesives were suitable for the gluing operation, and more. The 
blog posts also offered a peer perspective of what the assembly process involved as they documented 
difficulties from a first-person storytelling perspective. While the tutorials also indicated which steps are 
271 
 
more difficult, they did not offer the human perspective and the storytelling narrative that the blog posts 
from users offered. And that human perspective ultimately had the effect of making the whole experience 
more relatable to new readers. For example, one user from Germany discussed how she spent hours trying 
to figure out why she kept getting errors whenever she tried programming the device, and then after going 
through dozens of troubleshooting steps she ultimately identified that the problem was a faulty USB cable 
[245]. Another user from California shared how the main driver board wasn’t working after assembling it 
despite doing everything correct. After an extensive troubleshooting process, the steps of which he 
detailed in the post, it was determined that the problem was that one of the IC components on the board 
itself had burned out and needed to be replaced. After replacing it, the problem was solved [246].   A third 
person talked about an operating system specific issue she had to overcome to be able to flash the 
bootloader and upload firmware to the device [ 247]. There was even a case where one person wrote a 
post specifically aimed at helping people in China with procuring parts for the device and where some of 
the parts can be procured specifically in that geographic location [248].  
Many of the stories shared by people reveal challenges external to the FlowIO assembly process 
itself – which are thus not included in the official tutorials – and yet they are still incredibly important and 
helpful for others who may embark on this journe y. The most satisfying part of these stories is reading 
about the excitement, joy, and satisfaction that people express after having gone through the entire 
process and eventually after weeks of challenging work arriving at a fully functional device, as in  [249]. 
The effect this has on other readers is it makes them feel empowered that they too would be able to 
complete this task despite the high level of complexity and time required. Thus, ultimately the user -
generated blog posts not only help others by discussing specific challenges, but also, they empower others 
mentally to not be afraid of undertaking a complex project like this.  
 
 
Figure 8-10. Selected images from the blog posts featuring FlowIO assembly stories. 
8.4.4 Discussion – what is the best approach for a kit 
The goal of these assembly case studies was to understand how long it takes people to assemble the 
device by themselves, what challenges they encountered in the process, and what is the best way to move 
forward between providing a fully assembled FlowIO or  a kit of parts. A related goal was to understand 
what benefit, if any, is there to the user from the act of going through the assembly process themself, 
which users are able to assemble the device, and which class of users struggle with that.  
Given the benefits and drawbacks discussed earlier, and the experiences shared by users, the practical 
question is: what is the most appropriate way for providing a platform like FlowIO to people ? The 
approach that offers the most advantages and least disadvantages for all parties is one of providing a kit 

272 
 
of parts where the most complex subassemblies are already pre-assembled. In that way, the users still 
get the benefits associated with assembling the device themselves, but they don’t have to go through the 
most difficult or error-prone steps. In the case of the FlowIO Platform, that would mean including in the 
kit the PCBs in a preassembled state, the internal miniature 3D printed parts that require micron -level 
precision, and also the internal vinyl tube already cut to the exact length required. Then users don’t have 
to do any SMD soldering, cutting tubes to exact length  with millimeter precision, or printing parts  that 
require high dimensional accuracy. Those tasks are most difficult and most likely to be done incorrectly by 
first-time users. But they can be done quickly and error -free by someone who has gone through the 
process a few times. In this way, both the provider of the kit and the end user benefit; the provider saves 
time by not having to assemble everything, the end-user reaps all the learning benefits of assembling the 
device themselves. This partially-assembled kit approach offers most of the advantages associated with 
both the preassembled device approach hand the kit approach listed in the previous two subsections, 
while eliminating many of the drawbacks that come with each of those approaches.  
Offering pre-assembled sub-assemblies also has one other significant advantage not discussed 
previously. While automating the assembly of the entire FlowIO device is extremely challenging, 
automating the assembly of some of the components within the device is much more doable a nd more 
scalable. Thus, this approach also offers a gradual path towards mass production. Furthermore, this can 
also happen in stages where the kit progressively evolves to include more preassembled sub-assemblies. 
This gradual approa ch also makes the process towards mass production much more doable from a 
financial capital perspective. Mass producing just one subassembly may require just a few thousand dollars 
of investment, compared to the tens or even hundreds of thousands of dollar s in investment that would 
be required if one was to embark on mass-producing the entire device.  
 
8.5 Extending FlowIO – case studies 
Beyond assembly, many users have gone a step further, extending the FlowIO platform with 
additional hardware and software features to meet the specific needs of their projects. Those users meet 
the characteristics of Von Hippel’s definition of Lead Users, discussed and referenced in the beginning of 
this chapter.  Modification and extensions made by the users  were possible thanks to the open -source 
nature of the hardware and the software and its licensing under the Creative Commons Attribution Non-
Commercial Share-Alike 4.0 license. Additionally, the SoftoRobotics.io website explicitly invites, 
encourages, and welcomes users to tinker, hack, and enhance FlowIO, provided they share their work with 
the community under the same license.  
Some user modifications were simple, such as customizing the enclosure color or personalizing 
device aesthetics, but others were highly sophisticated, requiring weeks or even months of dedicated 
work. This section highlights three advanced cases where user s significantly modified or extended 
FlowIO—two are hardware modifications and one is a software stack modification. 
 
8.5.1 Case 1: Silent Compressed-Air Module for High Pressure 
Project Corsetto, discussed in the previous chapter, required pressures much greater than the 
maximum pressures afforded by FlowIO’s standard pump modules, limited to approximately 30psi (~2bar) 
at the time. However, the Controller module itself was capable of handling mu ch higher pressures. 
Moreover, FlowIO’s modular architecture with complete power independence and voltage independence 
between modules made it possible for users to easily create alternative modules.   
In the Corsetto project, Ozgun Afsar took full advantage of those FlowIO benefits and 
characteristics including the support for high pressures, the power independence between modules, and 
the ability to swap modules or even make new ones. Ozgun’s first attempt involved using an external high-
pressure electrical pump connected to the inlet valve. This approach made the system less portable 
273 
 
because the external pump was an order of magnitude larger than the default pumps. However, an even 
larger problem with this approach was the high volume of audible noise that was produced. Multiple 
pumps were tested, and they were all too loud. While this  approach was not a suitable solution for 
Corsetto because of its intended use during an opera performance, there had to be no unintended audible 
noise from the hardware system at all to not interfere with the sound of the musical performance.  
An alternative approach was devised where a portable compressed air tank was used instead of 
pumps. However, two challenges had to be overcome to make this approach work in a fully satisfactory 
way. The first had to do with pressure control, and the second one had to do with audible noise.  
The compressed air tank is pressurized at 200 bar – a pressure far too high for FlowIO and also 
posing high risk to people. Thus, a mechanical pressure regulator was first added to the compressed air 
tank so that the air pressure would be stepped down from 200 bar to less than 10 bar. A variable, rather 
than fixed, pressure regulator was chosen for this purpose, so that the outflow pressure can be adjusted
