to a desired value using a mechanical knob. After the mechanical regulator, a second electronic pressure 
regulator was connected, whose output is electronically controlled to be proportional to the input 
pressure – up to 5 bar. The mechanical pressure regulator was used for stepping down the tank’s pressure 
to a safer level, while the electronic pressure regulator was used for programmable pressure control. The 
output pressure of the second regulator is simply equal to the output pressure from the first regulator 
times a proportionality constant that is controlled by software or by an analog voltage signal. 
The FlowIO Controller can operate normally up to 4bar pressures, and anything higher than that 
starts causing delays in the valve switching speeds. However, at least 5 bar pressure was needed for the 
proper operation of Corsetto.  This limitation was overcome with a combination of hardware & firmware 
modifications to FlowIO.  The hardware modification was to bypass the voltage regulator on the FlowIO’s 
driver board, while the software modification was to eliminate from the firmware the command that 
invoked the optimizePower() method of the FlowIO library. The combined effect of these two changes 
resulted in actuating the valves at a higher voltage and then keeping that voltage unmodified during the 
holding state. This ultimately resulted in increased viable operating pressure of the Controller module from 
4 bar to nearly 6 bar. These hardware and software changes also came with tradeoffs in the form of 
reduced battery life and an increased risk of overheating. However, those tradeoffs were also mitigated 
later by using a larger battery and by improving air circulation around the device for better cooling.  
The second issue that had to be overcome with the compressed air tank approach was the audible 
noise. This approach already offered a reduction in noise compared to the previously attempted approach 
of using pumps of similar pressure capabilities. However,  audible noise was still present not from any 
moving parts in the system but merely from the air outflow itself. Especially during the release phase of 
an actuation cycle, air leaves the FlowIOs ports from 5 bar to atmospheric pressure over a very short 
distance. This large pressure gradient causes air to leave the system at a very high flow rate – and this 
airflow itself creates a significant amount of audible noise. A solution to this problem was also found by 
connecting tubes several meters in length to the outlet ports – to provide a less steep gradient descent in 
pressure. The tradeoff that came with adding such tubes was a lower flow rate of air leaving the system 
during the depressurization phases. Nevertheless, since only 4 ports per FlowIO device we re used for 
driving muscles, the remaining unconnected port was also designated as an outlet. Thus, by having those 
secondary outlets on each FlowIO module, they negated any of the delays in airflow caused by the long 
tubes. Ultimately, the result of this was a nearly complete noise reduction without any added delays.  
 
8.5.2 Case 2: Composer Web-App for Controlling Multiple FlowIOs 
Corsetto [236] saw the creation of a Composer web app for controlling multiple FlowIO devices — 
featuring a scheduler, a converter, and a sequencer tool.  The Composer web app required significant 
modifications to the underlying FlowIO software stack, including certain FlowIO firmware changes and the 
274 
 
development of a Python-based program for interacting with a large number of FlowIOs via USB instead 
of Bluetooth. The Composer along with the Python controller are based on the original FlowIO web app; 
the two together serve as an alternative to the FlowI O web app. This work was carried out primarily by 
Yoav Luft, one of the Corsetto team members, and it was documented in the Corsetto paper. 
 
 
Figure 8-11. Composer Web App for Corsetto. It includes a scheduler, a sequencer, and a converter tool. Many of 
the icons, button designs, and controls are borrowed from the default FlowIO Web App, and certain new features 
have been added including the ability to save, export, and load a schedule. 
8.5.2.1 Changes to FlowIO Firmware 
The default FlowIO firmware features an abstraction layer that enables a user to control the device 
using 3-byte commands. The three bytes encode for the command action, the port, and the flow rate, 
respectively. This command structure is the foundation on  which all higher layers of the stack including 
Bluetooth control, JavaScript API, and GUI are built. For the Corsetto project, the same command structure 
was used. However, rather than controlling a pump’s flow rate, the third byte was repurposed to control 
the externally connected electronic pressure regulator.  
Several additional changes to other aspects of the firmware were also made according to Yoav. 
“During the design process, our firmware version was first expanded to support new features such as more 
sensors and controlling a flow regulator instead of a pump. Then, it was rewritten to include only the 
minimal features needed for our specific use cas e of controlling 18 FlowIO devices using USB serial 
communications, instead of Web Bluetooth which is limited to 7 devices at a time. In the serial 
communication mode, the 3 bytes are represented textually as [Action Symbol], [Valves Binary String], 
[Base-10 Integer] terminated by a newline character.” 
 
8.5.2.2 Decoupling the FlowIO Scheduler and Controller 
The native FlowIO software stack features a JavaScript web application running in the web browser 
capable of controlling a BLE -connected FlowIO device using the 3 -byte command protocol described 
above. The web application also includes a task scheduler, en abling a user to compose and sequence of 
tasks using the graphical user interface. However, one drawback that Yoav identified with the native FlowIO 
web application is that it lacks a clear separation between the parts of the JavaScript code responsible for 
(a) sending BLE commands, (b) scheduling tasks, and (c) controlling graphical elements; instead, they are 
all coupled. Thus, the first modification by Yoav was to decouple the code and to make a dedicated 

275 
 
Controller entity: “Our controller had gone through several iterations, starting with abstracting the original 
controller code into a separate library, and then using this library with our multi-role scheduler to create a 
new web-based controller fully compatible with the original FlowIO controller.” This decoupling then made 
it possible to make multiple changes later on to both the controller and the scheduler. 
 
8.5.2.3 Multi-Device Scheduler 
The main limitation of the default FlowIO scheduler is that it can create instructions for only one 
FlowIO device at a time. Corsetto required a scheduler that can send instructions to multiple devices. Thus, 
a new scheduler was developed by starting with the default one, decoupling it, and then gradually 
modifying it and adding new features to it. Appendix A of the Corsetto paper states: 
“We required additional features, namely, saving and loading of schedules, and writing a single 
schedule for controlling multiple devices. Based on the original scheduler, we created a new scheduler 
program, also a web application, which was made compatible with the native FlowIO firmware. Our new 
scheduler introduced an abstraction of a “Role”, in order to decouple between individual devices and their 
instructions in the multi-device scheduler. When executing the instructions, each role can be assigned any 
number of devices that will receive the instructions. The schedules created by our Scheduler can be saved 
and loaded as JSON documents.” 
 
8.5.2.4 Python-based Controller 
The first Controller version was the result of decoupling and isolating the instruction code from 
the native FlowIO web app. However, since WebBluetooth supports only 7 connected devices and may 
have some latency, a new controller was needed using USB rath er than Bluetooth. A Python -based 
program was being developed at that time for another part of the project responsible for processing the 
singer’s voice. Thus, Yoav decided to also create the USB-based FlowIO controller in Python and integrate 
it with the other Python software. “In parallel, the machine-learning explorations led to the development 
of a control program written in Python. When it became obvious that Web -Bluetooth had latency and 
reliability issues when using 18 FlowIO devices simultaneously, the controller written in Python for 
machine-learning exploration was adapted for sending instructions over USB.” 
“The controller starts by reading a schedule file given to it as an argument when launching the 
controller. It proceeds searching for all USB serial connection devices by their MacOSX file name 
conventions, e.g. “/dev/cu.usbmodemN” where “N” stands for a number assigned by the operating system. 
It then attempts to establish a serial communications connection with the device using 230400 baud rate. 
Next, each connected device is sent the special command “name?”, to which it most responds with a device 
name starting with the string “FlowIO_”, followed by an identifier. The identifiers are matched against the 
roles in the supplied schedule according to predetermined role names. Then, the controller displays a 
message to the console listing the devices connected and waits for the user to hit the “Enter” key to start 
playback of the instructions.” 
 
8.5.2.5 Converter tool of the Composer web app 
One of the new tools found in the new FlowIO web app written for Corsetto is a Converter, which 
allows the singer to compose the sequence of instructions sent to the FlowIO powering Corsetto as if she 
were composing for a musical instrument. The paper describes the mapping as follows. “In this new 
instrument, each module is represented as a percussion instrument stave, where notes represent increase 
in pressure; pauses represent release of pressure; and “x” -head notes represent holding the current 
pressure. The notation is then closely matched with musical notation for the singing voices, where pauses 
are used by singer K. for inhaling. In addition, we included notation for a “Trill”, which is a fast alternation 
between pressurizing and depressurizing the module, and f or dynamics that represent the maximal 
276 
 
pressure for the module. A version in which multiple modules are represented on a single stave was tested, 
but singer K. found the single module per stave line to be easier to read and compose for.” 
The converter parses MusicXML files with music notation and transforms the scores into a 
representation where every instrument is indexed by its name and is associated with timing information.  
“The user is then presented with the list of instrument names and is asked to associate each name with a 
role and a port, thus establishing a mapping between the instrument to a specific Corsetto module. The 
user is also asked to set the dynamics values which control how each dynamic symbol is translated to pump 
or pressure regulator instructions, and is asked for the BPM that will be used to convert the musical timing 
information to concrete timing information.” The user can then initiate the conversion process to produce 
a schedule of FlowIO instructions. During this process, the software validates whether all instructions are 
possible and that no combinations exist that cannot be done by FlowIO (e.g. simultaneous inflation and 
deflation on different ports of the same device). The resulting schedule can then be sent to the Composer’s 
Scheduler or Sequencer tools, or it can be exported and saved to a text file in the JSON format. 
 
8.5.2.6 Sequencer tool of the Composer web app 
The Sequencer tool allows multiple schedules of instructions to be merged into a larger schedule. This 
tool was developed to accommodate the natural creative process of music composition, where a large 
number of short compositions are typically created and then combine d, and where often the composer 
experiments with different variations of the short compositions. The Sequencer allows the same workflow 
to be achieved with haptic compositions for Corsetto. “ The Sequencer allows loading JSON schedules, 
arranging them in a sequence, playing the sequence in a gapless manner, and exporting the sequence to a 
JSON file in a format very similar to the original schedule format. To make the sequence play in a gapless 
manner, the timing information for each schedule needs to be updated, and the last instruction of each 
schedule needs to be replaced with the first instruction of the next schedule, otherwise, we will end up with 
two instructions timed for exactly the same time.” 
 
8.5.3 Case 3: “AirPort” – A highly modified FlowIO 
 
 
Figure 8-12. AirPort hardware and graphical user interface 
8.5.3.1 AirPort Overview 
Ian Scholl, a visiting master’s student at Stanford University from ETH Zurich, created a highly 
modified version of FlowIO, called AirPort, to better serve the needs of a wearable haptics project named 
Haptiknit [250, 251]. Compared to FlowIO, AirPort has more pneumatic I/O ports, uses an alternative 
pneumatic configuration with two manifolds rather than one to enable simultaneous actions on different 
ports, and uses a set of two nonidentical pumps. Other hardware changes include a larger integrated 
battery, direct access to all the buttons, and a different enclosure design. All of these changes were made 
to better suit the needs of the Haptiknit project, which needed more than the 5 channels offered by a 

277 
 
FlowIO device, as well as simultaneous actuation and deactuation of different channels. However, some 
of the new features offered by the AirPort build negate other features available in the default FlowIO build, 
due to them being mutually exclusive, ultimately resulting in a set of tradeoffs. Some of those tradeoffs 
include inability to maintain different fixed pressures at different ports, depressurization when the system 
is turned off, and larger size and weight.  
In addition to hardware changes, software changes were also made to account for the differences 
in hardware and features in the AirPort build. The code for AirPort is based entirely on the FlowIO code 
base, and the functions and methods in the libraries have been kept with the same names. Changes in the 
libraries were made to reflect the differences in hardware features and capabilities. Moreover, a new basic 
graphical user interface was developed, though it is application-specific for the actuation of the Haptiknit 
project. 
Ian prepared a 23 -page PDF report that he shared via email , documenting his modification of 
FlowIO and describing the process of going from the FlowIO build to the AirPort build. This case study 
explores how the AirPort adaptation differs from FlowIO and what some of  its advantages and 
disadvantages are. 
 
8.5.3.2 Pneumatic Architecture Modification 
FlowIO uses a single -manifold pneumatic architecture, where all 7 valves are connected to the 
same channel as shown in Figure 8-13. The rightmost valve is used as an inlet valve, the leftmost as an 
outlet valve, and the remaining 5 valves in between are used as actuation ports. The valves themselves 
are 3-port halves, and by default there are two manifold channels connecting the valves – one channel is 
normally-open, and the channel is normally-closed to the outside world when the valves are off. However, 
in the FlowIO architecture, the channel that is normally open is permanently blocked with a 3D printed 
plug inserted into each valve. This is done to prevent any air in a connected chamber from escaping when 
a valve is turned off, and to allow different pressures to be maintained at different ports - even when the 
system is turned off. Otherwise, when the system is powered down, all t he pressures at the different 
channels would equalize through the normally -open channel. For example, if we have 5 balloons 
connected to the 5 ports and we inflate each balloon to a different pressure, then when we deactuate the 
valves or even when we turn  off the device, the pressures in all 5 balloons will be maintained because 
there are no places for the air to escape during the off state of the port valves.  
 
 
Figure 8-13. FlowIO Pneumatic architecture with a single manifold. 
The AirPort adaptation uses the same valves as FlowIO, but in a different pneumatic configuration 
as shown in Figure 8-14, where (a) both manifold channels are used, (b) none of the 3 -port valves have 
been modified to 2-port valves, (c) the two pumps are connected directly to the two manifold channels, 

278 
 
(d) and there are no inlet or outlet valves. This offers the ability to do two different actions on different 
ports at the same time, and since there is no inlet or outlet valve, all 7 valves can be used as port valves. 
For example, port 7 can be inflating while port 2 is deflating. Or ports 6 and 7 can be at a fixed pressure 
while port 3 is being released to atmospheric pressure. Only up to two simultaneous actions can be 
performed.  
However, you cannot have one port held at a fixed pressure, while another port is being inflated, 
while a third port is being released. You can also not have multiple ports being held at more than two 
different fixed pressures, because a port would always be open either to the blue or to the green channel; 
there isn’t a state where a port is completely closed to either manifold. Another limitation of this 
configuration is that the single sensor can now only be used for monitoring positive pressures. If negative 
pressures are of interest to be measured, then at least one other sensor is needed for the second (blue) 
manifold channel. 
 
 
 
Figure 8-14. AirPort pneumatic configuration with two manifolds. 
8.5.3.3 Other Hardware Modifications 
Besides pneumatic architecture, AirPort also has other hardware modifications compared to 
FlowIO. While FlowIO has several different pump modules where each module contains a pair of identical 
pumps, AirPort uses a pair of nonidentical pumps. Specifically, for positive pressure AirPort uses the 
FlowIO’s large pump, and for vacuum it uses the FlowIO’s medium pump. This offers a relative pressure 
range of (-70kPa, 130kPa), which is on-par with the pressure ranges for the FlowIO medium pump module. 
In terms of flow rate, this combination of pumps is especially useful in cases where the inflation flow rate 
needs to be higher than the vacuum flow rate.  
AirPort uses the 12V version of the pumps, and thus it integrates an 11.1V 1600mAh LiPo battery 
into the pump module. The battery is significantly larger in size, capacity, and voltage compared to the 
default battery used in FlowIO, thus we can expect a significa ntly longer battery life. There isn’t an on -
board integrated charger for this battery, thus the battery needs to be charged externally with a separate 
charging device. The casing of AirPort was designed in a way that makes the process of removing an d 
putting back in the large battery user-friendly and fast.  
The electrical connection between the main module and the pump module in AirPort is done via 
a 4-wire cable connected on the outside of the device. The signaling used for controlling the pumps is 
identical as that used in FlowIO – only the integrated magnetic connector has been replaced with an 
external cable. For simplicity, the phys ical implementation of AirPort combines the pump and the main 
module into a single monolithic entity. This eliminates the modular aspect of the device and the ability to 
swap modules, though it makes the enclosure much simpler to design and 3D print. In some regards, this 
makes AirPort similar to version 1 of FlowIO where it was a monolithic rather than a modular device. 
 

279 
 
 
Figure 8-15. Detailed view of the AirPort hardware design 
8.5.3.4 Software Modifications 
The AirPort software is based on the FlowIO code stack. There is a set of Arduino libraries for 
AirPort with objects, methods, and functions with the same names as those created for FlowIO, but with 
function definitions modified according to the different hardware specifications and features of AirPort. 
While most of the FlowIO functions have been ported to AirPort, some of them have not been ported such 
as the powerOff() and optimizePower() functions. Pressure-based control functions have also been 
added for AirPort – though only for positive relative pressures, because the integrated sensor is only 
connected to the positive pressure manifold channel.  
In addition to the libraries, all of the examples that come with the FlowIO stack for Arduino have 
also been ported to AirPort. Additional examples have also been created to bring the total number of 
example code sketches to 14.  
Besides Arduino libraries, a graphical user interface was also created with the new system, as 
indicated in Figure 8-16. However, the interface is application-specific to the Haptiknit project, rather than 
being general purpose. There are five action buttons and a stop button. Each action button plays a specific 
actuation sequence producing the haptic sensations of strokin g, brushing, squeezing, and tapping. This 
interface behaves effectively like a subset of the FlowIO task scheduler in the original GUI, where the 
schedules in this case are hard-coded. 
 

280 
 
 
Figure 8-16. Graphical User Interface for AirPort, make specifically for the Haptiknit project. 
8.5.3.5 Interview with the AirPort maker 
A survey was sent to Ian with questions specific to the device he created, his experience using it, 
the benefits and drawbacks he saw, and how it compared to using a single or a pair of FlowIO devices. 
When asked about the pneumatic architecture he created  and what advantages and limitations he 
encountered with it, Ian provided the following insightful response. “My architecture won’t ever allow to 
hold different pressures at different ports, you can only delay opening one valve with respect to another in 
order to delay the pressure build -up (so temporarily they’ll evolve at different pressures), but indeed as 
soon as you either turn off the pumps or switch the valves to the other channel they will automatically 
equalize. For our use case, being able to hold different pressures was not a main requirement, which is why 
this architecture worked just fine.”  
To the questions of why he chose a 2 -channel architecture and what applications he believes 
would most benefit from his architecture compared to the FlowIO 1 -channel architecture, he said:  “For 
me, the main argument for switching to a two-manifold architecture was being able to independently 
inflate or deflate any number of ports at any time – which in my use case was more crucial than holding 
different pressures. So, any application that really requires this independence (and doesn’t need different 
pressures) would probably profit from my architecture. In that sense any application of e.g. several 
pneumatic muscles or actuators that always need to be inflated to one and the same pressure could easily 
profit from my two-manifold architecture, having the full control of which port(s) I want to actively inflate 
or deflate at any given time. The single -manifold FlowIO architecture will work well whenever that 
independence is not required and of course be even favorable whenever you need to hold a different set of 
pressures at the ports.” 
Finally, when asked whether a pair of 2 FlowIO devices would have been suitable for his use case 
or whether AirPort would still have some advantages, he replied: “One can also argue that my architecture 
makes ‘full use’ of the valves rather than blocking off one channel of them, allowing to independently 
deflate and inflate with a more compact setup rather than using two separate FlowIOs. You can probably 
recreate all behaviors of my architecture using two FlowIOs but would have been less compact than using 
a single AirPort. You would probably just have to connect each actuator to a port of each of the two 
FlowIOs, using one FlowIO for inflation, and the other for deflation at all times. With that you would cover 

281 
 
pretty much everything my architecture can do, apart from the fact that you would still only be able to 
control 5 actuators, which my AirPort in the end was able to handle 8 actuators with my extension of an 
additional valve. But of course, with 4 FlowIOs you could then control 10 actuators etc. (or just expand 
FlowIO in a similar way as I expanded my AirPort).” He further stated that, “the advantage of having two 
FlowIOs then would also be that you would have two pressure sensors, one for the inflation and  one for 
the deflation. The limitation to one pressure sensor in AirPort means you can only control the inflation 
pressure. That means for deflation I have no awareness of how much the pressure has dropped; but again, 
for my use case this was not really crucial, as all I needed to do was deflate the actuators at given times 
and so I just knew that if I vacuum for XX ms then the actuator was basically deflated and not active 
anymore. That also means that having at least a second sensor would have been beneficial for AirPort, in 
order to be able to control the pressure at deflation (but no limitation in my case).” 
 
8.5.3.6 Discussion of AirPort 
The key advantage offered by AirPort is the ability to perform two different actions simultaneously 
on different ports. However, this comes at the expense of not being able to maintain differential fixed 
pressures at the ports, or to maintain a connected pneumatic device pressurized when the  system is 
powered off. Depending on the type of application, the default FlowIO or the AirPort configuration may 
be better suited than the other one. AirPort is more desirable in applications where the pressure does not 
have to be maintained at fixed valu e for any period of time  and where pressure continuously changes. 
AirPort also cannot maintain any pressure unless power is being drawn continuously. FlowIO’s default 
configuration would be more desirable in applications where there is a need for different pressures at 
different ports, where pressure needs to be maintained for some period of time, and where the system 
can maintain pressures even without drawing any power.  
Some of the more underutilized features of FlowIO, such as in closed-loop applications where fluid 
is to be transferred between chambers without it leaving the system (e.g. buoyancy control in a 
submersible) would be difficult or impossible to achieve with  AirPort. Another related application that 
would be difficult to achieve with AirPort is where fluid is to be transported directly from the inlet of 
Pump1 to the outlet of Pump2.  Achieving those applications with AirPort would require additional 
hardware, but no additional hardware would be required with FlowIO. 
The main advantage of the AirPort configuration – simultaneous different actions on different 
ports – is something that cannot be achieved with the default FlowIO General (+/-) configuration, but it is 
possible by either pairing two FlowIO devices in the General (+/ -) configuration if at least 5 ports are 
needed or by using a single FlowIO device in the Split (+/-) configuration if not more than 2 ports are 
needed. The AirPort system shows an alternative approach to achieving this, albeit with some tradeoffs. If 
there are specific cases in which AirPort would offer a superior outcome, and if there is enough interest 
from the community, we may in the future choose to create an official FlowIO module based on the dual 
channel configuration. This could be implemented as yet another version of the Controller module family 
– called Dual Channel (+/-) configuration. 
The fact that FlowIO could be modified in such a significant way as to create effectively a new 
system – AirPort — is a testament t o what is possible when hardware is designed specifically with 
hackability and openness in mind, and when it is distributed under a model that welcomes tinkering and 
modifications from users.  
  
282 
 
8.6 Chapter Discussion 
Most FlowIO users prefer GUI instead of coding, even if they have the skills to code, and even if the 
GUI is more limited. The vast majority of users would switch to a coding-based approach only when they 
exhaust all the possibilities of doing what they want to do with the GUI. People prefer simplicity.  
Most users did not utilize the majority of FlowIO’s features, but they rather used the platform for 
relatively simple scenarios. For instance, many users did not take advantage of most of the ports available 
and of the dynamic capabilities of the device. This suggests that there is a need and demand on the market 
even for a solution simpler than FlowIO. On the other hand, some users, especially those working on 
advanced technology research projects, did take full advantage of the capabilities offered by the platform. 
A few users even went beyond what the platform offered and that inspired new features to be added to 
the platform in subsequent iterations.
