9: Reflections 
This final chapter reflects and expands upon some of the findings, lessons, and takeaways form this 
research. I start by analyzing the role of modularity on each of the core projects presented in this work 
and the impact of modularity in  enabling users to find new affordances for the platforms, modify them, 
and extend them for their needs . I also discuss the critical  role of modularity and decomposition in the 
process of establishing and growing an ecosystem. Relatedly, I explore how the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem 
not only allowed completion of projects based on ideas people already had, but how it gave rise to entirely 
new project ideas. The second half of this chapter is a reflection on some of the lessons learned, limitations 
of this work, and opportunities for future work within each enabling artifact, framework, or philosophy. 
Two distinct types of future work opportunities are considered within each subsection. First are future 
work opportunities that arise directly from the limitations. And second, are aspirational future work 
opportunities – based on the motivational philosophies  and high-level objectives from Chapter 1 – and 
the many long -term pathways in which this work could be extended. The  chapter ends with a brief 
conclusion reiterating some of the key points covered in this dissertation and reflecting on their broader 
implications.  
9.1 Role and Impact of Modularity in this Research 
Four core platform and ecosystem projects were  discussed in depth in this thesis  – FlowIO, 
SoftRobotics.IO, SleeveIO, and ModiStrap – as well as various applications, case studies, and even 
proposals for other types of modular platforms with different approaches to actuations – as part of the 
SoftRobotics.IO family of platforms. One commonality among all th ese core projects is that they are all 
modular and decomposable. While the later projects like SleeveIO and ModiStrap had modularity as a 
design principle right from the start of those projects, the earlier works of FlowIO and SoftRobotics.IO saw 
modularity as an emergent property of those systems.  
 
Chapter  9 
285 
 
9.1.1 Role of Modularity in FlowIO and its Impact on Users 
9.1.1.1 Emergence of Modularity in FlowIO 
In the case of FlowIO, we already saw how initially it was a monolithic device, which was then 
decomposed into a Controller Module  and a Pump Module  in order accommodate a wider range of 
pressure and flow-rate needs by providing a simple and user -friendly way for people to remove original 
miniature pumps and replace them with more powerful pumps but at the expense of larger size and higher 
power requirements. This then led to the development of a dedicated set of pump modules for different 
needs. In this way, modularity emerged out of necessity to enable the existence – within the same system 
– the existence of need-based characteristics that are mutually exclusive. Some applications required the 
system to be as miniature as possible, with long battery life, and offering low pressures up to 10 psi. Other 
applications required the system to offer pressures up to 30 psi and did not have as strict miniaturization 
requirements.  
One approach to meeting those needs could have been to design two completely different systems, 
or the other was to have a system that was modular and decomposable with swappable modules. It was 
recognized that with time, users would find new applications for the system, and user needs and feature 
requirements would likely increase. But it was not known at the time what exactly those future needs and 
requirements would be. And designing a solution that must meet the needs of not only currently known 
problems, but also completely unknown problems that may emerge in the future , is one of the most 
difficult design engineering challenges . Modularity was, therefore, chosen as the design engineering 
strategy to address this challenge. The system was modified and redesigned in such a way that it would 
accommodate the ability of adding novel modules in the future without even knowing in advance what 
those future modules would be. The hardware was decomposed into discrete functional units, and each 
functional unit was then encapsulated into a block, hiding its complexity and only exposing its relevant 
inputs and outputs. Those inputs and outputs were then themselves encapsulated into interfaces that are 
as general as possible, standardized across functional blocks, and user-friendly. Similar decomposition and 
modularization were also done with the software.  
As anticipated, the system found itself used in new applications and use-cases over time – some of 
which expected others not. And those new applications demanded novel hardware and software features 
be added to the system including new types of sensors, actuators, and controls. Some of those new feature 
demands were resolved by making improvements within the modules, while others were met by 
developing different versions of existing modules, and some – by developing entirely new types of modules 
with interf aces compatible with the rest of the system. Creating entirely new modules and types of 
modules that work with the rest of the system was made possible because of the highly general interfaces 
designed and developed during the initial modularization phase. The importance of this point cannot be 
overstated. If the interfaces between modules are not sufficiently general, then that limits the types of 
new modules that can be developed and added to the system in the future.  
 
9.1.1.2 Design Engineering Lessons on Module Interface Design 
One of the most vital decisions for the architect of a modular system is in designing the module 
interfaces. The interface between modules includes factors like physical connectors, type and number of 
physical and digital signals carried between modules, communication protocol, as well as various other 
mechanical, electrical, informational, ergonomic, and aesthetic factors that must be considered. When 
designing new standardized module interfaces, one must consider not only the existing needs, but also 
the future needs of those interfaces – when new modules may be introduced – even without knowing 
what those new modules would be. Getting the interface design right – including all the factors associated 
with an interface – is critically important; changing an interface (or any part of it) at a much later time 
could be nearly impossible, because of how disruptive such an action would be in terms of compatibility. 
286 
 
Not only could a change in interface break compatibility with existing modules, but it would also require 
each module that makes use of that interface to be redesigned which would be costly. 
Additionally, if an interface change is needed  at a later time, this also creates the problem of 
preserving backward compatibility. And the best solution to this problem is to never have to encounter it 
in the first place. This is not to say that one should not optimize for backward compatibility, quite the 
contrary; if there is an opportunity to make something backward compatible – it should be pursued. 
However, the best outcome is to design the interfaces from the very beginning such that there would 
not be a need to modify interfaces and thus never giving rise to the problem of backward compatibility. 
That is because, at the very beginning is when the maximum freedom for interface optimization exists. 
This design freedom is far more limited later, not only because of the backward compatibility constraint, 
but because of how the system and modules evolved over time in response to the initial interface choices 
– and this evolution then imposes other constraints.  
There are several actions a system architect can take when designing a new standardized module-
interface that can ensure long-term compatibility and that would minimize the need for costly disruptive 
interface changes later on. First, in the case of physical connectors, one should choose a connector that 
has more pins than the actual number of pins by the current set of modules. Then, if new types of modules 
are invented in the future that necessitate connectors with more pins at the interface than the curr ent 
modules, there would be some un utilized pins available for that purpose . Second, for communication 
protocols, the architect should also evaluate whether the existing communication protocol may need 
some additional features later on if new modules are added. If the answer is yes, then the protocol should 
be implemented in such a way that it would leave room for changes or additional features later on in both 
the software and the hardware implementation . For example, an additional bit may be designated fo r 
future use. Third, if there is a choice between which microcontroller pins to expose at the interfaces and 
which pins to for the internal function of a module, one should always try to utilize internally the pins with 
the least amount of features first and leave the more feature-rich pins for the interfaces. And Fourth, if a 
time comes when a module interface must be redesigned to accommodate newer types of modules, this 
disruptive event should be utilized strategically not only to make the change at hand , but to also make 
other design changes preemptively that may further extend, expand, and generalize the interface to 
minimize the likelihood of requiring another interface upgrade anytime in the near future. All of the points 
discussed in this section  go squarely against  the doctrine of planned obsolescence practiced by many 
technology companies today. 
Those lessons were drawn from and applied to the case of FlowIO. Electrical connectors were 
chosen that had more pins that were needed in early versions of FlowIO. This proved to be a very wise 
choice, because as the system evolved, all of those extra pins eventually became necessary. This allowed 
upgrading the electronics in the device and making significant changes internally without affecting the 
interfaces–and thereby preserving compatibility with prior module versions. A similar strategy was 
employed f or the command -control communication protocol. Initially, the protocol used 2 -byte 
commands but was nevertheless implemented as a 3 -byte protocol. Subsequently, that third byte found 
its use as controlling flow -rate – a feature that was added only later. H owever, not everything was 
anticipated when it came to how FlowIO evolved and there were some upgrades that were highly costly 
and disruptive. Specifically, when the need for the AnalogIn module arose, the expansion port that existed 
at the time was inadequate for such a module, because it did not provide enough analog pins. Moreover, 
some of the analog pins were already being used-up for other purposes internal to the module and were 
thus unavailable for external use. It became evident that to accommodate the new module, a major 
modification had to be made to the device – that would have to completely remap the pinout of both 
internal components and the interfaces – and then also requiring significant changes to the software stack 
and libraries. Moreover, there was no way to achieve this while preserving backward compatibility, which 
also meant that existing users were not going to be able to take advantage of future update – unless they 
287 
 
also upgraded their device . This disruptive event that breaks compatibility had to be approached very 
carefully and very strategically. Thus, thus opportunity was used to make not just the one change needed 
by the new module being developed, but to also consider and implement many other changes that would 
better future-proof flowio from similar highly disruptive and costly upgrades. After those changes were 
made, no further interface modifications were needed in the years since, despite the introduction o f 
multiple new modules. This case illustrates the value of applying the module interface design principles 
discussed in the preceding paragraph.  
 
9.1.1.3 Modularity’s Impact on Users – in the case of FlowIO 
FlowIO’s modularity in itself provided a structure for users to more easily think of new features 
and improvements to the platform and to make suggestions. To explain this point, consider an arbitrary 
device, and suppose it has a modular and a non-modular version. Making suggestions for improvements 
would always be easier for the modular version. There are several reasons for this as listed below: 
- In a modular version, each module has a clear function. This makes it easier to identify which part 
is responsible for what, and where a limitation or new opportunity lies.  
- Since components are decoupled, suggestions can be made about individual modules, rather than 
needing to address the entire system holistically.  
- With modularity, it is  simpler to imagine swapping  one part for another. This encourages 
experimentation and alternative proposals.  
- The set of existing modules often directly prompts one to think of what new modules can be 
added to the existing set. 
- Knowing that changes are feasible without a complete redesign of the system prompts users to 
think of more bold suggestions.  
- A system with swappable modules  typically feels more familiar to people  – even when they do 
not know what it does – because they can associate it with other modular artifacts like LEGO. This 
familiarity allows them to start recognizing patters. By contrast, a nonmodular system does not 
make this connection and may feel very foreign to user.  
These effects of modularity were clearly visible in how users interacted with FlowIO. Many users 
intuitively grasped that new capabilities could be added—even if those features were not present in the 
current version—because the modular form implicitly invited extension. Just as importantly, the modular 
decomposition made the system more cognitively approachable. Rather than confronting a single opaque 
unit, users could understand FlowIO as a collection of smaller, logically distinct components —each with 
its own purpose and behavior. This shift in framing helped even non -experts develop accurate mental 
models of the system. In multiple observed cases, individuals who initially struggled to comprehend the 
platform when presented as a unified whole were able t o understand it far more easily once it was 
physically or visually decomposed into its functional modules. These insights emerged primarily through 
informal conversations and demonstrations, but they consistently illustrated the power of modularity to 
reduce perceived complexity, increase confidence, and encourage a deeper level of user engagement with 
the platform. 
 This increased clarity translated into more active forms of participation. Users were more likely to 
suggest new figures, suggest new modules, or even build their own modules. The modular design made 
such contributions feel accessible and meaningful because users could isolate their ideas to a single 
functional area without needing to understand the entire hardware or firmware stack. This dynamic helps 
transform catalyzes the emergence of contributors and helps transform users into co -creators. These 
patterns align with findings in HCI, innovation, and product design literature. Eric von Hippel [252] showed 
that users are often the source of the most relevant innovations, particularly when systems are open and 
modular enough for them to experiment with. Don Norman [253] emphasized that systems designed with 
288 
 
clear affordances and decomposable structures are more likely to invite exploration, adaptation, and 
creative engagement. In the case of FlowIO, modularity functioned not only as a technical enabler but also 
as a design principle that shaped how users perc eived, understood, and contributed to the platform , as 
well as how they used to for their projects. 
 
9.1.1.4 Modularity as a Future-Proofing Design Strategy 
Any technology eventually  becomes obsolete. Modularity offers a strategic means of future -
proofing against this inevitability by enabling systems to adapt incrementally rather than requiring 
complete redesigns. The development of new FlowIO modules in response to different user needs is a 
direct example of FlowIO’s adaptability and organic evolution. In rapidly changing technology research and 
innovation landscapes, adaptability and resilience to obsolescence are vital for the survival of any 
technological artifact. For instance, the current version of FlowIO employs valves, pumps, and electronics 
on rigid PCBs. But as new types of components are introduced on the market, alternative modules based 
on newer types of components can be easily made  - incorporating soft pneumatic components, flexible 
circuits, or more efficient actuators. Additionally, there can be entirely new kinds of modules, including 
ones with completely different pneumatic architectures, others using non-electronic logic gates, or even 
modules based on technologies not even conceived yet - as these become available. In a modular 
architecture, the emergence of new technologies represents an opportunity —new modules can be 
introduced to extend the system’s capabilities. In contrast, nonmodular systems are often locked into their 
original design: incorporating new technologies typically requires a full system overhaul, which is costly 
and discourages iteration. Modular systems, by design, isolate complexity and localize change, allowing 
individual modules to be replaced, upgraded, or reimagined without destabilizing the whole. This 
incremental extensibility is essential for sustaining progress in open -ended technological exploration, 
where requirements evolve continuously and cannot be fully anticipated at the outset. 
 
9.1.2 Role of Modularity in the SoftRobotics.IO Ecosystem 
Modularity has played a pivotal —if initially unintentional —role in the creation, evolution, and 
coherence of the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem. While modularity is often discussed in the context of product 
or software design, its application at the ecosystem level introduces a different set of benefits like cognitive 
manageability, parallel development, and long -term adaptability. These benefits became evident only 
through the lived process of building the ecosystem itself. 
The SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem is decomposable into the five macroconstituents  or functional 
domains: Technology Package, Content  & Resource s, Community, Online Platform, and Deployment 
Infrastructure. Within each of those constituents there are multiple sub -constituents. Each of the 
macroconstituents was developed semi -independently, shaped by its own design requirements, user 
needs, and development challenges. Together, they form a loosely coupled but interdependent system  
that enables continuous and incre ment evolution, that can happen in completely uncoordinated and 
synchronous ways. The macroconstituents are effectively modules, while the interrelationships between 
them (as indicated in the D&D Framework from Chapter 2) are the interfaces of those modules. And within 
each of five functional blocks there are multiple subconstituents, which are effectively modules within 
modules, creating a nested modular architecture. From that perspective, FlowIO as a whole, is just a single 
module inside the technology package functional block, which itself is a module within the SoftRobotics.IO 
ecosystem. 
The modularity of the ecosystem is an emergent property, rather than prescriptive design decision. 
Being able to decompose the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem into those macroconstituents – with clear 
boundaries between them – was critical in the process of building and growing this ecosystem.  
289 
 
In fact, building such a complex ecosystem would have been nearly impossible without a modular 
decomposition, as it would have likely led to the complexity catastrophe  resulting in management 
paralysis. This is when meaningful progress becomes impossible due to too many interdependencies as 
any substantive change requires a cascade of additional changes elsewhere [ 254, 255]. However, by 
decomposing it into different modul es, this complex challenge became manageable and tractable. It 
became possible to clearly separate the constituents from the interactions between those constituents, 
which then allowed separating the tasks of creating the different parts of the ecosystem from the tasks of 
integrating those parts together. 
There is a stark contrast between the modularity of a product and the modularity of an ecosystem. 
In the case of a product of a platform (FlowIO, SleeveIO, ModiStrap), the modularity is explicit and obvious 
to the users. In contrast, the modularity of SoftRobotics.IO is behind the scenes, known to the ecosystem 
architect but invisible and unobvious to the users and other members of the ecosystem. Users interact 
with the ecosystem as a unified whole, without knowing its underlying structure, the different 
constituents, the number of constituents, or the interactions between them. There is an asymmetry 
between the internal architecture of the ecosystem and the external perception of it. This asymmetry is, 
however, beneficial to users, because all the complexity of the ecosystem is encapsulated and hidden from 
users, thereby reducing cognitive overhead for users, and making the ecosystem feel approachable, 
inviting, unintimidating, and simple from the outside.  
This modularity of the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem can also be applied more broadly to  
understanding microbusinesses who only have a few people with no department structures . Customers 
perceive them as singular monolithic entities with financial capital as the input and products & services as 
the output. But internally, they are also modular and often composed of interlocking subsystems, 
Modularity is not just a design principle, but a complete enabling strategy for small and resource -
constrained initiatives attempting to the work comparable in complexity to that of much larger 
organization.  
Ultimately, modularity in SoftRobotics.IO has proven to be a critical enabler for strategic evolution. 
It allows each part of the ecosystem to improve at its own pace, adapt to changing needs, and incorporate 
feedback independently. This scaffolding has made it possible to develop and evolve this ecosystem far 
beyond what would have been possible with a tightly coupled design approach. 
 
9.1.3 Modularity in SleeveIO and ModiStrap 
Unlike FlowIO —where the plug -and-play modularity emerged gradually and as a response to 
conflicting user needs that could not be addressed otherwise —SleeveIO and ModiStrap adopted a 
modular architecture from the very outset. In these later projects, modularity was not an outcome of 
evolution but a foundational design str ategy, incorporated deliberately during the earliest stages of 
conceptualization and ideation. 
These projects were made modular and reconfigurable because of the countless lessons that were 
learned about modularity over the years of developing the FlowIO Platform and building the broader 
SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem. Additionally, significant technical knowhow and design expertise was already 
acquired about how to architect, design, decompose, abstract, and isolate complexity when building a 
modular system. Thus, for SleeveIO and ModiStrap almost the entire architecture of those systems could 
be conceived and imagined even before starting the physical designs. While the exact modules and the 
technical details of the interfaces between them still needed to be defined through development, the 
high-level decomposition of the systems  was already established from the beginning.  This was very 
different from how FlowIO was built, where modular decomposition emerged as a necessity  later, and 
where it was not even clear initially where to draw the boundaries between modules, how many modules 
to have, or how to interconnect the modules. For example, in the case of FlowIO, where were alternative 
290 
 
ways it could have been modularized which were all considered and evaluated experimentally during the 
development phase. By contrast, for SleeveIO and ModiStrap, the experience gained in modular design, 
allowed quick identification of where the separations  between modules should be, allowing for more 
targeted design decisions, clearer interface definitions, and faster progression from ideation to 
implementation. 
The question of how to decompose a system may appear obvious in hindsight but is often 
remarkably non-obvious during the initial stages of system design. This is well illustrated by the case of the 
desktop computer. Today, it seems intuitive that a computer is composed of distinct components such as 
a processor, RAM, motherboard, hard drive, display, power supply, etc. These separations feel natural and 
even inevitable to modern users.  But during the early history of computing, these distinctions were far 
from clear. The conceptual boundaries between computation, memory, control, and storage evolved 
gradually, shaped by technical constraints, experimentation, and market forces. In early machines, 
functionality was often tightly integrated, and modular separation was minimal or nonexistent. The history 
of how the computer came to be modularized as we recognized it today is discussed in the textbook 
“Design Rules: The Power of Modularity ” by Baldwin and Clark  [255]. H ad different abstractions or 
standards taken hold in the early days, today’s computers might have had entirely different modular 
boundaries. This example powerfully illustrates that modular decomposition is rarely obvious during the 
design process, even though it often appears intuitive and self -evident when observing a completed 
system in retrospect. 
The ability to anticipate the modular decomposition in SleeveIO and ModiStrap did not emerge in 
isolation but was a direct consequence of past hands-on learning, various trials and errors in earlier 
projects, and a gradual internalization of modular thinking – which then cultivates intuition for modular 
design. In both cases, modularity does not just benefit the developer by making the systems easier to build 
and evolve —it also benefits users. It enabled users to engage with, utilize, and extend the systems  
incrementally, starting with just a subset of modules, thereby also lowering entry barriers. This also makes 
the systems more accessible, extensible, and forgiving of experimentation. In contrast, tightly integrated 
systems tend to discourage such behavior, as even modifications can trigger cascading engineering 
challenges that make user-driven innovation difficult. 
 
9.2 Limitations and Opportunities for Future Work 
This thesis opens the door to many ideas and opportunities for future work in both (1) the 
evolution of the platforms and ecosystems  covered in this work, as well as in (2) the generalized 
frameworks, lessons, and findings that arise from development, deployment, and evaluation through case 
studies. Various areas of future work were already discussed throughout this dissertation  in almost all 
chapters, so this section is not meant to give exhaustive coverage of future work opportunities, but merely 
to recall some of the ones already covered and to suggest additional ones as well.  
There are two primary drivers of future work stemming from this research. The first I would call 
limitations-driven future work, which arises from the constraints and boundaries of the current research, 
its findings, and its outcomes. These limitations point to gaps that can be addressed in follow -up 
investigations, improvements, or complementary studies. Those limitations serve not as shortcomings but 
as a source of future exploration and invitations to explore and iterate further. I outline some of the key 
limitations identified throughout the research and suggest targeted directions for addressing each . The 
second I call mission-driven future work , motivated by the broader aspirations, goals, and vision s 
articulated in Chapter 1. This second category focuses on expanding the research in new directions aligned 
with the overarching mission, even if those directions are not tied to limitations of this research.  The 
mission driven future work does not even necessarily have to continue on the same trajectory th at this 
291 
 
research has been on – it can go back to the source and find a very different direction that still fully aligns 
with the goals, objectives, and mission outlined in the introduction chapter.  
 
9.2.1 Philosophy of Impact and Progress 
 In chapter 1, I laid the philosophical and strategic foundations and motivations for this dissertation 
by articulating a rational for focusing on general purpose tools and building blocks as high-leverage avenue 
for maximizing impact and for enabling and accelerating progress across fields . While this chapter does 
not present technical findings per se, it does offer a framework of reasoning that shapes the rest of the 
thesis. While the arguments presented were grounded in observed patterns, deductive logic, and case -
based reasoning, there remain several areas that merit deeper empirical examination and formal 
validation.  
For instance, the expression for quantifying technological impact provides a useful lens and serves 
its function as a working definition but is not based on rigorous formalization and empirical validation 
across a broad sample of projects. Future work could formalize this model, refine its parameters, and test 
it across a diverse set of historical and contemporary technology cases to assess its explanatory and 
predictive utility. Such validation would be especially valuable in contexts where it is important to assess 
impact—particularly in academia, where existing metrics such as publication count or citation indices are 
deeply flawed and often fail to capture real-world influence, long-term utility, or downstream innovation 
enabled by a given contribution. A more nuanced and multidimensional model of impact could help create 
more fair hiring and promotion practices and shift academic incentives toward work with broader societal 
relevance and lasting benefit. 
 I also articulated three mechanisms through which enabling artifacts – development platforms in 
particular – enable and accelerate technological progress, by (1) improving time efficiency, (2) broadening 
participation in innovation, and (3) inspiring new ideas. All three of these mechanisms were observed in 
the case of FlowIO, supported by evidence from case studies and interviews.  Notably, different users 
reported experiencing different combinations of these benefits—some citing one, others two, and some 
all three. However, the details of how these mechanisms interact, their relative importance, and how they 
vary across contexts and types of users remain underexplored in this research. This presents one possible 
opportunity for further research, which would require more structured, longitudinal user studies involving 
significantly larger participant groups, conducted over multiple years, ideally with a multitude of different 
development platforms, and over a range of different fields. 
 Beyond the limitation-driven future work, there are also ample opportunities for mission -driven 
future work when it comes to turning philosophy into practice and actionable strategy within both 
academia and industry. Coaching materials, institutional programs, and evaluative frameworks could be 
created that help researchers and technologists adopt a mindset oriented towards maximizing impact and 
societal progress when designing new artifacts.  
This mission-driven approach should also extend to the research funding ecosystem. Foundations, 
governments, and other funding bodies play a central role in deciding which projects move forward, and 
by extension, in shaping the future of innovation. To the great detriment of society, funding institutions 
often prioritize projects with immediate, domain-specific relevance over upstream tools and platforms that 
might enable a far broader range of downstream innovations. 
For example, a foundation that is interesting in finding cures for cancer today would rather fund a 
new research therapy with a low chance of success than to give those funds to support the development 
of an enabling artifact that is two-steps removed from the actual therapy research (e.g. a DNA sequencer 
that is as affordable as a 3D printer). Even if the downstream effect of an enabling artifact would be ten or 
even a hundred times more impactful for advancing cancer research at large, the upstream tool is often 
overlooked due to its perceived indirectness.  This lack of foresight reflects a systemic bias in funding 
292 
 
priorities and highlights the need for future work that not only advocates for, but also institutionalizes, 
better models for recognizing and supporting high-leverage, enabling innovations that catalyze long-term, 
cross-domain progress. 
 
9.2.2 D&D Framework 
The design and deployment framework for development platforms presented in Chapter 2 should 
be understood as a living model that has emerged through years of iterative refinement over the course 
of designing, deploying,  working with users, and reflecting upon multiple hardware development 
platforms, as well as through comparative analysis with deployment models from product design, 
entrepreneurship, human-centered design, modular systems theory, and open-source hardware. 
As such, the framework is not static; it continues to evolve as new findings, user experiences, and 
contextual variations are encountered.  Importantly, this model is modular and thus allows for 
components, subcomponents, and interrelationships between them to be added, subtracted, or changed 
in response to new information and new findings. Mutability is an integral part of the framework and is 
indeed the process through which each successive iteration of the framework has emerged leading to the 
most recent iteration. As this is an evolving model , it is not only likely but  also expected that the latest 
iteration will not be the final one.  The framework will continue to co -evolve with the progress of the 
development platforms discussed in this dissertation and with ongoing analysis of both these and other 
platforms in future work.  
This framework does not claim to capture every characteristic of impactful platform design. Future 
work may focus on evaluating and applying this model beyond the domains in which it was originally 
developed, as a way of identifying possible gaps within it  and then augmenting it to fill those gaps. One 
important limitation of the current version of the framework is that it has not yet been externally validated 
by other hardware platform designers—particularly those who are also focused on achieving real -world 
impact. Future work on this front could involve collaborative workshops or participatory design studies in 
which other platform creators apply the framework to their own work and provide structured feedback, 
enabling further refinement and broader validation. 
A direction for mission-driven future work is to scale the utility of this framework and embed it into 
broader communities of practice . Especially in research institutions, the end -goal of most research 
projects is simply publication, except for a small subset of projects that transition into commercial spinoffs. 
Technical researchers are therefore often led to believe that their only post-publication options are either 
to continue advancing their technology through more research or to leave academia to commercialize it. 
The Design & Deployment Framework shows that these are not the only paths but there are many things 
one can do post publication to make their work real-world impactful – that go beyond the technical work 
and don’t require raising capital or establishing a business entity – allowing researchers to advance their 
projects further impact-wise still within the boundaries of academia.  When this model was presented in 
invited talks and workshops, audience members frequently expressed that it reshaped their understanding 
of how to move their own research projects forward – even if they were not engaged in developing 
platforms. Many shared that it opened their eyes to new possibilities for research impact , and that they 
wished they had encountered this approach earlier. These reactions suggest a clear demand for such 
perspectives – not only for platform designers but for the broader HCI community. T herefore, 
dissemination of this work represents a compelling next step. 
 
293 
 
9.2.3 Earned Open-Source Deployment Model 
The Earned Open-Source deployment, introduced in Chapter 4, provides a continuum between closed 
and open -source – allowing a very balanced middle ground approach suitable for the needs of both 
developers and users. Developers don’t have to prematurely release source files  for all, while users still 
have the opportunity to access the source files through participation in the development process. This 
model is also a way of fostering active participation and long-term community engagement. It functions 
as a s elf-preservation mechanism for the underlying project  – from abandonment, exploitation, or 
premature forking . The model addresses many of the issues with both the traditional open -source 
deployment approach and with the proprietary closed -source approaches. By making access to source 
files something that is earned rather than given, sold, or withheld indefinitely, the model balances 
openness with accountability, and freedom with stewardship. It respects the freedom of users to access 
and modify the sources,  while at the same time greatly reduces the pitfalls and risk with conventional 
open-source.  
Nevertheless, the are challenges and limitations with this deployment approach, presenting 
opportunities for further refinement of these ideas and for creating generalized infrastructure for handling 
this process . Implementing Earned Open -Source, as proposed  in this dissertation , requires significant 
infrastructure to facilitate the process of progressively earning elevated access privileges and to track 
contributions and participation. In the case of FlowIO, this process has been done manually – which his 
manageable with a relatively small community. But as a community grows, it becomes imperative to 
automate this process. And this presents many, potentially opportunities for future work. Creating a 
generalized infrastructure for delegating tasks, managing contributions, and earing access privileges could 
be an exciting year-long project that could be integrated into Git.  
Additionally, there are also opportunities to leverage blockchain technology to track contributions and 
to assign rewards in the form of a new cryptocurrency. And then, from there, there are many different 
paths in which this project could grow and how this new type of cryptocurrency could be used. Perhaps, 
this is what a “proof of work” cryptocurrency should be – namely useful-work that actually has a real utility 
and creates value , rather than merely solving abstract computational puzzles that only waste energy 
without doing anything valuable in the process, as is the case of today’s proof of work cryptocurrencies. 
This proof of useful work concept would contribute to the development and sustainability of real projects. 
Much more thinking, brainstorming, and conceptual refinement would be needed for this idea should such 
a project be undertaken.  
 Opportunities for refinement of this deployment model itself are also plentiful, and input needs 
to be gathered from parties that lean towards either end of the open/closed spectrum this model spans.  
Voices should be heard from those opposed to open-source to see what other pitfalls they may raise with 
open-source, whether those pitfalls are mitigated with this new model, and whether they would be willing 
to adapt it if the appropriate automation infrastructure exists. Similarly, open-source purists should also 
be heard to see what pushback and arguments they may have against this model.  Listening to both of 
those groups of people could create fertile ground for refinements and modifications to the earned open-
source model. Perhaps this could be done as a workshop event where people with different viewpoints 
and strong opinions on this matter are brought to brainstorm and arrive at a solution acceptable to 
everyone. A place to start would be by giving a talk about this idea at the Hackaday annual conference , 
which could provide a productive forum for such an exchange.  
  
294 
 
9.2.4 FlowIO Platform 
Of all the projects presented in this thesis, FlowIO is the most mature, most widely used, and most 
studied. It is also the one for which most information is known about its limitations. And there are well 
over 100 open feature request tickets for FlowIO. 
One of the main limitations with FlowIO at this moment is its limited supply that hasn’t been able to 
meet the demand. The underlying cause of this problem has been the scarcity of time required to build 
devices or to even just prepare kits of parts. In the last two years some effort has been done to streamline 
this process, though this was not a high priority until now as far as this dissertation was concerned. Thus, 
the most immediate future work activity would be to make FlowIO more widely available. This would entail 
first and foremost having more kits of parts available, as well as having more fully assembled devices. 
Related to this would be cost reduction. Economies of scale alone would contribute to a major drop in the 
cost of a kit / device – both due to financial savings when purchasing parts in bulk and time savings when 
assembling multiple subassemblies of the same type simultaneously.  Automation in the fabrication 
process provides another avenue for cost reduction.  
On the technical front, there are hundreds of opportunities for future work – driven by both existing 
limitations and future aspirations. Some of them relate to Wi-Fi capability, USB -C port upgrade, BLE 
authentication, addition features in the GUI, P2P connectivity, device synchronization, additional 
pneumatic configurations, new types of modules, official support for Python and Scratch, Integration with 
Unity Game Engine, active  locomotion, task scheduler with multi -device support, enclosure 
improvements, and dozens more which are tracked in a private GitHub repository.  
On the information front, there are currently many examples of projects made with FlowIO by the 
community, and there are also over a dozen code examples provided with the FlowIO libraries. However, 
what is still missing is a guided self-paced course about FlowIO designed to teach people how to use the 
device and every one of its features. Currently, the examples that exist are not the most pedagogically 
friendly, they may feel scattered, and they don’t cover all or even most of the features offered by the 
FlowIO Platform hardware and software. Thus, there are opportunities to create a set of pedagogical 
examples that systematically walk learners through all the features, capabilities, and possibilities offered 
by the hardware and by each layer of the software stack. Having these would not only be highly beneficial 
for users and contributors, but it would also lessen the burden of providing technical support.  
One other limitation was the absence of a structured process for onboarding and guiding volunteer 
contributors. While many individuals expressed interest in contributing—often without even requesting a 
device—the infrastructure to effectively direct and harness tho se contributions was not in place. An 
important area of future  work would also be designing systems, tools, and contribution workflows that 
better leverage this volunteer interest. 
 
9.2.5 HydroIO, MagnetoIO, TendonIO, ElectroIO, ThermoIO 
Several proposed sibling platforms  were directly inspired by FlowIO. Future work could focus on 
developing these companion platforms and integrating them into a modular family, enabling cross-domain 
actuation and sensing using a unified hardware and software interface. These projects offer promising 
avenues for realizing the vision of a comprehensive ecosystem for programmable materials and soft 
robotics. As discussed earlier, implementation of these sibling platforms would be a relatively simple 
process as only some modifications would be needed for the hardware and software that was already 
developed for the FlowIO Platform, as it was already made with the forethought and with appropriate 
design choice that these companion platforms will be added in the future.  
 
295 
 
9.2.6 SoftRobotics.IO 
SoftRobotics.IO was envisioned as an ecosystem that brings together tools, people, and resources 
that simpl ify and accelerate the process of soft robotics prototyping. It plays a central role in this 
dissertation not only as a dissemination and coordination hub for projects like FlowIO, but also as a testing 
ground for the Design & Deployment Framework itself.  While significant progress has been made in 
establishing a functioning ecosystem with a growing user base, community infrastructure, content, 
resources, and live web tools, there are still gaps to be addressed – relative to what the D&D Framework 
prescribes.  
SoftRobotics.IO currently satisfies the high-level component requirements of the D&D Framework—
including technology package , content, community, interaction, web infrastructure, and deployment 
model – but lacks many of the specific subcomponents and feedback loops that the framework specifies 
for a mature and self-reinforcing ecosystem. Specifically, some of the feedback loops between components 
are weak, informal, or manual, and additional infrastructure is needed to strengthen those feedback loops, 
formalize them, and automate them. Additionally, as discussed in the previous subsection, some of the 
content and resources are not provided in the most pedagogically -friendly way. Most of the platforms 
envisioned for this ecosystem are yet to be built. And the earned open -source deployment model is 
currently practiced manually rather than in an automated way. This is viable for small communities but 
becomes unsustainable as the user base grows. 
From a mission-oriented perspective, SoftRobotics.IO has significant growth opportunities to become 
one-place stop for nearly all soft robotic needs. It could evolve into an open-access marketplace for sharing 
all kinds of enabling resources across the soft robotics and related fields, including platforms, components, 
projects, designs, materials, fabrication techniques, equipment, informational resources, and even 
services and expertise.  
Ultimately, SoftRobotics.IO is itself evolving that reflects the philosophies explored throughout this 
dissertation. As more projects join the ecosystem and as new enabling tools are developed, the site must 
also adapt—becoming not just a host for those platforms, but a model for how platforms and ecosystems 
in other fields can be structured.  
 
9.2.7 SleeveIO 
SleeveIO was developed as a step towards addressing the gap in operability between different kinds 
of wearable haptic actuators. The key technical innovation in SleeveIO is the unified interface that enables 
a very wide range of actuators – of different types and even different actuation approaches– to be made 
compatible with one another via a common interface. Additionally, in its role as a platform, SleeveIO was 
designed to enable HCI researchers, interaction designers, and artists to create new kinds of haptic 
feedback interactions involving any combination of different actuator types. And it also allows end-users 
to modify and reconfigure existing haptic feedback interactions – by swapping actuator modules or by 
repositioning them anywhere along the forearm.  
The user studies conducted on SleeveIO had only tested and confirmed its viability as a haptic device, 
as well as providing actuator characterization snowflake diagram to help interaction designers know what 
type of actuator to use for what type of interaction (among the existing set of actuator modules). However, 
what hasn’t been done yet are longitudinal studies where a SleeveIO device is given to designers for them 
to experiment with on their own over the course of weeks or months.  
While there is currently a set of five different modules for SleeveIO, that set was made due to 
convenience, time pressure, and primarily as a way of demonstrating the versatility of the coupling 
mechanism and interfaces that SleeveIO brought to the world.  However, nearly a dozen additional 
actuator modules have been considered as possible additions to the current set, and numerous more 
modules are possible even beyond those already considered. In addition to extending the set of modules, 
296 
 
plans were also considered allow placement of modules not just on the forearm but also elsewhere on the 
body, and to make even entire haptic garments based on the SleeveIO principles. Should the SleeveIO 
project resume, these would be the two most immediate areas of technical future work. This would extend 
the range of possible interactions with SleeveIO would be expanded by at least an order of magnitude, at 
which point longitudinal studies could also begin.  
 The SleeveIO chapter also discussed other areas for future work such as specific applications that 
could be made with the device, further technical modifications to the coupling mechan ism and the 
substrate, upgrades to the control hardware and software, developing a graphical user interface specific 
for SleeveIO, creating other forms of haptic breadboards, embedding sensors into the haptic modules, and 
many more future work opportunities which we need not repeat here. 
 Finally, deployment is a whole other area of work that is yet to be done for SleeveIO. Much of the 
resources and know-how learned from FlowIO can be directly applied to SleeveIO’s deployment. In the 
case of SleeveIO, there will not be a need to create a new ecosystem around it because it will be integrated 
into the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem. And as a result, this would creat e positive outcomes for both the 
publicity of SleeveIO and the expansion of the SoftRobotics.IO community. This integration would apply 
not only in terms of add ing content to the website, but also in terms of bringing SleeveIO to demos, 
exhibitions, workshops, and hackathons – alongside FlowIO which is already doing that.  
 
9.2.8 ModiStrap 
Among the projects discussed in this dissertation, ModiStrap is the least mature one, and still in active 
development. As such additional works remains on the technical development front first to bring the 
technical aspect to satisfactory completion. This would mainly entail implementing at least a few of the 
application scenarios that were discussed in the ModiStrap chapter, then conducting evaluations and user 
studies, and publishing the complete project. The process of building out these applications may also lead 
to the discovery of new hardware and software feature  needs—each of which, in turn, could enable an 
even broader range of derivative applications . Engagement with users, using the focused  iterative 
approach discussed in 4:  would also provide very valuable insight into the needs of real users which would 
lead to further refinements, modifications, and additions to the toolkit . User studies would also help 
identify what forces are most desirable for connecting and for disconnecting the different modules, and 
then the coupling mechanism can be optimized for those forces accordingly. 
Beyond the publication, all the other 4 areas of the D&D framework are still open opportunities for 
future work – including posting content and resources, building a community, integrating ModiStrap into 
the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem, and deploying the toolkit. Further discussion about future work 
opportunities was provided in the corresponding chapter. 
 
9.2.9 Projects Enabled by FlowIO 
While there are no known immediate plans to continue further the development or deployment of 
the FlowIO-enabled projects discussed in this dissertation, there are plans to bring together all those prior 
projects (and future ones) more coherently on the So ftRobotics.IO website. Currently, the posts about 
those projects are written almost exclusively by the originators of those projects – and as such those posts 
are often incomplete, with very different structure and style, and most of them are not pedagogic ally-
friendly. Additionally, even though the website features many more projects than those discussed in this 
dissertation, that still doesn’t represent even half of all the FlowIO-enabled projects made to date. Thus, 
a planned direction for future work would be to first collect all the known projects and post them on the 
website, and second to write very readable and complete articles about each project, which also includes 
revising and improving the ones currently posted to the website. Furthermore, those articles would also 
then serve as examples for future users on how to better structure the posts or tutorials that they write 
297 
 
for the website. There could possibly even be a few templates that could be created which future authors 
can then follow.  
Besides user -generated projects, there are also plans to create many additional FlowIO -enabled 
projects in-house to fill the gaps that user -generated projects still miss. Specifically, many of the more 
advanced sensing, actuation, and software features offered by FlowIO have not yet been explored by the 
user community in published projects. As a result, most people don’t even know about the existence of 
those features – unless they do a deep dive into the documentation or the software stack itself. Having 
projects that demonstrate those features would make their existence much more known and more easily 
discoverable to those interested in FlowIO. 
 
9.3 Conclusion 
Inspired by the MIT Media Lab’s legacy of transformative interdisciplinary innovations with real-world 
relevance—and Motivated by goals such as maximizing impact across fields, accelerating technological 
progress, and democratizing prototyping and innovation opportunities in emerging technological fields—
this dissertation focused on development platforms and platform ecosystems  as one of  the highest -
leverage paths towards those objectives. It explored how development platforms accelerate innovation by 
lowering prototyping complexity, enabling faster idea -realization, lowering barriers to entry , inspiring 
novel creative ideas, and even giving rise to entirely new enabling artifacts in adjacent domains—tools, 
building blocks, or other platforms—which in turn propagate the same effects in a recursive cycle.  
This research  investigated best practices for designing and deploying hardware development 
platforms and entire ecosystems around them that integrate communities, physical and digital resources, 
infrastructure to facilitate online and offline interactions, equitable deployment strategies, engineered 
incentive structures that encourage contributions, and mechanism for positive feedback loops and that 
promote the adaptation, evolution, and growth of an ecosystem and every constituent within it.  The 
research questions driving this work centered around how enabling artifacts can be designed, deployed, 
and sustained in ways that optimize for wide reach, access, participation, and reuse. 
The development and deployment of the FlowIO Platform served as a pivotal vehicle for investigating 
these questions. FlowIO catalyzed many projects, application scenarios, and case studies – across fields – 
and it even enabled entirely new  toolkits or  platforms including SleeveIO and ModiStrap, while also 
inspiring ideas for sister platforms like Hydr oIO, ThermoIO, TendonIO, and more . The SoftRobotics.IO 
ecosystem initially emerged around FlowIO and then became more generalized to encompass a broader 
array of platforms and other enabling artifacts  and resources. Initially, the platform development and 
ecosystem building processes were informed by comparative analysis of well-known impactful hardware 
and software platforms as well as other ones that failed to deliver impact beyond their publication. Over 
time, this journey was further informed and refined  by directly encountering challenges, overcoming 
obstacles, engaging with users, and conducting case studies.  
Ultimately, these experiences collectively gave rise to a generalized and evolving framework for 
platform design and deployment, which co -evolved alongside the development and deployment of 
SoftRobotics.IO and everything encompassed within it. This D&D Framework emerged from a convergence 
of comparative analysis, direct experience, and iterative refinement. FlowIO, SleeveIO, ModiStrap , 
SoftRobotics.IO, and other works that preceded them and those that were enabled by them revealed first-
hand the challenges,  strategic decisions, and trade -offs involved in creating , deploying, sustaining,  
platforms, communities, and ecosystems. This framework was then used to guide the further development 
and evolution of those projects, which in turn further refined the model and especially the feedback loops 
therein. The D&D Framework  provides platform creators with a structured way to reason about 
modularity, stakeholder alignment, feedback loops, ecosystem scaffolding, naming strategies, community 
infrastructure, community engagement, content strategies, and  deployment pathways. This framework 
298 
 
has served not only as a retrospective lens but also as a forward-looking tool—helping to identify platform 
opportunities, guide development decisions, and diagnose limitations. 
This work also introduced a multitude of ideas around deployment practices and strategies. A focused 
deployment approach was proposed in which each iteration of a platform is deployed to two carefully 
selected users—one whose needs are fully met by the cur rent version and one whose needs are only 
partially met —allowing for both validation and guided iteration. The Earned Open -Source model was 
introduced as a middle ground between open and closed models, enabling access to source files to be 
earned through meaningful contributions rather than granted by default or withheld entirely. A content 
optimization strategy was developed to support deployment, emphasizing layered documentation, 
tutorials, project showcases, and contributor pathways as critical tools fo r lowering the barrier to entry 
and sustaining engagement. A hardware sharing program was explored as a way to extend access to high-
cost development platforms, enabling global reach through limited physical resources. The FlowIO 
platform and the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem served as active testing grounds for these ideas, providing 
real-world conditions to implement, observe, and refine these deployment ideas in practice. 
Beyond the individual contributions of each platform, model, or other component of this thesis, the 
broader aim of this work is to serve as a guide for platform designers—particularly those seeking to create 
tools with real-world relevance and lasting impact. The platforms developed in this dissertation, along with 
the SoftRobotics.IO ecosystem, are not just novel research contributions; they also function as well -
documented case studies that make visible the full arc of platform design and deployment. They showcase 
what it takes to build a platform that people actually use, and what is required to grow an ecosystem 
around it —from technical architecture to community scaffolding. This work offers a unique lens by 
presenting that process from many different perspectives  and directions including mechanical 
engineering, electrical engineering, product design, software development, user engagement, content 
strategy, digital and physical community engagement, online infrastructure for enabling and fostering 
community interactions , website development, deployment logistics, and  even nuanced interplay 
between all of these layers. In doing so, it reveals the complexiti es that must be navigated, the 
intentionality required, and the often-difficult decisions that must be made in the process of  impact-
oriented platform creation. The Design & Deployment Framework distills these insights into a higher-level 
recipe—a generalizable model that encapsulates the core principles, structural patte rns, and strategic 
decisions that emerged through these deeply interdisciplinary and real-world design journeys. 
Opportunities for further work and refinements still exist at every point in this research, as elaborated 
extensively in the previous section. And just as the physical, digital, and conceptual tools presented in this 
dissertation have been continuously evolving, that evolution is expected to continue—further advanced 
by the readers of this work. A call to action for any reader of this dissertation is to not only consider how 
these findings and ideas may apply in your own research or practice, but also to consider how your work 
can help improve and extend the findings, ideas, and philosophies of this dissertation —thus continuing 
the iterative, co-evolutionary cycle where theory and practice shape and inform one another. 
The recursive and co-evolutionary nature of this work also extends to the writing of this thesis  itself. 
As an astute reader might recall, 1:  concluded with a n invitation for readers to revisit and reread that 
chapter after having read the full thesis, as a way of understanding it from a new angle informed by the 
work of this thesis. This invitation has now become timely…

10: Appendix A 
A.1 Interview Questions for the Classroom-utility case studies 
- Can you provide me with the course description and syllabus? 
- How many students are there in the course total? 
- How many people wanted to use FlowIO? 
- How many had access to it? 
- How many groups of students? 
- How many people per group? 
- Project requirements? 
- Project duration and available time? 
- How were they introduced to FlowIO? For How long? 
- Was FlowIO presented as part of the official curriculum? And How?  
- How did they obtain FlowIO? 
- What other options did students have available besides FlowIO? 
- What did you or students wish was different about anything in the process? 
- What were the students’ backgrounds and demographics? 
- Course theme during the year FlowIO was used? 
- What modifications did people make to the hardware or software? 
- What difficulties did students encounter? 
- Were there students who started using FlowIO but then they ended up not using it? 
- Were they able to complete and demonstrate their projects successfully? 
- Any additional comments or feedback? 
- What projects did the students make? 
- Were they shown all the hardware & software features available? 
- Did students have access to all the FlowIO modules or only a subset of modules? 
  
Appendix  A 
302 
 
A.2 Pre-Assembly Survey Questions 
- How did you become interested in FlowIO? Please write a few sentences to elaborate. 
- What level of experience do you have with making or assembling electronic devices? Can you 
give at least one example and describe what that assembly process involved? 
- What do you hope to gain, if anything, form the process of assembling FlowIO yourself? Please 
answer in complete sentences. 
- Have you looked over the entire assembly tutorial to know in advance what the assembly 
entails? 
- How confident do you feel in being able to assemble FlowIO by yourself on a scale of 1 (low) to 5 
(high)? Are there any parts in the process that you feel less confident about and why? 
- Do you expect the assembly process to go smoothly and that the device to work as soon as you 
are done assembling it? Or do you expect to run into some challenges during or after assembly? 
What makes you have this expectation? 
- Will you be assembling FlowIO by yourself or getting help from someone? What kind of help? 
- How many hours of work do you expect the assembly process to take you? How did you arrive 
at this estimate? 
- From the day you start the assembly to the day you finish, how many days do you think will have 
passed (inclusive of the starting day, ending day, and days when you are not working)? How did 
you arrive at this estimate? 
  
303 
 
A.3 FlowIO Request Application Form - Questions 
 
Use-Case Proposal 
In 200 to 500 words, explain why you are 
requesting FlowIO. What do you hope to 
achieve? What will its impact be? How many 
pneumatic ports do you need? When do you 
need FlowIO and for how long? 
 
Contribution Statement 
What financial or skill -based contributions can 
you make to help sustain and further this 
initiative? 
 
Collaboration Statement 
Is your project open for collaboration and 
potential coauthorship with MIT Media Lab and 
the FlowIO creator specifically? Please elaborate. 
 
Assembled Device or Kit 
If we can't provide an assembled device but 
only parts, how comfortable would you feel 
assembling FlowIO yourself using the online 
tutorials? 
 
Type of Use  
- Non-commercial use 
- Commercial use 
 
Writing a Post 
Would you agree to write a blog post on the 
SoftRobotics.IO website about your project 
and/or a review about your experience with 
assembling / using FlowIO?  
- Absolutely! When do I start? 
- Yes, though I need to be pushed to do it. 
- Sure, but I won't actually do it.

reliability, durability and performance of FlowIO. 
Furthermore, any party involved in the 
development or distribution of FlowIO accepts no 
liability whatsoever for the sa fety, reliability, 
durability and performance of FlowIO. 
 
Evaluation 
Your request will be evaluated based on the 
description you provide. Since our resources are 
very limited, only a fraction of the requests 
received can be processed at this time. You can 
help this effort by becoming a contributor.
